1 Introduction: Why Optimal Transport in Actuarial Science?
1.1 Learning objectives
This introductory lecture explains why optimal transport is a useful language for actuarial science.
After reading it, the reader should be able to:
(i) recognize actuarial quantities as probability measures rather than only as numbers;
(ii) explain the difference between comparing expectations and comparing full laws;
(iii) use the probability integral transform and inverse transform sampling as basic rank–quantile
tools;
(iv) interpret a transport cost as an actuarial modelling decision;
(v) identify actuarial problems in which distributional movement is the main object;
(vi) understand, at a high level, the historical path from Monge’s earthmoving problem to
modern statistical, computational and actuarial uses of optimal transport;
(vii) read the rest of the notes as a sequence of tools to compare, transform, interpolate and
stress actuarial distributions.
Further reading
These notes use optimal transport as a geometry for actuarial distributions: losses, pre-
miums, reserves, capital scenarios, climate losses, scores and counterfactual outcomes.
General references for optimal transport include Villani 2003; Villani 2008; Ambrosio
et al. 2021; Figalli and Glaudo 2021. For computational optimal transport, see Peyré and
Cuturi 2019; Solomon 2018. For economic and econometric perspectives, see Galichon
2016; Galichon and Henry 2026. The classical line from Monge, Kantorovich and Brenier
is recalled in Section 1.5; see also Monge 1781; Kantorovich 1942; Brenier 1991; McCann
1997. For actuarial background on risk models, dependence and statistical insurance, see
Denuit et al. 2005a; Wüthrich and Merz 2023; Charpentier 2024.
1.2 Insurance is a distributional business
Insurance is often introduced through expectations. A pure premium is written as
π0 = E[L],
where L is a loss. In the rest of these notes, π(X) will usually denote a premium or tariff rule
depending on rating variables X, while π0 denotes a scalar pooled pure premium. A reserve is
sometimes described as a best estimate. A solvency capital requirement is sometimes reported
as a single number. A pricing model may be summarized by its average loss ratio or by a few
validation statistics.
This scalar view is necessary, but it is incomplete. In actuarial science, the basic object is
usually a probability law. A policy does not generate only an expected loss; it generates a full
distribution of possible losses. A portfolio is not described only by its average premium; it is
described by the distribution of premiums, losses, residuals, reserves and capital contributions
across policyholders and scenarios. A climate intervention is not only a change in mean loss; it
is a change in the whole loss distribution. A fairness correction is not only a change in group
means; it is a transformation of group-specific score distributions.
Throughout the notes, we write laws such as
µL = Law(L), µπ = Law(π(X)), µR = Law(R), R = (R1,...,RT ).
Page 13/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

Figure 1: Discrete and continuous actuarial measures. The same actuarial object can be
represented either by atoms, point clouds, histograms or densities. In optimal transport, both
the weights and the geometry of their support matter.
where L is a loss, X is a vector of rating variables, π(X) is a pricing rule, and R is a vector of
future cash-flows. Many actuarial questions can then be written as questions about how one law
is transformed into another.
Definition 1.1 (Actuarial law). An actuarial law is a probability measure µ∈P(X ) representing
the distribution of an actuarial object on a chosen state space X. The state space may be monetary,
such as X = R+ for claim severities or premiums; multivariate, such as X = RT + for cash-flow
vectors; or mixed, such as a product of numerical, categorical and geographical covariates. The
modelling choice is therefore twofold: the law µ and the geometry put onX.
Figure 1 illustrates this point: a law may be represented by atoms, a point cloud, a histogram
or a density, but optimal transport keeps track of both probability mass and location.
Actuarial takeaway
The basic unit of analysis in these notes is often a probability measure, not a scalar. A
change in underwriting, a tariff revision, a prevention policy, a climate scenario or a fairness
repair can be represented as a movement from one distribution to another.
1.3 A first transport problem
Let µ and ν be two probability measures on a space X. For example, µ may be the current
distribution of claim severities and ν the distribution after inflation, prevention or climate stress.
A transport plan describes how mass from µ is matched to mass from ν. If c(x,y) is the cost of
moving an actuarial statex into an actuarial statey, the optimal transport problem is
inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
X×X
c(x,y) dγ(x,y).
Here Π(µ,ν) denotes the set of all couplings of µ and ν, that is, all joint laws with first marginal
µ and second marginal ν. The coupling γ says which parts of the source distribution are matched
to which parts of the target distribution.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 14/416

=== page 15 ===
1.4 Two rank–quantile facts used throughout
This formula is deliberately abstract. Its actuarial meaning depends on the choice of the
state space and of the cost. The same notation will be made more formal in Lecture 2, where
couplings are introduced in detail, and in Lecture 4, where the continuous Kantorovich problem
is stated.
Definition 1.2 (Ground cost). A ground cost is a measurable function c : X×X→ [0, +∞] such
that c(x,y) measures the cost, effort or inadmissibility of changing state x into state y. When
c(x,y) = d(x,y)p for a metric d and p≥ 1, the resulting optimal value defines the Wasserstein
distance Wp. In actuarial applications, however, c may encode business, regulatory or physical
constraints and need not always be a pure metric cost.
• If x and y are claim amounts, c(x,y) =|x−y| measures average monetary displacement.
• If x and y are premium levels, c(x,y) =|x−y|2 penalizes large tariff movements.
• If x and y are policyholder profiles, c encodes which covariate changes are actuarially
meaningful.
• Ifx andy are cash-flow vectors, c may combine timing and amount discrepancies.
• If x and y are geographical locations, c may be built from physical distance, hazard distance
or administrative distance.
The cost is therefore not a technical detail. It is the modelling language of the problem.
Actuarial takeaway
Optimal transport is not only a way to compute a distance. It is a way to state which
distributional changes are considered small, large, reasonable, costly or inadmissible.
Actuarial takeaway
A transport map or a coupling aligns two distributions. It does not by itself identify
what would have happened to a given policyholder, contract, claim or scenario under an
intervention. Such an interpretation requires a structural model, an intervention design,
or additional identifying assumptions. This distinction will matter later for prevention,
fairness and counterfactual analysis.
1.4 Two rank–quantile facts used throughout
A large part of these notes relies on the link between ranks, quantiles and one-dimensional
transport. The following two elementary facts explain why a quantile map sends one distribution
to another.
Let F be a distribution function on R. We use the lower generalized inverse
F−1(u) = inf{x∈ R :F (x)≥u}, u∈ (0, 1).
When the distribution is denoted by µ, we write interchangeably Qµ(u) = F−1 µ (u) for its quantile
function. This convention is used throughout the notes.
Actuarial takeaway
Throughout the notes, Qµ denotes the lower generalized inverse. In particular, VaRα is
read in this lower-quantile sense unless stated otherwise. With atoms, ties or software
quantile conventions, an implementation may not coincide exactly with this mathematical
convention. Whenever empirical quantiles are used in a lab, the convention should be
stated.
Page 15/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 16 ===
1 INTRODUCTION: WHY OPTIMAL TRANSPORT IN ACTUARIAL SCIENCE?
Proposition 1.1 (Probability integral transform). Let X be a real-valued random variable with
continuous distribution function F. Then
U =F (X)
has the uniform distribution on [0, 1].
Proof. For u∈ [0, 1], continuity of F gives F (F−1(u)) = u, with the usual endpoint convention.
Hence, up to null boundary sets,
P(F (X)≤u) = P(X≤F−1(u)) =F (F−1(u)) =u.
Thus F (X)∼ Unif(0, 1).
Proposition 1.2 (Inverse transform sampling). Let U∼ Unif(0, 1), and let F be a distribution
function on R. Then
X =F−1(U)
has distribution function F.
Proof. For any x∈ R, by definition of the generalized inverse,
F−1(U)≤x ⇐⇒ U≤F (x)
up to endpoint sets of Lebesgue measure zero. Therefore
P(F−1(U)≤x) = P(U≤F (x)) =F (x).
Thus F−1(U) has distribution function F.
Combining the two facts gives the basic one-dimensional transport mechanism.
Definition 1.3 (Rank-preserving quantile map). Let µ and ν be probability measures on R,
with distribution functions Fµ and Fν. Their quantile coupling is
(
Qµ(U),Qν(U)
)
, U∼ Unif(0, 1),
where Qµ = F−1 µ and Qν = F−1 ν . If Fµ is continuous, this coupling can be represented by the
rank-preserving map
Tµ→ν(x) =Qν(Fµ(x)).
The map in Definition 1.3 sends a source observation to the target observation with the
same percentile. With atoms, the quantile coupling remains well defined, while the deterministic
map Qν ◦Fµ should be read with care; a pointwise deterministic representation may require a
convention for ties, and randomized ranks give the cleanest formulation. This distinction will
reappear in one-dimensional transport, fairness repair and longevity stresses.
1.5 A short history of optimal transport
Optimal transport began as a problem about moving physical material. The classical origin is
Monge’s eighteenth-century problem Monge 1781: given a pile of soil and a hole, how should one
move the soil so as to minimize total work? In modern notation, one looks for a map T that
sends a source distribution µ to a target distribution ν, and solves
min
T
∫
c(x,T (x)) dµ(x).
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 16/416

=== page 17 ===
1.5 A short history of optimal transport
This is now called the Monge problem. Its key feature is determinism: each source point x is
sent to a single destination T (x).
The Monge formulation is intuitive, but mathematically difficult. The constraint T#µ = ν is
nonlinear, admissible maps may fail to exist, and mass cannot be split. These difficulties also
arise in actuarial work. A rating cell may need to be split across several target cells. An atom at
zero claim may need to be partially moved and partially kept. A scenario may not have a unique
natural successor. Such situations are awkward in Monge’s deterministic formulation.
A decisive shift came with Kantorovich’s relaxation Kantorovich 1942. Instead of requiring a
map, one allows a transport plan γ, a joint distribution with prescribed marginals. The problem
becomes
inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
c(x,y) dγ(x,y).
This is linear in γ. In finite spaces, it is a linear program. Its dual introduces potentials, shadow
prices and sensitivity quantities. At this point, optimal transport becomes an optimization
problem as much as a geometric problem. For this reason, OT sits naturally between probability,
convex analysis, operations research and economics.
The next major step was to understand when Kantorovich’s relaxed problem returns a
deterministic map. For the quadratic cost on Rd, Brenier’s theorem Brenier 1991 shows that,
under absolute continuity of the source measure, the optimal plan is induced by a map of the
form
T (x) =∇φ(x),
where φ is convex. This result links optimal transport with convex analysis, partial differential
equations and the geometry of probability measures. In one dimension, the same phenomenon is
simpler: the optimal map is the monotone quantile map
T (x) =F−1 ν (Fµ(x)).
This formula is one of the practical facts used most often in these notes.
During the late twentieth century, Wasserstein distances became central in probability theory,
analysis and geometry. The optimal value for the cost d(x,y)p defines
Wp(µ,ν) =
(
inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
d(x,y)p dγ(x,y)
)1/p
.
This turns spaces of probability measures into metric spaces. Probability laws can then be
compared, interpolated, averaged and studied geometrically. This is why one can speak of
Wasserstein barycenters, Wasserstein balls, Wasserstein geodesics and Wasserstein gradients.
A second renaissance came from computation. Entropic regularization, revived in large-scale
data analysis by Cuturi 2013, replaces the linear transport problem by a smoother problem,
typically of the form
inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)



∫
c dγ +ε
∑
i,j
γij(logγij − 1)



in the discrete case. This leads to Sinkhorn algorithms, fast iterative scaling methods that can
handle much larger problems than generic linear programming. The computational viewpoint
made OT widely usable in machine learning, statistics, imaging, economics and data science.
For actuarial science, the timing is natural. Modern insurance increasingly works with
distributions produced by models: simulated capital scenarios, reserving predictive distributions,
climate loss distributions, telematics profiles, premium distributions, fairness audit distributions
and stress-test outputs. These objects are not only scalar summaries. They are probability
masses on structured spaces. Optimal transport provides tools to compare and transform them.
Page 17/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 18 ===
1 INTRODUCTION: WHY OPTIMAL TRANSPORT IN ACTUARIAL SCIENCE?
Actuarial takeaway
The historical movement from Monge to Kantorovich to Wasserstein to Sinkhorn is also
a movement from deterministic transformation to probabilistic matching, from geometry
to optimization, and from abstract theory to scalable computation. This combination is
useful for modern actuarial applications.
1.6 Why not only classical distances?
Before using optimal transport, one might ask why classical distances between probability
distributions are not enough. Total variation, Hellinger distance, Kullback–Leibler divergence
and Kolmogorov distance are important. They answer useful questions. But they often ignore
the geometry of the state space.
Definition 1.4 (Geometry-aware distributional distance). A distance or divergence between
laws is geometry-aware if its value depends not only on how much mass differs, but also on where
the mass is located in the underlying state space. Wasserstein distances are geometry-aware
because they are built from the ground cost of Definition 1.2.
For example, a small shift in all losses by one monetary unit may have a large impact under
some non-geometric distances if the distributions are singular or empirical. Wasserstein distance
recognizes that moving a claim from 1000 to 1001 is small, while moving it from 1000 to 100000
is large. This matters because the magnitude of the move is part of the actuarial question.
The following toy example is useful. Let
µ =δ0, ν =δε.
These two distributions are mutually singular whenever ε̸= 0, so total variation sees them as far
apart. But Wasserstein gives
Wp(µ,ν) =|ε|.
Thus Wasserstein distance is continuous with respect to physical or economic movement in the
underlying space.
Actuarial takeaway
For losses, premiums, scores, covariates and cash-flows, the state space has meaning. A
distance between distributions should often know whether two states are close or far. This
is the central advantage of optimal transport.
1.7 Running actuarial examples
The same mathematical objects will appear repeatedly in different actuarial contexts. The
following examples will guide the notes.
Severity and inflation. Let L be a claim severity and let µ = Law(L). Inflation, legal
changes or claims handling changes may move µ to a new distribution ν. In one dimension, the
rank-preserving stress map is
T (x) =F−1 ν (Fµ(x)).
This sends each baseline claim to the target claim at the same percentile. This example is
developed later in the lectures on continuous OT, Wasserstein quantiles and risk measures.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 18/416

=== page 19 ===
1.8 What optimal transport adds
Risk measures and robustness. Risk measures such as VaR, TVaR and spectral risk
measures are functionals of loss distributions. Wasserstein balls
{ν :Wp(ν,ˆµ)≤r}
allow one to ask how large a risk measure can become under a controlled perturbation of the
baseline model. This is the subject of the lecture on risk measures and Wasserstein robustness.
Portfolio drift and domain adaptation. Let µt = Law(Xt) and µt+1 = Law(Xt+1) be
portfolio covariate distributions at two dates. Wasserstein distance measures how much the
portfolio has moved in covariate space. Transport plans can also be used to reweight or match
portfolios. This is developed in the lecture on portfolio drift, calibration and domain adaptation.
Reserving and cash-flow distributions. A stochastic reserving model produces a distribution
of future cash-flow vectors (R1,...,RT ). OT compares complete predictive distributions, not
only best estimates. Costs can account for amount differences and timing differences. This is
developed in the lectures and labs on reserving and cash-flow distributions.
Climate risk and prevention. A prevention measure may move a baseline loss distribution
µ0 to a post-prevention distribution µ1. Wasserstein distance measures the size of the whole
distributional change. Dynamic OT and Schrödinger bridges then add a path from µ0 to µ1.
This connects to the lectures on reserving, climate prevention and dynamic transport.
Fairness and discrimination. Let S be a sensitive group variable and let Z be a score or
predicted premium. The group-specific laws Law(Z|S = s) can be compared using Wasserstein
distances. Transport maps can align these distributions with a reference law, producing a
distributional post-processing rule. This is developed in the fairness lecture and in the Wasserstein
fairness repair lab.
Reinsurance and dependence uncertainty. Aggregate losses depend on the coupling
between lines of business. With fixed marginal laws, different dependence structures lead to
different capital requirements. Multi-marginal transport provides a language for such problems;
this appears in the lecture on reinsurance, capital allocation and multi-marginal transport.
1.8 What optimal transport adds
Optimal transport adds six ingredients to the actuarial toolkit.
A geometry of distributions. The Wasserstein distance Wp(µ,ν) compares full distributions
while respecting the geometry of the state space. This is useful when the state is monetary,
geographical, temporal or multivariate.
A transformation map. When an optimal map exists, OT does not only give a distance. It
gives a transformation T such that T#µ = ν. This is useful for stress testing, calibration and
counterfactual analysis, subject to the caution above about causal interpretation.
A coupling or audit trail. When no deterministic map is appropriate, OT gives a plan γ.
This plan records how source mass is matched to target mass. In actuarial applications, this can
be read as an audit trail for portfolio migration, tariff change, reserve scenario transformation or
climate stress.
Page 19/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 20 ===
1 INTRODUCTION: WHY OPTIMAL TRANSPORT IN ACTUARIAL SCIENCE?
A robustness device. Wasserstein balls define neighbourhoods around empirical or fitted
laws. They allow actuaries to compute worst-case expectations, prudent premiums and robust
capital over plausible distributional perturbations; see, for example, Esfahani and Kuhn 2018;
Blanchet and Murthy 2019 for distributionally robust optimization with Wasserstein ambiguity
sets.
A barycenter. Wasserstein barycenters provide a way to average probability laws without
washing out their geometry Agueh and Carlier 2011. They will be used for consensus scenarios,
common reference distributions and mortality-scenario summaries.
A path. Displacement interpolation, dynamic optimal transport and Schrödinger bridges
provide trajectories between laws McCann 1997; Benamou and Brenier 2000; Léonard 2014.
They are useful when the actuarial question is not only how far two distributions are, but how
one distribution could evolve into another over time.
Actuarial takeaway
Optimal transport is useful because it gives a distance, a map, a coupling, a path, a
barycenter and a robustness neighbourhood. These become actuarial objects once losses,
premiums, reserves and scores are treated as distributions.
1.9 How the notes are organized
The notes are organized in three layers.
Mathematical foundations. The first lectures introduce couplings, push-forwards, discrete
optimal transport, continuous Kantorovich duality, Wasserstein distances, barycenters, entropic
regularization and statistical aspects. These lectures provide the common language used through-
out the applications.
Actuarial applications. The middle part studies risk measures, robust capital, portfolio
drift, reserving, climate prevention, fairness, reinsurance, capital allocation and longevity. Each
application uses the same mathematical objects in a different state space with a different cost.
Extensions and modelling choices. The later lectures and appendices cover counterfactual
transport, dynamic OT, unbalanced OT, longevity transport, sliced Wasserstein distances, cost
engineering and computational labs. These sections emphasize modelling choices: which cost,
which radius, which reference distribution, which path and which diagnostic.
1.10 A small dictionary
1.11 What to remember
The guiding message of these notes is that actuarial science is increasingly distributional. We do
not only estimate means; we compare laws. We do not only report capital numbers; we analyze
loss distributions. We do not only revise premiums; we study premium distributions. We do
not only state fairness metrics; we compare group-specific score laws. We do not only compare
climate scenarios; we construct paths between them.
Optimal transport provides a coherent mathematical language for these tasks. It starts from
a simple question: how should one move mass from one distribution to another? The rest of the
notes explore how far this question can go in actuarial science.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 20/416

1.12 Exercises
Exercise 1.1 (Distributional objects). Give three actuarial quantities that are often reported as
scalars but are more naturally viewed as probability distributions. For each quantity, specify the
underlying state space.
Exercise 1.2 (Cost as modelling choice). Using the language of ground costs from Definition 1.2,
suppose x and y are two premium levels. Compare the actuarial meaning of c(x,y) = |x−y|,
c(x,y) = |x−y|2, and c(x,y) = | logx− logy|. Which one is most appropriate for measuring
premium disruption?
Exercise 1.3 (Wasserstein versus total variation). Let µ = δ0 and ν = δε on R. Compute
W1(µ,ν). Compare with total variation distance. Explain which answer is more natural for a
monetary displacement.
Exercise 1.4 (A first quantile stress). Let µ be an exponential distribution with rate λ and ν
an exponential distribution with rate θ. Derive the rank-preserving map T (x) = F−1 ν (Fµ(x)).
Interpret the case θ<λ as a severity stress.
Exercise 1.5 (Scalar versus distributional comparison). Construct two distributions with the
same mean but different dispersion. Explain why comparing only expected losses misses an
actuarial difference that Wasserstein distance can detect.
Exercise 1.6 (Running example). Choose one of the running examples in Section 1.7. Identify
the source distribution, the target distribution, the state space and a reasonable first choice of
ground cost.

=== page 22 ===
2 COUPLINGS, PUSH-FORWARDS AND INSURANCE DEPENDENCE
2 Couplings, Push-Forwards and Insurance Dependence
Learning objectives
This lecture sets up the probabilistic language used throughout the notes. After this lecture, the
reader should be able to:
(i) describe actuarial transformations as push-forwards of probability measures;
(ii) distinguish marginal modelling from dependence modelling;
(iii) manipulate the set of couplings Π(µ,ν) and interpret it as a feasible set;
(iv) use deterministic maps, stochastic kernels, quantile couplings and copulas as different
representations of dependence;
(v) explain why aggregation, capital and reinsurance problems are often problems over couplings.
Further reading
The probabilistic notation used here is standard in optimal transport; see Villani 2003;
Villani 2008; Ambrosio et al. 2021; Figalli and Glaudo 2021. For background on couplings,
weak convergence and regular conditional probabilities, see also Billingsley 1999; Kallenberg
2021. For computational and statistical perspectives, see Peyré and Cuturi 2019; Chewi
et al. 2025. The actuarial motivation from dependence, risk orders and aggregation is
closely related to Denuit et al. 2005a; Dhaene et al. 2002a; Embrechts et al. 2002. For
copulas and Fréchet classes, see Sklar 1959; Nelsen 2006; Joe 1997. These notes use
couplings rather than copulas as the primary language because couplings are the feasible
objects in Kantorovich’s formulation of optimal transport, and because couplings keep the
original actuarial state space on which a ground cost is defined.
2.1 From marginal distributions to joint models
Insurance modelling often begins with marginal distributions. A motor book may have a severity
distribution for bodily injury, another distribution for property damage, a frequency model for
claims counts and a distribution of policyholder covariates. Reserving models provide predictive
distributions for future cash-flows. Climate models provide distributions of event intensities and
losses. Pricing models provide distributions of premiums across a portfolio; if Π(X) denotes the
tariff assigned to covariatesX, the premium law is typically Law(Π(X)).
Optimal transport treats a distribution not only as an object to be estimated, but also as an
object that can be transformed, compared, interpolated and constrained. The first operation
we need is the push-forward: a probability law is transported through a measurable map. The
second is the coupling: two probability laws are embedded into a joint law. Push-forwards
describe how distributions change under transformations; couplings describe how marginal laws
can be made jointly compatible.
The distinction is actuarially important. Knowing the marginal distributions of two lines
of business does not determine the distribution of their aggregate loss. Knowing the marginal
distributions of current and future losses does not determine a scenario path. All these missing
pieces are couplings.
2.2 Measurable spaces and push-forwards
Let (X,B(X )) and (Y,B(Y)) be measurable spaces. In most of these notes, X and Y will be
Polish spaces, that is, complete separable metric spaces, equipped with their Borel σ-fields.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 22/416

=== page 23 ===
2.3 Couplings
This assumption covers most actuarial applications on Rd and gives the regular conditional
probabilities and compactness properties used below.
Definition 2.1 (Push-forward). Let µ ∈P(X ), and let T : X →Y be measurable. The
push-forward of µ by T, denoted T#µ, is the probability measure onY defined by
T#µ(B) =µ(T−1(B)), B∈B(Y).
Equivalently, if X∼µ, then T (X)∼T#µ.
Push-forwards formalize the basic statistical operation “apply a model to a random input”. If
X is a vector of rating variables and Π(X) is a premium, then Law(Π(X)) = Π# Law(X). If L
is a gross loss and T (L) = (L−d)+ ∧u is an insured payment with deductible d and limit u,
then Law(T (L)) =T# Law(L).
Proposition 2.1 (Change of variables for push-forwards). Let T : X→Y be measurable and let
µ∈P(X ). For every nonnegative measurable function φ :Y→ [0,∞],
∫
Y
φ(y) (T#µ)( dy) =
∫
X
φ(T (x))µ( dx).
The same identity holds for any integrable real-valued φ.
Proof. This is the standard change-of-variables formula for image measures; see, for example,
the measure-theoretic preliminaries in Villani 2008 and the introductory material in Ambrosio
et al. 2021. Sketch of the proof: first prove the identity for indicators φ = 1B, where it is
exactly the definition of T#µ. Extend by linearity to nonnegative simple functions. Then use
monotone convergence for nonnegative measurable functions, and finally decompose an integrable
real-valued function into positive and negative parts.
Example 2.1 (Deductible and policy limit). Let L≥ 0 be a claim amount with law µ, deductible
d≥ 0, and policy limit u∈ (0,∞]. The insurer’s payment is
T (L) = (L−d)+ ∧u.
The distribution of the insured payment is T#µ. This distribution may have atoms at 0 and at
u, even when µ is continuous. A policy transformation changes the full probability measure, not
only its mean or tail summaries.
Example 2.2 (Distribution of premiums). Let X be policyholder covariates and let Π : X→ R+
be a pricing rule. The premium distribution in the portfolio is
µprem = Law(Π(X)) = Π# Law(X).
Replacing Π0 by Π1 moves the portfolio from (Π0)# Law(X) to (Π1)# Law(X). Later, Wasser-
stein distances will measure how far this tariff change moves the whole premium distribution,
not just its mean.
2.3 Couplings
Let µ∈P(X ) and ν∈P(Y). Let p1(x,y) = x and p2(x,y) = y be the canonical projections
fromX×Y.
Definition 2.2 (Coupling). A coupling of µ and ν is a probability measure γ∈P(X×Y) such
that
(p1)#γ =µ, (p2)#γ =ν.
The set of all such couplings is denoted by
Π(µ,ν) =
{
γ∈P(X×Y) : (p1)#γ =µ, (p2)#γ =ν
}
.
Page 23/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 24 ===
2 COUPLINGS, PUSH-FORWARDS AND INSURANCE DEPENDENCE
Definition 2.3 (Product coupling). The product coupling of µ and ν is the product measure
µ⊗ν∈ Π(µ,ν). If (X,Y )∼µ⊗ν, then X∼µ, Y ∼ν, and X and Y are independent.
Equivalently, γ∈ Π(µ,ν) if
γ(A×Y) =µ(A), γ(X×B) =ν(B)
for all measurable A⊆X, B⊆Y. If (X,Y ) ∼γ, then X∼µ and Y ∼ν. Conversely, the joint
law of any pair (X,Y ) with these marginals is a coupling.
The terminology varies across the literature. Villani uses transference plan; probabilists often
say joint distribution with fixed marginals; actuaries may say dependence model or Fréchet class
element. These are the same objects.
Proposition 2.2 (Non-emptiness and convexity). For any µ∈P(X ) and ν∈P(Y), the set
Π(µ,ν) is non-empty and convex.
Proof. Non-emptiness follows because the product measure µ⊗ν has marginals µ and ν. For
convexity, let γ0,γ1 ∈ Π(µ,ν) and θ∈ [0, 1]. Then γθ = (1 −θ)γ0 + θγ1 is a probability measure,
and for every measurable A⊆X,
γθ(A×Y) = (1−θ)µ(A) +θµ(A) =µ(A).
The same argument gives the second marginal ν.
Remark 2.1 (Independence is one coupling, not the coupling). The product coupling µ⊗ν
corresponds to independence. It is often chosen for convenience, but it is only one feasible
element of Π(µ,ν). In capital modelling, replacing independence by another coupling can leave
all marginal calibrations unchanged while substantially changing the tail of the aggregate loss.
Proposition 2.3 (Compactness under weak convergence). Assume that X and Y are Polish
spaces. Then Π(µ,ν) is tight and closed in P(X×Y) for the topology of weak convergence.
Together with Prokhorov’s theorem, this implies that Π(µ,ν) is compact for weak convergence.
Proof. This is Lemma 4.4 and the compactness argument in the proof of Theorem 4.1 of Villani
2008. Sketch of the proof: the singleton sets {µ} and {ν} are tight. Hence, for every ε> 0, there
exist compact sets Kε ⊆X and Lε ⊆Y such that µ(Kc ε) ≤ε and ν(Lc ε) ≤ε. For any γ∈ Π(µ,ν),
γ
(
(Kε ×Lε)c) ≤γ(Kc ε ×Y) +γ(X×Lc ε)≤ 2ε.
This proves tightness. Closedness follows because weak convergence preserves marginals: if
γn ⇒γ, then for every bounded continuous f onX,
∫
f(x)γ( dx, dy) = limn→∞
∫
f(x)γn( dx, dy) =
∫
f dµ,
and similarly for ν.
Actuarial takeaway
A coupling is a dependence model with fixed marginals. In actuarial language, Π(µ,ν) is
the feasible set of all joint models compatible with the two marginal calibrations. Optimal
transport adds a cost function and selects one coupling by optimization.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 24/416

=== page 25 ===
2.4 Deterministic couplings and transport maps
2.4 Deterministic couplings and transport maps
Recall the push-forward notation from Definition 2.1. A special class of couplings is induced by
measurable maps. If T : X→Y satisfies T#µ = ν, then the joint law of (X,T (X)), with X∼µ,
is a coupling. Such couplings are concentrated on the graph of T. They represent deterministic
transport: once the source point is known, the target point is known.
Proposition 2.4 (Graph coupling). Let T :X→Y be measurable. Then
T#µ =ν ⇐⇒ (Id,T )#µ∈ Π(µ,ν).
Moreover, (Id,T )#µ is concentrated on the graph{(x,T (x)) :x∈X}.
Proof. This is the standard graph-plan construction used in Monge’s problem; see Chapter 1 of
Villani 2003, Chapter 1 of Figalli and Glaudo 2021, and Ambrosio et al. 2021. Sketch of the
proof: set γ = (Id,T )#µ. For measurable A⊆X,
γ(A×Y) =µ{x : (x,T (x))∈A×Y} =µ(A).
For measurable B⊆Y,
γ(X×B) =µ{x :T (x)∈B} =T#µ(B).
Thus the second marginal is ν exactly when T#µ = ν. Concentration on the graph follows from
(x,T (x))∈ graph(T ) for µ-almost every x.
Not every coupling is induced by a map. A map cannot split one source point into several
possibletargetpoints. Kantorovich’srelaxationreplacesdeterministicmapsbyarbitrarycouplings,
allowing mass splitting.
Example 2.3 (Splitting a risk class). Suppose a group of policyholders with current premium x
is split into two possible adjusted premiums y1 and y2, with probabilities depending on x. Unless
one probability is zero, this is not a deterministic map T (x). It is naturally a kernel K(x, dy),
hence a coupling γ( dx, dy) =µ( dx)K(x, dy).
2.5 Couplings as stochastic kernels
A coupling can be read globally as a joint distribution or locally as a transition rule. The local
description uses Markov kernels and disintegration.
Definition 2.4 (Markov kernel). Let (X,B(X )) and (Y,B(Y)) be measurable spaces. A Markov
kernel fromX toY is a map
K :X×B(Y)→ [0, 1]
such that, for each x∈X, K(x,·) is a probability measure on Y, and for each B∈B(Y), the
map x↦→K(x,B) is measurable.
Theorem 2.1 (Disintegration of a coupling). Let X and Y be Polish spaces, and let γ∈ Π(µ,ν).
Then there exists a Markov kernel K :X×B(Y)→ [0, 1] such that
γ( dx, dy) =µ( dx)K(x, dy),
meaning that for every nonnegative measurable h,
∫
X×Y
h(x,y)γ( dx, dy) =
∫
X
[∫
Y
h(x,y)K(x, dy)
]
µ( dx).
The second marginal condition becomes
ν(B) =
∫
X
K(x,B)µ( dx), B∈B(Y).
Page 25/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 26 ===
2 COUPLINGS, PUSH-FORWARDS AND INSURANCE DEPENDENCE
Proof. This is the standard disintegration theorem for probability measures on Polish spaces; see
the measure-theoretic appendix of Villani 2008 and the preliminaries in Ambrosio et al. 2021.
Sketch of the proof: since X and Y are standard Borel spaces, regular conditional probabilities
exist. Apply this to the coordinate projection (X,Y ) ↦→ X under γ. The conditional law of
Y given X = x is the kernel K(x,·). Integrating conditional expectations gives the displayed
identity.
The deterministic case is recovered by K(x, dy) = δT (x)( dy). Thus kernels interpolate
between deterministic transport maps and arbitrary stochastic reallocations.
Actuarial takeaway
A coupling may be read as an audit trail. The kernel K(x, dy) says how mass starting at
actuarial state x is redistributed toward possible target states y. This is useful for portfolio
migration, climate scenario transformation and stress testing.
2.6 Basic couplings on the real line
The real line is special because quantile functions provide explicit couplings. As in Definition 1.3,
let Fµ(x) = µ((−∞,x]) be the distribution function of µ∈P(R). We use the generalized inverse
Qµ(u) =F−1 µ (u) = inf{x∈ R :Fµ(x)≥u}, u∈ (0, 1),
as its quantile function. This is the same lower-quantile convention as in Lecture 1; it is important
when atoms are present.
Proposition 2.5 (Quantile representation). Let U∼ Unif(0, 1). Then Qµ(U) ∼µ. Consequently,
for µ,ν∈P(R), (
Qµ(U),Qν(U)
)
induces a coupling of µ and ν.
Proof. This is the classical quantile transform, used repeatedly in one-dimensional optimal
transport; see Chapter 2 of Villani 2003, Chapter 2 of Figalli and Glaudo 2021, and Ambrosio
et al. 2021. Sketch of the proof: by the definition of the generalized inverse,
Qµ(u)≤x ⇐⇒ u≤Fµ(x)
with the usual endpoint convention for the lower generalized inverse. Hence
P(Qµ(U)≤x) = P(U≤Fµ(x)) =Fµ(x).
The two coordinates of the displayed pair therefore have marginal laws µ and ν.
The coupling
X =Qµ(U), Y =Qν(U)
is called the comonotonic or increasing coupling. High quantiles are matched with high quantiles.
The coupling
X =Qµ(U), Y =Qν(1−U)
is the countermonotonic or decreasing coupling in dimension two. It matches high quantiles of
one marginal with low quantiles of the other.
Definition 2.5 (Comonotonicity). A pair (X,Y ) is comonotonic if there exists a random variable
U and nondecreasing functions f,g such that X = f(U) and Y = g(U). For one-dimensional
marginals, the quantile construction with a common uniform random variable is the canonical
comonotonic coupling.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 26/416

2.7 Aggregation as a push-forward of a coupling
Example 2.4 (Severity dependence). Let X be a bodily injury severity and Y a property
damage severity. The marginal fits Law(X) = µ, Law(Y ) = ν do not determine the law of X + Y.
The independent, comonotonic and countermonotonic couplings have the same marginal models
but different aggregate tails. This is a basic reason dependence modelling matters for solvency
capital.
2.7 Aggregation as a push-forward of a coupling
Let A : R2 → R be the aggregation map A(x,y) = x + y. If γ∈ Π(µ,ν), then the aggregate loss
distribution is
Law(X +Y ) =A#γ, (X,Y )∼γ.
Thus the set of aggregate-loss distributions compatible with two fixed marginal models is
A(µ,ν) ={A#γ :γ∈ Π(µ,ν)}.
Proposition 2.6 (Marginals do not identify aggregate risk). Let µ and ν be probability measures
on R+. The law of X +Y is generally not determined by µ and ν. More precisely, it is determined
by the choice of γ∈ Π(µ,ν) through A#γ.
Proof. This is a direct consequence of the push-forward formalism, and it is the dependence
viewpoint developed in actuarial risk theory; see Denuit et al. 2005a; Dhaene et al. 2002a;
Embrechts et al. 2002. Sharp aggregation bounds under fixed marginals are also related to
Makarov 1981; Rüschendorf 1982a. Sketch of the proof: if (X,Y ) ∼γ, then Proposition 2.1 gives
Law(X + Y ) = A#γ. Different couplings can give different push-forward laws. For instance, if
both marginals are Bernoulli(1/2), then under the comonotonic coupling X = Y, X + Y ∈{0, 2},
whereas under the countermonotonic coupling Y = 1−X, X +Y = 1 almost surely.
If ρ is a law-invariant risk measure, dependence uncertainty can be written as an optimization
problem over couplings:
sup
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
ρ(A#γ), inf γ∈Π(µ,ν) ρ(A#γ).
These problems have the same feasible set as optimal transport. They become optimal transport
problems when the objective can be written as
∫
c(x,y)γ( dx, dy) for a cost function c.
Example 2.5 (Capital with fixed line marginals). Suppose L1 ∼µ1 and L2 ∼µ2 are calibrated
line-of-business losses. The solvency capital for L1 + L2, such as VaR99.5%(L1 + L2), is not
determined by µ1 and µ2 alone. It depends on γ∈ Π(µ1,µ2). Independence is a modelling
assumption. Comonotonicity is a conservative benchmark. Neither is forced by the marginal
calibrations.
2.8 Fréchet classes and copulas
For fixed marginals µ1,...,µd, the coupling notation extends directly.
Definition 2.6 (Fréchet class). Let µk ∈P(Xk), k = 1,...,d. The Fréchet class with marginals
µ1,...,µd is
Π(µ1,...,µd) ={γ∈P(X1 ×···×Xd) : (pk)#γ =µk, k = 1,...,d}.
It is the set of all joint laws compatible with the prescribed marginal models.
Definition 2.7 (Copula). A d-dimensional copula is a distribution function on [0, 1]d whose
one-dimensional marginals are uniform on [0, 1]. Equivalently, it is the joint law of a random
vector (U1,...,Ud) such that each Uk ∼ Unif(0, 1).
Page 27/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 28 ===
2 COUPLINGS, PUSH-FORWARDS AND INSURANCE DEPENDENCE
Copulas provide another representation of elements of the Fréchet class when Xk = R.
The representation is unique when the marginal distribution functions are continuous; without
continuity, copulas still exist but need not be unique.
Theorem 2.2 (Sklar representation, informal form). Let H be a joint distribution function on
Rd with continuous marginals F1,...,Fd. Then there exists a unique copula C such that
H(x1,...,xd) =C(F1(x1),...,Fd(xd)).
Conversely, any copula C combined with marginals F1,...,Fd defines a joint distribution with
those marginals.
Proof. This is Sklar’s theorem Sklar 1959; in an actuarial dependence context, see Denuit et
al. 2005a; Nelsen 2006; Joe 1997. Sketch of the proof: when the marginals are continuous,
define Uk = Fk(Xk). Each Uk is uniform on [0, 1], and the joint law of (U1,...,Ud) is the
copula C. Conversely, if (U1,...,Ud) ∼ C and Qk = F−1 k denotes the generalized inverse,
then (Q1(U1),...,Qd(Ud)) has marginals F1,...,Fd. Uniqueness follows from continuity of the
marginals.
Remark 2.2 (Couplings versus copulas). Copulas separate marginal distributions from dependence
by transforming variables to ranks. Couplings keep the original state spaces and therefore allow
a cost c(x,y) to depend on actual levels. This matters in insurance: moving a loss from 100 to
120 is not the same actuarial operation as moving a loss from 10,000 to 10,020, even if the rank
displacement is similar.
2.9 Fréchet–Hoeffding bounds
For two real-valued random variables with distribution functions F and G, every joint distribution
function H with these marginals satisfies the Fréchet–Hoeffding bounds
max{F (x) +G(y)− 1, 0}≤H(x,y)≤ min{F (x),G(y)}.
The upper bound is attained by the comonotonic coupling. For two marginals, the lower bound
is attained by the countermonotonic coupling. In dimensions larger than two, the lower Fréchet–
Hoeffding expression is not always itself a copula, so pairwise intuition should not be transferred
mechanically to all multivariate settings.
Theorem 2.3 (Fréchet–Hoeffding bounds). Let (X,Y ) have marginal distribution functions F
and G, and joint distribution function H. Then, for all (x,y)∈ R2,
max{F (x) +G(y)− 1, 0}≤H(x,y)≤ min{F (x),G(y)}.
Both bounds are sharp.
Proof. This is the classical Fréchet–Hoeffding bound for bivariate distributions; see Fréchet 1951;
Hoeffding 1940 and the dependence treatment in Denuit et al. 2005a; Nelsen 2006. Sketch of the
proof: since {X≤ x,Y ≤ y}⊆{X≤ x} and similarly for Y, we have H(x,y) ≤ F (x) ∧G(y).
For the lower bound, use
P(A∩B) = 1− P(Ac ∪Bc)≥ 1− P(Ac)− P(Bc) = P(A) + P(B)− 1
with A = {X≤ x} and B = {Y ≤ y}, and also H≥ 0. Sharpness follows from the quantile
couplings (F−1(U),G−1(U)) and (F−1(U),G−1(1 − U)), with the usual generalized-inverse
conventions.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 28/416

2.10 Expectation objectives over couplings
2.10 Expectation objectives over couplings
Many dependence problems have the form
sup
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
X×Y
h(x,y)γ( dx, dy)
or the corresponding infimum. This is the Kantorovich problem with cost −h or h. The next
statement records the elementary variational structure.
Proposition 2.7 (Linear objectives over couplings). Let h : X×Y→ R be bounded measurable.
The map
γ↦−→
∫
h dγ
is affine on Π(µ,ν). If h is upper semicontinuous and bounded above, then the supremum
over Π(µ,ν) is attained under the Polish-space assumptions of Proposition 2.3; if h is lower
semicontinuous and bounded below, then the infimum is attained.
Proof. This is the same compactness and semicontinuity mechanism as Theorem 4.1 of Villani
2008; see also the Kantorovich formulation in Villani 2003. Sketch of the proof: affinity follows
from linearity of integration. For existence, apply compactness of Π(µ,ν) from Proposition 2.3.
If h is lower semicontinuous and bounded below, γ↦→
∫
h dγ is lower semicontinuous by the
portmanteau theorem. Thus a minimizing sequence has a weakly convergent subsequence, and
the limit attains the infimum. The upper semicontinuous case follows by applying the same
argument to−h.
Example 2.6 (Maximum covariance with fixed marginals). For square-integrable real marginals µ
and ν, maximizing E[XY ] over Π(µ,ν) is an optimal transport problem with reward h(x,y) = xy.
Indimensionone, theincreasingquantilecouplingmaximizesthisexpectation, whilethedecreasing
quantile coupling minimizes it under suitable integrability. This is the rearrangement principle
behind comonotonic extremality.
Proposition 2.8 (Rearrangement principle on the line). Let µ,ν∈P(R) have finite second
moments. Then
sup
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
xyγ( dx, dy) =
∫ 1
0
Qµ(u)Qν(u) du,
and
inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
xyγ( dx, dy) =
∫ 1
0
Qµ(u)Qν(1−u) du.
Proof. This is the Hardy–Littlewood rearrangement inequality Hardy et al. 1952, which is also the
one-dimensional optimal transport structure discussed in Villani 2003; Figalli and Glaudo 2021.
Sketch of the proof: for the supremum, approximate the marginals by empirical distributions
with equal weights. The finite-dimensional result is the classical rearrangement inequality: two
sorted sequences maximize the scalar product, while opposite sorting minimizes it. Passing to
quantile limits gives the formulas. An alternative proof uses cyclical monotonicity for the cost
c(x,y) = −xy: optimality requires that mass is supported on a monotone set, which is exactly
the increasing rearrangement in one dimension.
2.11 A complete two-point example
Let X∼ Bernoulli(p) and Y ∼ Bernoulli(q), with state space {0, 1}. A coupling is a 2 × 2 table
Y = 0 Y = 1
X = 0 γ00 γ01 1−p
X = 1 γ10 γ11 p
1−q q 1
Page 29/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 30 ===
2 COUPLINGS, PUSH-FORWARDS AND INSURANCE DEPENDENCE
The table is determined by a =γ11. The constraints imply
γ10 =p−a, γ01 =q−a, γ00 = 1−p−q +a.
Nonnegativity gives
max{0,p +q− 1}≤a≤ min{p,q}.
Thus Π(µ,ν) is an interval. The lower endpoint is the strongest negative dependence compatible
with the marginals; the upper endpoint is the comonotonic endpoint.
If S =X +Y, then
P(S = 2) =a, P(S = 1) =p +q− 2a, P(S = 0) = 1−p−q +a.
The aggregate distribution varies linearly with the coupling parameter a. The marginals are
fixed throughout.
Actuarial takeaway
This two-point example is the smallest possible model of dependence uncertainty. It is
also the finite-dimensional picture behind discrete optimal transport: the feasible set is a
transportation polytope, the objective is linear in the entries of γ, and optimal solutions
often live on faces or extreme points of that polytope.
2.12 Empirical couplings
In applications we often work with empirical measures
ˆµn =
n∑
i=1
aiδxi, ˆνm =
m∑
j=1
bjδyj,
where ai,bj ≥ 0, ∑ iai = ∑ j bj = 1. A coupling is then a nonnegative matrix Γ = (γij) satisfying
m∑
j=1
γij =ai,
n∑
i=1
γij =bj.
This is the transportation polytope. It is the object studied in detail in the next lecture.
Proposition 2.9 (Empirical couplings are matrices). Let ˆµn = ∑ iaiδxi and ˆνm = ∑ j bjδyj.
Every coupling γ∈ Π(ˆµn, ˆνm) can be written as
γ =
n∑
i=1
m∑
j=1
γijδ(xi,yj),
where Γ = (γij) is a nonnegative matrix with row sums ai and column sums bj. Conversely, every
such matrix defines a coupling.
Proof. This is the finite-support specialization of Kantorovich’s transference plans; see the discrete
formulation in Peyré and Cuturi 2019 and Solomon 2018. Sketch of the proof: since the first
marginal is supported on {xi} and the second on {yj}, the coupling is supported on {xi}×{yj}.
Set γij = γ({(xi,yj)}). The marginal constraints are exactly the row and column sum equations.
The converse follows by direct summation.

2.13 Mini-lab: three couplings, three aggregate losses
2.13 Mini-lab: three couplings, three aggregate losses
The following short R code illustrates the same marginals under three different couplings.
set.seed(123)
n <- 20000
# two severity marginals
qX <- function(u) qlnorm(u, meanlog = 8.0, sdlog = 0.7)
qY <- function(u) qlnorm(u, meanlog = 7.5, sdlog = 0.9)
u <- runif(n)
# comonotonic
X _com <- qX(u)
Y _com <- qY(u)
S _com <- X _com + Y _com
# countermonotonic
X _ctr <- qX(u)
Y _ctr <- qY(1-u)
S _ctr <- X _ctr + Y _ctr
# independent
X _ind <- qX( runif(n))
Y _ind <- qY( runif(n))
S _ind <- X _ind + Y _ind
rbind(
independent = quantile(S _ind, c(.95, .99, .995)),
comonotonic = quantile(S _com, c(.95, .99, .995)),
countermonotone = quantile(S _ctr, c(.95, .99, .995))
)
The output shows that the high quantiles of the aggregate are typically much larger under
comonotonicity than under countermonotonicity. The marginal quantiles of X and Y are
unchanged. This small experiment is a preview of the later sections on risk aggregation,
reinsurance and capital allocation, and of the computational labs where empirical couplings are
represented by matrices.
2.14 Couplings as scenario design
Couplings are not only tools for bivariate dependence. They also provide a language for scenario
design.
• A coupling between today’s portfolio distribution and next year’s portfolio distribution
describes a migration scenario.
• A coupling between baseline climate losses and post-prevention losses describes how losses
are displaced by an intervention.
• A coupling between simulated reserving cash-flow vectors and stress cash-flow vectors
describes how a stress test moves the distribution of liabilities.
In each case, the source and target marginal distributions are not enough. The coupling
records which source states are matched to which target states. Optimal transport will select this
matching by minimizing a cost. These examples are developed later in the lectures on portfolio
drift and domain adaptation, reserving and cash-flow distributions, counterfactual worlds, and
reinsurance/capital allocation.
Page 31/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

with two marginals; see Definition 2.2

2.15 A warning: coupling is not causality
A coupling is a joint probability measure with fixed marginals. It does not, by itself, define
a structural causal mechanism. For instance, a coupling between a baseline loss L0 and a
post-intervention loss L1 may be useful for measuring distributional displacement, but it does not
prove that a given policyholder’s loss would have changed from L0 to L1. Causal interpretation
requires additional assumptions, such as a structural model, ignorability, rank preservation or an
explicit intervention design; see, for example, the structural-causal perspective in Pearl 2009;
Peters et al. 2017.
Actuarial takeaway
A coupling aligns distributions. It does not identify unit-level effects unless additional
causal assumptions are supplied.
This warning will matter later for prevention and counterfactual modelling. Wasserstein
maps can provide useful distributional attributions, but individual counterfactual claims require
more structure than a coupling alone.
2.16 Small dictionary for actuarial use
2.17 From couplings to optimal transport
The next lecture adds a ground cost, in the sense of Definition 1.2, c : X×Y→ R∪{+∞} and
studies
inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
X×Y
c(x,y)γ( dx, dy).
The coupling set is the feasible set; the cost selects the preferred dependence or matching. In
actuarial language, optimal transport asks: among all ways to match two actuarial distributions,
which one has the smallest total transformation cost?

3 DISCRETE OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
3 Discrete Optimal Transport
Learning objectives
This lecture develops optimal transport in its most concrete form: a finite-dimensional linear
program. This is the right entry point for actuarial applications because many data sets are
empirical, grouped, tabulated or discretized. Claims are observed as samples; rating variables
are grouped into cells; reserving triangles are arrays; scenario models are simulated particles.
After this lecture, the reader should be able to:
(i) write the transportation problem between two discrete probability measures;
(ii) identify transport plans with matrices satisfying row and column constraints;
(iii) interpret the transportation polytope as a set of dependence models;
(iv) derive the linear-programming dual and interpret the dual variables;
(v) use complementary slackness to understand the support of an optimal plan;
(vi) solve by hand small examples, especially the 2× 2 case;
(vii) recognize special cases such as assignment, matching, one-dimensional monotone transport
and empirical Wasserstein distances;
(viii) connect discrete OT to actuarial questions: portfolio drift, reserving scenarios and risk
aggregation.
Further reading
This chapter is the finite-dimensional entry point into optimal transport. For computational
and geometric introductions to discrete OT, see Solomon 2018; Peyré and Cuturi 2019;
Santambrogio 2015. The classical transportation and assignment problems are rooted in
linear programming and network-flow theory; see Hitchcock 1941; Kantorovich 1942; Ahuja
et al. 1993; Schrijver 2003. The assignment special case relies on the Birkhoff–von Neumann
theorem and is closely related to the Hungarian and auction algorithms Birkhoff 1946;
Kuhn 1955; Munkres 1957; Bertsekas 1992. Actuarial links with dependence uncertainty
and aggregation are developed in Denuit et al. 2005a; Dhaene et al. 2002a; Embrechts
et al. 2002; Puccetti and Rüschendorf 2012.
Actuarial takeaway
Discrete optimal transport is not merely a numerical approximation to a continuous theory.
It is also a natural actuarial object. A portfolio is a finite population, a rating grid is finite,
a reserving triangle is a finite array, and a simulation output is a finite cloud of particles.
In all these cases, OT begins as a matrix problem.
3.1 Why start with the discrete problem?
Let µ and ν be two probability measures. In the previous lecture, we introduced the set of
couplings Π(µ,ν); see Definition 2.2. A coupling specifies how mass from µ is paired with mass
from ν. Optimal transport attaches a cost to each possible pairing and selects a coupling with
minimal total cost.
When µ and ν have finite support, this becomes a linear program. This is useful for three
reasons.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 34/416

=== page 35 ===
3.2 Discrete probability measures
First, the notation is transparent. A transport plan is a matrix. Its row sums and column
sums are prescribed. The cost is the scalar product between a cost matrix and the transport
matrix.
Second, the economic interpretation is immediate. The rows are origins, the columns are
destinations, and γij is the amount of mass moved from origin i to destination j. In insurance,
origins and destinations can be loss levels, rating classes, reserve scenarios, premium bins,
climate-risk zones or risk classes.
Third, the finite problem contains the main ideas of the general theory: primal and dual
formulations, shadow prices, complementary slackness, geometry of feasible couplings, and
computational algorithms. More advanced topics, such as entropic regularization, Sinkhorn
scaling and Wasserstein barycenters, are easier to understand once the discrete problem is clear.
Standard presentations of computational OT follow this route; see for example Solomon 2018;
Peyré and Cuturi 2019.
3.2 Discrete probability measures
Definition 3.1 (Discrete probability measure). A probability measure µ is discrete with finite
support if there are support points x1,...,xn and weights a1,...,an ≥ 0, with ∑ iai = 1, such
that
µ =
n∑
i=1
aiδxi.
The vector a = (a1,...,an)⊤ records probability masses, whereas the support points xi carry
the geometry of the actuarial state space.
Let X = {x1,...,xn} and Y = {y1,...,ym} be two finite subsets of a space, often R or Rd.
We consider two discrete probability measures, in the sense of Definition 3.1,
µ =
n∑
i=1
aiδxi, ν =
m∑
j=1
bjδyj,
where
ai ≥ 0, bj ≥ 0,
n∑
i=1
ai = 1,
m∑
j=1
bj = 1.
We write a = (a1,...,an)⊤∈ Rn and b = (b1,...,bm)⊤∈ Rm. Thus a and b are probability
vectors.
The support points xi and yj contain the geometry or actuarial meaning of the problem.
The weights ai and bj contain the probability masses. For instance:
• xi and yj may be observed claim amounts in two years;
• they may be representative rating-cell centroids in a finite rating grid;
• they may be simulated aggregate losses under two climate scenarios;
• they may be reserve cash-flow vectors produced by two stochastic reserving methods.
Example 3.1 (Empirical distributions). If X1,...,Xn and Y 1,...,Y m are two samples, their
empirical laws are
ˆµn = 1 n n∑ i=1 δXi, ˆνm = 1 m m∑ j=1 δYj.
This corresponds to ai = 1/n and bj = 1/m. In actuarial work, the observations may be individual
claims, policies, simulated losses, reserve outcomes or other simulated actuarial quantities.
Page 35/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 36 ===
3 DISCRETE OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
Example 3.2 (Grouped loss distributions). Suppose claim amounts are grouped into bins
I1,...,In, with bin midpoints xi. If the proportion of claims in bin i is ai, we approximate the
severity distribution by ∑ iaiδxi. Comparing two such grouped distributions is a natural discrete
OT problem.
3.3 Transport plans as matrices
A coupling γ∈ Π(µ,ν) is a probability measure on X×Y. Since X×Y is finite, γ is fully
described by the numbers
γij =γ({xi}×{yj}), i = 1,...,n, j = 1,...,m.
Thus γ is an n×m matrix Γ = (γij).
The marginal constraints are simply
m∑
j=1
γij =ai, i = 1,...,n,
and
n∑
i=1
γij =bj, j = 1,...,m.
Together with γij ≥ 0, these equations define the transportation polytope of Definition 3.2.
Definition 3.2 (Transportation polytope). For probability vectors a∈ Rn and b∈ Rm, the
transportation polytope is
U(a,b) =
{
Γ∈ Rn×m + : Γ1m =a, Γ⊤1n =b } .
Equivalently,U(a,b) is the set of all couplings between µ = ∑ iaiδxi and ν = ∑ j bjδyj.
The notation U(a,b) is common in computational OT. It emphasizes that the feasible set
depends only on the weights, not on the locationsxi,yj, nor on the cost function.
Proposition 3.1 (Basic properties). The set U(a,b) is non-empty, convex and compact. If all
weights are strictly positive, its affine dimension is
(n− 1)(m− 1).
Proof. Non-emptiness follows from the independent coupling Γ = ab⊤, whose entries are γij =
aibj. Convexity follows because the constraints defining U(a,b) are linear and the inequalities
are convex. Compactness follows because 0≤γij ≤ 1 and the set is closed.
There are nm variables. The row and column constraints give n + m linear equations, but
one is redundant because both total masses equal one. Hence the affine hull has codimension
n + m− 1, and the dimension is nm− (n + m− 1) = (n− 1)(m− 1), provided no marginal
constraint is degenerate.
Remark 3.1 (Dependence, not only transportation). In actuarial risk aggregation, U(a,b) is the
set of all joint distributions with fixed marginals. Thus a transport plan is also a dependence
model. The same matrix Γ may be read as a shipping plan, a matching, a contingency table, a
copula discretization, or a joint risk model.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 36/416

=== page 37 ===
3.4 The discrete optimal transport problem
3.4 The discrete optimal transport problem
Let c : X×Y→ R be a cost function. Recall from Definition 1.2 that the ground cost encodes
the modelling meaning of moving one actuarial state to another.
Definition 3.3 (Cost matrix). Given support points x1,...,xn and y1,...,ym, the cost matrix
associated with a ground cost c is the matrix C∈ Rn×m defined by
Cij =c(xi,yj), i = 1,...,n, j = 1,...,m.
The expected transportation cost under plan Γ is
n∑
i=1
m∑
j=1
Cijγij =⟨C, Γ⟩.
Definition 3.4 (Discrete optimal transport). The discrete optimal transport problem between
µ and ν, with cost matrix C, is
OTC(a,b) = min Γ∈U(a,b) ⟨C, Γ⟩
Any minimizer Γ⋆ ∈U(a,b) is called an optimal transport plan. This is the finite-support version
of the Kantorovich problem introduced at the end of Lecture 2.
When c(x,y) = d(x,y)p for a metric d and p≥ 1, the value of the problem gives the
p-Wasserstein distance:
Wp(µ,ν)p = min Γ∈U(a,b) ∑ i,j d(xi,yj)pγij.
For p = 1, the cost is linear in distance. For p = 2, the quadratic cost penalizes large movements
more strongly and is central in the geometry of Wasserstein space.
Example 3.3 (Portfolio drift). Let xi ∈ Rd be representative profiles in a portfolio at time t,
and letyj ∈ Rd be representative profiles at time t + 1. We may take
c(xi,yj) =∥xi −yj∥2.
Then OTC(a,b) measures how much the portfolio distribution has moved. If coordinates include
age, exposure, insured value, past claims and territory risk, the value is a distributional drift
metric.
3.5 A network-flow interpretation
The discrete transport problem can be represented as a minimum-cost flow on a bipartite network.
We have source nodes i = 1,...,n, target nodes j = 1,...,m, and an arc from every source i to
every target j. Source i supplies mass ai, target j demands mass bj, and sending one unit of
mass from i to j costs Cij.
x1
x2
x3
y1
y2
a1
a2
a3
b1
b2
arc cost Cij
This interpretation is especially useful for algorithms. The transportation problem is a special
case of minimum-cost flow, which explains why network simplex and related combinatorial
algorithms are often efficient. It also clarifies the connection with classical operations research
and with the assignment problem. For background on finite-dimensional optimization and
network-flow algorithms, see Ahuja et al. 1993; Schrijver 2003; Galichon 2016; Solomon 2018.
Page 37/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 38 ===
3 DISCRETE OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
3.6 Existence of an optimal plan
In the discrete setting, existence is immediate.
Proposition 3.2 (Existence). For any finite cost matrix C∈ Rn×m, the discrete OT problem
admits at least one optimal plan.
Proof. The map Γ ↦→⟨C, Γ⟩ is continuous and linear. The feasible set U(a,b) is compact and
non-empty. Therefore the minimum is attained.
Uniqueness is more delicate. The optimal value is unique, but the optimal plan need not be.
Because the problem is linear, if two different plans are optimal, any convex combination of them
is also optimal. Non-uniqueness occurs naturally when the cost contains symmetries or when
mass can be split at the same cost.
Example 3.4 (Non-uniqueness). Leta =b = (1/2, 1/2) and
C =
(
0 0
0 0
)
.
Every feasible transport plan is optimal. The cost does not distinguish among dependence
structures.
3.7 The 2× 2 transportation problem
The 2× 2 case is worth solving explicitly. Let
a = (α, 1−α), b = (β, 1−β),
with 0≤α,β≤ 1, and let
C =
(
c11 c12
c21 c22
)
.
A feasible transport plan has the form
Γ(t) =
(
t α−t
β−t 1−α−β +t
)
,
where non-negativity imposes
max{0,α +β− 1}≤t≤ min{α,β}.
The cost is
L(t) =c11t +c12(α−t) +c21(β−t) +c22(1−α−β +t)
= constant +t (c11 −c12 −c21 +c22).
Thus the optimum is always at one endpoint, unless
c11 +c22 =c12 +c21,
in which case every feasible t is optimal.
Actuarial takeaway
In a two-risk aggregation problem with fixed marginals, all dependence structures are
indexed by a single number t. The sign of c11 −c12 −c21 + c22 decides whether the optimal
coupling pushes toward positive or negative dependence. This is the finite version of the
rearrangement principle that later appears as monotone transport on the real line.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 38/416

3.8 The dual problem
3.8 The dual problem
The primal problem is
minimize
n∑
i=1
m∑
j=1
Cijγij
subject to
m∑
j=1
γij =ai, i = 1,...,n,
n∑
i=1
γij =bj, j = 1,...,m,
γij ≥ 0.
We now derive its dual. Introduce Lagrange multipliers ui for the row constraints and vj for the
column constraints. Formally, the dual variables are constrained by
ui +vj ≤Cij.
The dual objective is
n∑
i=1
aiui +
m∑
j=1
bjvj.
Theorem 3.1 (Kantorovich duality: finite case). The value of the discrete OT problem is equal
to
max
u∈Rn,v∈Rm



n∑
i=1
aiui +
m∑
j=1
bjvj :ui +vj ≤Cij ∀i,j


.
Moreover, the maximum is attained.
Proof. This is the linear-programming dual of the primal transportation problem. Weak duality
follows directly: for any feasible Γ and any feasible (u,v),
∑
i
aiui +
∑
j
bjvj =
∑
i,j
γij(ui +vj)≤
∑
i,j
γijCij.
Taking the supremum over feasible (u,v) and the infimum over feasible Γ gives dual value ≤
primal value. Since the primal is a feasible finite-dimensional linear program with finite optimum,
strong linear-programming duality yields equality and existence of a dual optimizer.
The components ui and vj of the dual vectors u and v are called Kantorovich potentials.
They are not unique: adding a constant α to all ui and subtracting it from all vj leaves both
constraints and objective unchanged.
Remark 3.2 (Insurance interpretation of potentials). Suppose xi are source portfolio states and
yj are target portfolio states. The potential ui can be interpreted as the shadow value of one
additional unit of mass at origin xi; vj is the shadow value of one additional unit of demand
at destination yj. In reserving or portfolio comparison, these potentials measure marginal
distributional pressure: where mass is expensive to remove and where mass is expensive to create.
3.9 Complementary slackness
The dual constraints say
ui +vj ≤Cij.
At an optimal pair (Γ⋆,u⋆,v⋆), complementary slackness states that mass can move only along
tight edges.
Page 39/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 40 ===
3 DISCRETE OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
Proposition 3.3 (Complementary slackness). Let Γ⋆ be primal optimal and (u⋆,v⋆) dual optimal.
Then
γ⋆ ij > 0 =⇒ u⋆ i +v⋆ j =Cij.
Equivalently,
γ⋆ ij ( Cij −u⋆ i −v⋆ j ) = 0 ∀i,j.
Proof. By strong duality,
∑
i,j
γ⋆ ijCij = ∑
i
aiu⋆ i + ∑
j
bjv⋆ j = ∑
i,j
γ⋆ ij(u⋆ i +v⋆ j ).
Therefore ∑
i,j
γ⋆ ij ( Cij −u⋆ i −v⋆ j ) = 0.
Each term is non-negative because Γ⋆ ≥ 0 and (u⋆,v⋆) is dual feasible. Hence every term must
vanish.
Complementary slackness is often the simplest way to understand an optimal plan. The
potentials define a set of admissible active edges, and the optimal plan routes mass only through
those edges.
3.10 The discrete c-transform
Given a vectoru∈ Rn, define its discrete c-transform by
(uc)j = min 1≤i≤n {Cij −ui}, j = 1,...,m.
Then ui + (uc)j ≤Cij for all i,j, so (u,uc) is dual feasible. Conversely, if (u,v) is feasible, then
vj ≤ (uc)j for all j. Since the dual objective is increasing in the relevant components of v, at
optimum one may takev =uc.
Similarly, forv∈ Rm, define
(vc)i = min 1≤j≤m {Cij −vj}.
At optimum, potentials can be chosen to satisfy the mutual relations
v =uc, u =vc.
These are the finite analogues of c-concavity in the general Kantorovich theory.
Computational note
In computations, the potentials are often as useful as the transport plan. For large problems,
storing Γ may be expensive, while the potentials u and v contain information about active
constraints, sensitivity and marginal values.
3.11 Special case: the assignment problem
Suppose n =m, and both measures are uniform:
ai =bj = 1 n.
A transport plan Γ ∈U(a,b) is a doubly stochastic matrix scaled by 1/n. The next classical
result explains why this case reduces to matching.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 40/416

=== page 41 ===
3.12 One-dimensional discrete transport
Theorem 3.2 (Birkhoff–von Neumann theorem, scaled form). The extreme points of the polytope
of doubly stochastic n×n matrices are the permutation matrices. Equivalently, the extreme points
ofU(a,b), when ai =bj = 1/n, are the permutation matrices scaled by 1/n.
Since the objective is linear, at least one optimum is attained at an extreme point. Hence
there exists an optimal transport plan corresponding to a matching:
γi,σ(i) = 1 n, γij = 0 otherwise.
The OT problem becomes
min
σ∈Sn
1
n
n∑
i=1
Ci,σ(i).
This is the classical linear assignment problem; see Kuhn 1955; Munkres 1957; Bertsekas 1992;
Burkard et al. 2009.
Example 3.5 (Matching simulated scenarios). Suppose two stochastic capital models produce n
equally weighted scenarios x1,...,xn and y1,...,yn, where each scenario is a vector of line-of-
business losses. With Cij = ∥xi −yj∥2, the assignment problem matches scenarios from the first
model to scenarios from the second model. The resulting cost measures the discrepancy between
the two simulated distributions.
Remark 3.3 (Splitting versus matching). When weights are not uniform or when n̸= m, mass
may split. In insurance this is natural: one rating cell may be redistributed across several new
cells, or one loss bin may contribute mass to several target bins.
3.12 One-dimensional discrete transport
When xi,yj ∈ R and c(x,y) = |x−y|p, p≥ 1, the optimal plan is monotone: mass should not
cross. This fact is the discrete form of monotone rearrangement and will be used again for
quantile formulas and risk measures.
Assume x1 ≤···≤xn and y1 ≤···≤ym. A plan Γ has a crossing if there exist i<k and
j >ℓ such that γij > 0 and γkℓ > 0. In words, some lower source point is matched to a higher
target point while a higher source point is matched to a lower target point.
Proposition 3.4 (No-crossing property). Let c(x,y) = |x−y|p, p≥ 1, on R. There exists an
optimal transport plan with no crossings.
Sketch. Suppose i<k and j >ℓ. Move a small mass ε> 0 from the crossed pairs (xi,yj), (xk,yℓ)
to the uncrossed pairs (xi,yℓ), (xk,yj). The row and column sums are unchanged. Convexity of
z↦→|z|p, or the Monge property of the cost matrix, gives
|xi −yj|p +|xk −yℓ|p ≥|xi −yℓ|p +|xk −yj|p.
Thus uncrossing does not increase cost. Repeating this operation eliminates crossings.
This gives a simple greedy algorithm: match the smallest remaining xi with the smallest
remaining yj, move as much mass as possible, then continue.
Computational note
For one-dimensional empirical distributions, exact Wp can be computed by sorting and
matching quantiles. This is much faster and more stable than solving a generic linear
program. It is also the bridge between OT and actuarial quantile methods.
Page 41/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 42 ===
3 DISCRETE OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
3.13 Empirical Wasserstein distances
Definition 3.5 (Empirical Wasserstein distance). Let X1,...,Xn and Y 1,...,Y m be two
samples in Rd, and let
ˆµn = 1 n n∑ i=1 δXi, ˆνm = 1 m m∑ j=1 δYj.
The empirical Wasserstein distance is the Wasserstein distance between these empirical probability
measures.
With the notation of Definition 3.5, it is obtained by solving
Wp(ˆµn, ˆνm)p = min Γ∈U(a,b)
n∑
i=1
m∑
j=1
∥Xi −Y j∥pγij,
where ai = 1/n and bj = 1/m. If n = m, this is closely related to matching; if n̸= m, mass
splitting is generally needed.
For actuarial data, empirical OT distances can be used to compare:
• claim severity samples between accident years;
• loss-ratio distributions between regions;
• simulated aggregate-loss distributions under baseline and stress scenarios;
• reserve cash-flow scenarios from two reserving models;
Example 3.6 (Two years of claim severities). Let X1,...,Xn be claims from year t and
Y1,...,Ym claims from year t + 1. If claims are one-dimensional and p = 1, W1 measures an
average absolute displacement between quantiles. It is sensitive to distributional shifts, not only
to changes in mean severity.
3.14 Discrete OT and risk aggregation
A transport plan can be pushed forward through an aggregation map. Let (X,Y ) ∼ Γ, where
X∼µ and Y ∼ν. The aggregate loss is
S =X +Y.
The distribution of S depends on Γ, even when µ and ν are fixed. In finite form,
Law(S) =
n∑
i=1
m∑
j=1
γijδxi+yj.
Thus the set U(a,b) represents all possible aggregate-loss distributions consistent with the
marginal severity models.
This is a classical actuarial theme: dependence uncertainty can dominate marginal uncertainty.
Optimal transport offers a unified language for optimization over dependence structures.
Example 3.7 (Worst-case expected stop-loss). Let S = X + Y and consider the stop-loss
function
ℓd(S) = (S−d)+.
Among all couplings Γ∈U(a,b), the largest expected stop-loss is
max
Γ∈U(a,b)
∑
i,j
(xi +yj −d)+γij.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 42/416

=== page 43 ===
3.15 Discrete OT as a linear program in standard form
This is a linear program over the transportation polytope. It is not written as a minimum
transport problem, but it is the same object with cost
Cij =−(xi +yj −d)+.
The corresponding minimization gives the worst case.
Actuarial takeaway
Discrete OT is a flexible way to optimize actuarial quantities under fixed marginals.
Depending on the cost, it can compute best-case or worst-case dependence, minimum
distributional displacement, or robust risk bounds.
3.15 Discrete OT as a linear program in standard form
It is sometimes useful to vectorize the transport matrix. Let
γ = vec(Γ)∈ Rnm, c = vec(C)∈ Rnm.
The constraints can be written as
Aγ =r, r =
(
a
b
)
, γ≥ 0,
where A is the node-arc incidence matrix of the complete bipartite graph, except that one
redundant row may be removed. The primal problem becomes
min
γ≥0
c⊤γ subject to Aγ =r.
This form is helpful for implementing the problem using generic linear-programming solvers.
It also connects the problem to convex optimization and duality, as in standard treatments of
linear programming and convex optimization Galichon 2016; Peyré and Cuturi 2019.
3.16 Computational approaches
There are several ways to solve the discrete OT problem.
Generic linear programming
For small and medium problems, one may use a generic linear-programming solver. This is
convenient and reliable, but the number of variables is nm. With two samples of size 10,000, the
transport matrix has 108 entries, which is already too large for naive dense methods.
Network simplex and minimum-cost flow
Because the transportation problem is a network-flow problem, specialized algorithms can exploit
the bipartite structure. Network simplex methods are often effective for exact solutions on sparse
or moderate-size problems.
Assignment algorithms
When n = m and all weights are equal, the problem becomes an assignment problem. Specialized
algorithms such as the Hungarian algorithm or auction algorithms can be used.
Page 43/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 44 ===
3 DISCRETE OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
One-dimensional sorting
On the real line with convex distance cost, sorting gives the solution. This is the fastest and
most interpretable case, and it is especially relevant for actuarial quantities based on quantiles.
Entropic regularization
For large-scale problems, one often solves the regularized problem
min
Γ∈U(a,b)
⟨C, Γ⟩ +ε
∑
i,j
γij(logγij − 1)
with ε> 0, using the convention 0 log 0 = 0. This leads to Sinkhorn’s algorithm, which will be
studied in the lecture on entropic regularization. Regularization makes the problem smooth and
computationally scalable, at the cost of introducing bias; see Cuturi 2013; Peyré and Cuturi
2019.
Computational note
Exact OT is often best for interpretation and small examples. Entropic OT is often best
for large actuarial portfolios, simulations and repeated computations. The two should be
viewed as complementary tools.
3.17 Mini-lab: computing discrete OT in R
The following code computes a small discrete OT problem using a generic linear-programming
solver. It is intentionally written in transparent matrix form rather than optimized form. This
template will reappear in the later labs on empirical Wasserstein noise and reserving cash-flow
distributions.
# install.packages("lpSolve")
library(lpSolve)
x <- c(0, 1, 4)
y <- c(0, 2)
a <- c(0.30, 0.40, 0.30)
b <- c(0.55, 0.45)
C <- outer(x, y, function(u, v) (u - v)^2)
n <- length(x)
m <- length(y)
# variables are gamma _11, gamma _21, ..., gamma _n1,
# then gamma _12, ..., gamma _nm under R’s column-major order
obj <- as. vector( C)
# row constraints: sum _j gamma _ij = a _i
A _ row <- matrix(0, nrow = n, ncol = n *m)
for(i in 1:n){
for(j in 1:m){
A _ row[i, i + (j-1) *n] <- 1
}
}
# column constraints: sum _i gamma _ij = b _j
A _ col <- matrix(0, nrow = m, ncol = n *m)
for(j in 1:m){

for(i in 1:n){
A _ col[j, i + (j-1) *n] <- 1
}
}
A <- rbind(A _ row, A _ col)

For one-dimensional empirical distributions with equal sample sizes, we can compute Wp by
sorting.
set.seed(123)
X <- rgamma(1000, shape = 2, rate = 1)
Y <- rgamma(1000, shape = 3, rate = 1)
p <- 2
Wp _p <- mean( abs( sort(X) - sort(Y))^p)
Wp <- Wp _p^(1 /p)
Wp
3.18 What the discrete problem teaches us
The discrete problem gives several lessons that will remain valid throughout the notes.
(i) OT is optimization over couplings.
(ii) A coupling is a dependence model with fixed marginals.
(iii) The primal object is a transport plan; the dual object is a pair of potentials.
(iv) Geometry enters through the cost matrix C, not through the feasible setU(a,b).
(v) In one dimension, optimal transport is monotone and connected to quantiles.
(vi) In high dimension, computation becomes the central difficulty.
(vii) Actuarial applications often begin with empirical or grouped distributions, so the discrete
formulation is not optional; it is the working model.
Further reading
For a computational introduction to discrete OT, see Solomon 2018; Peyré and Cuturi
2019; Santambrogio 2015. For the economic interpretation of duality and assignment,
see Galichon 2016. For network-flow and assignment algorithms, see Ahuja et al. 1993;
Schrijver 2003; Burkard et al. 2009. For actuarial dependence and risk aggregation, see
Denuit et al. 2005a; Dhaene et al. 2002a; Embrechts et al. 2002; Puccetti and Rüschendorf
2012.

transport plan has the form
Γ(t) =

)
for
max{0,α +β− 1}≤t≤ min{α,β}.
For a generic cost matrix C, determine which endpoint is optimal.

.
Find an optimal primal plan and optimal dual potentials (u,v). Verify complementary slackness.
Exercise 3.3 (Independence is usually not optimal). Let Γind = ab⊤. Give a numerical example
with n = m = 2 where Γind is not optimal for the cost Cij = |xi −yj|2. Compute the cost gap
between the independent plan and the optimal plan.
Exercise 3.4 (No-crossing argument). Let x1 <x2 and y1 <y2. Show that for c(x,y) = |x−y|p,
p≥ 1,
c(x1,y1) +c(x2,y2)≤c(x1,y2) +c(x2,y1).
Interpret this inequality as saying that crossing transport is suboptimal.
Exercise 3.5 (Worst-case stop-loss). Let
µ = 0.5δ0 + 0.5δ10, ν = 0.5δ0 + 0.5δ10.
Among all couplings of µ and ν, maximize

Solve the problem by enumerating the 2 × 2 transportation plans. Which dependence structure
is worst?
Exercise 3.6 (Empirical computation). Simulate two samples of size n = 500 from lognormal

change in optimal value with the difference in the corresponding dual potentials.
