=== page 47 ===
4 Continuous Optimal Transport and Kantorovich Duality
Learning objectives
The previous lecture presented optimal transport as a finite-dimensional linear program. This
lecture moves to the continuous setting. The main point is not to abandon the discrete viewpoint,
but to understand the population-level object that the discrete problem approximates. In
actuarial science, the continuous formulation is natural when losses, covariates, capital scenarios
or cash-flow vectors are modelled by probability laws rather than by finite histograms.
After this lecture, the reader should be able to:
(i) distinguish the Monge formulation from the Kantorovich relaxation;
(ii) interpret push-forward constraints as distributional constraints;
(iii) understand why mass splitting is necessary in general;
(iv) state the Kantorovich primal and dual problems;
(v) prove weak duality and interpret dual potentials;
(vi) use the c-transform and complementary slackness in continuous form;
(vii) identify the one-dimensional optimal map through quantiles;
(viii) state the quadratic-cost Brenier theorem and understand its actuarial meaning;
(ix) recognize continuous OT models in severity modelling, portfolio drift, climate stress testing,
reserving and dependence analysis.
Further reading
This chapter is the population-level counterpart of the discrete formulation in Lecture 3.
The Monge and Kantorovich formulations go back to Monge 1781; Kantorovich 1942;
modern accounts of existence, duality and optimal maps include Villani 2003; Villani 2008;
Ambrosio et al. 2021; Santambrogio 2015; Figalli and Glaudo 2021. The quadratic-cost map
is tied to Brenier’s theorem Brenier 1991, McCann’s displacement interpolation McCann
1997, and convex analysis in the sense of Rockafellar 1970. Computational and statistical
viewpoints are developed in Peyré and Cuturi 2019; Panaretos and Zemel 2020. Actuarial
uses below should be read together with the later chapters on risk measures, drift, reserving,
reinsurance and dependence.
Actuarial takeaway
Discrete OT is the matrix version of optimal transport. Continuous OT is the modelling
version: it tells us what it means to transform one risk law into another, independently of
a particular discretization. For an actuary, the key question is often not “which individual
claim moves where?” but “what is the least costly distributional transformation from one
risk environment to another?”
4.1 From finite matrices to probability laws
In Lecture 3, two probability measures were written as
µ =
n∑
i=1
aiδxi, ν =
m∑
j=1
bjδyj,
Page 47/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 48 ===
4 CONTINUOUS OPTIMAL TRANSPORT AND KANTOROVICH DUALITY
and a coupling was an n×m matrix Γ = (γij) with prescribed row and column sums. The
continuous version replaces the matrix by a probability measure on a product space.
Recall from Definition 2.2 that a coupling is a joint law with prescribed marginals. Let X
and Y be measurable spaces. Usually, they are subsets of Rd, or more generally Polish spaces.
Let µ∈P(X ) and ν∈P(Y). A coupling is a probability measure γ∈P(X×Y) whose first
marginal is µ and whose second marginal is ν. We write
γ∈ Π(µ,ν).
Equivalently, if (X,Y )∼γ, then
X∼µ, Y ∼ν.
Thus Π(µ,ν) is the set of all joint laws with fixed marginals.
This already has an actuarial interpretation. If µ is the marginal law of motor losses and ν is
the marginal law of home losses, then Π(µ,ν) is the set of all possible dependence structures
between the two lines, keeping the marginal models unchanged.
Example 4.1 (Continuous severity models). Let µ be a lognormal severity distribution and ν
be a gamma severity distribution calibrated to another portfolio. A coupling γ∈ Π(µ,ν) is a
joint model (X,Y ) where X has the first severity law and Y has the second. OT selects the joint
model minimizing an average transformation cost, for example E|X−Y| or E|X−Y|2.
Example 4.2 (Scenario vectors). Let µ and ν be laws on Rd, where each vector contains losses
by line of business:
x = (x(1),...,x(d)).
A cost such as c(x,y) = ∥x−y∥2 compares complete multivariate loss scenarios. The resulting
transport problem measures distributional discrepancy between two capital models, two climate
scenarios, or two reserving models.
4.2 Push-forwards and deterministic transformations
Recall the push-forward notation of Definition 2.1. A measurable map T : X →Y pushes µ
forward to ν, written
T#µ =ν,
if
ν(B) =µ(T−1(B))
for every measurable set B⊆Y. Equivalently, if X∼µ, then
T (X)∼ν.
A deterministic map T induces a coupling
γT = (Id,T )#µ,
meaning that under γT, the pair is (X,T (X)). Such a coupling is concentrated on the graph of
T.
Definition 4.1 (Monge admissible map). A measurable map T : X→Y is admissible for the
Monge problem from µ to ν if
T#µ =ν.
The set of admissible maps is denoted informally by
T (µ,ν) ={T :T#µ =ν}.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 48/416

=== page 49 ===
4.3 The Monge problem
In actuarial modelling, a push-forward map is a deterministic distributional transformation.
Examples include:
• applying inflation to claims: T (x) = (1 +i)x;
• applying a deductible or cap: T (x) = min{(x−d)+,M};
• transforming baseline climate losses into stressed losses;
Example 4.3 (Inflation as push-forward). Let X ∼ µ be a severity distribution and let
T (x) = (1 +i)x. The inflated severity law is
ν =T#µ.
If Fµ is continuous and strictly increasing, then
Fν(y) =Fµ
( y
1 +i
)
.
This is a deterministic transformation of the full distribution, not only of the mean.
Example 4.4 (Deductibles). Let T (x) = (x−d)+. Even if µ is continuous, the push-forward
T#µ has an atom at zero, with mass µ((−∞,d]). This example is useful because it shows how
deterministic maps can create atoms. Conversely, deterministic maps cannot always split atoms,
which is why Monge’s formulation can be too restrictive.
4.3 The Monge problem
Let c : X×Y→ R∪{+∞} be a ground cost, in the sense of Definition 1.2. The Monge problem
is
inf
T :T#µ=ν
∫
X
c(x,T (x)) dµ(x).
When an admissible map T exists and attains the infimum, it is called an optimal transport map.
This is the original formulation: each point x is sent to exactly one point T (x). In finite
insurance language, each policyholder, claim amount or scenario is matched deterministically to
a new one. No splitting is allowed.
Definition 4.2 (Monge problem). The Monge value is
Mc(µ,ν) = inf
{∫
c(x,T (x)) dµ(x) :T#µ =ν
}
,
with the convention that the infimum is +∞ if there is no admissible map.
Example 4.5 (No admissible Monge map). Let µ = δ0 and ν = 1 2 δ−1 + 1 2 δ1. Any map T sends
the single point 0 to one point T (0). Hence T#µ = δT (0), which is a Dirac mass. It cannot equal
ν. Thus the Monge problem has no feasible map.
The previous example is not a pathology. In insurance, atoms are common: zero claims,
deductible effects, policy limits, discretized rating classes, and grouped reserving states. A
formulation that forbids splitting mass is therefore too rigid for many actuarial purposes.
Remark 4.1 (Monge is nonlinear). The constraint T#µ = ν is nonlinear in T, and the set of
admissible maps is not convex. If T1 and T2 both push µto ν, the map (T1+T2)/2 usually does not.
This is the first reason why the Kantorovich relaxation is mathematically and computationally
easier.
Page 49/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 50 ===
4 CONTINUOUS OPTIMAL TRANSPORT AND KANTOROVICH DUALITY
4.4 The Kantorovich relaxation
Kantorovich’s idea is to optimize over all couplings, not only deterministic ones. Instead of
choosing a map T, one chooses a joint law γ with marginals µ and ν. The problem becomes
inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
X×Y
c(x,y) dγ(x,y).
Definition 4.3 (Kantorovich problem). The Kantorovich optimal transport value is
OTc(µ,ν) = inf γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
c(x,y) dγ(x,y).
Any minimizer γ⋆ ∈ Π(µ,ν) is called an optimal transport plan.
The Monge problem embeds into the Kantorovich problem. The following elementary result
is often the safest way to remember the relation between deterministic maps and transport plans.
Proposition 4.1 (Monge plans are Kantorovich plans). If T#µ = ν, then γT = (Id,T )#µ belongs
to Π(µ,ν) and ∫
c(x,y) dγT (x,y) =
∫
c(x,T (x)) dµ(x).
Consequently, whenever the Monge problem is feasible,
OTc(µ,ν)≤Mc(µ,ν).
Proof. The first marginal of γT is µ, and its second marginal is T#µ = ν, so γT ∈ Π(µ,ν). The
equality of costs is the change-of-variables formula for push-forwards, applied to the function
(x,y) ↦→c(x,y) and the map x↦→ (x,T (x)). The inequality follows by minimizing over the larger
Kantorovich feasible set.
Proposition 4.2 (Convexity of the Kantorovich formulation). The set Π(µ,ν) is convex. The
objective
γ↦→
∫
c dγ
is linear in γ. Hence the Kantorovich problem is an infinite-dimensional linear optimization
problem.
Proof. If γ1,γ2 ∈ Π(µ,ν) and λ∈ [0, 1], then λγ1 + (1 −λ)γ2 is a probability measure. Its first
marginal is
λµ + (1−λ)µ =µ,
and similarly its second marginal is ν. Linearity of the objective follows from linearity of
integration with respect to the measure.
Actuarial takeaway
The Kantorovich relaxation is well suited to aggregate distributions, cells and scenarios. It
allows one unit of source mass to be split across several target states. This is natural for
grouped claims, rating cells, reserve outcomes and dependence models.
4.5 A picture: deterministic map versus transport plan
In a Monge solution, all mass starting from x goes to one destination T (x). In a Kantorovich
plan, mass starting from x can be spread according to a conditional distribution γ( dy|x).
This distinction matters in applications. A deterministic map may be desirable when the
transformation must act directly on an observed state, while a non-deterministic plan may be
more natural for scenario matching or dependence uncertainty.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 50/416

=== page 51 ===
4.6 Existence of optimal transport plans
Monge: deterministic map Kantorovich: mass splitting
Figure 2: Monge map versus Kantorovich plan. In Monge’s formulation, each source point is sent
to a single target point. In Kantorovich’s relaxation, mass can be split across several targets,
producing a coupling or transport plan.
4.6 Existence of optimal transport plans
The finite problem always has a minimizer because the transportation polytope is compact. In
the continuous setting, compactness is replaced by tightness and weak convergence of probability
measures.
The following statement is a standard existence result; it is the continuous analogue of
Proposition 3.2. The precise hypotheses can be relaxed or strengthened depending on the space
and cost; the formulation below is enough for most actuarial uses.
Theorem 4.1 (Existence, standard form). Let X and Y be Polish spaces, let µ∈P(X ) and
ν∈P(Y), and let c : X×Y→ [0, +∞] be lower semicontinuous. Then the Kantorovich problem
admits an optimal plan, possibly with value +∞. If at least one coupling has finite cost, then the
optimal value is finite and attained.
Proof sketch. The set Π(µ,ν) is tight because its marginals are fixed. By Prokhorov’s theorem,
every minimizing sequence admits a weakly convergent subsequence. The limit has marginals µ
and ν, so it remains feasible. Lower semicontinuity of c implies lower semicontinuity of γ↦→
∫
c dγ
under weak convergence. Thus the limit is optimal.
Remark 4.2 (Why lower semicontinuity matters). If c is lower semicontinuous, jumps in the
limiting plan cannot artificially reduce cost. This is the continuous analogue of having a
well-defined finite cost matrix in the discrete problem.
Further reading
For rigorous treatments of existence, duality and optimal maps, see Villani 2003; Villani
2008; Ambrosio et al. 2021; Figalli and Glaudo 2021. For an economics-oriented presentation
close to the finite-dimensional linear-programming intuition, see Galichon 2016. For a
computational perspective, see Peyré and Cuturi 2019.
4.7 Kantorovich duality
The continuous dual is the analogue of the finite dual from the previous lecture. In the finite
case, the dual constraints were
ui +vj ≤Cij.
In the continuous case, the vectors u and v become functions φ and ψ, and the constraint becomes
φ(x) +ψ(y)≤c(x,y) for all x,y.
Page 51/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 52 ===
4 CONTINUOUS OPTIMAL TRANSPORT AND KANTOROVICH DUALITY
Theorem 4.2 (Kantorovich duality, standard version). Under standard regularity assumptions
onX,Y, µ, ν and c,
OTc(µ,ν) = sup
φ,ψ
{∫
φ dµ +
∫
ψ dν :φ(x) +ψ(y)≤c(x,y)
}
The functions φ and ψ are called Kantorovich potentials.
The exact functional class can vary. For example, if c is lower semicontinuous and bounded
below on Polish spaces, one can formulate the dual with suitable integrable potentials; in compact
settings, continuous potentials are often enough. For the purpose of these notes, the important
point is the structure: primal variables are couplings, dual variables are prices or potentials. This
is the continuous counterpart of Theorem 3.1.
Proposition 4.3 (Weak duality). For any γ∈ Π(µ,ν) and any measurable functions φ,ψ such
that φ(x) +ψ(y)≤c(x,y), ∫
φ dµ +
∫
ψ dν≤
∫
c dγ.
Proof. Since γ has marginals µ and ν,
∫
φ(x) dγ(x,y) =
∫
φ dµ,
∫
ψ(y) dγ(x,y) =
∫
ψ dν.
Therefore ∫
φ dµ +
∫
ψ dν =
∫
{φ(x) +ψ(y)} dγ(x,y)≤
∫
c(x,y) dγ(x,y).
Weak duality has a useful actuarial interpretation. If φ and ψ are any functions satisfying
φ(x) +ψ(y)≤c(x,y), then ∫
φ dµ +
∫
ψ dν
is a certified lower bound on the cost of any transformation from µ to ν. In risk aggregation
problems where one maximizes a payoff over all couplings, the same argument provides certified
upper or lower bounds on worst-case risk.
Remark 4.3 (Shadow prices). The dual potential φ(x) is the shadow value of mass at source
state x, while ψ(y) is the shadow value of mass at target state y. In a portfolio drift problem,
they identify states where distributional movement is costly.
4.8 The c-transform
The dual problem can be simplified by the c-transform. The following definition is the continuous
counterpart of the discrete c-transform in Section 3.10.
Definition 4.4 (c-transform and c-concavity). Given a function φ : X → R∪{−∞}, its
c-transform is the function onY defined by
φc(y) = inf
x∈X
{c(x,y)−φ(x)}.
Similarly, for ψ :Y→ R∪{−∞}, define
ψc(x) = inf
y∈Y
{c(x,y)−ψ(y)}.
A function φ is called c-concave if there exists a function ψ such that
φ =ψc.
Equivalently, φ can be written as an infimum of shifted cost functions.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 52/416

=== page 53 ===
4.9 Optimality and complementary slackness
By construction,
φ(x) +φc(y)≤c(x,y)
for all x,y. Thus (φ,φc) is dual feasible whenever the integrals are meaningful.
In the finite problem, replacing v by uc can only improve the dual objective. The same
intuition holds in the continuous case. At optimality, one may often choose potentials satisfying
ψ =φc, φ =ψc.
Example 4.6 (Squared Euclidean cost). Let c(x,y) = 1 2 ∥x−y∥2 on Rd. Then
φ(x) +ψ(y)≤ 1 2∥x−y∥2.
By rewriting
1
2∥x−y∥2 = 1 2∥x∥2 + 1 2∥y∥2 −x·y,
one can relate c-concavity to ordinary convex analysis. This is the route leading to Brenier’s
theorem.
Proposition 4.4 (Quadratic cost and convex conjugacy). Let c(x,y) = 1 2 ∥x−y∥2 on Rd. If
(φ,ψ) is dual feasible and
u(x) = 1 2∥x∥2 −φ(x), v(y) = 1 2∥y∥2 −ψ(y),
then
u(x) +v(y)≥x·y.
In particular, at optimality one may interpret v as a convex-conjugate-type partner of u, and the
contact condition becomes
y∈∂u(x).
If u is differentiable, this gives the Brenier map T (x) =∇u(x).
Proof. The dual inequality φ(x) +ψ(y)≤ 1 2 ∥x−y∥2 is equivalent to
1
2∥x∥2 −φ(x) + 1 2∥y∥2 −ψ(y)≥x·y.
This is precisely u(x) + v(y) ≥x·y. Equality on the contact set is the equality condition in the
Fenchel inequality, hencey∈∂u(x). If u is differentiable, ∂u(x) ={∇u(x)}.
This is the first place where convex analysis enters explicitly. A short appendix insert on
convex conjugacy, subgradients and Fenchel equality is proposed in the revision notes for the
mathematical toolbox.
4.9 Optimality and complementary slackness
In the discrete problem, mass moves only on edges where
ui +vj =Cij.
In the continuous problem, the same principle holds. If γ⋆ is optimal and (φ,ψ) are optimal
potentials, then
φ(x) +ψ(y) =c(x,y)
for γ⋆-almost every (x,y).
Page 53/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 54 ===
4 CONTINUOUS OPTIMAL TRANSPORT AND KANTOROVICH DUALITY
Proposition 4.5 (Continuous complementary slackness). Assume strong duality holds and let
γ⋆ be primal optimal and (φ,ψ) be dual optimal. Then
c(x,y)−φ(x)−ψ(y) = 0 γ⋆-a.s.
Proof. By feasibility,
c(x,y)−φ(x)−ψ(y)≥ 0.
By equality of primal and dual values,
∫
{c(x,y)−φ(x)−ψ(y)} dγ⋆(x,y) = 0.
A non-negative random variable with expectation zero is zero almost surely.
This result says that the support of an optimal plan is contained in the contact set
{(x,y) :φ(x) +ψ(y) =c(x,y)}.
When this contact set is the graph of a function, the optimal plan is induced by a Monge map.
When it is not, mass splitting may occur.
4.10 Cyclical monotonicity
There is another way to describe the support of optimal plans.
Definition4.5 (c-cyclicalmonotonicity). Let Γ ⊆X×Y. Theset Γiscalledc-cyclicallymonotone
if for every finite family (x1,y1),..., (xN,yN)∈ Γ and every permutation σ of{1,...,N},
N∑
k=1
c(xk,yk)≤
N∑
k=1
c(xk,yσ(k)).
This means that once the pairs in Γ have been chosen, no finite reshuffling of destinations
can reduce the total cost. In the discrete assignment problem, this is exactly the condition that
no cycle exchange improves the matching. For the quadratic cost, this condition is closely related
to cyclic monotonicity of the graph of a subgradient of a convex function.
Theorem 4.3 (Optimality and c-cyclical monotonicity, standard version). Under standard
assumptions, a transport plan γ is optimal only if its support is c-cyclically monotone. Conversely,
a plan concentrated on a suitable c-cyclically monotone set is optimal.
Example 4.7 (Two-pair exchange). For two pairs (x1,y1), (x2,y2), c-cyclical monotonicity gives
c(x1,y1) +c(x2,y2)≤c(x1,y2) +c(x2,y1).
In dimension one with convex distance cost, this inequality rules out crossings. Thus the monotone
rearrangement is optimal.
4.11 The one-dimensional case: quantile transport
A central actuarial special case is dimension one. Let µ and ν be probability distributions on R,
with distribution functions Fµ and Fν. Following the convention introduced in Definition ??,
write
Qµ(u) =F−1 µ (u) = inf{x :Fµ(x)≥u}, Qν(u) =F−1 ν (u) = inf{y :Fν(y)≥u}.
Let U∼ Uniform(0, 1). Then
X =Qµ(U)∼µ, Y =Qν(U)∼ν.
The coupling (X,Y ) is comonotonic. It matches equal probability levels.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 54/416

=== page 55 ===
4.12 Wasserstein distances
Theorem 4.4 (Monotone rearrangement). Let c(x,y) = h(x−y), where h is convex. Then the
coupling
(X,Y ) =
(
Qµ(U),Qν(U)
)
is optimal. In particular, for p≥ 1,
Wp(µ,ν)p =
∫ 1
0
|Qµ(u)−Qν(u)|p du.
If Fµ is continuous and strictly increasing, the optimal map from µ to ν is
T (x) =Qν(Fµ(x)).
This is the quantile-to-quantile transformation.
Actuarial takeaway
The formula
T (x) =Qν(Fµ(x))
is a useful practical formula in OT for actuaries. It transforms a severity observation
into the corresponding severity level under another distribution, preserving its rank. This
is the basis for distributional calibration, stress transformations and quantile-based risk
comparisons.
Example 4.8 (Severity stress by quantile mapping). Let µ be the baseline severity law and ν a
stressed severity law. A claim at percentile u =Fµ(x) is mapped to the stressed claim
T (x) =Qν(u).
This produces a stress transformation that preserves ranks. High baseline claims remain high
stressed claims, but the scale and tail thickness are changed according to ν.
Remark 4.4 (Atoms and randomized ranks). The coupling (Qµ(U),Qν(U)) is always well defined.
The deterministic map x↦→Qν(Fµ(x)), however, should be used with care when µ has atoms:
ordinary ranks are then not uniform without a randomization convention inside flat parts of the
distribution function. In actuarial applications, atoms arise naturally at zero claims, deductibles
and policy limits.
4.12 Wasserstein distances
The following definition fixes the convention used throughout the rest of the notes.
Definition 4.6 (p-Wasserstein distance). Let (X,d) be a metric space and let p≥ 1. Define
Pp(X ) =
{
µ∈P(X ) :
∫
d(x0,x)p dµ(x)<∞ for some x0 ∈X
}
.
For µ,ν∈Pp(X ), the p-Wasserstein distance is
Wp(µ,ν) =
(
inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
d(x,y)p dγ(x,y)
)1/p
.
The quantity inside the parentheses is often denoted Wp(µ,ν)p.
The condition defining Pp(X ) does not depend on the particular choice of x0, as soon as d is
a metric.
Page 55/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 56 ===
4 CONTINUOUS OPTIMAL TRANSPORT AND KANTOROVICH DUALITY
Proposition 4.6 (Metric property). For p≥ 1, Wp from Definition 4.6 defines a metric on
Pp(X ), under standard assumptions on (X,d).
Proof idea. Non-negativity and symmetry are immediate from the definition. If Wp(µ,ν) = 0,
then any optimal coupling must be concentrated on x = y, so µ = ν. The triangle inequality is
obtained by gluing an optimal coupling between µ and ν with one between ν and η, producing a
joint law (X,Y,Z), and then using Minkowski’s inequality:
(Ed(X,Z)p)1/p ≤ (Ed(X,Y )p)1/p + (Ed(Y,Z)p)1/p.
4.13 The Kantorovich–Rubinstein dual for W1
For p = 1, the dual has a particularly important form.
Theorem 4.5 (Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality). If c(x,y) =d(x,y) and µ,ν∈P1(X ), then
W1(µ,ν) = sup
f:Lip(f)≤1
{∫
f dµ−
∫
f dν
}
.
Here Lip(f)≤ 1 means
|f(x)−f(y)|≤d(x,y) ∀x,y.
Actuarial takeaway
The W1 distance compares distributions through their worst possible difference in expecta-
tion over all 1-Lipschitz test functions. Thus it asks: among all stable actuarial indicators,
what is the largest change in expected value between two distributions?
Example 4.9 (Stable portfolio indicators). A function f of policyholder characteristics is 1-
Lipschitz if similar policyholders have similar indicator values. The W1 distance between two
portfolio distributions is the largest possible difference in expected value over all such stable
indicators.
4.14 Quadratic cost and Brenier maps
A central multivariate cost is the quadratic cost on Rd:
c(x,y) = 1 2∥x−y∥2.
It leads to W2, Hilbert-like geometry, barycenters, gradient flows, and many applications in
statistics and machine learning.
The central structural result is Brenier’s theorem.
Theorem 4.6 (Brenier theorem, standard version). Let µ,ν ∈P2(Rd). Suppose that µ is
absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure. Then for the quadratic cost c(x,y) =
1
2 ∥x−y∥2, there exists a unique optimal transport plan. It is induced by a µ-a.e. unique map
T (x) =∇ϕ(x),
where ϕ : Rd → R is a convex function. Thus
γ⋆ = (Id,T )#µ, T#µ =ν.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 56/416

=== page 57 ===
4.15 The Gaussian case
The theorem says that, under regularity of the source distribution, the Kantorovich relaxation
recovers a deterministic Monge map. Moreover, this map has a special form: it is the gradient of
a convex potential.
Remark 4.5 (Why convex gradients?). For quadratic cost,
1
2∥x−y∥2 = 1 2∥x∥2 + 1 2∥y∥2 −x·y.
Optimality becomes linked to convex conjugacy. The map T = ∇ϕ arises from the subdifferential
of a convex function, and cyclical monotonicity becomes ordinary monotonicity of a convex
gradient.
Example 4.10 (Multivariate risk transformation). Let X∈ Rd represent a vector of losses by
line of business under a baseline model, and let ν be a stressed multivariate loss distribution.
Brenier’s map, when it exists, gives the least-squares distributional transformation
T# Law(X) =ν.
Unlike componentwise quantile mapping, Brenier’s map respects multivariate dependence.
4.15 The Gaussian case
A useful explicit example is the transport between multivariate Gaussian distributions. Closed-
form Gaussian formulas for the quadratic Wasserstein distance go back at least to Dowson and
Landau 1982; Gelbrich 1990 and are now standard in OT and statistics.
Theorem 4.7 (Gaussian W2 formula). Let
µ =N (m0, Σ0), ν =N (m1, Σ1),
where Σ0 and Σ1 are positive definite. For quadratic cost, the optimal map is affine:
T (x) =m1 +A(x−m0),
where
A = Σ−1/2 0 ( Σ1/2 0 Σ1Σ1/2 0 )1/2 Σ−1/2 0 .
The squared Wasserstein distance is
W2 2(µ,ν) =∥m0 −m1∥2 + tr ( Σ0 + Σ1 − 2(Σ1/2 0 Σ1Σ1/2 0 )1/2 ) .
Example 4.11 (Capital-model comparison). Suppose two internal capital models approximate
the vector of line-of-business losses by Gaussian distributions with different mean vectors and
covariance matrices. The formula separates a mean shift from a covariance-structure shift. This
is useful when distinguishing a change in expected losses from a change in diversification.
4.16 Beyond Brenier: triangular and sliced Gaussian maps
The Gaussian formula in the previous section gives the quadratic optimal transport map between
two multivariate normal laws. It is the Brenier map: it is the gradient of a convex function and
minimizes the mean squared displacement. However, it is not the only useful map sending one
Gaussian law to another.
In actuarial applications, the way the transformation is constructed may matter. For instance,
when a bivariate vector represents two guarantees, two lines of business, two cash-flow dates, or
two risk components, one may want either:

• a sequential transformation, where one coordinate is transformed first and the second is

• a projection-wise transformation, where selected one-dimensional directions are matched.
These lead to different maps, even when the source and target distributions are the same.
Further reading
Triangular transports are closely related to the Knothe–Rosenblatt rearrangement Knothe
1957; Rosenblatt 1952. Projection-wise and sliced constructions are related to sliced
Wasserstein distances and their computational variants Bonneel et al. 2015; Kolouri et al.
2019; Nadjahi et al. 2019. These maps are useful modelling devices, but only the Brenier
map is quadratic-cost optimal under the assumptions of Theorem 4.6.

This symmetry condition is important. Among all affine maps sending N (m0, Σ0) to N (m1, Σ1),

optimal transport map.
Actuarial takeaway
If Z = (Z1,Z2) represents two dependent actuarial quantities, the Brenier map gives the
least-squares joint stress from the baseline Gaussian model to the target Gaussian model.
It treats the two coordinates symmetrically through the covariance matrices.
4.16.2 A lower-triangular Knothe–Rosenblatt map
Another natural construction is sequential. First transport the first marginal, then transport the
conditional law of the second coordinate given the first.
For i∈{0, 1}, define
βi = ci ai , vi =bi − c2 i ai .
Then
Xi ∼N (¯xi,ai), Yi |Xi =x∼N (¯yi +βi(x− ¯xi),vi).

Proposition 4.7 (Lower-triangular Gaussian transport). The map TKR,↓ pushes µ0 forward to
µ1.
Proof. The first coordinate Tx(X0) has lawN (¯x1,a1). Conditional on X0 =x, the residual
Y0 − ¯y0 −β0(x− ¯x0)
has law N (0,v0). Multiplying it by
√
v1/v0 gives a residual with law N (0,v1). Adding the
conditional mean
¯y1 +β1(Tx(x)− ¯x1)
therefore gives the conditional law of Y1 given X1 = Tx(x). Hence the joint law is N (m1, Σ1).
This construction is the Gaussian version of the conditional quantile transform. It is exact,
but it is not symmetric in the two coordinates: the first coordinate is transformed first, and the
second one follows conditionally.
4.16.3 An upper-triangular Knothe–Rosenblatt map
Changing the order gives another exact transport map. Now one first transports the y-marginal
and then the conditional law of x given y.

γi = ci bi , wi =ai − c2 i bi , i∈{0, 1}.
Then
Yi ∼N (¯yi,bi), Xi |Yi =y∼N (¯xi +γi(y− ¯yi),wi).

Proposition 4.8 (Upper-triangular Gaussian transport). The map TKR,↑ pushes µ0 forward to
µ1.
Proof. The proof is the same conditional-Gaussian argument as in Proposition 4.7, with the
roles of the two coordinates reversed. The transformed marginal Ty(Y0) has law N (¯y1,b1), and
the conditional residual in the first coordinate is rescaled from variance w0 to variance w1. The
resulting conditional law is the conditional law of X1 given Y1 = Ty(y), hence the joint law is
µ1.
Remark 4.6 (Order matters). Both TKR,↓ and TKR,↑ transport µ0 to µ1, but they are generally
different. The lower map gives priority to the first coordinate; the upper map gives priority to
the second coordinate. Neither is usually equal to the Brenier map.
Actuarial takeaway
Triangular maps are useful when the actuarial vector has a natural order. For example,
one may first transport the main guarantee and then the rider conditional on it; or first
transport the early cash-flow and then later cash-flows conditionally. The price paid for
this sequential interpretation is that the map depends on the chosen ordering.
4.16.4 A projection-wise sliced Gaussian map
A third construction uses one-dimensional Gaussian projections. This is related to sliced
Wasserstein diagnostics, which are developed later in Lab 2 on sliced and projected Wasserstein
distances. Let θ∈Sd−1 be a unit direction. IfZi ∼N (mi, Σi), then
θ⊤Zi ∼N ( θ⊤mi,θ⊤Σiθ ) .
The one-dimensional monotone Gaussian transport along direction θ is therefore
tθ(s) =θ⊤m1 +
√
θ⊤Σ1θ
θ⊤Σ0θ
(
s−θ⊤m0
)
.
Now fix an orthonormal basis
B = (θ1,...,θd).
For each k, set
λi,k =θ⊤ k Σiθk, rk =
√
λ1,k
λ0,k
, i∈{0, 1}.
A projection-wise Gaussian scaling map is
TB(z) =m1 +B diag(r1,...,rd)B⊤(z−m0).

4.17 Monge–Ampere equation, briefly
In dimension two, with B = (θ,θ⊥), this becomes
TB(z) =m1 +B

Remark 4.7 (Projection-wise matching is not full transport). The map TB matches means and
variances along the selected orthonormal directions. However, it does not generally push µ0
exactly to µ1. Indeed, the covariance of TB(Z0) is
B diag(r1,...,rd)B⊤Σ0B diag(r1,...,rd)B⊤,
which need not equal Σ1. Equality holds in particular when Σ0 and Σ1 are diagonal in the same
basis B. Thus TB should be interpreted as a projection-wise stress map, not as the full Brenier
map.
4.16.5 Comparison
The three constructions answer different questions:
TOT is symmetric and quadratic-cost optimal;
TKR,↓ is exact and sequential in the order x→y;
TKR,↑ is exact and sequential in the order y→x;
TB matches selected projected variances.
Only the first one is the Brenier map. The two Knothe–Rosenblatt maps are exact transport maps
but depend on the ordering of the coordinates. The sliced map is a diagnostic or approximate
stress map unless the covariance matrices are compatible with the chosen basis.
Example 4.12 (Two guarantees). Let Z = (Z1,Z2) represent the present values of two guarantees
in a life or P&C contract. A Gaussian approximation may be used for the baseline law and for a
stressed law. The Brenier map gives the least-squares joint stress. A lower Knothe–Rosenblatt
map can be used when Z1 is the primary guarantee and Z2 is adjusted conditionally. An upper
map reverses this priority. A sliced map may be used to stress the total Z1 +Z2 and the difference
Z1 −Z2 by taking directions proportional to (1, 1) and (1,−1).
Computational note
In Gaussian stress testing, one should report which map is used. Saying that a baseline
Gaussian model is transported to a stressed Gaussian model is incomplete: Brenier,
triangular and projection-wise maps may produce different transformed scenarios, even
when they share the same endpoint distributions.
4.17 Monge–Ampere equation, briefly
If µ and ν have densities f and g on Rd, and if the Brenier map T = ∇ϕ is smooth and invertible,
the constraint T#µ =ν can be written as a change-of-variables formula:
f(x) =g(∇ϕ(x)) det(D2ϕ(x))
This is the Monge–Ampere equation; see, for example, Caffarelli 1992; Figalli 2017 for regularity-
oriented treatments.
Page 63/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 64 ===
4 CONTINUOUS OPTIMAL TRANSPORT AND KANTOROVICH DUALITY
For actuarial purposes, the main message is not that one should solve this PDE in routine
applications. Rather, it clarifies that multivariate OT is a nonlinear distributional calibration
problem. The Jacobian of the map adjusts density: where target density is high, the map
compresses mass; where target density is low, it expands mass.
Actuarial takeaway
A transport map or coupling aligns two probability laws. It does not by itself identify what
would have happened to a given policyholder, claim or scenario under an intervention.
Such an interpretation requires a structural model, an intervention design or additional
identifying assumptions.
4.18 Actuarial interpretations of continuous OT
Severity models
If µ and ν are two severity laws, Wp(µ,ν) measures the full distributional discrepancy. It is
sensitive to differences in spread and tails, not only to mean severity. In dimension one, the
quantile formula makes this especially transparent:
Wp(µ,ν)p =
∫ 1
0
|Qµ(u)−Qν(u)|p du.
Portfolio drift
Let µt be the law of policyholder covariates at time t, and µt+1 the law one year later. A
Wasserstein distance between µt and µt+1 gives a geometrically meaningful drift measure. If
the coordinates include age, region, insured value and claim history, the distance captures joint
movement in the portfolio mix.
Climate stress
Let µ be the distribution of annual losses under a baseline climate and ν the distribution under
a stressed climate. The transport map describes how baseline scenarios are transformed into
stressed scenarios. In multivariate settings, this may include changes in both marginal hazard
intensities and dependence between regions.
Reserving distributions
Let µ and ν be predictive distributions of future claim payments produced by two reserving
methods. OT compares the full cash-flow distributions. If the state space is Rd, each coordinate
may represent a future development year.
Actuarial takeaway
Continuous OT gives a common language for several actuarial transformations: inflation,
stress testing, calibration, model comparison and scenario alignment. In each case, the
mathematical object is the same: transform one law into another at minimum cost.
4.19 A mini-lab: quantile transport for severity distributions
The following R code illustrates one-dimensional quantile transport. It also prepares the later
labs on empirical Wasserstein noise and longevity transport. It maps a baseline lognormal severity
distribution to a heavier-tailed Pareto-type distribution through ranks.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 64/416

=== page 65 ===
4.20 What changes from discrete to continuous?
set.seed(1)
n <- 5000
x <- rlnorm(n, meanlog = 8, sdlog = 0.8) # baseline losses
# Target distribution: shifted Pareto-type quantile function
q _target <- function(u, xm = 1000, alpha = 2.2) {
xm * (1 - u)^(-1 / alpha)
}
# Empirical rank map
u <- rank(x, ties.method = "average") / (n + 1)
y <- q _target(u)
# Empirical W2 between baseline and transported target in 1D
w2 _sq <- mean(( sort(x) - sort(y))^2)
sqrt(w2 _sq)
par(mfrow = c(1, 2))
plot( sort(x), sort(y), pch = 19, cex = .25,
xlab = "baseline␣quantiles", ylab = "target␣quantiles",
main = "Quantile␣transport␣map")
abline(0, 1, lty = 2)
plot( quantile(x, seq(.01, .99, by = .01)),
quantile(y, seq(.01, .99, by = .01)), type = "l",
xlab = "Q _baseline(u)", ylab = "Q _target(u)",
main = "Severity␣stress␣by␣ranks")
abline(0, 1, lty = 2)
The map is monotone: larger baseline losses are sent to larger target losses. The rank of each
claim is preserved, but the tail is reshaped.
Computational note
In one dimension, empirical Wp is best computed by sorting rather than by solving a
generic transport linear program. In higher dimensions, exact OT becomes substantially
harder, which motivates discrete algorithms, entropic regularization and sliced Wasserstein
approximations.
4.20 What changes from discrete to continuous?
The following table summarizes the parallel between the discrete and continuous theories.
The conceptual structure is the same. The main technical differences are compactness,
measurability, lower semicontinuity and the possibility of optimal maps.
4.21 Summary
This lecture introduced continuous optimal transport. The Monge problem searches for a
deterministic map pushing µ to ν, but may be infeasible or nonconvex. The Kantorovich
formulation relaxes the problem to all couplings and becomes a linear optimization problem over
probability measures. Duality introduces Kantorovich potentials, whose contact set determines
where optimal mass can move. In one dimension, optimal transport is monotone and given
by quantile matching. For quadratic cost in Rd, Brenier’s theorem says that, under absolute
continuity, the optimal plan is induced by the gradient of a convex function.
Page 65/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 66 ===

Object Discrete case Continuous case
Source law ai on xi µ∈P(X )
Target law bj on yj ν∈P(Y)
Coupling matrix Γ = (γij) measure γ∈P(X×Y)
Marginals row/column sums projections of γ
Cost ∑Cijγij ∫ c dγ
Dual potentials vectors ui,vj functions φ,ψ
Dual constraint ui +vj ≤Cij φ(x) +ψ(y)≤c(x,y)
Optimal support tight edges contact set
For actuarial science, continuous OT is a theory of distributional transformation. It gives a
coherent way to compare and align loss laws, reserving scenarios, dependence models and climate
stress distributions.

Exercise 4.1 (Monge plans are Kantorovich plans). Let T : X→Y be measurable and suppose

coupling.

5 WASSERSTEIN DISTANCES, QUANTILES AND BARYCENTERS
5 Wasserstein Distances, Quantiles and Barycenters
Learning objectives
The previous lectures introduced optimal transport first as a finite-dimensional linear program,
then as a continuous optimization problem over couplings. This lecture turns the optimal value
itself into a distance between probability laws. The resulting Wasserstein distances are among
the most useful objects in actuarial applications, because they compare entire distributions while
respecting the geometry of the underlying risk variable.
After this lecture, the reader should be able to:
(i) define the Wasserstein distance Wp on a metric space;
(ii) understand the finite-moment condition and why it is needed;
(iii) prove the main metric properties at a working level;
(iv) compute Wp in dimension one using quantile functions;
(v) relate Wasserstein distances to VaR, TVaR and distributional changes in actuarial risk;
(vi) compute simple examples, including Dirac masses, uniform laws and Gaussian laws;
(vii) define displacement interpolation and Wasserstein barycenters;
(viii) use one-dimensional barycenters through averages of quantile functions;
(ix) understand actuarial uses of barycenters, such as expert aggregation, model blending and
climate-scenario summaries.
Further reading
This chapter builds on the definitions of couplings and push-forwards in Lecture 2, the
discrete formulation in Lecture 3, and the continuous Kantorovich problem in Lecture 4.
Wasserstein distances and their geometric properties are standard in Villani 2003; Villani
2008; Ambrosio et al. 2021; Santambrogio 2015; Figalli and Glaudo 2021. One-dimensional
quantile representations connect the theory to actuarial quantiles, VaR and TVaR; see
also Denuit et al. 2005a; McNeil et al. 2015a. Barycenters are developed in Agueh and
Carlier 2011; Peyré and Cuturi 2019; Panaretos and Zemel 2020. Vector ranks and vector
quantiles are related to Brenier maps and to the econometric formulation of multivariate
quantiles in Brenier 1991; Chernozhukov et al. 2017; Galichon 2016.
Actuarial takeaway
A Wasserstein distance does not only ask whether two loss distributions are different. It
asks how much work is needed to turn one into the other. This gives a useful geometry
for actuarial objects whose changes have economic meaning: inflation, prevention, climate
stress, portfolio drift and reserve-scenario changes.
5.1 From optimal values to distances
Let (X,d) be a metric space. In most actuarial examples, X is R, Rd, a finite set of risk cells,
or a space of cash-flow vectors. Given two probability measures µ and ν on X, the optimal
transport problem with cost
c(x,y) =d(x,y)p, p≥ 1,
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 68/416

=== page 69 ===
5.1 From optimal values to distances
is
inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
X×X
d(x,y)p dγ(x,y).
The Wasserstein distance is the p-th root of this optimal value. It is the metric-cost specialization
of the Kantorovich problem in Definition 4.3.
Definition 5.1 (Finite p-th moment). Let (X,d) be a metric space and let x0 ∈X. A probability
measure µ has finite p-th moment if
∫
X
d(x,x0)p dµ(x)<∞.
The set of such probability measures is denoted byPp(X ).
The definition does not depend on the choice of x0. Indeed, by the triangle inequality,
d(x,x1)p ≤ 2p−1{d(x,x0)p +d(x0,x1)p},
so if the moment is finite around one point, it is finite around any other point.
Definition 5.2 (Wasserstein distance). For p≥ 1 and µ,ν∈Pp(X ), the p-Wasserstein distance
is
Wp(µ,ν) =
(
inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
d(x,y)p dγ(x,y)
)1/p
.
When p = 1, we write W1. When p = 2, we write W2.
The interpretation is straightforward. The notation Π(µ,ν) is the coupling set introduced in
Definition 2.2. If µ is the distribution before a change and ν the distribution after a change, then
Wp(µ,ν)p is the minimum average p-cost required to transform µ into ν. The coupling describes
which parts of the source distribution are matched to which parts of the target distribution.
Example 5.1 (Dirac masses). Let µ = δx and ν = δy. There is only one coupling, namely
γ =δ(x,y). Hence
Wp(δx,δy) =d(x,y).
Thus Wasserstein distance extends the ground distance from points to probability laws.
Example 5.2 (A deterministic shift). Let X = R, d(x,y) = |x−y|, and let ν be the law of
X +a when X∼µ. The deterministic coupling (X,X +a) gives
Wp(µ,ν)p ≤|a|p.
In fact equality holds on the real line by Theorem 5.2, because quantiles are shifted by the same
amount. Thus a pure inflation shift by a has Wasserstein size |a|, independently of the shape of
the distribution.
Example 5.3 (Scaling a severity distribution). Let X∼µ on R+ and let Y = (1 + i)X, where
i> 0 represents claims inflation. The coupling (X, (1 +i)X) yields
Wp(µ, Law(Y ))p ≤ipE[Xp].
In dimension one, equality holds by the monotone rearrangement theorem, provided E[Xp] <∞.
This gives a direct distributional measure of inflation stress.
Page 69/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

5 WASSERSTEIN DISTANCES, QUANTILES AND BARYCENTERS
5.2 Why Wasserstein rather than only moments?
Actuarial practice often compares distributions through a few summaries: mean, standard
deviation, VaR, TVaR, skewness, probability of zero claim, or selected quantiles. These summaries
are important, but they can miss the geometry of the full distribution.
Two distributions can have the same mean and variance but different tail shapes. Two
stochastic reserving models can have the same best estimate but very different predictive tails.
Two prevention strategies can reduce expected loss by the same amount but have different effects
on extreme losses. A Wasserstein distance keeps track of the full movement of mass.
Example 5.4 (Same mean, different shape). Let
µ = 1 2δ0 + 1 2δ2, ν =δ1.
Both distributions have mean 1. But ν is concentrated at the mean, while µ is dispersed. For
p = 2,
W2 2(µ,ν) = 1 2(0− 1)2 + 1 2(2− 1)2 = 1.
This quantity is the amount of squared displacement needed to collapse the distribution to its
mean.
5.3 Basic metric properties
The fact that Wp is a distance is not only a formal mathematical property. It is what allows us
to compare portfolio shifts, climate scenarios, model outputs and reserving distributions in a
coherent geometry.
Theorem 5.1 (Metric property). Let (X,d) be a Polish metric space and p≥ 1. Then Wp is a
metric onPp(X ). That is, for µ,ν,η∈Pp(X ),
(i) Wp(µ,ν)≥ 0;
(ii) Wp(µ,ν) = 0 if and only if µ =ν;
(iii) Wp(µ,ν) = Wp(ν,µ);
(iv) Wp(µ,η)≤ Wp(µ,ν) + Wp(ν,η).
Proof sketch. Non-negativity follows from non-negativity of dp. Symmetry follows because if γ
couples µ and ν, the swapped coupling couples ν and µ, and d(x,y) =d(y,x).
If µ = ν, the diagonal coupling (X,X) has zero cost. Conversely, if Wp(µ,ν) = 0, there are
couplings with arbitrarily small expected d(X,Y )p. In the limit, the mass is concentrated on
x =y, implying equal marginals.
For the triangle inequality, take nearly optimal couplings γ12 ∈ Π(µ,ν) and γ23 ∈ Π(ν,η). By
the gluing lemma, there exists a joint law of (X,Y,Z) such that (X,Y ) ∼γ12 and (Y,Z) ∼γ23.
Then
d(X,Z)≤d(X,Y ) +d(Y,Z).
Taking the Lp-norm and using Minkowski’s inequality gives
(Ed(X,Z)p)1/p ≤ (Ed(X,Y )p)1/p + (Ed(Y,Z)p)1/p.
Minimizing over the two couplings yields the result.
Remark 5.1 (Gluing). The gluing step is a formal version of the following actuarial idea: if
portfolio A can be matched to portfolio B, and portfolio B can be matched to portfolio C, then
one can construct a joint scenario linking A, B and C. This is what allows Wasserstein distances
to compare distributional paths consistently.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 70/416

=== page 71 ===
5.4 The order in p
5.4 The order in p
For a fixed pair of distributions, Wp tends to increase with p. The following simple inequality is
often useful when deciding how tail-sensitive the metric should be.
Proposition 5.1 (Monotonicity in p). If 1≤p≤q and µ,ν∈Pq(X ), then
Wp(µ,ν)≤ Wq(µ,ν).
Proof. For any coupling (X,Y ), Jensen’s inequality gives
(Ed(X,Y )p)1/p ≤ (Ed(X,Y )q)1/q.
Taking the infimum over couplings proves the inequality.
The choice of p is therefore not neutral.
• W1 gives a linear cost of displacement. It is robust and has a useful dual representation
through Lipschitz functions.
• W2 penalizes long movements more strongly and gives a rich geometric structure, including
barycenters and geodesics.
• Higher p values place increasing emphasis on tail mismatches and large distributional
moves.
Actuarial takeaway
For severity distributions, W1 often has a natural monetary interpretation: average amount
of claim displacement. W2 is more geometric and often better suited for barycenters and
displacement interpolation. For tail-sensitive applications, the choice of p should be aligned
with the risk question.
5.5 The one-dimensional quantile formula
The most useful explicit formula in actuarial applications is the one-dimensional quantile formula.
Let Fµ denote the distribution function of µ. Throughout these notes we use the lower generalized
inverse
Qµ(u) =F−1 µ (u) = inf{x∈ R :Fµ(x)≥u}, u∈ (0, 1).
In actuarial language, Qµ(u) is VaRu for the loss distribution µ. The notation Qµ will be used
below whenever quantile functions are manipulated as functions in Lp(0, 1). With atoms or ties,
this convention is the mathematical object; empirical implementations must state how ranks are
discretized.
Theorem 5.2 (Quantile formula). Let µ,ν∈Pp(R). Then
Wp(µ,ν)p =
∫ 1
0
|Qµ(u)−Qν(u)|p du.
An optimal coupling is obtained by taking U∼U(0, 1) and setting
X =Qµ(U), Y =Qν(U).
Proof idea. The coupling above is comonotonic: the same rank U is used in both distributions.
It has the required marginals by the probability integral transform. For convex costs of the
form |x−y|p, matching equal ranks is optimal. Intuitively, crossings are inefficient: if x1 <x2
but y1 >y2, swapping the destinations reduces the total cost because the cost is Monge in one
dimension. Passing from finite samples to general distributions yields the integral formula.
Page 71/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 72 ===
5 WASSERSTEIN DISTANCES, QUANTILES AND BARYCENTERS
Remark 5.2 (Quantiles as coordinates). In dimension one, the map
µ↦→Qµ
embedsPp(R) into Lp(0, 1). In these coordinates,
Wp(µ,ν) =∥Qµ −Qν∥Lp(0,1).
For p = 2, this means that one-dimensional W2-geometry is Hilbertian after the quantile transform.
This is one reason why univariate actuarial examples are so transparent.
5.6 Connection with VaR and TVaR
For a loss variable L∼µ, the Value-at-Risk at level α is
VaRα(L) =F−1 µ (α) =Qµ(α)
with the usual convention for generalized quantiles. Thus the quantile function used in Wasserstein
distance is the complete VaR curve.
For an integrable loss, the Tail Value-at-Risk can be written as an average of tail quantiles.
We use the notation TVaRα(µ) when the argument is the law rather than a random variable:
TVaRα(L) = TVaRα(µ) = 1 1−α ∫ 1 α Qµ(u) du, 0<α< 1
under the integrated-quantile convention. With atoms, this convention should be stated explicitly.
Hence W1 and W2 compare distributions through differences of full quantile curves, while VaR
and TVaR extract local or tail averages from those curves.
Proposition 5.2 (Average quantile stability). Let µ,ν∈Pp(R). For any interval [a,b] ⊂ (0, 1),
∫ b
a
|Qµ(u)−Qν(u)| du≤ (b−a)1−1/pWp(µ,ν).
In particular, average quantile differences over a range of confidence levels are controlled by
Wasserstein distance.
Proof. By Hölder’s inequality,
∫ b
a
|Qµ(u)−Qν(u)| du≤ (b−a)1−1/p
(∫ b
a
|Qµ(u)−Qν(u)|p du
)1/p
,
where Qµ = F−1 µ and Qν = F−1 ν . The last term is bounded by the full Lp(0, 1) norm, which is
Wp(µ,ν).
Proposition 5.3 (TVaR stability). For p≥ 1,
|TVaRα(µ)− TVaRα(ν)|≤ (1−α)−1/pWp(µ,ν),
provided both TVaR quantities are defined by integrated quantiles.
Proof. Use the previous proposition with a =α, b = 1, and divide by 1−α.
A small Wp distance does not automatically control |VaRα(µ) − VaRα(ν)| at a single level α.
A small amount of mass can shift around a point and change a discontinuous quantile. Pointwise
VaR stability requires additional assumptions, such as positive density near the quantile.
Actuarial takeaway
A small Wasserstein distance implies that average tail quantiles cannot move too much.
This is useful for stress testing: it gives a distributional notion of closeness that has
consequences for capital measures such as TVaR.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 72/416

5.7 Explicit examples in dimension one
5.7 Explicit examples in dimension one
5.7.1 Uniform distributions
Let µ =U[a,b] and ν =U[c,d]. Their quantile functions are
Qµ(u) =a + (b−a)u, Qν(u) =c + (d−c)u.
Thus
W2 2(µ,ν) =
∫ 1
0
{(a−c) + [(b−a)− (d−c)]u}2 du.
Writing A =a−c and B = (b−a)− (d−c),
W2 2(µ,ν) =A2 +AB + B2 3 .
If the two intervals have the same length, then B = 0, and W2 = |a−c|: only the location shift
matters. If they have the same midpoint but different lengths, then the distance measures the
scale mismatch.
5.7.2 Exponential distributions
Let µ = Exp(λ) and ν = Exp(θ). The quantile functions are
Qµ(u) =− 1 λ log(1−u), Qν(u) =−1 θ log(1−u).
Therefore
W2 2(µ,ν) =
( 1

Since− log(1−U)∼ Exp(1), the integral is 2, and
W2 2(µ,ν) = 2

5.7.3 Gaussian distributions on the real line
Let µ = N (m0,σ2 0) and ν = N (m1,σ2 1). If Φ is the standard normal distribution function, then
Qµ(u) =m0 +σ0Φ−1(u), Qν(u) =m1 +σ1Φ−1(u).
Hence
W2 2(µ,ν) =
∫ 1
0
{
(m0 −m1) + (σ0 −σ1)Φ−1(u) }2 du.
Since
∫ 1
0 Φ−1(u) du = 0 and ∫ 1 0 [Φ−1(u)]2 du = 1,
W2 2(µ,ν) = (m0 −m1)2 + (σ0 −σ1)2.
The optimal map is affine:
T (x) =m1 + σ1 σ0 (x−m0).
Example 5.5 (Severity model comparison). Suppose two actuarial teams fit normal approxi-
mations to transformed losses. If one model differs mainly in location and the other mainly in
dispersion, the formula
W2 2 = (∆m)2 + (∆σ)2
separates these two contributions. This is often more interpretable than comparing likelihoods
or means alone.

5.8 The multivariate Gaussian formula
In Rd with quadratic cost, the Wasserstein distance between multivariate Gaussian distributions
has a closed form. If
µ =N (m0, Σ0), ν =N (m1, Σ1),
with positive definite covariance matrices, then
W2 2(µ,ν) =∥m0 −m1∥2 + tr ( Σ0 + Σ1 − 2(Σ1/2 0 Σ1Σ1/2 0 )1/2 ) .
Equivalent symmetric forms are also common. When d = 1, this reduces to the formula above.
Remark 5.3 (Why actuaries may care). Multivariate Gaussian approximations appear in capital
aggregation, reserving uncertainty, large-portfolio approximations and scenario generation. The
Wasserstein formula separates mean displacement from covariance displacement. Thus two
capital models can be compared not only by total SCR, but by how their entire scenario cloud
has moved.
5.9 Wasserstein convergence and distributional stability
Wasserstein distance metrizes more than weak convergence. It also controls moments. The stan-
dard characterization is the following; see, for instance, Villani 2008, Chapter 6 or Santambrogio
2015, Chapter 5.
Theorem 5.3 (Wasserstein convergence). Let (X,d) be a Polish metric space and let p≥ 1. For
µn,µ∈Pp(X ),
Wp(µn,µ)→ 0
if and only if µn ⇒µ weakly and
∫
d(x,x0)p dµn(x)→
∫
d(x,x0)p dµ(x)
for one, hence every, x0 ∈X.
In actuarial terms, this is important because weak convergence alone may ignore tail mass
escaping to infinity. A sequence of models may converge weakly but still have unstable capital if
small probabilities of very large losses move outward. Wasserstein convergence with moment
control rules out that behaviour at the chosen moment order.
Example 5.6 (Weak convergence can miss tail escape). Let
µn =
(
1− 1 n ) δ0 + 1 nδn.
Then µn converges weakly to δ0, because the mass at n has probability 1/n. But
W1(µn,δ0) = 1,
and
W2 2(µn,δ0) =n.
The weak limit misses a small probability of a large loss; Wasserstein distance detects it.
Actuarial takeaway
This example is a warning for capital modelling. Rare large claims may be invisible under
weak convergence but highly visible under Wasserstein distances, especially for larger p.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 74/416

5.10 Wasserstein balls and robust actuarial thinking
5.10 Wasserstein balls and robust actuarial thinking
Definition 5.3 (Wasserstein ball). A Wasserstein ball around a baseline distribution ˆµ is
Bp(ˆµ,r) ={ν∈Pp(X ) : Wp(ν, ˆµ)≤r}
The radius r has the same units as the ground distance d.
This object is central in distributionally robust optimization. Instead of trusting a single
empirical or fitted distribution, one considers all distributions within transport distance r.
In actuarial science, this has several interpretations:
• model uncertainty around an empirical severity distribution;
• robustness of capital to tail perturbations;
• uncertainty around climate scenario distributions;
• ambiguity in portfolio drift after a change in underwriting;
Example 5.7 (Robust expected loss). Let ℓ : X→ R be a loss functional. A robust expectation
takes the form
sup
ν:Wp(ν,ˆµ)≤r
∫
ℓ(x) dν(x).
If ℓ is Lipschitz and p = 1, the Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality gives a direct bound:
⏐⏐⏐⏐ ∫ ℓ dν− ∫ ℓ dˆµ ⏐⏐⏐⏐≤ Lip(ℓ)W1(ν, ˆµ).
Thus the worst-case expectation over a W1-ball is controlled by the Lipschitz sensitivity of the
quantity of interest.
5.11 Displacement interpolation
A Wasserstein distance does not only give a number. When an optimal map exists, it also gives
a path between distributions.
SupposeX = Rd, p = 2, and an optimal map T transports µ0 to µ1. For t∈ [0, 1], define
Tt(x) = (1−t)x +tT (x),
and
µt = (Tt)#µ0.
Definition 5.4 (Displacement interpolation). The curve (µt)0≤t≤1 defined above is called the
displacement interpolation, or constant-speed W2-geodesic, between µ0 and µ1, when the optimal
map formulation applies.
Example 5.8 (Interpolating severity distributions). Let µ0 be a baseline severity distribution
and µ1 a stressed severity distribution. In dimension one, the interpolation has quantile function
Qt(u) = (1−t)Q0(u) +tQ1(u).
Thus each rank u moves linearly from its baseline quantile to its stressed quantile. This gives a
full path of intermediate stress scenarios.
Page 75/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 76 ===
5 WASSERSTEIN DISTANCES, QUANTILES AND BARYCENTERS
Remark 5.4 (Displacement interpolation is not mixture interpolation). Displacement interpolation
is different from mixture interpolation. The mixture path is
(1−t)µ0 +tµ1.
It puts some mass from the baseline distribution and some mass from the stressed distribution.
Thedisplacementpathmovesthedistributionitself. Forlocationshifts, displacementinterpolation
shifts the whole law gradually, while mixture interpolation creates two overlapping populations.
Actuarial takeaway
For climate stress testing, displacement interpolation is often more plausible than mixture
interpolation. It should still be read as a scenario path, not as an identified physical or
causal transition.
5.12 Wasserstein barycenters
A barycenter is an average. In Euclidean space, the weighted average of points x1,...,xK
minimizes the weighted sum of squared distances:
¯x = arg min
x
K∑
k=1
λk∥x−xk∥2.
The Wasserstein barycenter extends this idea from points to probability distributions.
Definition 5.5 (Wasserstein barycenter). Let µ1,...,µK ∈P2(X ) and letλk ≥ 0 with∑ kλk = 1.
A W2-barycenter is any solution of
¯µ∈ arg min
µ∈P2(X )
K∑
k=1
λkW2 2(µ,µk)
Barycenters are useful because they average distributions in a geometry-aware way. If the
µk are severity laws, a barycenter is not obtained by averaging means or parameters; it is the
distribution that minimizes total squared transport discrepancy.
Example 5.9 (Expert aggregation). Suppose three experts provide predictive loss distributions
µ1,µ2,µ3, and the credibility weights are λ1,λ2,λ3. The Wasserstein barycenter is a consensus
distribution. It keeps distributional information, unlike averaging only expected losses or capital
figures.
5.13 One-dimensional barycenters
In dimension one, barycenters are especially simple because of quantiles.
Theorem 5.4 (Quantile formula for barycenters). Let µ1,...,µK ∈P2(R) and let λk ≥ 0,∑
kλk = 1. A W2-barycenter ¯µ has quantile function
Q¯µ(u) =
K∑

λkQµk(u), u∈ (0, 1).
Proof. Using the quantile embedding, the barycenter problem becomes

5.14 Barycenters versus mixtures
where Qk = Qµk, and Q must be a nondecreasing left-continuous function. Pointwise in u, the
minimizer of ∑ kλk|q−Qk(u)|2 is q = ∑ kλkQk(u). Since a weighted average of nondecreasing
functions is nondecreasing, this pointwise minimizer is a valid quantile function. Hence it defines
the barycenter.
Example 5.10 (Barycenter of Gaussian laws on R). Let µk = N (mk,σ2 k). The quantile function
is
Qk(u) =mk +σkΦ−1(u).
Thus the barycenter has quantile function
¯Q(u) = ¯m + ¯σΦ−1(u),
where
¯m =
∑
k

∑
k
λkσk.
Therefore the barycenter is
¯µ =N ( ¯m, ¯σ2).
Notice that standard deviations, not variances, are averaged in one-dimensional W2-geometry.
5.14 Barycenters versus mixtures
A Wasserstein barycenter should not be confused with a mixture distribution. Given µ1,...,µK,
their mixture is
µmix =
K∑
k=1
λkµk.
This corresponds to first selecting a model k with probability λk, then sampling from µk. It
preserves model heterogeneity.
A Wasserstein barycenter instead seeks a central distribution. In dimension one, it averages
quantiles. It is therefore closer to aligning ranks across models and averaging corresponding risk
levels.
Example 5.11 (Two shifted distributions). Let µ1 = N (−m,σ2) and µ2 = N (m,σ2), with
equal weights. The mixture is bimodal when m is large. The Wasserstein barycenter is N (0,σ2).
The mixture represents uncertainty about which regime applies; the barycenter represents a
central regime.
Actuarial takeaway
For model risk, mixtures and barycenters answer different questions. A mixture says: one
of these models may be true. A barycenter says: construct a consensus model that is
central among them. Both are useful, but they should not be confused.
5.15 Barycentric projection of a transport plan
In applications, especially in higher dimension or in discrete problems, an optimal coupling may
not be induced by a deterministic map. A common way to extract a map-like object from a plan
is the barycentric projection.
Definition 5.6 (Barycentric projection). Let γ∈ Π(µ,ν) on Rd× Rd, and assume the conditional
first moment in y is finite. If γ( dy|x) is a conditional distribution, the barycentric projection
of γ is
Tγ(x) =
∫
yγ( dy|x).
This is the conditional mean destination of mass starting atx.
Page 77/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 78 ===
5 WASSERSTEIN DISTANCES, QUANTILES AND BARYCENTERS
Remark 5.5 (Projection is not exact transport). The barycentric projection usually does not
push µ exactly to ν. It summarizes a possibly randomized plan by a deterministic map. This
may be useful for interpretability, but one should not confuse it with an exact Monge map.
5.16 Computing one-dimensional Wasserstein distances
For empirical one-dimensional data, computation is simple. The statistical behaviour of empirical
Wasserstein distances is treated later, but one-dimensional sorting is the computational backbone
already here. Let x1,...,xn and y1,...,yn have equal weights. Sort both samples:
x(1) ≤···≤x(n), y(1) ≤···≤y(n).
Then
Wp(ˆµn, ˆνn)p = 1 n n∑ i=1 |x(i) −y(i)|p.
If sample sizes differ, one can compute quantiles on a common grid.
Computational note
For one-dimensional actuarial data, no linear programming solver is needed. Sorting or
empirical quantiles are enough. This makes Wasserstein distances easy to add to severity,
reserving and drift diagnostics; this code is reused conceptually in Lab 1.
# Empirical Wp between two one-dimensional samples
emp _ quantile _left <- function(x, u) {
xs <- sort(x)
n <- length(xs)
xs[ pmax(1, pmin(n, ceiling(n * u)))]
}
wasserstein _1d <- function(x, y, p = 2, grid _size = 1000) {
u <- seq(1 / ( grid _size + 1), grid _size / ( grid _size + 1), length.out
= grid _size)
qx <- emp _ quantile _left(x, u)
qy <- emp _ quantile _left(y, u)
mean( abs(qx - qy)^p)^(1 / p)
}
# Example: compare two loss samples
# W2 <- wasserstein _1d(losses _2025, losses _2026, p = 2)
# W1 <- wasserstein _1d(losses _2025, losses _2026, p = 1)
5.17 Computing a one-dimensional barycenter
The same quantile idea gives a very simple empirical barycenter.
# One-dimensional Wasserstein barycenter by quantile averaging
barycenter _1d <- function(samples, weights = NULL, grid _size = 1000) {
K <- length(samples)
if ( is. null( weights)) weights <- rep(1 / K, K)
weights <- weights / sum( weights)
u <- seq(1 / ( grid _size + 1), grid _size / ( grid _size + 1), length.out
= grid _size)
Q <- sapply(samples, function(x) emp _ quantile _left(x, u))
qbar <- as. numeric( Q % * % weights)
data. frame(u = u, qbar = qbar)

}
# Example:
# b <- barycenter _1d(list(loss _model _A, loss _model _B, loss _model _C),
# weights = c(.25, .50, .25))
The output is a quantile function. One can sample approximately from the barycenter by
drawing U∼U(0, 1) and interpolating ¯Q(U).
5.18 Actuarial applications
5.18.1 Comparing loss distributions
Given two severity distributions µ and ν, Wp(µ,ν) gives a full-distribution discrepancy. In
dimension one, this discrepancy is simply an Lp-distance between VaR curves.
Possible uses include:
• comparing claim severity before and after inflation adjustment;
• validating a fitted distribution against the empirical distribution;
• comparing experience between two territories;
• monitoring distribution drift in claim sizes over time.
5.18.2 Portfolio drift
Let µt = Law(Xt) and µt+1 = Law(Xt+1) be covariate distributions at two dates. A Wasserstein
distance between them measures how much the portfolio has shifted. If the ground metric weights
risk-relevant variables, this provides a model-monitoring diagnostic.
5.18.3 Climate and prevention
Let µ0 be the distribution of losses without a prevention measure and µ1 the distribution after
prevention. A reduction in expected loss is
∫
x dµ0(x)−
∫
x dµ1(x).
A Wasserstein distance adds another question: how much did the entire loss distribution move?
Two prevention measures may have the same expected benefit but different distributional effects.
One may reduce frequent moderate losses; another may reduce tail losses. Their Wasserstein
paths and quantile differences reveal this distinction.
Further reading
The applications in this subsection are expanded later: portfolio drift in the drift and
domain-adaptation chapter, and climate and prevention in the reserving/climate chapter.
The one-dimensional computational routines in Sections 5.16–5.17 are intended to be
reused in the empirical Wasserstein labs.

In one dimension, optimal transport is almost synonymous with quantile matching. If X∼µ,
Y ∼ν, and U∼U(0, 1), then
X =Qµ(U), Y =Qν(U)

defines the increasing coupling. The scalar U =Fµ(X) is the rank of X, and the map
T (x) =Qν(Fµ(x))
sends µ to ν. Thus, in dimension one, transport can be described as follows: transform
observations into ranks, then transform ranks into the target distribution.
In higher dimension, there is no canonical total order on Rd. Hence there is no unique distri-
bution function F (x) whose inverse could play the role of a multivariate quantile. Nevertheless,
quadratic optimal transport provides a useful substitute. The idea is to replace the scalar uniform
rank by a reference probability measure on Rd, and to define ranks and quantiles through optimal
transport maps.

The construction below is closely related to vector quantiles and vector ranks in Carlier

theorem in Lecture 4 and with the convex-analysis insert in the mathematical appendix.
Definition 5.7 (Reference measure and vector rank). Let µ0 ∈P2(Rd) be a reference measure,
for instance the uniform distribution on [0, 1]d or the standard Gaussian distribution N (0,Id).

Figure 7: Transport between two bounded distributions. The red curve shows the empirical

LetX∼µ∈P2(Rd). A vector rank map forX, relative to µ0, is a map
Rµ : Rd → Rd
such that
(Rµ)#µ =µ0.
When Rµ is chosen as the quadratic optimal transport map from µ to µ0, it is called the optimal
transport rank map. Brenier uniqueness for this map requires the source law µ to be absolutely
continuous; otherwise a rank map may be non-unique or plan-based.
The analogy with the univariate case is direct. In dimension one, if µ0 is the uniform
distribution on (0, 1), then
Rµ(x) =Fµ(x)
is exactly the usual rank transform. In dimension d, Rµ(x) is a vector-valued rank: it places the
pointx inside the reference geometry µ0.
Definition 5.8 (Vector quantile). Let µ0 ∈P2(Rd) be absolutely continuous and let µ∈P2(Rd).
A vector quantile map for µ, relative to µ0, is a map
Qµ : Rd → Rd
Page 81/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 82 ===
5 WASSERSTEIN DISTANCES, QUANTILES AND BARYCENTERS
such that
(Qµ)#µ0 =µ.
When Qµ is chosen as the quadratic optimal transport map from µ0 to µ, it is called the optimal
transport vector quantile map.
Thus the vector quantile map plays the role of F−1 µ , while the vector rank map plays the role
of Fµ. Under the hypotheses of Brenier’s theorem, the vector quantile has the form
Qµ(u) =∇φ(u),
where φ is a convex function. Similarly, when µ is absolutely continuous, the optimal rank map
is the gradient of a convex function transporting µ back to µ0.
Proposition 5.4 (Univariate ranks as a special case). Let d = 1, let µ0 be the uniform distribution
on (0, 1), and let µ be a probability distribution on R with continuous strictly increasing distribution
function Fµ. Then the optimal transport rank and quantile maps are
Rµ(x) =Fµ(x), Qµ(u) =F−1 µ (u).
Proof. This is the one-dimensional monotone rearrangement theorem; see Villani 2003; Villani
2008; Figalli and Glaudo 2021. The increasing map from µ to the uniform distribution is Fµ,
because Fµ(X) ∼U(0, 1) when Fµ is continuous. Conversely, the increasing map from the
uniform distribution to µ is Qµ = F−1 µ . Since the increasing coupling is optimal for convex costs
on the line, these are the optimal transport rank and quantile maps.
Proposition 5.5 (Vector quantiles from Brenier maps). Let µ0,µ∈P2(Rd), and assume that
µ0 is absolutely continuous. For the quadratic cost c(u,x) = ∥u−x∥2/2, there exists a convex
function φ such that
Qµ(u) =∇φ(u)
pushes µ0 forward to µ. Moreover, Qµ is the unique quadratic optimal transport map from µ0 to
µ, up to µ0-null sets.
Proof. This is Brenier’s theorem; see Brenier 1991; Villani 2003; Villani 2008. Because the source
measure µ0 is absolutely continuous and the cost is quadratic, the optimal Kantorovich plan is
induced by a deterministic map. This map is the gradient of a convex function. Since it pushes
µ0 to µ, it plays the role of a multivariate quantile map.
Actuarial takeaway
A scalar quantile ranks a claim, cash-flow value or univariate risk amount along one risk
dimension. A vector rank embeds a multivariate actuarial profile into a common reference
distribution. This matters when risk is genuinely multivariate: losses by line of business,
reserving cash-flow vectors, telematics summaries, or climate hazard vectors.
Rank-preserving transport. Once vector ranks are available, one can define a multivariate
analogue of quantile mapping. Suppose X∼µ is a baseline actuarial vector and ν is a target
distribution. Let Rµ be the rank map from µ to µ0, and let Qν be the vector quantile map from
µ0 to ν. Then
Tµ→ν(x) =Qν(Rµ(x))
pushes µ to ν. This is the multivariate counterpart of
Qν ◦Fµ.
It transports a point x by first finding its vector rank in the reference space, then reading off the
corresponding target quantile.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 82/416

=== page 83 ===
5.19 From univariate ranks to vector ranks
Example 5.12 (Multivariate severity stress). Let X = (X1,...,Xd) be a vector of losses by
line of business under a baseline model, and let ν be a stressed multivariate distribution. A
componentwise quantile stress would transform each coordinate separately:
Xk ↦→Qνk(Fµk(Xk)).
This preserves marginal ranks but may distort dependence. A vector-quantile stress instead uses
Tµ→ν =Qν ◦Rµ,
and therefore transports the joint distribution. It can modify marginal tails and dependence
simultaneously.
Example 5.13 (Portfolio profiles). Let X∈ Rd contain policyholder-level features such as age,
insured value, exposure, past claims, territory indicator and telematics summaries. The rank
map Rµ(X) embeds each policyholder into a common reference space. Two portfolios can then
be compared not only through marginal distributions of each feature, but through the geometry
of their joint profiles.
Vector ranks and multivariate comonotonicity. In dimension one, two random variables
X and Y are comonotonic if they can be written as increasing functions of the same scalar rank:
X =F−1 X (U), Y =F−1 Y (U).
Vector ranks suggest the following extension.
Definition 5.9 (Reference comonotonicity). Fix a reference measure µ0 and let U ∼µ0. Two
random vectors X∼µ and Y ∼ν are µ0-comonotonic if there exist convex functions φ and ψ
such that
X =∇φ(U), Y =∇ψ(U).
Thus both vectors are generated from the same vector rank U through optimal transport maps.
Proposition 5.6 (Maximal correlation interpretation). Let U ∼ µ0 and X∼ µ. Under the
quadratic transport setting, the vector quantile map Qµ =∇φ solves
sup
~X∼µ
E[~X· U],
and the optimizer is ~X =Qµ(U).
Proof. The problem is equivalent to minimizing
E∥~X− U∥2
over all ~X∼µ, because
E∥~X− U∥2 = E∥~X∥2 + E∥U∥2 − 2E[~X· U],
and the first two terms are fixed by the marginal laws. Hence maximizing correlation with U
is the same as solving the quadratic optimal transport problem between µ0 and µ. Brenier’s
theorem gives the optimizer as Qµ(U) =∇φ(U).
This interpretation is useful in risk theory. In one dimension, comonotonicity corresponds
to maximal positive dependence and is closely related to worst-case aggregation for many law-
invariant risk measures. In higher dimension, µ0-comonotonicity provides an analogous notion,
but it depends on the chosen reference distribution µ0. There is no reference-free total order on
Rd.
Page 83/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 84 ===
5 WASSERSTEIN DISTANCES, QUANTILES AND BARYCENTERS
Example 5.14 (Gaussian vector ranks). Let µ0 = N (0,Id) and let µ = N (m, Σ), where Σ is
positive definite. The quadratic optimal vector quantile map is affine:
Qµ(u) =m + Σ1/2u.
More generally, if µ0 =N (0, Σ0), then
Qµ(u) =m + Σ−1/2 0 ( Σ1/2 0 ΣΣ1/2 0 )1/2 Σ−1/2 0 u.
Thus, for Gaussian laws, vector ranks and vector quantiles reduce to linear transformations.
This is useful for multivariate capital models, where changes in covariance encode changes in
diversification.
Why this matters for actuarial science. Vector ranks are useful whenever componentwise
quantiles are too poor to describe the risk.
• In capital modelling, the vector X may contain losses by line of business. Componentwise
quantile matching ignores diversification; vector transport preserves the joint law.
• In reserving, the vector X may contain future cash-flows by development year. Vector
ranks compare complete cash-flow scenarios rather than marginal payments year by year.
• In climate risk, the vector X may contain losses in several regions or hazards. Vector
transport can represent changes in spatial dependence.
• In portfolio monitoring, vector ranks allow one to compare the joint distribution of policy-
holder profiles across time.
Computational note
In practice, exact vector rank maps are difficult to estimate in high dimension. Common
computational approximations include discrete optimal transport, entropic regularization,
semi-discrete transport, normal-score Gaussian approximations, and sliced Wasserstein
methods. Thus vector ranks are conceptually clean, but their empirical use requires
regularization and careful validation.
Limitations. The vector-rank construction depends on the reference measure µ0. In dimension
one, the uniform distribution on (0, 1) is canonical. In higher dimension, the choice is part of
the modelling decision. A uniform reference on a cube emphasizes bounded ranks; a Gaussian
reference emphasizes elliptical geometry; an empirical reference may better reflect a portfolio
benchmark. Different choices of µ0 may lead to different notions of multivariate rank and
comonotonicity.
There is also no simple ordering interpretation. A large scalar rank means “large loss” in
a clear sense. A vector rank does not say that one point is larger than another; it says where
the point lies in a reference geometry. Thus vector ranks are best interpreted geometrically, not
ordinally.
Actuarial takeaway
The univariate formula Qν ◦Fµ is rank-preserving because there is a natural order on the
real line. In several dimensions, the analogue is Qν ◦Rµ, where ranks and quantiles are
defined by optimal transport to and from a reference distribution. This construction is
useful, but it makes the choice of reference geometry an actuarial modelling decision.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 84/416

=== page 85 ===
5.20 Summary
5.20 Summary
This lecture introduced Wasserstein distances as optimal transport costs with metric ground cost.
Unlike scalar summaries, Wasserstein distances compare complete distributions. In one dimension,
they reduce to Lp-distances between quantile functions, making them directly connected to
VaR curves and TVaR. The lecture also introduced displacement interpolation and Wasserstein
barycenters. In dimension one, barycenters are computed by averaging quantile functions. For
actuarial science, these tools provide a geometry for losses, reserves, scenario distributions,
climate stresses and multivariate actuarial profiles.
5.21 Further reading
Further reading
The metric and geometric foundations of Wasserstein distances are treated in depth
in Villani 2003; Villani 2008; Santambrogio 2015. A concise introduction emphasizing
Wasserstein distances and gradient flows is Figalli and Glaudo 2021; the statistical viewpoint
is developed in Panaretos and Zemel 2020, with empirical convergence questions treated
for example in Barrio et al. 1999a; Fournier and Guillin 2015. Computational aspects,
including barycenters, are covered in Peyré and Cuturi 2019. Wasserstein barycenters
originate in Agueh and Carlier 2011; vector quantiles and vector ranks are developed in
Chernozhukov et al. 2017; Carlier et al. 2016; Galichon 2016.
5.22 Exercises
Exercise 5.1 (Dirac and two-point laws). Let µ = δ0 and ν = pδ0 + (1 −p)δa, where a > 0.
Compute Wp(µ,ν) for a general exponent p≥ 1. Interpret the result as a rare-loss perturbation.
Exercise 5.2 (Uniform laws). Let µ = U[0, 1] and ν = U[0,a], with a> 0. Compute W1(µ,ν)
and W2(µ,ν). What happens when a→∞?
Exercise 5.3 (TVaR stability). Let µ,ν∈P1(R). Prove that
|TVaRα(µ)− TVaRα(ν)|≤ 1 1−α W1(µ,ν).
Then improve the bound using W2 and Hölder’s inequality.
Exercise 5.4 (Gaussian laws). Let µ = N (m0,σ2 0) and ν = N (m1,σ2 1). Use quantiles to derive
W2 2(µ,ν) = (m0 −m1)2 + (σ0 −σ1)2.
What is W1(µ,ν) when σ0 =σ1? What if m0 =m1?
Exercise 5.5 (Barycenter of two distributions). Let µ0,µ1 ∈P2(R) and let λ∈ [0, 1]. Show that
the barycenter of µ0 and µ1 with weights 1−λ and λ has quantile function
Qλ(u) = (1−λ)Q0(u) +λQ1(u).
Explain why this is also the displacement interpolation between µ0 and µ1 in dimension one.
Exercise 5.6 (Mixture versus barycenter). Let µ1 = δ0 and µ2 = δ2, with equal weights.
Compute the mixture and the W2-barycenter. Repeat with µ1 = N (0, 1) and µ2 = N (2, 1).
Discuss the actuarial difference between model averaging by mixture and model averaging by
barycenter.

6 Entropic Regularization and Sinkhorn Algorithms
Learning objectives
The previous lecture introduced Wasserstein distances as a geometry on probability distributions.
This lecture explains how these distances can be computed at scale. Exact discrete optimal
transport is a linear program. It is conceptually clean, but it becomes expensive when the
number of support points is large. Entropic regularization replaces the original linear program
by a strictly convex problem whose solution can be computed by simple matrix scaling.
After this lecture, the reader should be able to:
(i) formulate the entropically regularized transport problem for two discrete distributions;
(ii) derive the scaling form of the regularized optimal plan;
(iii) state and implement Sinkhorn’s algorithm;
(iv) understand the role of the regularization parameter ε;
(v) explain the numerical bias introduced by entropic regularization;
(vi) define the Sinkhorn divergence and explain why it corrects part of that bias;
(vii) use log-domain stabilization in simple computations;
(viii) identify actuarial situations where fast approximate transport is more useful than exact
transport;
(ix) connect entropic transport to portfolio drift, reserving scenarios and climate model aggre-
gation.
Further reading
This lecture builds on the discrete formulation of Lecture 3, the continuous Kantorovich
problem of Lecture 4, and the Wasserstein geometry of Lecture 5. Entropic regularization
and Sinkhorn scaling are standard tools in computational optimal transport; see Cuturi
2013; Peyré and Cuturi 2019. The matrix-scaling viewpoint goes back to Sinkhorn
and Knopp 1967; Franklin and Lorenz 1989; Knight 2008, and the entropy-projection
interpretation is closely related to Csiszár 1975; Bregman 1967; Benamou et al. 2015.
Sinkhorn divergences and their interpolation between optimal-transport and kernel-type
behavior are developed in Feydy et al. 2019. Stabilized and large-scale implementations
are discussed in Schmitzer 2019; Altschuler et al. 2017.
Actuarial takeaway
Entropic regularization turns optimal transport from a linear programming problem into a
smooth scaling problem. In actuarial language, it replaces a sparse cheapest matching by
a cheap matching penalized for excessive concentration. The parameter ε controls how
much diversification is imposed on the coupling.
6.1 Why regularize optimal transport?
Let
µ =
n∑

aiδxi, ν =
m∑

bjδyj,
