6 ENTROPIC REGULARIZATION AND SINKHORN ALGORITHMS
where a = (a1,...,an)⊤ ∈ Rn +, b = (b1,...,bm)⊤ ∈ Rm + , and ∑ iai = ∑ j bj = 1. For a cost
matrix C∈ Rn×m, with Cij = c(xi,yj), the discrete optimal transport problem, introduced in
Definition 3.4, is
OTC(a,b) = min Γ∈U(a,b) ⟨C, Γ⟩,
where the transportation polytope, defined in Definition 3.2, is
U(a,b) =
{
Γ∈ Rn×m + : Γ1m =a, Γ⊤1n =b } ,
and

CijΓij.
This problem is a linear program. It has a clear economic interpretation: Γij is the mass trans-
ported from xi to yj. But several computational and statistical difficulties appear immediately.
First, the number of variables is nm. If n = m = 104, the transport plan has 108 entries.
Even storing it can be difficult. Second, exact optimal plans are often sparse and non-smooth.
This is useful if the goal is an exact combinatorial matching, but it can be unstable if the
empirical distributions are noisy. Third, many actuarial uses of optimal transport require
repeated computation: monthly drift monitoring, bootstrap inference, scenario aggregation, or
optimization over transport losses.
Entropic regularization addresses these issues by adding a convex penalty to the transport plan.
The result is no longer an exact optimal transport cost, but it is smooth, stable, and computable
by Sinkhorn scaling. The main reference for this computational viewpoint is Peyré and Cuturi
2019; the same ideas are also closely connected with matrix scaling, entropy minimization and
the static Schrödinger bridge problem.
6.2 Entropy of a transport plan
For a nonnegative matrix Γ, define the entropy-type functional
H(Γ) =
∑
i,j
Γij(log Γij − 1),
with the convention 0 log 0 = 0. Some authors use −∑ ij Γij log Γij and call this entropy. Here
the sign is chosen so that H is convex. Indeed, the function t↦→t logt−t is strictly convex on
R+.
The entropically regularized transport problem is
OTε(a,b;C) = min Γ∈U(a,b)


where ε> 0.
The additional term makes the objective strictly convex on the positive orthant. Since U(a,b)
is compact when a and b are probability vectors, the minimum exists. If ai > 0 and bj > 0 for
all i,j, the minimizer is unique and strictly positive.
Definition 6.1 (Entropic optimal transport). Let a∈ Rn + and b∈ Rm + be probability vectors and
let C∈ Rn×m. For ε> 0, the entropic transport cost is the value of (1). The unique minimizer
is denoted by Γε. When the cost matrix needs to be shown explicitly, we write OTε,C(a,b).
Definition 6.2 (Discrete Kullback–Leibler divergence). For nonnegative matrices P = (pij) and

Proposition 6.1 (Entropic OT as a KL projection). Let Kij = exp(−Cij/ε). Up to the additive
constant ε∑ i,j Kij, the objective in (1) is

Remark 6.1 (A sign convention). The term ∑ ij Γij(log Γij − 1) is negative for many entries
smaller than one. This is not a problem: adding the constant ε or using ∑ Γij log Γij instead
would change the value by a fixed amount on U(a,b), because ∑ ij Γij = 1. The optimizer is
unchanged.
Actuarial takeaway
The entropy penalty discourages deterministic or overly concentrated matchings. For noisy
empirical portfolios, this is often desirable: the resulting transport plan is less sensitive to
a few individual policies or claims. In this sense, ε acts like a smoothing parameter.
6.3 First-order conditions and the scaling form
The central reason why entropic transport is computationally attractive is that the optimal plan
has a multiplicative scaling form. We derive it in the finite setting.

6 ENTROPIC REGULARIZATION AND SINKHORN ALGORITHMS
Theorem 6.1 (Scaling form of the entropic plan). Assume ai > 0, bj > 0, and Kij =
exp(−Cij/ε)> 0. Then the unique minimizer of (1) has the form
Γε = diag(u)K diag(v),
for two positive vectors u and v. These vectors are unique up to multiplication of u by a positive
constant and division ofv by the same constant.
Proof sketch. The first-order conditions above show that any interior optimum must have the
stated form. The entropy term forces the optimizer to be strictly positive when all marginals
are positive and all entries of K are positive. The scaling vectors are then determined by the
marginal constraints. If (u,v) and (˜u, ˜v) yield the same positive matrix with the same kernel K,
then ~ui/ui must be constant across rows and ~vj/vj the reciprocal constant across columns.
Remark 6.2 (Computational meaning). The original linear program has nm unknowns. The
regularized problem still has a transport plan with nm entries, but the optimizer is determined
by only n +m scaling parameters. Sinkhorn’s algorithm exploits exactly this reduction.
6.4 Sinkhorn’s algorithm
From the marginal constraints, the scaling vectors must satisfy
u = a Kv, v = b K⊤u,
where divisions are componentwise. This suggests the iterative scheme
u(ℓ+1) = a Kv(ℓ) , v(ℓ+1) = b K⊤u(ℓ+1) . (3)
This is Sinkhorn’s algorithm, also known as iterative proportional fitting or matrix balancing.
Definition 6.3 (Sinkhorn iteration). Let K∈ Rn×m ++ , a∈ Rn ++, b∈ Rm ++, with equal total
mass. Starting from v(0) = 1m, Sinkhorn’s algorithm alternates the two updates in (3). The
approximate transport plan after ℓ iterations is
Γ(ℓ) = diag(u(ℓ))K diag(v(ℓ)).
Computational note
Each Sinkhorn step requires one multiplication by K and one multiplication by K⊤. If K
is dense, this costs O(nm) per iteration. If the cost has special structure, for example a
grid or a convolution kernel, the multiplication can sometimes be accelerated.
Theorem 6.2 (Sinkhorn convergence, informal statement). If a and b are positive probability
vectors and K has strictly positive entries, then Sinkhorn’s algorithm converges to scaling vectors
(u,v) such that diag(u)K diag(v)∈U(a,b). Consequently, Γ(ℓ) → Γε.
Proof idea. The algorithm alternately enforces the row sums and the column sums. In entropy
geometry, these two operations are projections onto affine marginal constraint sets. The conver-
gence follows from classical results on alternating projections in Kullback–Leibler divergence or,
equivalently, from matrix scaling theory.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 90/416

=== page 91 ===
6.5 A small actuarial example
6.5 A small actuarial example
Suppose an insurer compares two empirical severity distributions on three levels:
x = (0, 1, 4), y = (0, 2, 5),
with weights
a = (0.50, 0.30, 0.20), b = (0.40, 0.40, 0.20).
Use squared cost Cij = (xi −yj)2, where xi and yj are the components ofx andy. Then
C =

 0 4 25 1 1 16
16 4 1

.
For a small value of ε, Kij = exp(−Cij/ε) puts almost all weight on low-cost movements. For a
larger value of ε, even relatively expensive movements remain possible. The optimizer is therefore
more diffuse.
With ε = 0.5, entries with cost 25 or 16 are almost zero. The plan resembles exact optimal
transport. With ε = 5, the plan keeps more cross-matching between severity levels. This may be
computationally helpful, but the value differs more from the unregularized OT value.
Actuarial takeaway
In a severity comparison, a small ε emphasizes sharp tail matching. A larger ε produces
smoother comparisons and is less sensitive to individual observations. The choice depends
on whether the actuarial question is tail attribution, routine monitoring, or exploratory
visualization.
6.6 The dual problem
Entropic regularization also gives a smooth dual problem. Starting from the Lagrangian above
and minimizing over Γij ≥ 0, one obtains
OTε(a,b;C) = max α∈Rn,β∈Rm


α⊤a +β⊤b−ε ∑ i,j exp (αi +βj −Cij ε )   (4)
up to the harmless additive convention associated with the entropy definition. At optimality,
Γij = exp
(αi +βj −Cij
ε
)
.
The dual variables α and β are entropic analogues of Kantorovich potentials. As ε→ 0, they
approach optimal transport potentials under suitable conditions.
A useful way to express the dual updates is through a soft minimum. Givenβ, maximizing
over αi yields the exact update
αi =ε logai −ε log

∑
j
exp
(βj −Cij
ε
)
.
Equivalently,
αi =ε logai + softminε{Cij −βj :j = 1,...,m}.
The corresponding update for βj is obtained by exchanging rows and columns. Thus the hard c-
transform of classical optimal transport, introduced in Definition 4.4, is replaced by a log-sum-exp
transform.

Definition 6.4 (Soft minimum). For numbers z1,...,zm and ε> 0, define
softminε(z1,...,zm) =−ε log



As ε→ 0, softminε(z1,...,zm)→ minj zj.
Remark 6.3 (Smooth duality). The log-sum-exp smoothing is a recurrent theme in convex
optimization. Entropic OT replaces a non-smooth linear programming dual by a smooth concave
maximization problem. This is one reason why entropic costs can be differentiated and used
inside larger statistical or machine-learning procedures.
6.7 What does ε do?
The parameter ε controls the compromise between transport cost and entropy. This can be
understood in three complementary ways.
1. The small-ε regime
When ε is small, the penalty is weak. The optimizer is close to an exact OT plan. The kernel
Kij =e−Cij/ε
becomes sharply concentrated on low-cost pairs. Numerically, this is the most challenging regime:
many entries of K may underflow to zero.
In actuarial terms, small ε is useful when the exact geometry matters: tail matching, stress
testing, precise decomposition of distributional differences.
2. The large-ε regime
When ε is large, the entropy term dominates. The optimizer approaches the independent coupling
ab⊤, because the independent coupling has maximal entropy among all couplings with marginals
a and b. The transport cost then resembles an average cost under random matching rather than
optimal matching.
In actuarial terms, large ε gives a diffuse comparison. It is useful for exploratory analysis
and robust smoothing, but it can overstate the amount of economically meaningful movement.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 92/416

=== page 93 ===
6.8 Bias of the regularized cost
3. The intermediate regime
In practice, one often chooses ε so that the plan is neither too sparse nor too diffuse. A common
rule of thumb is to scale the cost matrix so that meaningful movements have costs of order one,
then choose ε in a range where Sinkhorn converges stably and the plan remains interpretable.
Computational note
The value of ε is not dimensionless. If costs are multiplied by λ, then ε should also be
multiplied by λ to obtain the same kernel. It is therefore usually preferable to standardize
costs before comparing results across portfolios.
6.8 Bias of the regularized cost
The entropic cost OTε,C(a,b) is not the same as OTC(a,b). In the convention used in (1), the
value can be shifted by entropy constants; more importantly, the optimizer is different. The
regularization introduces bias.
There are two related effects.
First, because the entropy term favors diffuse plans, the minimizer may use transport routes
that are not used by exact OT. The expected transport cost ⟨C, Γε⟩ is therefore typically larger
than the exact optimal value OTC(a,b). Second, if one uses the full regularized objective, the
entropy contribution changes the scale and can make self-comparisons nonzero:
OTε(a,a;C)
need not be zero. This is undesirable if one wants a divergence between distributions.
Example 6.1 (Self-cost). Let a = b and suppose Cii = 0 while Cij > 0 for i̸= j. Exact OT
can use the diagonal plan and has zero cost. Entropic OT spreads some mass away from the
diagonal unless ε is extremely small. Therefore the regularized plan has positive transport cost,
and the full regularized value includes a nontrivial entropy contribution.
6.9 Sinkhorn divergence
A common correction is the Sinkhorn divergence. It is best written at the level of measures,
because the self-comparisons generally use different cost matrices from the cross-comparison. Let
ˆµ =
n∑
i=1
aiδxi, ˆν =
m∑
j=1
bjδyj.
Write OTε(ˆµ, ˆν) for the entropic value computed with the cross-cost c(xi,yj). Then define
Sε(ˆµ, ˆν) = OTε(ˆµ, ˆν)− 1 2OTε(ˆµ, ˆµ)− 1 2OTε(ˆν, ˆν), (5)
where the two self-terms are computed with the costs c(xi,xk) and c(yj,yℓ), respectively.
Definition 6.5 (Sinkhorn divergence). Given a regularized transport cost OTε, the Sinkhorn
divergence between two empirical measures ˆµ and ˆν is the debiased quantity in (5).
The correction ensures that
Sε(ˆµ, ˆµ) = 0.
It also reduces the entropic bias while keeping the favorable computational and differentiability
properties of entropic transport. In many applications, Sε interpolates between Wasserstein-like
behavior for small ε and kernel/MMD-like behavior for large ε.
Page 93/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 94 ===
6 ENTROPIC REGULARIZATION AND SINKHORN ALGORITHMS
Remark 6.4 (Notation for self-costs). In matrix notation, OTε(a,b; Cxy), OTε(a,a; Cxx), and
OTε(b,b; Cyy) generally use three different cost matrices. Writing the Sinkhorn divergence only
as a function of a, b, and a single matrix C can be misleading unless all measures live on the
same support.
Actuarial takeaway
For monitoring a portfolio over time, a self-comparison should be zero. This makes
Sinkhorn divergences more natural than raw entropic costs when the goal is drift detection
or dashboard reporting. The same warning applies to reserving-model comparisons: the
debiased divergence is usually easier to interpret than the raw regularized cost.
6.10 Numerical stability and log-domain Sinkhorn
When ε is small or the costs are large, entries of
Kij =e−Cij/ε
can underflow to zero. Then the simple updates
u←a/(Kv), v←b/(K⊤u)
can become unstable. The standard remedy is to work in the log domain.
Let
fi =ε logui, gj =ε logvj.
Then
Γij = exp
(fi +gj −Cij
ε
)
.
The row constraint ∑ j Γij =ai becomes
∑
j
exp
(fi +gj −Cij
ε
)
=ai.
Solving for fi,
fi =ε logai −ε log

∑
j
exp
(gj −Cij
ε
)
.
Similarly,
gj =ε logbj −ε log
(∑
i
exp
(fi −Cij
ε
))
.
These updates can be evaluated stably using the log-sum-exp trick:
log
∑
k
ezk =zmax + log ∑
k
ezk−zmax, zmax = max k zk.
Computational note
For teaching examples, ordinary Sinkhorn updates are often enough. For real actuarial
data, especially with costs based on monetary losses, log-domain stabilization is essential.
Otherwise the algorithm may fail for exactly the small values of ε that are most Wasserstein-
like.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 94/416

=== page 95 ===
6.11 Stopping criteria
6.11 Stopping criteria
A simple stopping criterion is based on marginal violations:
rrow =∥Γ1m −a∥1, rcol =∥Γ⊤1n −b∥1.
The algorithm stops when
rrow +rcol ≤τ,
for a prescribed tolerance τ.
Another criterion uses the relative change in the objective:
|J(ℓ+1) −J(ℓ)|
1 +|J(ℓ)| ≤τ.
For most actuarial reporting tasks, marginal feasibility is the most interpretable criterion: the
transport plan should move the correct amount of mass from each source cell and into each
target cell.
Remark 6.5 (Approximate feasibility). When the plan is used only to compute a scalar divergence,
small marginal errors may be acceptable. When the plan is interpreted economically, for example
as a redistribution of losses, marginal errors should be monitored carefully.
6.12 Differentiability and sensitivity
One major advantage of entropic OT is differentiability. The unregularized OT cost is typically
non-smooth as a function of the weights, support points, or model parameters. Entropic
regularization smooths it.
Suppose the source locationsxi(θ) depend on a parameter θ, and the cost matrix is
Cij(θ) =c(xi(θ),yj).
Under regularity conditions, the derivative of the entropic value with respect to Cij is
∂OTε
∂Cij
= Γε,ij.
By the chain rule,
d
dθOTε(a,b;C(θ)) =
∑
i,j
Γε,ij
∂Cij(θ)
∂θ .
This is an envelope theorem: the derivative of the optimized value is computed by differentiating
the cost at the optimal plan.
Actuarial takeaway
This differentiability is useful when a reserving model, severity model or climate adjustment
is calibrated by minimizing a transport loss. Exact OT can be non-smooth; entropic OT is
easier to optimize inside larger estimation problems.
6.13 Implementation in R
The following minimal R code implements Sinkhorn’s algorithm for two empirical one-dimensional
distributions. It is deliberately simple. It is suitable for lecture notes, but not for production use.
Page 95/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 96 ===
6 ENTROPIC REGULARIZATION AND SINKHORN ALGORITHMS
sinkhorn <- function(x, y, a = NULL, b = NULL, eps = 0.1,
p = 2, maxit = 5000, tol = 1e-9) {
n <- length(x)
m <- length(y)
if ( is. null(a)) a <- rep(1 / n, n)
if ( is. null(b)) b <- rep(1 / m, m)
stopifnot( abs( sum(a) - 1) < 1e-10, abs( sum(b) - 1) < 1e-10)
stopifnot( all(a > 0), all(b > 0), eps > 0)
C <- outer(x, y, function(u, v) abs(u - v)^p)
K <- exp(- C / eps)
u <- rep(1, n)
v <- rep(1, m)
for (it in seq _len(maxit)) {
u _ old <- u
u <- a / as. vector(K % * % v)
v <- b / as. vector( t(K) % * % u)
if ( max( abs(u - u _ old)) < tol) break
}
Gamma <- K * outer(u, v)
value _transport <- sum( C * Gamma)
value _regularized <- value _transport +
eps * sum( Gamma * ( log( pmax( Gamma, 1e-300)) - 1))
list(plan = Gamma,
cost = C,
transport _value = value _transport,
regularized _value = value _regularized,
iterations = it,
row _error = sum( abs(rowSums( Gamma) - a)),
col _error = sum( abs(colSums( Gamma) - b)))
}
set.seed(123)
x <- rgamma(80, shape = 2, rate = 1)
y <- rgamma(100, shape = 2.2, rate = 0.9)
fit <- sinkhorn(x, y, eps = 0.2, p = 2)
fit $transport _value
fit $ row _error + fit $ col _error
For large datasets, one should avoid forming dense diagonal matrices. The expression
Gamma <- K * outer(u, v)
is clearer and less error-prone than explicitly multiplying by two diagonal matrices.
6.14 A log-domain R implementation
A more stable version updates the dual potentials. The helper function below computes
log ∑ j exp(zj) stably.
logsumexp <- function(z) {
zmax <- max(z)
zmax + log( sum( exp(z - zmax)))
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 96/416

=== page 97 ===
6.15 Actuarial application 1: portfolio drift monitoring
}
sinkhorn _ log <- function(x, y, a = NULL, b = NULL, eps = 0.1,
p = 2, maxit = 2000, tol = 1e-8) {
n <- length(x)
m <- length(y)
if ( is. null(a)) a <- rep(1 / n, n)
if ( is. null(b)) b <- rep(1 / m, m)
C <- outer(x, y, function(u, v) abs(u - v)^p)
f <- rep(0, n)
g <- rep(0, m)
loga <- log(a)
logb <- log(b)
for (it in seq _len(maxit)) {
f _ old <- f
for (i in seq _len(n)) {
z <- (g - C[i, ]) / eps
f[i] <- eps * (loga[i] - logsumexp(z))
}
for (j in seq _len(m)) {
z <- (f - C[, j]) / eps
g[j] <- eps * (logb[j] - logsumexp(z))
}
if ( max( abs(f - f _ old)) < tol) break
}
eps _logGamma <- outer(f, g, "+") - C
logGamma <- eps _logGamma / eps
Gamma <- exp(logGamma)
list(plan = Gamma,
transport _value = sum( C * Gamma),
iterations = it,
marginal _error = sum( abs(rowSums( Gamma) - a)) +
sum( abs(colSums( Gamma) - b)))
}
This version is slower because it uses explicit loops, but it is much more stable. It is useful
for teaching the dual-potential interpretation.
6.15 Actuarial application 1: portfolio drift monitoring
Suppose a company observes a portfolio at two dates. Each policyholder is represented by a
vector
z = (age,vehicle value,region score,predicted frequency,predicted severity).
Let µt and µt+1 be the empirical distributions of these feature vectors at two dates. A Wasserstein
or Sinkhorn divergence between µt and µt+1 measures portfolio drift.
This is more informative than comparing coordinate-wise means. A portfolio can keep the
same average age and average vehicle value while changing the association between age and
vehicle value. A transport divergence captures the joint distributional movement.
This application is developed further in the chapter on portfolio drift and domain adaptation,
and it is close to the exercises in Lab 1 on empirical Wasserstein distances. A practical workflow
is:
(i) choose a standardized feature vectorz;
Page 97/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 98 ===
6 ENTROPIC REGULARIZATION AND SINKHORN ALGORITHMS
(ii) define a ground cost c(z,z′), often squared Euclidean distance after scaling;
(iii) compute Sε(µt,µt+1) monthly;
(iv) decompose the movement by feature groups or by projected one-dimensional transports;
(v) trigger actuarial review when the divergence exceeds a threshold.
Actuarial takeaway
A portfolio drift indicator based on Sinkhorn divergence detects changes in distribution,
not only changes in averages. This is useful for portfolio monitoring, reserving assumptions
and model governance.
6.16 Actuarial application 3: reserving distributions
Reserving applications are developed later in the chapter on cash-flow distributions and in Lab 5.
Reserving models produce predictive distributions for future payments. Suppose several models
are fitted to the same triangle: Mack chain ladder, overdispersed Poisson GLM, bootstrap chain
ladder, Bayesian model and individual claims model. Each model yields a predictive distribution
µk for the outstanding loss.
Pairwise Sinkhorn divergences can be used to compare these predictive distributions. A
barycenter can summarize them. In one dimension, a Wasserstein barycenter is often preferable
and easier to compute through quantiles. In higher-dimensional cash-flow reserving, where each
observation is a payment vector over future development years, entropic transport becomes
useful.
The cost may be
c(x,y) =
T∑
t=1
wt(xt −yt)2,
where xt and yt are future payments at development time t, and wt are discounting or materiality
weights.
Actuarial takeaway
For reserving, the object of interest is often not only the ultimate claim, but the cash-flow
pattern. Entropic OT can compare full predictive cash-flow distributions, not just best
estimates or one-year risk measures.
6.17 Actuarial application 4: climate and catastrophe scenarios
Catastrophe and climate applications are developed later in the reserving-and-prevention chapter
and in the chapter on unbalanced transport. Catastrophe models often generate spatial dis-
tributions of losses. A scenario can be represented by a discrete distribution over geographic
cells:
µ =
∑
i
aiδxi,
wherexi isalocationandai isnormalizedexpectedloss, probabilityofclaim, orexposure-weighted
loss. Comparing two climate scenarios then becomes a transport problem on geography.
Entropic regularization is particularly useful here because spatial grids can be large. The
regularized plan can be interpreted as a smooth displacement of loss intensity from one region to
another. A small ε detects localized shifts; a larger ε detects broad regional changes.
Possible uses include:
(i) comparing current and future flood risk maps;
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 98/416

=== page 99 ===
6.18 Unbalanced entropic transport
(ii) measuring the effect of adaptation on the spatial distribution of losses;
(iii) aggregating catastrophe model outputs from several vendors;
(iv) identifying whether risk is shifted geographically or simply reduced in total.
If total mass changes, balanced OT may not be appropriate. A prevention measure may
reduce total expected loss. A climate scenario may increase it. This motivates unbalanced
transport.
6.18 Unbalanced entropic transport
The classical problem requires equal total masses and exact marginal constraints. In some
actuarial situations, this is too restrictive. A prevention action may remove expected loss mass.
A new hazard may create additional mass. A portfolio may grow or shrink.
Unbalanced OT relaxes the marginal constraints and penalizes deviations from the prescribed
marginals. A common formulation is
min
Γ≥0


⟨C, Γ⟩ +ε
∑
ij
Γij(log Γij − 1) +λaKL(Γ1m |a) +λbKL(Γ⊤1n |b)


.
Here KL(r|a) is the vector version of Definition 6.2,
KL(r|a) =
∑
i
{
ri log ri ai −ri +ai
}
,
and it penalizes the discrepancy between the transported source marginal r and the nominal
source mass a. The parameters λa and λb control how strongly the marginals are enforced. This
is a preview of the dedicated chapter on unbalanced transport.
When λa,λb →∞, one recovers balanced entropic OT. When they are small, mass can be
created or destroyed at lower penalty.
Actuarial takeaway
Balanced OT is natural for redistributing a fixed amount of probability mass. Unbalanced
OT is natural for comparing expected loss maps before and after prevention, because the
total expected loss may change.
6.19 Choosing the ground cost
Entropic regularization makes computation easier, but it does not remove the need to choose a
meaningful ground cost. The cost matrix C is the actuarial modeling choice. It defines what it
means for two policies, losses, cells or scenarios to be close.
Some typical choices are:
(i) Severity cost: Cij =|xi −yj|p, for scalar losses.
(ii) Feature cost: Cij =∥zi −z′ j∥2, after standardizing features.
(iii) Geographic cost: Cij =dgeo(xi,yj)2, for spatial risk.
(iv) Cash-flow cost: Cij = ∑ twt(xit −yjt)2, for reserving distributions.
A poor ground cost can make a transport analysis misleading. If age, income, claim frequency
and region are mixed in a Euclidean cost without scaling, the variable with the largest numerical
scale may dominate. In actuarial work, cost design should be documented as part of model
governance.
Page 99/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 100 ===
6 ENTROPIC REGULARIZATION AND SINKHORN ALGORITHMS
Remark 6.6 (Cost calibration). A useful practice is to choose the scaling of each coordinate
so that a one-unit standardized movement has comparable actuarial meaning. For instance,
moving one standard deviation in predicted frequency should be comparable to moving one
standard deviation in predicted severity only if the analyst believes these movements have similar
relevance.
6.20 Exact OT versus entropic OT
Exact and entropic transport answer slightly different questions. Exact OT asks for the cheapest
feasible coupling. Entropic OT asks for a coupling that is cheap but also entropically spread.
Feature Exact OT Entropic OT
Optimization Linear program Strictly convex smooth problem
Plan Often sparse Positive and diffuse
Computation LP/network methods Matrix scaling
Stability Can be non-smooth Smooth in inputs
Self-cost Zero Usually biased
Best for exact geometry large-scale repeated computation
Table 4: Exact optimal transport versus entropic optimal transport.
Neither is universally better. Exact OT is conceptually closer to the mathematical Wasserstein
distance. Entropic OT is often more practical and statistically smoother.
Actuarial takeaway
For a one-off regulatory calculation, exact OT or one-dimensional quantile OT may be
preferable. For repeated dashboards, high-dimensional portfolios, bootstrap loops or
optimization-based calibration, entropic OT is often the practical choice.
6.21 Mini-lab: severity distribution drift
Consider two samples of claim severities. The following code simulates a mild distributional drift
and computes a debiased Sinkhorn quantity.
set.seed(1)
n <- 200
severity _ sample _1 <- exp( rnorm(n, mean = log(800), sd = 0.35))
severity _ sample _2 <- exp( rnorm(n, mean = log(850), sd = 0.38))
fit12 <- sinkhorn(severity _ sample _1, severity _ sample _2, eps = 5000, p =
2)
fit11 <- sinkhorn(severity _ sample _1, severity _ sample _1, eps = 5000, p =
2)
fit22 <- sinkhorn(severity _ sample _2, severity _ sample _2, eps = 5000, p =
2)
sinkhorn _div <- fit12 $regularized _value -
0.5 * fit11 $regularized _value -
0.5 * fit22 $regularized _value
sqrt( max(sinkhorn _div, 0))
The square root is reported only for interpretability, as an analogue of a distance. The value
depends heavily on the scaling of the cost and on ε. In a real monitoring system, one would
compute the same indicator consistently over time and compare it to historical variability.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 100/416

=== page 101 ===
6.22 Mini-lab: visualizing the plan
6.22 Mini-lab: visualizing the plan
The transport plan can be visualized as an image. Rows correspond to source severities and
columns to target severities. Sorting the supports makes the structure easier to read.
o1 <- order(severity _ sample _1)
o2 <- order(severity _ sample _2)
G <- fit12 $plan[o1, o2]
image( log(G + 1e-12), axes = FALSE,
main = "Log␣Sinkhorn␣transport␣plan")
For small ε, the image concentrates near a monotone band in one dimension. For larger ε,
the band becomes wider. This is a useful diagnostic: if the plan is nearly uniform, ε is probably
too large for a Wasserstein-like interpretation.
6.23 Reading guide
Further reading
For computational optimal transport, including entropic regularization, Sinkhorn algo-
rithms, Sinkhorn divergences and barycenters, see Cuturi 2013; Peyré and Cuturi 2019.
For discrete OT and algorithmic intuition, see Solomon 2018. The matrix-scaling and
iterative-proportional-fitting literature includes Sinkhorn and Knopp 1967; Franklin and
Lorenz 1989; Rüschendorf 1995; Knight 2008. For stabilized algorithms and computational
complexity, see Altschuler et al. 2017; Schmitzer 2019. For debiased Sinkhorn divergences,
see Feydy et al. 2019. The convex optimization background behind smooth duality, log-
sum-exp regularization, Bregman projections and envelope arguments is closely related to
Rockafellar 1970; Boyd and Vandenberghe 2004; Bregman 1967; Csiszár 1975.
6.24 Summary
Entropic regularization modifies optimal transport by adding a convex entropy penalty to the
transport plan. The regularized optimizer is unique, positive and has the scaling form
Γε = diag(u)K diag(v), Kij =e−Cij/ε.
The scaling vectors are computed by Sinkhorn iterations. The algorithm is simple, fast and
differentiable, but the resulting cost is biased relative to exact OT. The Sinkhorn divergence
corrects self-similarity bias and is often better suited for comparing distributions.
For actuarial science, entropic OT is especially useful when distributional comparisons must be
repeated many times or performed in high dimension: portfolio drift, climate scenario comparison,
reserving cash-flow distributions and model aggregation.

a = (1/3, 1/3, 1/3), and squared cost Cij = (xi −xj)2. Explain why exact OT from a to itself
has zero cost. Then explain why the entropic optimizer has positive off-diagonal entries for every
ε> 0, and hence positive transport cost⟨C, Γε⟩.
Exercise 6.5 (Implementation). Implement Sinkhorn’s algorithm for two empirical samples
of losses. Compare the approximate transport cost for ε∈{0.01, 0.1, 1, 10}, after scaling losses
to have unit standard deviation. Plot the transport plan for each value of ε. Comment on the
sparsity or diffuseness of the plan.
Exercise 6.6 (Unbalanced prevention). A prevention program reduces expected losses in some
geographic cells and leaves others unchanged. Explain why balanced OT may be inappropriate
for comparing the before and after loss maps. Write an unbalanced entropic formulation with a
KL penalty on marginal deviations. Interpret the parameters λa and λb.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 102/416

=== page 103 ===
7 Statistical Optimal Transport
Learning objectives
The previous lectures introduced optimal transport as a modelling and computational tool:
couplings, optimal plans, Wasserstein distances, barycenters, and entropic regularization. This
lecture asks a statistical question. In actuarial work, the laws µ and ν are rarely known. They
are estimated from claims, exposure data, simulated scenarios, posterior predictive samples, or
model outputs. Thus any Wasserstein distance, map or barycenter computed from data is itself
a statistical object.
After this lecture, the reader should be able to:
(i) define empirical measures and plug-in Wasserstein estimators;
(ii) distinguish population quantities from sample quantities;
(iii) explain why empirical Wasserstein distances converge slowly in high dimension;
(iv) understand the special role of the one-dimensional case;
(v) use bootstrap and permutation methods for uncertainty quantification;
(vi) design a Wasserstein two-sample test for portfolio drift;
(vii) understand why entropic regularization can improve statistical stability;
(viii) discuss uncertainty for barycenters and transport maps;
(ix) translate these ideas into actuarial workflows: claims monitoring, reserving and climate
scenarios.
Actuarial takeaway
A Wasserstein number computed from data is not automatically evidence of a structural
difference. It contains both signal and sampling noise. Statistical optimal transport is the
set of tools that helps us decide whether an observed distributional movement is material,
stable and interpretable.
This lecture builds on the Wasserstein distance of Definition 5.2, the empirical transport
matrix of Definition 3.2, and the entropic regularization discussed in Lecture 6. It also prepares
the empirical labs in Appendix D and Appendix E.
7.1 Population distances and empirical distances
Let µ and ν be two probability measures on a metric space (X,d). The population Wasserstein
distance is
Wp(µ,ν) =
(
inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
d(x,y)p dγ(x,y)
)1/p
.
In most actuarial applications, µ and ν are unknown. We observe samples
X1,...,Xn ∼µ, Y 1,...,Y m ∼ν,
possibly independent, and estimate the laws by empirical measures
ˆµn = 1 n n∑ i=1 δXi, ˆνm = 1 m m∑ j=1 δYj.
Page 103/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 104 ===
7 STATISTICAL OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
Definition 7.1 (Empirical measure). Given observations x1,...,xn in a measurable space
(X,B(X )), the empirical measure is
ˆµn = 1 n n∑ i=1 δxi.
If the observations carry exposure weights wi ≥ 0 with ∑ iwi = 1, the weighted empirical measure
is ˆµw = ∑ iwiδxi. In actuarial applications, weights may represent exposure volume, claim count
or scenario probability.
The most direct estimator is the plug-in estimator.
Definition 7.2 (Plug-in Wasserstein estimator). For samples from µ and ν, the plug-in estimator
of Wp(µ,ν) is
ˆWp,n,m = Wp(ˆµn,ˆνm).
It is obtained by replacing the unknown laws by empirical laws and solving the same transport
problem.
This object is conceptually simple and is exactly what many software packages compute. Its
interpretation, however, is statistical: it estimates a population distance and inherits sampling
variability from both samples.
Example 7.1 (Claim severity drift). Let Xi be claim severities from year t, and Yj claim severities
from year t + 1, after exposure normalization. The empirical distance W1(ˆµn,ˆνm) measures the
minimal average absolute shift needed to transform the observed severity distribution in year t
into the observed severity distribution in year t + 1. A large value can indicate inflation, changes
in claim handling, different mix of business, or genuine portfolio drift.
7.2 The empirical transport problem
Suppose for simplicity that n =m and empirical weights are uniform. Then
ˆµn = 1 n n∑ i=1 δXi, ˆνn = 1 n n∑ j=1 δYj.
The empirical p-Wasserstein problem is
Wp p(ˆµn,ˆνn) = min Γ∈Un
n∑
i=1
n∑
j=1
d(Xi,Y j)pΓij,
where
Un =
{
Γ∈ Rn×n + : Γ1n = 1 n 1n, Γ⊤1n = 1 n 1n
}
.
When the weights are equal on both sides, extreme points of Un correspond to permutation
matrices divided by n. Therefore, in many non-degenerate cases, the problem reduces to an
assignment problem:
Wp p(ˆµn,ˆνn) = 1 n min σ∈Sn
n∑
i=1
d(Xi,Y σ(i))p,
where Sn is the set of permutations of {1,...,n}. This formula is useful for intuition, but
it should not obscure the fact that unequal weights, repeated observations, bins and entropic
regularization lead back to general transport matrices.
Remark 7.1 (Matched and unmatched samples). Two empirical distributions can arise from
matched or unmatched observations. If every policy has two measurements, before and after
an update, then the data are paired. If two periods have different portfolios, the data are not
paired. OT treats both as distributional comparisons. The distinction matters for interpretation:
matched comparisons estimate the implementation change; unmatched OT estimates a minimal
distributional change.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 104/416

=== page 105 ===
7.3 Consistency
7.3 Consistency
The first statistical question is whether the plug-in estimator converges to the population target.
The key deterministic step is the following stability inequality.
Proposition 7.1 (Stability of Wasserstein distances). Let µ,ν,µ′,ν′∈Pp(X ). Then
⏐⏐Wp(µ,ν)− Wp(µ′,ν′) ⏐⏐≤ Wp(µ,µ′) + Wp(ν,ν′).
In particular, estimating the two marginal laws accurately in Wp is enough to estimate their
distance accurately.
Proof. By the triangle inequality,
Wp(µ,ν)≤ Wp(µ,µ′) + Wp(µ′,ν′) + Wp(ν′,ν).
Rearrange and repeat the argument with (µ,ν) and (µ′,ν′) interchanged.
Under standard moment conditions, the empirical version is therefore consistent.
Proposition 7.2 (Consistency of empirical Wasserstein distances). Let (X,d) be a Polish metric
space and let p≥ 1. Suppose µ,ν∈Pp(X ). If X1,X2,... are i.i.d. with law µ and Y 1,Y 2,...
are i.i.d. with law ν, then, almost surely,
Wp(ˆµn,µ)→ 0, Wp(ˆνm,ν)→ 0,
Consequently,
Wp(ˆµn,ˆνm)→ Wp(µ,ν)
whenever n,m→∞.
Proof sketch. A standard characterization of Wasserstein convergence states that Wp(µn,µ) → 0
if and only if µn converges weakly to µ and the p-th moments converge. The empirical measure
converges weakly to the population law almost surely, and the empirical p-th moment converges
by the law of large numbers. The second statement follows from the triangle inequality:
|Wp(ˆµn,ˆνm)− Wp(µ,ν)|≤ Wp(ˆµn,µ) + Wp(ˆνm,ν).
Consistency is reassuring but insufficient. It says that, with enough data, empirical transport
distances are correct. It does not say how much data are needed, nor whether a distance observed
in a finite portfolio is statistically meaningful.
7.4 Rates and the curse of dimensionality
The main difficulty in statistical OT is that empirical Wasserstein distances can converge slowly,
especially when the underlying dimension is larger than one. A useful informal message is:
E [Wp(ˆµn,µ)] often behaves like n−1/d
when µ is a reasonably regular distribution on Rd, at least in dimensions where the geometry
dominates the central-limit scaling. The precise rate depends on p, d, tails and regularity. A
representative compact-support statement is
E
[
Wp p(ˆµn,µ) ] =



O(n−1/2), d< 2p,
O(n−1/2 logn), d = 2p,
O(n−p/d), d> 2p,
Page 105/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 106 ===
7 STATISTICAL OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
under regularity conditions. Written for Wp rather than Wp p, the high-dimensional regime gives
the heuristic n−1/d. See Fournier and Guillin 2015; Weed and Bach 2019; Bobkov and Ledoux
2019 for precise conditions, constants and tail assumptions. The key point for actuaries is that d
matters.
This is not just a technical nuisance. Many actuarial objects are high-dimensional:
• a risk profile may contain many rating variables;
• a reserve scenario may be a vector of future cash flows;
• a capital model scenario may contain losses by line, region and peril;
• a climate exposure may contain spatial coordinates and hazard summaries;
In such cases, a naive empirical Wasserstein distance may be dominated by sampling variability.
This is why one often uses dimension reduction, sliced Wasserstein distances, projections,
regularization, parametric models, or problem-specific summaries.
Actuarial takeaway
In one dimension, empirical OT is often statistically friendly. In high dimension, empirical
OT is statistically expensive. Before using a Wasserstein distance as a KPI, ask: what is
the effective dimension of the object being transported?
7.5 The one-dimensional advantage
In dimension one, Wasserstein distances have the quantile representation
Wp p(µ,ν) =
∫ 1
0
⏐⏐⏐F−1 µ (u)−F−1 ν (u) ⏐⏐⏐ p du.
The empirical version is obtained by replacing quantile functions by empirical quantile functions.
If n =m, uniform weights, and sorted samples
X(1) ≤···≤X(n), Y(1) ≤···≤Y(n),
then
Wp p(ˆµn,ˆνn) = 1 n n∑ i=1 |X(i) −Y(i)|p.
This simple formula is one of the reasons Wasserstein distances are particularly attractive for
severity distributions, reserve summaries and affordability ratios. The formula uses the generalized
inverse convention fixed earlier in the notes.
Proposition 7.3 (Empirical formula on the real line). Let ˆµn = n−1 ∑ iδXi and ˆνn = n−1 ∑ iδYi
be empirical measures on R with equal weights. Then, for p≥ 1,
Wp p(ˆµn,ˆνn) = 1 n n∑ i=1 |X(i) −Y(i)|p.
For unequal sample sizes or exposure weights, the same statement holds by matching the two
weighted empirical quantile functions.
Example 7.2 (Severity inflation through quantiles). Suppose Y is a severity after inflation and
X is before inflation. If the quantile functions satisfy approximately
F−1 Y (u)≈a +bF−1 X (u), b> 0,
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 106/416

=== page 107 ===
7.6 Bias under equality of distributions
then W2 2(µ,ν) decomposes approximately as
∫ 1
0
(
a + (b− 1)F−1 X (u) )2 du.
This separates a level effect a from a scale effect b. In practice, plotting F−1 Y (u) −F−1 X (u) against
u is often more informative than reporting a single number.
7.6 Bias under equality of distributions
A common mistake is to interpret Wp(ˆµn,ˆνm) > 0 as evidence that µ̸= ν. But even when µ = ν,
two independent empirical samples are different. Thus
Wp(ˆµn,ˆνm)> 0
with probability one in continuous models.
Definition 7.3 (Wasserstein noise floor). For a sampling design under a null model µ = ν, the
Wasserstein noise floor is the distribution, or a high quantile, of
Wp(ˆµn,ˆνm)
when the two empirical measures are generated from the same population law. It is a baseline
for deciding whether an observed empirical distance is material.
The relevant question is not whether the empirical distance is positive. It is whether it is
large relative to the noise floor.
Example 7.3 (Two samples from the same portfolio). Split one homogeneous portfolio randomly
into two halves. Compute W1 between the severity distributions of the two halves. The result is
positive, but it should not be interpreted as a business drift. It estimates the noise floor of the
Wasserstein monitoring procedure.
This observation motivates permutation tests and bootstrap calibration.
7.7 Permutation tests for two-sample problems
Consider the two-sample testing problem
H0 :µ =ν versus H1 :µ̸=ν.
A natural statistic is
Tn,m = Wp(ˆµn,ˆνm).
Under H0, the labels “sample X” and “sample Y” are exchangeable. This leads to a permutation
test; see also Ramdas et al. 2017 for Wasserstein two-sample testing.
Definition 7.4 (Permutation-calibrated Wasserstein test). A permutation-calibrated Wasserstein
test uses Tn,m = Wp(ˆµn,ˆνm) as the statistic and estimates its null distribution by repeatedly
permuting the labels of the pooled sample. The procedure is exact under exchangeability of the
labels, up to Monte Carlo error from using finitely many permutations.
Step 1. Pool the data into Z1,...,Zn+m.
Step 2. Compute the observed statistic Tobs = Wp(ˆµn,ˆνm).
Step 3. Randomly permute the labels, split the pooled data into pseudo-samples of sizes n and m,
and compute T (b).
Page 107/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 108 ===
7 STATISTICAL OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
Step 4. Repeat for b = 1,...,B.
Step 5. Estimate the p-value by
ˆp = 1 + ∑B b=1 1{T (b) ≥Tobs} B + 1 .
The procedure has a direct interpretation: it asks how often a distance as large as the observed
one would appear if the two portfolios were randomly relabelled.
Actuarial takeaway
Permutation tests are useful for governance because they turn a Wasserstein dashboard
into a statistical monitoring procedure: the point is not only to report a distance, but to
compare it with the distance produced by random portfolio splitting.
Remark 7.2 (Choice of cost and test sensitivity). The choice of p and the ground distance
matters. With p = 1, the test is often robust and interpretable in monetary units. With p = 2,
large discrepancies receive more weight. For multivariate vectors, the ground distance should
reflect actuarial materiality: dollars, log-dollars, normalized scores, standardized risk factors, or
business-weighted cash-flow vectors.
7.8 Bootstrap confidence intervals
A bootstrap procedure estimates the sampling variability of a statistic by resampling from
the observed data. For a Wasserstein distance between two independent samples, a simple
nonparametric bootstrap is:
Step 1. Resample X∗ 1 ,...,X∗ n with replacement from X1,...,Xn.
Step 2. Resample Y∗ 1 ,...,Y∗ m with replacement from Y1,...,Ym.
Step 3. Compute T∗ = Wp(ˆµ∗ n,ˆν∗ m).
Step 4. Repeat many times and use the empirical quantiles of T∗ to summarize uncertainty.
For reporting, one may use percentile intervals, bias-corrected intervals, or simply a bootstrap
standard error. In actuarial communication, a bootstrap band around a Wasserstein trend is
often more useful than a single point estimate.
Remark 7.3 (Bootstrap caution). Bootstrap methods for Wasserstein quantities are not uniformly
regular in all dimensions and all null settings. In particular, under equality of distributions, the
limiting law of empirical OT can be nonstandard and the naive bootstrap may be misleading.
For two-sample testing under H0, the permutation calibration in Definition 7.4 is often the
safer default. Bootstrap bands remain useful as descriptive uncertainty diagnostics, especially in
one-dimensional or smoothed settings.
7.9 Regularization as statistical smoothing
Entropic optimal transport was introduced in the previous lecture for computational reasons. It
also has statistical consequences. The entropic problem
OTε(a,b;C) = min Γ∈U(a,b)


⟨C, Γ⟩ +ε
∑
i,j
Γij(log Γij − 1)



produces a smoother plan than exact OT. This can reduce sensitivity to small perturbations in
the data.
There is a trade-off:
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 108/416

=== page 109 ===
7.10 Sliced and projected Wasserstein distances
• small ε: closer to exact OT, less bias, more variance and less numerical stability;
• large ε: smoother, more stable, more biased toward independent or diffuse couplings.
The Sinkhorn divergence corrects part of the regularization bias by subtracting self-costs:
Sinkε,c(µ,ν) = OTε,c(µ,ν)− 1 2OTε,c(µ,µ)− 1 2OTε,c(ν,ν).
In the empirical discrete case, the self-costs must be computed with the within-sample cost
matrices, not by reusing the cross-cost matrix. The Sinkhorn divergence is often preferable to
OTε,c when comparing distributions statistically, because OTε,c(µ,µ) need not be zero.
Remark 7.4 (Bias–variance language). Exact OT is not automatically the best statistical estimator.
Entropic OT can be understood as a regularized estimator: it introduces bias, but may reduce
variance and improve stability. This is familiar to actuaries from credibility, penalized GLMs,
smoothing splines and reserving models.
7.10 Sliced and projected Wasserstein distances
High-dimensional OT can be statistically and computationally difficult. A common workaround
is to project the data to one dimension and average one-dimensional Wasserstein distances.
Definition 7.5 (Sliced Wasserstein distance). Forθ∈ Rd with∥θ∥ = 1, define the projection
πθ(x) =θ⊤x.
The sliced Wasserstein distance is
SWp p(µ,ν) =
∫
Sd−1
Wp p((πθ)#µ, (πθ)#ν) dθ,
where dθ denotes the uniform probability measure on the unit sphere. In practice, one replaces
the integral by an average over finitely many random or actuarially chosen directions.
For actuarial science, projections are not only numerical tricks. They can encode business
questions:
• total loss projection: θ⊤x = ∑ kxk;
• line-of-business stress: θ emphasizes selected lines;
• tail-factor projection: θ points toward adverse scenarios;
• affordability projection: θ maps features to affordability ratios.
Example 7.4 (Cash-flow vectors). Let X∈ Rd be a discounted cash-flow vector over d future
years. Exact W2 between two empirical laws on Rd may be unstable. Sliced distances compare
many one-dimensional summaries of cash-flow trajectories. Some directions can be chosen
randomly; others can be chosen actuarially, such as total discounted cost, early development
years, late tail years, or regulatory one-year view.
7.11 Distributional confidence bands in one dimension
In one-dimensional actuarial problems, it is often useful to accompany a Wasserstein number
with a plot of quantile differences.
Definition 7.6 (Quantile-difference function). For two laws µ and ν on R, the quantile-difference
function is
∆µ,ν(u) =Qν(u)−Qµ(u), 0<u< 1,
where Qµ =F−1 µ and Qν =F−1 ν . It localizes the Wasserstein discrepancy across ranks.
Page 109/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 110 ===
7 STATISTICAL OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
The empirical version is
ˆ∆(u) =Qˆν(u)−Qˆµ(u).
Bootstrap resampling can produce pointwise or simultaneous bands for ∆(u). This is more
informative than a scalar distance because it shows where the distributions differ.
Example 7.5 (Tail inflation). A severity distribution can have almost no change below the
median but strong inflation above the 95th percentile. A single W1 value aggregates this effect.
A quantile-difference plot reveals whether the drift is central, tail-driven or uniform across the
distribution.
7.12 Uncertainty for barycenters
Suppose K probability measures µ1,...,µK are combined through a Wasserstein barycenter
¯µ∈ arg min
µ
K∑
k=1
λkW2 2(µ,µk).
In practice, the µk’s are estimated. We may have empirical laws ˆµk, simulated samples, posterior
predictive distributions, or expert scenarios. The empirical barycenter is
ˆ¯µ∈ arg min
µ
K∑
k=1
λkW2 2(µ,ˆµk).
Uncertainty comes from several sources:
• sampling uncertainty within each source distribution;
• model uncertainty across the K sources;
• uncertainty in the weights λk;
• numerical uncertainty from discretization or regularization.
In one dimension, if Qk(u) =F−1 µk (u), the barycenter quantile is
¯Q(u) =
K∑
k=1
λkQk(u).
Thus bootstrap uncertainty is straightforward: resample each source, recompute each empirical
quantile, average the quantiles, and build bands for ¯Q.
Example 7.6 (Expert aggregation for climate loss). Suppose K catastrophe models produce
distributions of annual aggregate flood losses for the same portfolio. A one-dimensional Wasser-
stein barycenter gives a consensus distribution whose quantile function is the weighted average of
model quantile functions. Bootstrap or scenario resampling provides uncertainty bands around
the consensus quantile curve.
7.13 Uncertainty for transport maps
Transport maps are attractive because they provide transformations, not only distances. In one
dimension, the optimal increasing map from µ to ν is
T (x) =F−1 ν (Fµ(x)).
The empirical map is obtained by replacing Fµ and F−1 ν by empirical versions. With atoms or
ties, the rank convention should be stated explicitly, or randomized ranks should be used. The
map can be used for quantile mapping, inflation adjustment or distributional calibration.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 110/416

=== page 111 ===
7.14 Application: portfolio drift monitoring
However, empirical maps can be unstable in the tails. A small number of extreme claims can
create large changes in F−1 ˆν (u) when u is close to one. This is especially relevant in actuarial severity modelling.
Actuarial takeaway
Transport maps are often more sensitive than transport distances. A distance averages the
cost over the distribution. A map estimates a pointwise transformation. Tail maps should
be treated with the same caution as tail quantiles.
7.14 Application: portfolio drift monitoring
Let Zi,t denote an actuarial quantity for policy i at time t: claim frequency score, predicted
severity, exposure value or affordability ratio. Define the empirical law
ˆµt = 1 nt nt∑ i=1 δZi,t.
A Wasserstein drift monitor tracks
Dt = Wp(ˆµt,ˆµt−1)
or a distance to a reference period,
Dref t = Wp(ˆµt,ˆµ0).
A practical dashboard should include:
(a) the point estimate Dt;
(b) a bootstrap or permutation noise benchmark;
(c) quantile-difference plots when Z is one-dimensional;
(d) decomposition by line of business, geography or channel;
(e) documentation of the ground metric and scale transformation.
This workflow is implemented in the empirical uncertainty lab of Appendix D.
7.15 Application: reserving and predictive distributions
Modern reserving increasingly produces predictive distributions rather than single best estimates.
Let µA and µB be predictive laws of ultimate losses or discounted cash flows under two reserving
methods. Empirical samples may come from bootstrap, Bayesian MCMC, or simulation.
A Wasserstein comparison can answer questions such as:
• Are two reserving methods distributionally close, even if their means differ slightly?
• Is the difference concentrated in the tail?
• How far is a new model from the current approved model?
• Does the one-year reserve risk distribution drift materially after data updates?
Because reserving samples are often generated by simulation, Monte Carlo error should be
included. If the model output is a sample from a predictive distribution, increasing the simulation
size reduces numerical Monte Carlo error but not model uncertainty. The cash-flow version of
this comparison is revisited in Appendix H.
Page 111/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 112 ===
7 STATISTICAL OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
7.16 Application: climate scenarios and prevention
Let µbefore and µafter denote loss distributions before and after a prevention measure. The
Wasserstein distance
Wp(µbefore,µafter)
measures the distributional displacement between the two loss laws. It does not, by itself, identify
the causal effect of prevention on individual risks. If both distributions are estimated from
simulations, the empirical distance also has sampling or Monte Carlo uncertainty.
A prevention study should ideally report several quantities:
∆E[L], ∆VaRα, ∆TVaRα, Wp(ˆµbefore,ˆµafter),
with uncertainty intervals. Wasserstein distances complement classical risk measures because
they reflect the whole distribution, not only a mean or one tail level. This connects to the
prevention discussion in Section 11.10 and the climate-scenario workflow in Section 15.13.
7.17 Mini-lab: Wasserstein uncertainty in R
For one-dimensional samples, empirical Wp is easy to compute by sorting. The following code
gives a minimal implementation for one-dimensional equal-weight samples, including unequal
sample sizes.
wass1d <- function(x, y, p = 2) {
x <- sort(x)
y <- sort(y)
n <- length(x)
m <- length(y)
wx <- rep(1 / n, n)
wy <- rep(1 / m, m)
i <- 1
j <- 1
val <- 0
while (i <= n & & j <= m) {
mass <- min(wx[i], wy[j])
val <- val + mass * abs(x[i] - y[j])^p
wx[i] <- wx[i] - mass
wy[j] <- wy[j] - mass
if (wx[i] <= . Machine $ double.eps) i <- i + 1
if (wy[j] <= . Machine $ double.eps) j <- j + 1
}
val^(1 / p)
}
set.seed(123)
n <- 500
x <- rlnorm(n, meanlog = 0, sdlog = 1)
y <- rlnorm(n, meanlog = 0.1, sdlog = 1.05)
wass1d(x, y, p = 1)
wass1d(x, y, p = 2)
A simple two-sample permutation test is:
perm _test _wass <- function(x, y, p = 2, B = 999) {
n <- length(x)
z <- c(x, y)
t _obs <- wass1d(x, y, p = p)
t _perm <- numeric(B)
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 112/416

=== page 113 ===
7.18 Practical guidelines
for (b in seq _len(B)) {
ind <- sample( seq _along(z), n, replace = FALSE)
xb <- z[ind]
yb <- z[-ind]
t _perm[b] <- wass1d(xb, yb, p = p)
}
(1 + sum( t _perm >= t _obs)) / (B + 1)
}
perm _test _wass(x, y, p = 2, B = 499)
Computational note
The function wass1d computes exact one-dimensional empirical Wp for equal-weight
samples, including unequal sample sizes. For weighted samples, use weighted empirical
quantile functions or a package implementation. For multivariate data, exact OT requires
solving a transport problem; for large samples, use entropic, sliced or approximate methods.
7.18 Practical guidelines
The following checklist is useful when reporting empirical OT results in actuarial work.
(1) Define the object. Are we comparing losses, ratios, cash-flow vectors, covariates or
simulated scenarios?
(2) Choose the scale. Dollars, log-dollars, standardized variables and ranks lead to different
distances.
(3) Choose the ground metric. It should encode actuarial materiality.
(4) Report sample sizes. Small samples produce noisy empirical distributions.
(5) Calibrate noise. Use bootstrap, permutation or repeated random splits.
(6) Inspect where the difference lies. In one dimension, use quantile-difference plots.
(7) Check robustness. Vary p, trimming, regularization, groups and time windows.
(8) Avoid overinterpreting maps. Maps are pointwise objects and can be unstable in tails.
Further reading
For mathematical background on statistical optimal transport, see Panaretos and Zemel
2020; Chewi et al. 2025. For empirical rates and concentration, see Fournier and Guillin
2015; Weed and Bach 2019; Bobkov and Ledoux 2019. For limit distributions and
inference in Wasserstein spaces, see Barrio et al. 1999b; Sommerfeld and Munk 2018. For
computational aspects and entropic regularization, see Peyré and Cuturi 2019; Genevay
et al. 2019. For the general theory of Wasserstein convergence, see Villani 2008. The
actuarial uses in these notes emphasize model validation, reserving and prevention rather
than asymptotic theory for its own sake.

|Wp(µn,νn)− Wp(µ,ν)|≤ Wp(µn,µ) + Wp(νn,ν).

Use this inequality to explain why consistency of empirical measures implies consistency of the
plug-in Wasserstein distance.
Exercise 7.2 (One-dimensional computation). Let

answer as a comparison of two small claim samples.

size and several repeated values. Explain qualitatively how increasing the entropic regularization
parameter ε changes the optimal plan. What kind of bias does this introduce? Why might it
still be useful in a monitoring dashboard?

=== page 115 ===
8 Risk Measures, Quantiles and Wasserstein Robustness
Learning objectives
The preceding lectures developed optimal transport as a geometry for probability measures and
introduced the statistical issues that arise when the relevant measures are estimated from data.
We now turn to one of the most natural actuarial uses of that geometry: measuring, comparing
and stress-testing risk.
After this lecture, the reader should be able to:
(i) express classical actuarial risk measures in terms of quantile functions;
(ii) explain why Wasserstein distances are particularly well adapted to quantile-based risk
measures;
(iii) prove Lipschitz stability bounds for expectations, TVaR and spectral risk measures;
(iv) understand the instability of VaR under small tail perturbations;
(v) formulate Wasserstein ambiguity sets and robust risk measures;
(vi) derive one-dimensional robust formulas using quantile functions;
(vii) connect dependence uncertainty to couplings and multi-marginal optimal transport;
(viii) interpret Wasserstein robustness in insurance capital, reinsurance and stress testing.
Actuarial takeaway
Risk measures are functionals of loss distributions. Optimal transport gives a way to ask:
how much can a risk measure change if the loss distribution is moved by a small amount?
This question is central for model risk, capital uncertainty, stress testing and regulatory
robustness.
This lecture uses the lower generalized inverse convention introduced in Definition 1.3, the
Wasserstein distance of Definition 5.2, and the statistical plug-in viewpoint of Definition 7.2. It
prepares the robust-capital discussion in Section 8.17, the tail-aware modelling tools in Section 8.7,
the dependence and reinsurance material in Lecture 13, and the empirical lab on robust VaR
and TVaR in Section 8.15.
8.1 Loss distributions and quantile functions
Let L denote a non-negative insurance loss. Its distribution function is
FL(x) = P(L≤x).
Definition 8.1 (Loss quantile function). For a loss law µ∈P(R) with distribution function Fµ,
the generalized quantile function is
Qµ(u) =F−1 µ (u) = inf{x∈ R :Fµ(x)≥u}, u∈ (0, 1).
If L∼µ, we also write QL = Qµ. This is the same convention as in Definition 1.3; the notation
Qµ will be preferred in the risk-measure chapters because quantiles are actuarial objects such as
VaR and TVaR.
Page 115/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 116 ===
8 RISK MEASURES, QUANTILES AND WASSERSTEIN ROBUSTNESS
The quantile function is the basic bridge between optimal transport and actuarial risk. In
one dimension,
Wp p(µ,ν) =
∫ 1
0
|Qµ(u)−Qν(u)|p du.
Thus, in dimension one, Wp is simply the Lp-distance between quantile functions.
Example 8.1 (Severity models). Suppose µ is a fitted severity distribution and ˆµn is the
empirical severity distribution. Then Wp(ˆµn,µ) measures the average vertical discrepancy
between empirical and fitted quantiles. This is more distributional than comparing only the
mean, variance or a few selected percentiles.
Example 8.2 (Inflation and claim development). If L0 is a loss before inflation and L1 after
inflation, an approximate multiplicative inflation model corresponds to
QL1(u)≈ (1 +i)QL0(u).
Then
Wp(Law(L0), Law(L1))≈i
(∫ 1
0
QL0(u)p du
)1/p
.
The same inflation rate produces a larger Wasserstein movement for heavy-tailed portfolios.
8.2 Classical actuarial risk measures
Definition 8.2 (Law-invariant actuarial risk measure). A law-invariant actuarial risk measure is
a functional ρ that assigns a number to a loss distribution and depends on a random loss L only
through its law µ = Law(L). We write either ρ(L) or ρ(µ) depending on whether the random
variable or its distribution is the object of interest.
In actuarial science, the most common examples are the mean, Value-at-Risk and Tail
Value-at-Risk.
8.2.1 Mean loss
The net premium principle uses
ρ(L) = E[L].
When L≥ 0, this can also be written in quantile form as
E[L] =
∫ 1
0
QL(u) du,
provided L is integrable.
8.2.2 Value-at-Risk
For α∈ (0, 1), the Value-at-Risk is
VaRα(L) =QL(α).
It is the loss threshold exceeded with probability at most 1 −α. In regulatory capital, α is often
very high, such as 99%, 99.5%, or 99.9%, depending on the context.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 116/416

=== page 117 ===
8.3 Wasserstein stability of expectations and spectral risks
8.2.3 Tail Value-at-Risk and Expected Shortfall
The Tail Value-at-Risk, also called Expected Shortfall, is
TVaRα(L) = ESα(L) = 1 1−α ∫ 1 α QL(u) du.
If the distribution is continuous at the relevant quantile, this equals the usual conditional tail
expectation
E[L|L≥ VaRα(L)].
The quantile definition is the safer convention in these notes because it remains well defined with
atoms.
Remark 8.1 (TVaR as a tail average). VaR reads one point of the quantile curve. TVaR averages
the whole tail of the quantile curve. This distinction is crucial for Wasserstein stability: averages
of quantiles are smoother than point evaluations of quantiles.
8.2.4 Spectral and distortion risk measures
A spectral risk measure has the form
ρϕ(L) =
∫ 1
0
QL(u)ϕ(u) du,
where ϕ≥ 0 and
∫ 1
0 ϕ(u) du = 1. The weight ϕ determines which parts of the loss distribution
matter most. The mean corresponds to ϕ(u) = 1, while TVaR corresponds to
ϕα(u) = 1 1−α 1{u≥α}.
More general distortion risk measures can be written as weighted integrals of quantiles, or as
integrals with respect to a distortion function. For these notes, the spectral form is the most
convenient because it connects directly to Lp-geometry.
Further reading
For actuarial background on dependence, stochastic orders and risk measures, see Denuit
et al. 2005a. For coherent, convex and spectral risk measures, see Artzner et al. 1999;
Acerbi 2002; Föllmer and Schied 2016; Rockafellar and Uryasev 2000. For extreme-value
methods in insurance and finance, see Embrechts et al. 1997; Coles 2001; Beirlant et al.
2004; Haan and Ferreira 2006. For Wasserstein distributionally robust optimization, see
Esfahani and Kuhn 2018; Blanchet and Murthy 2019; Gao and Kleywegt 2016. For the
optimal transport background behind Wasserstein distances and quantile couplings, see
Villani 2003; Villani 2008; Peyré and Cuturi 2019.
8.3 Wasserstein stability of expectations and spectral risks
A central actuarial question is: if two loss distributions are close in Wasserstein distance, are
their risk measures close?
8.3.1 Expectation and W1
Let µ,ν∈P1(R). Since x↦→x is 1-Lipschitz on R, Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality implies
⏐⏐⏐⏐ ∫ x dµ(x)− ∫ x dν(x) ⏐⏐⏐⏐≤ W1(µ,ν).

Equivalently, using quantiles,
|Eµ[L]− Eν[L]| =
⏐⏐⏐⏐ ∫ 1 0 {Qµ(u)−Qν(u)} du ⏐⏐⏐⏐≤ ∫ 1 0 |Qµ(u)−Qν(u)| du.
The right-hand side is W1(µ,ν).
Proposition 8.1 (Expectation is Wasserstein-Lipschitz). For any p≥ 1 and any µ,ν∈Pp(R),
|Eµ[L]− Eν[L]|≤ Wp(µ,ν).
Proof. By the quantile representation,
|Eµ[L]− Eν[L]|≤

|Qµ(u)−Qν(u)|p du

.
The last term is Wp(µ,ν).

ρϕ(µ)−ρϕ(ν) =

Hölder’s inequality yields the basic stability bound.

This is the desired inequality.

ϕα(u) = 1 1−α 1{u≥α}.
Its Lq-norm is

=== page 119 ===
8.4 VaR instability
Proposition 8.3 (Wasserstein stability of TVaR). For p≥ 1 and one-dimensional loss laws
µ,ν∈Pp(R),
|TVaRα(µ)− TVaRα(ν)|≤ (1−α)−1/pWp(µ,ν).
Actuarial takeaway
The factor (1−α)−1/p is the price of tail focus. At α = 99.5%, the factor is large, especially
for p = 1. Tail risk is necessarily more sensitive to distributional perturbations than mean
risk.
8.4 VaR instability
VaR behaves differently. Since VaRα is a point evaluation of the quantile function, it is not Lips-
chitz with respect to Lp-distance between quantile functions. Small perturbations in probability
mass can produce large changes in a high quantile.
Example 8.3 (A small tail contamination). Let
L = 0 almost surely,
and let
LM =
{
0, with probability 1−ε,
M, with probability ε.
Then
Wp(Law(L), Law(LM)) =Mε1/p.
If ε is small, this can be made moderate even for large M, provided p> 1 or ε is tiny. However,
VaRα(LM) =M
whenever α> 1−ε. Thus a tiny amount of tail mass can dominate very high VaR.
The example does not mean that VaR is useless. It means that VaR is a local functional of
the quantile curve, and local functionals require local regularity. If FL has a positive density
near VaRα(L), then small perturbations of the distribution can lead to controlled perturbations
of the quantile. But without such regularity, VaR is fragile.
Remark 8.2 (Density near the quantile). Suppose F is continuous and strictly increasing near
qα = F−1(α), with density f(qα) > 0. Then a small vertical error in the distribution function
translates into a horizontal error of approximate size 1/f(qα). Quantile stability is therefore
poor when the density is small near the target quantile, which is common in far tails.
8.5 Wasserstein ambiguity sets
Definition 8.3 (Wasserstein ambiguity set). Let ˆµ∈Pp(X ) be a reference law on a metric space
(X,d) and let ρ≥ 0. The p-Wasserstein ambiguity set with radius ρ is
Bp(ˆµ,ρ) ={ν∈Pp(X ) : Wp(ν,ˆµ)≤ρ}.
The reference law ˆµ may be empirical, parametric, simulated or expert-based.
Definition 8.4 (Wasserstein robust risk measure). For a law-invariant risk measure ρ, its robust
version overBp(ˆµ,ρ) is
ρrob p,ρ (ˆµ) = sup
ν∈Bp(ˆµ,ρ)
ρ(ν).
It asks for the worst risk value among all loss distributions within Wasserstein distance ρ of the
reference model. The value may be +∞ if the risk functional grows too fast relative to the cost.
Page 119/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 120 ===
8 RISK MEASURES, QUANTILES AND WASSERSTEIN ROBUSTNESS
Example 8.4 (Capital model uncertainty). Suppose an internal model produces simulated
annual losses L1,...,Ln and the empirical law ˆµn. A regulator or risk committee may not trust
ˆµn exactly. Instead, it considers all distributions within Wp-distance ρ. The robust capital is
then
sup
ν:Wp(ν,ˆµn)≤ρ
TVaR99%(ν).
The parameter ρ encodes model ambiguity, estimation error, climate non-stationarity, or expert
conservatism.
8.6 Robust expectations and TVaR in one dimension
The quantile representation makes Wasserstein robustness transparent in dimension one.
8.6.1 Robust expectation
Let Q be the quantile function of ˆµ. Any alternative distribution ν has quantile ~Q, and the
Wasserstein constraint becomes
∥~Q−Q∥Lp ≤ρ.
The expected loss is
Eν[L] =
∫ 1
0
~Q(u) du.
By Hölder’s inequality, ∫ 1
0
~Q(u) du≤
∫ 1
0
Q(u) du +ρ.
The upper bound is attained by shifting the whole quantile function upward by ρ, namely
~Q(u) =Q(u) +ρ. Hence
Proposition 8.4 (Robust mean over a one-dimensional Wasserstein ball). For any p≥ 1 and
any one-dimensional reference loss law ˆµ∈Pp(R),
sup
ν:Wp(ν,ˆµ)≤ρ
Eν[L] = Eˆµ[L] +ρ.
This formula is independent of p because the mean averages all quantile levels equally.
8.6.2 Robust TVaR
For TVaR,
TVaRα(ν) = 1 1−α ∫ 1 α ~Q(u) du.
By the stability bound,
TVaRα(ν)≤ TVaRα(ˆµ) + (1−α)−1/pρ.
The upper bound is attained by shifting only the upper tail:
~Q(u) =Q(u) +a1{u≥α}, a =ρ(1−α)−1/p.
Indeed,
∥~Q−Q∥Lp =a(1−α)1/p =ρ,
and the TVaR increases by a. Therefore,

8.7 Tail-aware transport and extreme-value modelling
Proposition 8.5 (Robust TVaR over a one-dimensional Wasserstein ball). For any p≥ 1 and
any one-dimensional reference loss law ˆµ∈Pp(R),
sup
ν:Wp(ν,ˆµ)≤ρ
TVaRα(ν) = TVaRα(ˆµ) +ρ(1−α)−1/p.
Actuarial takeaway
A Wasserstein robust TVaR has a simple interpretation: start from the estimated TVaR,
then add a tail uncertainty loading. The loading increases as the confidence level approaches
one and decreases as p increases.
Example 8.5 (Numerical scale of the loading). Take α = 99% and ρ = 1000. Then
ρ(1−α)−1 = 100000 for p = 1,
while
ρ(1−α)−1/2 = 10000 for p = 2.
The choice of ground metric and exponent p has a material impact on robust capital. A W1-ball
permits very concentrated tail perturbations at relatively low average cost, while a W2-ball
penalizes large tail movements more strongly.
8.7 Tail-aware transport and extreme-value modelling
Wasserstein distances compare whole distributions. This is an advantage when one wants a global
geometry of losses, reserves or capital. It can also be a limitation in tail problems. Two loss
distributions may be close in Wp because most of the mass lies in the body of the distribution,
while being materially different for high quantiles, excess losses or reinsurance layers.
This is particularly important for quantities such as
VaRα(L), TVaRα(L), E[(L−u)+], E[(L−d)+ ∧ℓ],
where the upper tail, or a specific layer of the loss distribution, drives the actuarial conclusion.
Actuarial takeaway
Standard Wasserstein distances are global distributional distances. Extreme-value mod-
elling is local in the tail. Tail-aware optimal transport combines the two viewpoints: it
keeps the geometric interpretation of transport, but focuses the cost, the radius or the
comparison on the part of the distribution that matters for tail risk.
8.7.1 Tail conditional laws
Definition 8.5 (Tail mass and excess law). Let L be a non-negative loss with distribution µ.
For a high threshold u, the exceedance probability is
pµ(u) =µ((u,∞)) = P(L>u),
and the conditional excess law is
µ+ u = Law(L−u|L>u).
Thus µ+ u is the law of the excess loss above u.
Page 121/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 122 ===
8 RISK MEASURES, QUANTILES AND WASSERSTEIN ROBUSTNESS
A tail-aware comparison between two loss laws µ and ν should compare both:
pµ(u) and pν(u),
and
µ+ u and ν+ u .
Definition 8.6 (Tail-aware exceedance discrepancy). A simple tail-aware discrepancy is
Dp p,u(µ,ν) =λ|pµ(u)−pν(u)|p + ¯puWp p (µ+ u,ν+ u ),
where λ> 0 is a scaling parameter and ¯pu is a reference tail mass, for example
¯pu = pµ(u) +pν(u) 2 .
The first term compares tail frequencies; the second term compares tail severities conditional on
exceedance. This definition assumes positive exceedance probabilities when conditional excess
laws are used; if one tail probability is zero, the tail mass discrepancy should be reported
separately.
Remark 8.3. The quantity Dp,u is best viewed as an actuarial diagnostic, not necessarily as a
canonical mathematical distance. It depends on the threshold u, the scaling λ, and the choice of
the tail mass ¯pu. These choices should be documented as modelling decisions.
8.7.2 Tail quantile Wasserstein distance
In one dimension, a particularly transparent construction uses quantile functions. Let
Qµ(t) =F−1 µ (t), Qν(t) =F−1 ν (t).
Definition 8.7 (Tail quantile Wasserstein discrepancy). For α∈ (0, 1), define the upper-tail
Wasserstein discrepancy
W tail p,α (µ,ν)p = 1 1−α ∫ 1 α |Qµ(t)−Qν(t)|p dt.
This is simply a Wasserstein comparison restricted to the upper tail quantiles.
For p = 1,
W tail 1,α (µ,ν) = 1 1−α ∫ 1 α |Qµ(t)−Qν(t)| dt.
This is closely aligned with TVaRα, since
TVaRα(µ) = 1 1−α ∫ 1 α Qµ(t) dt.
Proposition 8.6 (Tail TVaR stability). Let µ and ν be one-dimensional loss distributions with
finite first moments. Then
|TVaRα(µ)− TVaRα(ν)|≤W tail 1,α (µ,ν).
Proof. Using the quantile representation of TVaR,
TVaRα(µ)− TVaRα(ν) = 1 1−α ∫ 1 α ( Qµ(t)−Qν(t) ) dt.
Taking absolute values and applying the triangle inequality gives
|TVaRα(µ)− TVaRα(ν)|≤ 1 1−α ∫ 1 α |Qµ(t)−Qν(t)| dt =W tail 1,α (µ,ν).
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 122/416

8.7 Tail-aware transport and extreme-value modelling
Actuarial takeaway
The usual W1 distance controls differences in expectations of Lipschitz functions. The
tail-restricted version controls differences in tail averages. This is why W tail 1,α is often more
informative than global W1 when the actuarial question concerns TVaRα, excess-of-loss
layers or capital.
8.7.3 Peaks-over-threshold modelling
Extreme-value theory provides a parametric approximation for the conditional excess law µ+ u.
For a high threshold u, the peaks-over-threshold principle suggests approximating the law of
L−u|L>u
by a generalized Pareto distribution.
Definition 8.8 (Generalized Pareto excess model). For shape parameter ξ ∈ R and scale
parameter β > 0, the generalized Pareto distribution has distribution function
Gξ,β(y) = 1−
(
1 + ξy β )−1/ξ , y≥ 0,
when ξ̸= 0, with the usual support condition
1 + ξy β > 0.
For ξ = 0, the limit is the exponential distribution
G0,β(y) = 1− exp(−y/β).
Here ξ is the tail index and β > 0 is a scale parameter.
If
pu = P(L>u),
then for α>F (u), the high quantile can be approximated by
VaRα(L)≈u + β ξ
[( pu
1−α
)ξ
− 1
]
, ξ̸= 0.
For ξ = 0,
VaRα(L)≈u +β log
( pu
1−α
)
.
If ξ < 1, the corresponding tail mean approximation is
TVaRα(L)≈ VaRα(L) +β−ξu 1−ξ .
Remark 8.4 (Tail index and actuarial sensitivity). The parameter ξ is crucial. When ξ > 0,
the tail is heavy and high quantiles are very sensitive to parameter uncertainty. When ξ≥ 1,
the mean of the fitted generalized Pareto tail is infinite, which makes expectation-based risk
quantities unstable or undefined under the fitted tail model.

8.7.4 Transport between fitted tails
Suppose two tail models are fitted above the same threshold u:
L0 −u|L0 >u∼Gξ0,β0, L1 −u|L1 >u∼Gξ1,β1.
The one-dimensional monotone transport between the two excess laws is
T + u (e) =G−1 ξ1,β1 (Gξ0,β0(e)), e≥ 0.
Thus a tail-only stress map can be written as
T tail(l) =



l, l≤u,
u +T + u (l−u), l>u.
This map leaves the body unchanged and transforms the conditional tail.
Example 8.6 (Tail-thickening stress). Let a baseline excess distribution above u be fitted by
Gξ0,β0, and suppose the stress scenario increases the tail index from ξ0 to ξ1 >ξ0. The map
e↦→G−1 ξ1,β1(Gξ0,β0(e))
moves high excesses further into the tail. This gives an explicit scenario-level stress, not only a
change in a fitted parameter.
Remark 8.5 (Changing the tail mass). The map T tail above preserves the exceedance probability
P(L > u). If the target scenario also changes the tail mass, for example by increasing the
probability of crossing u, one needs either a mixture adjustment or an unbalanced transport
formulation. This connects tail-aware transport with unbalanced OT.
8.7.5 Layer-aware costs
Many insurance contracts do not respond to the full loss L, but to a layer. For a deductible d
and limit ℓ, the layer payment is
hd,ℓ(L) = (L−d)+ − (L−d−ℓ)+.
Definition 8.9 (Layer-aware transport cost). A transport cost can be designed to emphasize
the layer:
cd,ℓ(x,y) =|hd,ℓ(x)−hd,ℓ(y)|p.
Then the corresponding transport discrepancy compares distributions through the lens of the
layer payment rather than through raw loss amounts:
inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
cd,ℓ(x,y) dγ(x,y).
This is useful for excess-of-loss reinsurance, catastrophe layers, stop-loss covers and capital layers.
More generally, one can define a weighted tail cost
cτ(x,y) =wτ(x,y)|x−y|p,
where wτ(x,y) is larger when x or y lies in the tail. For instance,
wτ(x,y) = 1 +τ
(max(x,y)
u
)κ
1{max(x,y)>u}, τ≥ 0, κ≥ 0.
Increasing τ makes the distance more sensitive to tail movements.
Computational note
Tail-aware costs are modelling devices. They make the transport problem answer a specific
actuarial question, such as “how different are these two models for an excess-of-loss layer?”
or “how much of the model change is concentrated above the capital threshold?”
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 124/416

=== page 125 ===
8.7 Tail-aware transport and extreme-value modelling
8.7.6 Tail-aware Wasserstein DRO
Tail-aware transport also gives a natural ambiguity set for robust capital. Instead of perturbing
the whole loss distribution with a global Wasserstein ball, one can define
Btail(ˆµ,r) ={ν :Dp,u(ν,ˆµ)≤r}.
A tail-robust capital diagnostic is then
Captail r = sup
ν∈Btail(ˆµ,r)
ρ(ν),
where ρ may be VaRα, TVaRα, or a layer-based risk measure.
Alternatively, one can use the tail quantile distance and define
Btail α (ˆµ,r) = { ν :W tail p,α (ν,ˆµ)≤r } .
This ambiguity set is explicitly focused on upper quantiles.
Actuarial takeaway
A global Wasserstein ball asks: how bad can the model become after a transport perturba-
tion of size r? A tail-aware Wasserstein ball asks: how bad can the capital layer become
after a tail perturbation of size r?
8.7.7 Threshold choice and validation
Tail-aware transport inherits the main practical difficulty of extreme-value modelling: the
threshold u. If u is too low, the generalized Pareto approximation may be biased. If u is too
high, there are too few exceedances and the estimate becomes unstable.
A practical workflow is:
1. choose a range of candidate thresholds u;
2. fit a tail model above each threshold;
3. compute tail-aware distances and capital quantities across thresholds;
4. report sensitivity to u, not only one selected value;
5. compare parametric EVT transport with empirical tail-quantile transport.
Remark 8.6 (No automatic tail truth). Extreme-value theory provides asymptotic guidance,
not automatic truth. A tail-aware OT analysis should therefore report threshold sensitivity,
parameter uncertainty and the effect of the chosen transport cost.
8.7.8 A small R illustration
The following code compares two samples through a global Wasserstein distance and a tail-
restricted Wasserstein distance.
emp _ q _left <- function(x, probs) {
x <- sort(x)
n <- length(x)
idx <- pmax(1, ceiling(probs * n))
x[idx]
}

emp _tvar <- function(x, alpha = 0.99) {
