}
set.seed(123)
n <- 5000
x <- rlnorm(n, meanlog = 8, sdlog = 0.8)
# Same body, heavier tail through a mixture perturbation
y <- x
idx <- runif(n) < 0.04
y[idx] <- y[idx] * rlnorm( sum(idx), meanlog = 0.5, sdlog = 0.4)
grid _global <- seq(0.001, 0.999, length.out = 1000)
grid _tail <- seq(0.95, 0.999, length.out = 300)
W1 _global <- W1 _ quantile(x, y, grid _global)
W1 _tail95 <- W1 _ quantile(x, y, grid _tail)
c(
W1 _global = W1 _global,
W1 _tail _95 = W1 _tail95,
VaR99 _x = emp _ q _left(x, 0.99),
VaR99 _y = emp _ q _left(y, 0.99),
TVaR99 _x = emp _tvar(x, 0.99),
TVaR99 _y = emp _tvar(y, 0.99)
)
The global distance may look moderate because most observations lie in the body. The tail-
restricted distance is more sensitive to the perturbation, and is more aligned with high-quantile
and tail-mean changes.
8.7.9 What to remember
Tail-aware transport is useful when the actuarial object of interest is driven by rare but severe
losses. It can be implemented in several complementary ways:
• compare conditional excess laws above a high threshold;
• compare only upper quantile functions;
• use EVT models for the tail and transport fitted excess distributions;
• design costs that emphasize reinsurance or capital layers;
• define ambiguity sets directly on tail discrepancies.
The purpose is not to replace extreme-value theory, but to give EVT a transport geometry.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 126/416

8.8 Robust spectral risk measures
Further reading
The peaks-over-threshold approximation is classical in extreme-value theory; see Balkema
and Haan 1974; Pickands 1975; Coles 2001; Haan and Ferreira 2006. Tail dependence
and insurance aggregation are discussed in Embrechts et al. 1997; McNeil et al. 2015a.
Layer-based interpretations connect naturally with reinsurance pricing; see the reinsurance

8.8 Robust spectral risk measures
For a spectral risk measure
ρϕ(µ) =
∫ 1
0
Qµ(u)ϕ(u) du,
the same argument gives
ρϕ(ν)≤ρϕ(ˆµ) +ρ∥ϕ∥Lq.
When the extremal perturbation is compatible with a nondecreasing quantile function, this upper
bound is attained. This is the case for many standard spectral weights, including the mean and
TVaR.
Formally, for 1<p<∞, if ϕ≥ 0,
∫ 1
0 ϕ = 1, and the function
h(u) =ϕ(u)q−1
is nondecreasing, then setting
~Q(u) =Q(u) +ρ h(u)
∥ϕ∥q−1 Lq
produces the worst-case direction in quantile space. This shows why the robust loading depends
on the Lq-norm of the spectral weight. The monotonicity condition is not cosmetic: without it,
the formal worst-case direction in Lp need not be a nondecreasing quantile function.
Proposition 8.7 (Robust spectral loading, monotone case). Under the monotonicity condition
above,
sup
ν:Wp(ν,ˆµ)≤ρ
ρϕ(ν) =ρϕ(ˆµ) +ρ∥ϕ∥Lq.
Remark 8.7 (Robustness as a premium principle). The formula
ρrob ϕ (ˆµ) =ρϕ(ˆµ) +ρ∥ϕ∥Lq
looks like a premium principle: best estimate plus risk loading. The loading is not imposed
externally; it is induced by the geometry of the ambiguity set and the tail sensitivity of the risk
measure.

VaR is not Lipschitz in Wp, but one can still describe its robust version in one dimension.
Let Q be the reference quantile function. To make VaRα at least z, it is enough to move the
upper (1−α)-tail of the quantile curve above z. The minimal Lp-cost of achieving this is
Cp(z)p =
∫ 1
α
{z−Q(u)}p + du.
Therefore, the worst-case upper VaR is characterized as follows.

Proposition 8.8 (Robust VaR by tail lifting). With the quantile convention above,
sup
ν:Wp(ν,ˆµ)≤ρ
VaRα(ν) = sup
{
z∈ R :
∫ 1
α
{z−Q(u)}p + du≤ρp
}
.
This formula is useful computationally: one can solve for z by one-dimensional root finding.
Example 8.7 (Empirical robust VaR). Let the empirical losses be sorted as
L(1) ≤···≤L(n).
The empirical quantile function is constant on intervals ((i− 1)/n,i/n]. The robust VaR formula
becomes a finite sum over the upper tail:
1
n
∑
i>αn
{z−L(i)}p + ≤ρp,
with the obvious convention when αn is not an integer. This gives a direct stress-testing algorithm
for empirical VaR.
Computational note
Robust VaR under a one-dimensional Wasserstein ball can be computed by bisection over
z. Robust TVaR does not require bisection: it is the empirical TVaR plus the explicit
loading ρ(1−α)−1/p.
8.10 Dependence uncertainty and couplings
So far the loss distribution itself was uncertain. A different source of model risk arises when
marginal loss distributions are known, but dependence is uncertain.
Let L1,...,Ld be losses for d lines of business, regions, perils or policy types. Suppose the
marginal laws µ1,...,µd are known or estimated, but the joint distribution is not.
Definition 8.10 (Multi-marginal coupling set). The admissible joint laws with fixed marginals
µ1,...,µd are
Π(µ1,...,µd) =
{
γ∈P(Rd) : (projk)#γ =µk, k = 1,...,d } .
The aggregate loss is
S =L1 +··· +Ld.
A worst-case aggregate risk problem is
sup
γ∈Π(µ1,...,µd)
ργ(S).
This is a multi-marginal optimal transport problem whenever ρ can be represented as an
expectation of a function of S. For example, the stop-loss premium
E[(S−r)+]
leads to
sup
γ∈Π(µ1,...,µd)
∫
(x1 +··· +xd −r)+ dγ(x1,...,xd).
The cost function is
c(x1,...,xd) = (x1 +··· +xd −r)+.
Example 8.8 (Two lines of business). An insurer models motor liability and property losses
separately. Marginal severity models may be credible, but dependence during inflationary
or climate-stressed years may be uncertain. The set of all couplings describes all admissible
dependence structures with those marginals. Worst-case stop-loss or TVaR over this set gives a
dependence-robust capital measure.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 128/416

=== page 129 ===
8.11 Comonotonicity and worst-case aggregation
8.11 Comonotonicity and worst-case aggregation
In dimension one, the comonotonic coupling is obtained by taking a single uniform variable
U∼ Unif(0, 1) and setting
Lk =Qk(U), k = 1,...,d.
The aggregate comonotonic loss is
Scomon =Q1(U) +··· +Qd(U).
Its quantile function is
QScomon(u) =Q1(u) +··· +Qd(u),
at continuity points. This gives the additivity of VaR and TVaR under comonotonicity:
VaRα(Scomon) =
d∑
k=1
VaRα(Lk),
and
TVaRα(Scomon) =
d∑
k=1
TVaRα(Lk).
For convex increasing functions ℓ, the expectation
E[ℓ(L1 +··· +Ld)]
is maximized by comonotonic dependence among all couplings with fixed marginals. Thus, for
many stop-loss and tail-sensitive objectives, comonotonicity is the adverse dependence benchmark.
Actuarial takeaway
Comonotonicity is not a realistic dependence model in most portfolios, but it is a useful
upper benchmark. It answers the question: what if all marginal risks materialize in the
same quantile state?
8.12 Wasserstein balls versus coupling ambiguity
There are two distinct but related robustness problems:
(i) Distributional ambiguity: the entire loss law may move within a Wasserstein ball,
ν∈Bp(ˆµ,ρ).
(ii) Dependence ambiguity: the marginal laws are fixed, but the joint law may vary over
couplings,
γ∈ Π(µ1,...,µd).
Both are optimal transport problems. In the first, the constraint is a distance from a reference
measure. In the second, the constraint is a set of fixed marginals. In actuarial applications, both
may appear simultaneously:
sup
νk:Wp(νk,ˆµk)≤ρk
γ∈Π(ν1,...,νd)
ργ(L1 +··· +Ld).
This formulation allows marginal model uncertainty and dependence uncertainty at the same
time.
Example 8.9 (Climate and catastrophe risk). A catastrophe model may produce marginal loss
distributions by region and peril. Climate uncertainty may perturb each marginal distribution,
while physical dependence between regions or perils may also change. A combined Wasserstein–
coupling ambiguity set captures both effects: the marginal distributions can move, and the
dependence structure can vary.
Page 129/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 130 ===
8 RISK MEASURES, QUANTILES AND WASSERSTEIN ROBUSTNESS
8.13 Choosing the radius ρ
The robustness radius ρ is not a purely mathematical parameter. It should be interpreted and
calibrated. Common strategies include:
(i) Statistical calibration: choose ρso thatBp(ˆµn,ρ) contains the unknown law with a prescribed
confidence level.
(ii) Backtesting calibration: choose ρ from historical model errors, out-of-sample Wasserstein
discrepancies, or forecast residuals.
(iii) Expert calibration: choose ρ to reflect plausible shifts in severity, inflation, climate hazard
or claims handling.
(iv) Regulatory calibration: choose ρ to obtain a conservative but explainable capital buffer.
(v) Scenario calibration: set ρ equal to the Wasserstein distance between a baseline scenario
and a specified adverse scenario.
Remark 8.8 (Units). If the ground metric is measured in dollars, then ρ is measured in dollars.
This is an advantage of Wasserstein ambiguity sets: the radius has a physical interpretation as an
average transportation budget. But it also means that scaling and normalization choices matter.
8.14 Ground cost and actuarial meaning
The Wasserstein distance depends on the ground cost introduced in Definition 1.2. For one-
dimensional losses, the default choice is
c(x,y) =|x−y|p.
For multivariate actuarial vectors, the choice is less obvious. If
x = (x1,...,xd)
contains losses by line of business, one may use
c(x,y) =
d∑
k=1
wk|xk −yk|p,
where wk reflects line-specific materiality. Ifx contains future cash flows, one may discount:
c(x,y) =
T∑
t=1
vt|xt −yt|p.
Ifx contains spatial losses, one may combine financial and geographical displacement.
Actuarial takeaway
The ground cost is a modelling choice. It determines which distributional changes are
considered small. A Wasserstein robust capital model is only as meaningful as the cost
used to define the Wasserstein ball.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 130/416

8.15 Mini-lab: empirical robust TVaR and VaR
8.15 Mini-lab: empirical robust TVaR and VaR
The following R code computes empirical VaR, TVaR, robust TVaR and robust VaR for a vector
of simulated losses.
emp _ var <- function(x, alpha = 0.99) {
as. numeric( quantile(x, probs = alpha, type = 1))
}
emp _tvar <- function(x, alpha = 0.99) {
q <- sort(x)
n <- length( q)
k <- ceiling(alpha * n)
mean( q[k:n])
}
robust _tvar <- function(x, alpha = 0.99, rho = 1, p = 2) {
emp _tvar(x, alpha) + rho * (1 - alpha)^(-1 / p)
}
robust _ var <- function(x, alpha = 0.99, rho = 1, p = 2) {
q <- sort(x)
n <- length( q)
idx <- seq _len(n) / n >= alpha
tail _ q <- q[idx]
cost <- function(z) mean( pmax(z - tail _q, 0)^p) * length(tail _ q) / n
lower <- emp _ var(x, alpha)
upper <- lower + 10 * rho * (1 - alpha)^(-1 / p) + sd(x)
while (cost( upper) <= rho^p) upper <- 2 * upper
uniroot( function(z) cost(z) - rho^p, c( lower, upper)) $root
}
set.seed(1)
x <- rlnorm(5000, meanlog = 9, sdlog = 1)
alpha <- 0.995
rho <- 1000
p <- 2
emp _ var(x, alpha)
emp _tvar(x, alpha)
robust _tvar(x, alpha, rho, p)
robust _ var(x, alpha, rho, p)
Computational note
The code is intentionally simple. For production work, pay attention to quantile conventions,
interpolation, tail sample size, numerical bracketing and the treatment of policy limits or
deductibles.
8.16 Insurance interpretations
8.16.1 Capital and solvency
A solvency capital requirement is often a high-quantile or tail-average functional of annual loss.
Wasserstein robustness turns
ρ(ˆµ)
Page 131/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 132 ===
8 RISK MEASURES, QUANTILES AND WASSERSTEIN ROBUSTNESS
into
sup
ν:Wp(ν,ˆµ)≤ρ
ρ(ν).
This can be interpreted as a capital requirement under distributional model uncertainty. The
additional term for TVaR gives a transparent uncertainty loading.
8.16.2 Reinsurance pricing
Stop-loss reinsurance depends on
E[(L−r)+].
The function x↦→ (x−r)+ is 1-Lipschitz. Therefore
|Eµ[(L−r)+]− Eν[(L−r)+]|≤ W1(µ,ν).
This gives a direct Wasserstein stability bound for stop-loss pure premiums.
Example 8.10 (Layer pricing). A layer payoff
ℓ(L) = min{(L−a)+,b−a}
is also 1-Lipschitz. Hence its expected value changes by at most W1(µ,ν) when the severity
distribution moves from µ to ν. This is a useful diagnostic when comparing two fitted severity
models for reinsurance pricing.
8.17 Actuarial DRO: prudent pricing, prudent capital and radius selection
The previous sections introduced Wasserstein ambiguity sets as local neighbourhoods around a
baseline distribution. We now make the actuarial interpretation more explicit. In actuarial work,
a fitted distribution is rarely the final object. It is an estimate built from finite data, modelling
choices, portfolio assumptions and expert judgement. A Wasserstein ball represents a family of
plausible alternatives around that baseline model.
Let ˆµ be a fitted or empirical distribution of a loss, a covariate vector, or a future cash-flow
vector. For a transportation cost c(x,y), define the transport discrepancy
Wc(µ,ν) = inf γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
c(x,y) dγ(x,y).
For instance, c(x,y) =d(x,y)p gives Wc =Wp p. A Wasserstein ambiguity set is
Bc(ˆµ,r) ={ν∈P(X ) : Wc(ν,ˆµ)≤r},
where r≥ 0 is the radius of ambiguity.
Actuarial takeaway
A Wasserstein ball should not be read only as a statistical confidence set. It is also a
stress-testing device. It says: among all distributions that can be obtained from the
baseline law by spending at most r units of transportation cost, what is the worst actuarial
quantity?
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 132/416

=== page 133 ===
8.17 Actuarial DRO: prudent pricing, prudent capital and radius selection
8.17.1 Robust expectations and the basic dual formula
Let h : X→ R be an actuarial functional of the state. Examples include a claim severity h(x) = x,
a pure premium h(x) = E[L|X = x], a loss-ratio contribution, or a discounted cash-flow value.
The distributionally robust expectation is
Er(h; ˆµ) = sup
ν∈Bc(ˆµ,r)
∫
h(y) dν(y).
This quantity is the largest expected value of h over all distributions within transport radius r of
the baseline.
Proposition 8.9 (Dual formula for Wasserstein robust expectations). Under standard lower-
semicontinuity, compactness or growth assumptions,
Er(h; ˆµ) = inf λ≥0
{
λr +
∫
sup
y∈X
[
h(y)−λc(x,y)
]
dˆµ(x)
}
.
This formula is the DRO analogue of Kantorovich duality.
Proof. This is the standard Lagrangian argument for Wasserstein distributionally robust opti-
mization; see the optimal-transport duality background in Villani 2008; Peyré and Cuturi 2019;
Chewi et al. 2025. Sketch of the proof: introduce a Lagrange multiplier λ≥ 0 for the constraint
Wc(ν,ˆµ)≤r. The robust expectation is bounded above by
sup
ν
{∫
h dν +λ
(
r− Wc(ν,ˆµ) )} .
Using the definition of Wc, this can be rewritten as an optimization over couplings between ˆµ
and the adversarial law ν. After maximizing over the second coordinate, one obtains
λr +
∫
sup
y
{h(y)−λc(x,y)} dˆµ(x).
Taking the infimum over λ≥ 0 gives the dual formula. Under the usual lower semicontinuity,
compactness or growth assumptions, there is no duality gap.
The inner term
hλ c (x) = sup y {h(y)−λc(x,y)}
is a penalized worst-case perturbation of state x. The adversary can move x to another state y,
improving the payoff h, but pays the transport penalty λc(x,y). The multiplier λ is therefore
the shadow price of robustness.
8.17.2 Prudent pure premiums
Let X denote policyholder covariates and let m(X) be the predicted pure premium, for example
m(x) = E[L|X = x]. The empirical portfolio law is ˆµX. The usual average technical premium
is
ˆΠ =
∫
m(x) dˆµX(x).
A Wasserstein-prudent premium is
ˆΠDRO r = sup
ν∈Bc(ˆµX,r)
∫
m(x) dν(x).
Page 133/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 134 ===
8 RISK MEASURES, QUANTILES AND WASSERSTEIN ROBUSTNESS
This is not a loading applied uniformly to all policies. It is a distributional loading: it asks how
large the average premium would become if the portfolio distribution were perturbed within
radius r.
If c(x,y) =d(x,y) and m is Lm-Lipschitz, then by the Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality,
ˆΠDRO r ≤ ˆΠ +Lmr.
In many regular cases this bound is sharp:
sup
ν:W1(ν,ˆµX)≤r
∫
m dν =
∫
m dˆµX +r Lip(m).
Actuarial takeaway
The robust premium decomposes into a baseline premium and a sensitivity loading. The
loading is large when the pricing function m changes sharply with respect to the portfolio
metric. Thus DRO penalizes not only uncertainty in the portfolio distribution, but also
instability of the pricing rule.
Example 8.11 (Territorial drift). Suppose X contains a territory-risk score and m(X) is
highly sensitive to that score. A small Wasserstein movement of the portfolio toward higher-risk
territories can increase the robust pure premium. The term Lip(m)r makes this dependence
explicit: a more unstable tariff requires a larger prudence margin for the same radius.
8.17.3 Prudent capital
Let L denote an annual aggregate loss with baseline law ˆµL. For a law-invariant capital functional
R, such as VaRα, TVaRα or a spectral risk measure, define the robust capital
Capr(R) = sup
ν∈Bc(ˆµL,r)
R(ν).
For expected loss, this reduces to the robust expectation above. For tail risk, the interpretation
is more conservative: the adversary is allowed to move probability mass toward worse loss levels,
subject to the transport budget r.
When R = TVaRα, and losses are one-dimensional with finite first moment, the quantile
representation gives
TVaRα(ν) = 1 1−α ∫ 1 α F−1 ν (u) du.
Hence robust TVaR over a Wasserstein ball can be interpreted as a worst-case tail-quantile
average over all quantile functions within transport distance r of the baseline.
For W1, the following useful bound follows from the TVaR stability inequality:
TVaRα(ν)≤ TVaRα(ˆµL) + 1 1−αW1(ν,ˆµL).
Therefore
sup
ν:W1(ν,ˆµL)≤r
TVaRα(ν)≤ TVaRα(ˆµL) + r 1−α.
The multiplier 1/(1 −α) shows why tail capital is more sensitive to distributional ambiguity
than the mean.
Remark 8.9 (VaR requires care). Robust VaRα is more delicate than robust TVaRα. A small
amount of probability mass moved across the α-quantile can change VaRα discontinuously.
Wasserstein balls control the amount and distance of such movements, but pointwise quantile
stability usually requires additional regularity, such as a positive density near the quantile.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 134/416

=== page 135 ===
8.17 Actuarial DRO: prudent pricing, prudent capital and radius selection
Example 8.12 (Capital add-on from transport ambiguity). Suppose a baseline loss model gives
TVaR99% = 100 million. If the actuary accepts a W1-radius r = 0.5 million in the loss scale, the
bound above gives
TVaRrobust 99% ≤ 100 + 0.5 0.01 = 150
million. The example is intentionally simple, but it highlights the scale effect: a small average
transportation budget can imply a substantial tail capital margin at very high confidence levels.
8.17.4 Robust pricing versus robust capital
Pricing and capital use the same ambiguity set, but they answer different questions.
For pricing, the object is often a conditional expectation:
m(x) = E[L|X =x].
The robust premium asks how the average technical premium changes when the portfolio mix
changes within radius r.
For capital, the object is a tail functional of the aggregate loss distribution:
R(Law(L)).
The robust capital asks how the tail of the aggregate law can deteriorate within the same
transport radius.
Thus the same Wasserstein ball can be used twice:
ˆΠDRO r = sup
ν∈B(ˆµX,r)
∫
m(x) dν(x),
for portfolio-mix uncertainty, and
Capr = sup
ν∈B(ˆµL,r)
R(ν),
for loss-distribution uncertainty. The first lives on the covariate space; the second lives on the
loss space.
Actuarial takeaway
A robust premium is not automatically a robust capital charge. The premium problem
perturbs the distribution of risks or policyholder profiles. The capital problem perturbs
the distribution of aggregate losses. They may use different ground costs and different
radii.
8.17.5 Choosing the radius
The radius r is the most important modelling parameter in Wasserstein DRO. If r = 0, the model
reduces to the baseline distribution. If r is too large, the robust quantity may become excessively
conservative or even infinite when the payoff grows faster than the transportation cost. A useful
radius should therefore combine statistical, actuarial and governance considerations.
1. Statistical radius. When ˆµn is an empirical law, one can choose r so that
P
(
Wp(ˆµn,µ0)≤r ) ≈ 1−δ,
where µ0 is the unknown data-generating law. In practice, µ0 is unknown, so one may use
bootstrap or subsampling:
rstat = Quantile1−δ (Wp(ˆµ∗ n,ˆµn)),
where ˆµ∗ n is a bootstrap empirical distribution.
Page 135/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 136 ===
8 RISK MEASURES, QUANTILES AND WASSERSTEIN ROBUSTNESS
2. Backtesting radius. For time-indexed portfolios, one can compute historical distributional
changes
Wp(ˆµt,ˆµt+1),
and choose r as a high quantile of these observed shifts. This makes the radius reflect historically
observed portfolio drift.
3. Stress radius. The radius can be calibrated to a named stress. For instance, if a regulatory
or internal stress distribution µstress is available, define
rstress =Wp(ˆµ,µstress).
Then fractions of rstress correspond to partial stresses, while larger multiples correspond to more
severe scenarios.
4. Materiality radius. One may choose r by specifying an acceptable change in an actuarial
quantity. For example, choose r so that
ˆΠDRO r −ˆΠ
equals a target prudence margin, or so that robust capital increases by a pre-specified percentage.
This reverses the DRO calculation: the radius is chosen to match a governance or materiality
threshold.
5. Combined radius. In practice, the radius often combines several sources:
r =rstat +rmodel +rstress,
or, when the components are viewed as roughly independent,
r =
(
r2 stat +r2 model +r2 stress )1/2.
The first form is conservative; the second is closer to a root-sum-square aggregation of uncertainty
budgets.
Computational note
A practical radius-selection workflow is:
1. compute a statistical radius by bootstrap;
2. compute historical Wasserstein drift between successive portfolio periods;
3. compute the distance to at least one named stress scenario;
4. report robust premium and robust capital as functions of r;
5. choose the operational radius through governance, not only through a statistical
formula.
8.17.6 A mini-lab: robust mean and radius selection in R
The following code illustrates a simple one-dimensional W1-DRO calculation. For a Lipschitz
payoff h(x) =x, the robust expectation over a W1-ball is the empirical mean plus the radius.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 136/416

set.seed(123)
# Baseline empirical losses
n <- 1000
loss <- rlnorm(n, meanlog = 8, sdlog = 0.8)
# Empirical W1 by sorting on a common grid and using the left empirical
quantile
emp _ q _left <- function(x, probs) {
x <- sort(x)
n <- length(x)
idx <- pmax(1, ceiling(probs * n))
x[idx]
}
W1 _1d <- function(x, y, grid _size = 1000) {
u <- seq(1 /( grid _size + 1), grid _size /( grid _size + 1),
length.out = grid _size)
qx <- emp _ q _left(x, u)
qy <- emp _ q _left(y, u)
mean( abs(qx - qy))
}
# Bootstrap distribution of W1 distance to the empirical law
B <- 300
w _boot <- numeric(B)
for (b in 1:B) {
loss _b <- sample(loss, size = n, replace = TRUE)
w _boot[b] <- W1 _1d(loss _b, loss)
}
r _ stat <- as. numeric( quantile(w _boot, 0.95))
baseline _ mean <- mean(loss)
robust _ mean <- baseline _ mean + r _ stat
c(
baseline _ mean = baseline _ mean,
radius _95 = r _ stat,
robust _ mean _W1 = robust _ mean
)
For a tail functional such as TVaRα, one can combine the empirical TVaR with the stability
bound:
TVaRrobust α ≤ \TVaRα + r 1−α.
TVaR _emp <- function(x, alpha = 0.99) {
q <- sort(x)
n <- length( q)
k <- pmax(1, ceiling(alpha * n))
mean( q[k:n])
}
alpha <- 0.99
baseline _tvar <- TVaR _emp(loss, alpha)
robust _tvar _bound <- baseline _tvar + r _ stat / (1 - alpha)

8.17.7 Interpretation and limitations
Wasserstein DRO is attractive because it is geometrically meaningful. The adversarial distri-
bution cannot be arbitrary: it must be reachable from the baseline distribution at a controlled
transportation cost. This makes the method more interpretable than worst-case analysis over
very broad model classes.
However, the approach is not automatic. The result depends on:
• the ground cost c(x,y);
• the radius r;
• the state space on which the ambiguity is defined;
• the growth of the actuarial payoff h or risk functionalR;
• the distinction between perturbing covariates, individual losses, aggregate losses, or cash-
flow vectors.
A good DRO analysis should therefore report a sensitivity curve:
r↦−→ ˆΠDRO r , r↦−→ Capr.
The curve is often more informative than a single robust value. It shows how quickly the actuarial
conclusion deteriorates as one moves away from the baseline model.
Actuarial takeaway
The radius r is not a technical nuisance. It is the actuarial statement of how much model
uncertainty, portfolio drift or stress displacement the insurer is willing to consider. In that
sense, Wasserstein DRO is as much a governance tool as a mathematical one.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 138/416

=== page 139 ===
8.18 Summary
Actuarial takeaway
Professional lens: Solvency II and Wasserstein capital.
Under Solvency II, capital is framed as a one-year risk measure on the economic balance
sheet. The Solvency Capital Requirement is calibrated as a high-quantile loss of own funds
over a one-year horizon, and the regulatory architecture separates risk modules such as life
underwriting, non-life underwriting, health underwriting, market, credit and operational
risk.
This makes Wasserstein robustness a natural actuarial diagnostic. A baseline internal
model provides a distribution of one-year own-fund changes,
∆OF =OF1 −OF0.
A Wasserstein ambiguity set around its fitted law,
Bp(ˆµ,r) ={ν :Wp(ν,ˆµ)≤r},
does not replace the regulatory calculation. Rather, it asks a model-risk question: how
much would the capital requirement change if the one-year loss distribution were perturbed
within a controlled transportation budget?
For example, a robust capital diagnostic may report
SCRr = sup
ν∈Bp(ˆµ,r)
VaRν 99.5%(−∆OF ),
or a tail-average analogue based on TVaR. The radius r can be interpreted as a model
uncertainty, portfolio drift, parameter uncertainty or stress-displacement budget.
Further reading
The dual formula in Proposition 8.9 is the standard Wasserstein-DRO duality; see Esfahani
and Kuhn 2018; Blanchet and Murthy 2019; Gao and Kleywegt 2016. Dependence uncer-
tainty and comonotonic bounds are connected to the Fréchet-class and copula literature;
see Dhaene et al. 2002a; Embrechts et al. 2002; Puccetti and Rüschendorf 2013.
8.18 Summary
This lecture introduced Wasserstein robustness for actuarial risk measures. The main messages
are:
(i) Quantile-based risk measures are naturally expressed in Wasserstein geometry.
(ii) Expectations, TVaR and spectral risk measures are Lipschitz with respect to Wp, with
constants determined by tail weights.
(iii) VaR is less stable because it reads a single quantile level.
(iv) Wasserstein ambiguity sets produce robust risk measures with interpretable loadings.
(v) Dependence uncertainty is a coupling problem and leads to multi-marginal optimal trans-
port.
(vi) Actuarial interpretation depends critically on the ground cost and the radius ρ.
Page 139/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

9 PRICING AND PREMIUM DISTRIBUTIONS
9 Pricing and Premium Distributions
Learning objectives
The preceding lecture studied risk measures as functionals of loss distributions. This lecture
applies the same distributional viewpoint to insurance pricing. A tariff is a measurable function
on a portfolio, and therefore induces a probability distribution of premiums. The objective is to
compare premium books, separate policy-level changes from distributional changes, and relate
classical pricing dispersion measures to Wasserstein geometry.
After this lecture, the reader should be able to:
(i) distinguish individual risk prediction from the distribution of premiums induced by a tariff;
(ii) represent tariffs as measurable functions and premium books as push-forward measures;
(iii) formalize pooled, class-based and individualized pure premiums through sigma-algebras;
(iv) interpret variance and Wasserstein distances as complementary measures of premium
dispersion;
(v) prove that W2 2(Law(Π),δE[Π]) = Var(Π);
(vi) compare tariff revisions using both policy-level displacement and distributional transport
distance;
(vii) understand shrinkage paths between a pooled benchmark and a technical premium;
(viii) use one-dimensional quantile formulas to compute distances between empirical premium
books;
(ix) study affordability through the distribution of premium burdens.
Actuarial takeaway
Atariffisarandomvariableonaportfolio. Oncethisisaccepted, pricinghasadistributional
component: not only “what premium should each policyholder pay?”, but also “what
premium distribution does the tariff create?”
This lecture uses the push-forward notation of Definition 2.1, the coupling notation of
Definition 2.2, the discrete OT formulation of Definition 3.4, and the Wasserstein distance
of Definition 5.2. It prepares the portfolio-drift material in Lecture 10 and the empirical
tariff-comparison computations in Section 9.18.
9.1 Why pricing is a transport problem
Let L≥ 0 denote the future claim cost of a policy over a fixed coverage period, and let X denote
the information used for pricing. A standard pure-premium model estimates
m(x) = E[L|X =x].
A premium may then be written as
Π(x) =ℓ(m(x),x),
where ℓ includes expenses, profit loading, reinsurance cost, taxes, caps, floors, credibility adjust-
ments, regulatory constraints, or commercial smoothing.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 142/416

=== page 143 ===
9.2 Pure premium, loaded premium and tariff random variables
IfX is random under the portfolio distribution, then the charged premium
Π = Π(X)
is a random variable. Its law is
µΠ = Law(Π) = Law(Π(X)).
This is the premium distribution induced by the tariff. More generally, if the tariff uses multiple
outputs, such as predicted frequency, predicted severity and a risk loading, one may consider the
joint law
Law
(ˆλ(X),ˆs(X), Π(X) ) ,
or even the law of vectors of premiums across coverages.
This is the key viewpoint of the lecture: pricing is not only an estimation problem, but also a
transformation problem. The portfolio distribution Law(X) is pushed forward by the premium
function Π into a distribution on R+:
µΠ = Π# Law(X).
When a tariff changes from Π0 to Π1, the corresponding premium distribution changes from
µ0 = Law(Π0(X)) to µ1 = Law(Π1(X)).
Optimal transport compares these two distributions.
Example 9.1 (A tariff revision). An insurer replaces a generalized linear model by a gradient-
boosted model. On the same portfolio, the old premium is Π0(Xi), and the new premium is
Π1(Xi). The average change
1
n
n∑
i=1
|Π1(Xi)− Π0(Xi)|
measures policy-level disruption under the identity matching of policies. The Wasserstein distance
W1
(ˆµΠ0,ˆµΠ1 )
measures the smallest possible average distributional displacement between the old and new
premium books. The first is an implementation quantity; the second is a distributional lower
bound.
9.2 Pure premium, loaded premium and tariff random variables
A basic actuarial pricing model may decompose total claim cost across coverages or perils as
L =L(1) +··· +L(K),
and, for a single coverage, more commonly as a frequency–severity process
L =
N∑
j=1
Zj.
Given covariatesX =x, the conditional pure premium is
m(x) = E[L|X =x] = E[N|X =x]E[Z|X =x]
when, conditionally on X = x, claim counts and individual severities are independent and
severities are identically distributed with finite conditional mean.
Page 143/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 144 ===
9 PRICING AND PREMIUM DISTRIBUTIONS
Definition 9.1 (Pure premium and charged premium). Let L∈L1 be the future loss over the
coverage period and letG⊆F be the information used for pricing. TheG-pure premium is
mG = E[L|G].
A charged premium is a non-negative random variable of the form
Π = Π(X),
where the tariff map Π may add loadings, expenses, capital charges, caps, floors or smoothing to
the pure-premium estimate.
A loaded premium may take the form
Π(x) = (1 +η)m(x) +e(x),
where η is a proportional loading and e(x) is an expense or fixed charge. More generally, a tariff
can include a risk margin:
Π(x) = E[L|X =x] +θr(x),
where r(x) may be a conditional standard deviation, a conditional TVaR, a capital allocation, or
a reinsurance-cost allocation.
In all cases, once X is random under the portfolio, Π(X) is random. Its distribution may
have policy, regulatory and social meaning. For example:
• a concentrated distribution indicates limited premium differentiation;
• a dispersed distribution indicates stronger segmentation of the premium book;
• a heavy upper tail indicates that a small set of policyholders receives very high premiums;
• a tariff revision can be viewed as a transport from the old premium distribution to the new
one;
• the distribution of Π(X)/Y, where Y is income or wealth, is an affordability distribution.
9.3 Push-forward formulation of a tariff
Let (Ω,F, P) be the probability space describing a randomly selected policy from the portfolio.
A pricing model is a measurable map
Π : Ω→ R+.
Usually, Π is written as Π(X), whereX : Ω→X is the vector of rating variables.
The induced premium distribution is
µΠ = Π#P.
Equivalently, for every Borel set A⊆ R+,
µΠ(A) = P(Π∈A).
Thus all distributional questions about the premium book can be written as questions about a
push-forward measure.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 144/416

=== page 145 ===
9.4 Pooled, class-based and individualized pure premiums
Definition 9.2 (Premium book). Given a portfolio probability measure PX = Law(X) and a
tariff Π :X→ R+, the premium book induced by Π is the probability measure
µΠ = Π#PX.
IfX1,...,Xn is a finite portfolio, the empirical premium book is
ˆµΠ = 1 n n∑ i=1 δΠ(Xi).
Remark 9.1 (Portfolio weights). In practice, policies may have exposure weights wi ≥ 0, with∑
iwi = 1. Then the empirical premium book is
ˆµΠ =
n∑
i=1
wiδΠ(Xi).
All Wasserstein definitions apply without change. The weights may represent exposure units,
earned premium, policy duration, insured value, or sampling weights.
Definition 9.3 (Policy-level and distributional tariff comparison). Let Π0 = Π0(X) and
Π1 = Π1(X) be two premium random variables on the same portfolio probability space. For
p≥ 1, the policy-level displacement is
∆id p (Π0, Π1) = (E|Π1 − Π0|p)1/p,
where the same policyholder is compared before and after the tariff change. The distributional
displacement is
∆dist p (Π0, Π1) = Wp ( Law(Π0), Law(Π1) ) ,
where policy identities are ignored and only the two marginal premium books are compared.
Proposition 9.4 shows that ∆dist p ≤ ∆id p .
9.4 Pooled, class-based and individualized pure premiums
Pricing uses information at different levels of granularity. At one extreme, all policyholders pay
the same pure premium. At the other, the price is individualized using all admissible rating
variables. Between the two lie class tariffs, credibility tariffs, regulated tariffs and smoothed
commercial tariffs.
A useful formalization uses sigma-algebras. Let L be the loss and let G⊆F denote the
information used for pricing. TheG-based pure premium is
mG = E[L|G].
IfG is trivial, then
mG = E[L],
and the tariff is constant. IfG =σ(X), then
mG = E[L|X],
and the tariff uses all rating variables in X. If G is generated by a coarse classification, the tariff
is a class tariff.
Definition 9.4 (Pricing information). Let G ⊆F be a sigma-algebra. The corresponding
pure-premium tariff is
ΠG = E[L|G].
The induced premium distribution is
µG = Law(ΠG).
Page 145/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 146 ===
9 PRICING AND PREMIUM DISTRIBUTIONS
If G1 ⊆G2, then G2 is more informative. The tariff ΠG2 may be more segmented than ΠG1.
The classical variance decomposition gives
Var(E[L|G2]) = Var(E[L|G1]) + E
[
Var
(
E[L|G2]|G1
)]
when G1 ⊆G2. Thus increasing the pricing information cannot decrease the variance of the pure
premium.
Proposition 9.1 (Segmentation increases premium variance). Let G1 ⊆G2 ⊆F, and assume
L∈L2. Then
Var(E[L|G1])≤ Var(E[L|G2]).
Proof. By the tower property,
E[L|G1] = E[E[L|G2]|G1].
Applying the law of total variance to Z = E[L|G2] conditional onG1,
Var(Z) = Var(E[Z|G1]) + E[Var(Z|G1)].
Since the second term is nonnegative, the result follows.
Corollary 9.1 (Nested information and distance to pooling). Under the assumptions of Propo-
sition 9.1,
W2 2 ( Law(ΠG1),δE[L] ) ≤ W2 2 ( Law(ΠG2),δE[L] ) .
Thus nested refinement of the pricing information cannot decrease the squared W2-distance from
the constant pure-premium benchmark.
Actuarial takeaway
More information does not necessarily mean better pricing in every social or regulatory
sense, but it does mechanically increase the dispersion of the conditional pure premium,
unless the additional information is irrelevant for the conditional pure premium.
9.5 Wasserstein distance to a pooled benchmark
Let Π ∈L2 be a premium random variable. A natural pooled benchmark is the constant premium
¯π = E[Π].
The corresponding premium distribution is δ¯π. The squared 2-Wasserstein distance to this pooled
benchmark is exactly the variance.
Proposition 9.2 (Variance as Wasserstein distance to pooling). Let Π ∈L2, with law µ = Law(Π)
and mean ¯π = E[Π]. Then
W2 2(µ,δ¯π) = Var(Π).
More generally, for p≥ 1,
Wp p(µ,δa) = E[|Π−a|p].
Proof. There is only one coupling between µ and δa: it is the law of (Π,a). Therefore
Wp p(µ,δa) = E[|Π−a|p].
Taking p = 2 and a = E[Π] gives the variance.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 146/416

=== page 147 ===
9.6 Shrinkage paths between a benchmark and a technical tariff
Proposition 9.2 is useful because it connects a classical actuarial measure of segmentation to
Wasserstein geometry. It also clarifies what the variance misses. Variance measures distance to a
single pooled premium. It does not compare two non-degenerate tariff distributions. Wasserstein
distances do.
Definition 9.5 (Distance-to-pooling index). Let Π∈L2 be a tariff and let ¯π = E[Π]. Define
D2(Π) = W2 2 ( Law(Π),δ¯π ) = Var(Π).
More generally, for p≥ 1, define
Dp(Π) = Wp p ( Law(Π),δ¯π ) = E[|Π− ¯π|p].
In Definition 9.5, the case p = 1 gives a mean absolute deviation from the constant benchmark.
The case p = 2 gives variance. Larger p emphasizes extreme premium deviations.
9.6 Shrinkage paths between a benchmark and a technical tariff
Let Π be an individualized tariff with mean ¯π. A simple path from full pooling to full profiling is
Πλ = ¯π +λ(Π− ¯π), λ∈ [0, 1].
At λ = 0, all policyholders pay ¯π. At λ = 1, the technical tariff is used. Intermediate values
shrink premiums toward the constant benchmark.
Proposition 9.3 (Scaling along a shrinkage path). Let Π∈L2, ¯π = E[Π], and
Πλ = ¯π +λ(Π− ¯π).
Then
D2(Πλ) =λ2D2(Π).
More generally,
Dp(Πλ) =λpDp(Π).
Proof. Since E[Πλ] = ¯π,
Dp(Πλ) = E[|Πλ − ¯π|p] = E[|λ(Π− ¯π)|p] =λpDp(Π).
This path is also the one-dimensional displacement interpolation from δ¯π to Law(Π), as in
Definition 5.4, when the map x↦→ ¯π + λ(x− ¯π) is applied to the premium distribution. In one
dimension, the quantile function of Πλ is
Qλ(u) = ¯π +λ(Q(u)− ¯π),
where Q is the quantile function of Π. Hence
Wp p ( Law(Πλ), Law(Πλ′) ) =|λ−λ′|pE[|Π− ¯π|p].
Example 9.2 (Commercial smoothing). A company may decide not to deploy the full technical
premium Π, but instead apply
Πλ = (1−λ)¯π +λΠ.
For p = 2, the distance-to-pooling index is multiplied by λ2. Thus λ = 0.5 uses half the technical
displacement but only one quarter of the variance from the constant benchmark.
Page 147/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 148 ===
9 PRICING AND PREMIUM DISTRIBUTIONS
9.7 Class tariffs and Wasserstein comparison
Suppose the portfolio is partitioned into K rating classes C1,...,CK. A class tariff charges a
constant premium πk to all policies in class k. The premium random variable is
ΠC =
K∑
k=1
πk1{X∈Ck}.
Its distribution is discrete:
Law(ΠC) =
K∑
k=1
pkδπk, pk = P(X∈Ck).
If a more refined tariff uses subclasses Ckℓ, the new distribution has more support points.
Wasserstein distances can compare the coarse and refined tariffs.
Using the terminology of Definition 9.3, there are two distinct comparisons.
First, the policy-level distance uses the actual same policy under the two tariffs:
∆id p (ΠC, ΠR) = (E|ΠC(X)− ΠR(X)|p)1/p.
Second, the distributional distance ignores the identity of policies and compares only the two
premium distributions:
∆dist p (ΠC, ΠR) = Wp (Law(ΠC), Law(ΠR)).
The second is always no larger than the first.
Proposition 9.4 (Distributional lower bound). Let Π0, Π1 ∈Lp. Then
Wp
(
Law(Π0), Law(Π1)
)
≤ (E|Π1 − Π0|p)1/p.
Proof. The joint law of (Π0, Π1) is a coupling of Law(Π0) and Law(Π1). The Wasserstein distance
is the infimum over all couplings, hence no larger than the cost under this particular coupling.
Actuarial takeaway
Policy-level premium change is what customers experience. Wasserstein distance is the
smallest distributional change compatible with the two premium books. The gap between
them is informative: it measures how much of the disruption comes from policy identities
rather than from the marginal premium distributions alone.
9.8 A two-class example
Consider a portfolio with two equally weighted groups. The pooled premium is ¯π. A class tariff
charges
¯π−d to group 1, ¯π +d to group 2.
The premium distribution is
µd = 1 2δ¯π−d + 1 2δ¯π+d.
Then
W2 2(µd,δ¯π) =d2.
If a refined model further splits each group symmetrically by an amount e, the premium
distribution becomes
νd,e = 1 4δ¯π−d−e + 1 4δ¯π−d+e + 1 4δ¯π+d−e + 1 4δ¯π+d+e.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 148/416

=== page 149 ===
9.9 Segmentation, prediction and the variance decomposition
Assuming 0≤e≤d, the monotone coupling gives
W2 2(µd,νd,e) =e2.
Thus the first split creates segmentation d2 from pooling, while the refinement creates an
additional distributional movement e2.
This example is deliberately simple, but it illustrates how OT separates levels of pricing
granularity. Each new set of rating variables changes the premium distribution and can increase
its distance from a constant benchmark.
9.9 Segmentation, prediction and the variance decomposition
Let m(X) = E[L|X] be the full technical pure premium. Let G⊆ σ(X) be the information
used by a coarser tariff. The corresponding pure premium is
mG = E[L|G] = E[m(X)|G].
The total predictive heterogeneity of the full model is
Var(m(X)).
The heterogeneity captured by the coarser tariff is
Var(mG).
The remaining within-class heterogeneity is
E [Var(m(X)|G)].
The variance decomposition gives
Var(m(X)) = Var(mG) + E [Var(m(X)|G)].
This identity has a direct pricing interpretation:
• Var(m(X)) is the dispersion of fully individualized pure premiums;
• Var(mG) is the dispersion actually introduced by the class tariff;
• the residual term is predictive heterogeneity left pooled within classes.
Definition 9.6 (Captured profiling ratio). Assume m(X) ∈ L2 and Var(m(X)) > 0. The
captured profiling ratio ofG is
Rprof(G) = Var(E[L|G]) Var(E[L|X]).
It lies in [0, 1] whenG⊆σ(X).
This ratio is not a Wasserstein distance by itself, but by the previous proposition it is the
ratio of squared Wasserstein distances to the pooled mean:
Rprof(G) = W2 2(Law(E[L|G]),δE[L]) W2 2(Law(E[L|X]),δE[L]).
Page 149/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 150 ===
9 PRICING AND PREMIUM DISTRIBUTIONS
9.10 From technical premium to market premium
The technical premium m(X) is not always the charged premium. Insurers may apply transfor-
mations:
Π(X) =T (m(X),X),
where T may include:
• minimum premiums;
• maximum premiums or caps;
• bonus–malus smoothing;
• credibility shrinkage;
• territorial smoothing;
• regulatory constraints;
• commercial discounts;
• expense and commission loadings.
Each transformation changes the premium distribution. If T is a deterministic map on
premiums, for example Π =T (m), then
Law(Π) =T# Law(m).
A cap T (z) = min(z,M) moves all mass above M to M. A floor T (z) = max(z,m0) moves all
mass below m0 to m0. A shrinkage T (z) = ¯π + λ(z− ¯π) contracts the distribution around the
mean.
Example 9.3 (Premium cap). Let Π be a technical premium and define the capped premium
ΠM = min(Π,M).
Then
E[|Π− ΠM|p] = E[(Π−M)p +].
Therefore
Wp
(
Law(Π), Law(ΠM) ) ≤ ( E[(Π−M)p +] )1/p.
In one dimension, if the cap is applied monotonically to the same premium variable, the right-hand
side is the cost of the induced monotone coupling and is often the natural implementation cost.
9.11 Tariff revisions as transport plans
Suppose an insurer moves from Π0 to Π1. The joint law
γid = Law(Π0(X), Π1(X))
is the identity-policy coupling between the old and new premium distributions. Its cost is
Cid p = E|Π1(X)− Π0(X)|p.
The optimal transport cost is
COT p = Wp p(Law(Π0), Law(Π1)).
Since COT p ≤Cid p , the difference
Cid p −COT p
measures how much extra movement is caused by the identity of policyholders rather than by
the marginal distributional change.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 150/416

=== page 151 ===
9.12 One-dimensional formulas for premium books
Definition 9.7 (Reallocation gap). For Π0, Π1 ∈Lp, define
Gp(Π0, Π1) = E|Π1 − Π0|p − Wp p(Law(Π0), Law(Π1)).
The quantity Gp ≥ 0 is the reallocation gap between the policy-level implementation and the
distributionally optimal matching.
A small reallocation gap means that the actual policy-level changes are close to the optimal
distributional rearrangement. A large gap means that two tariffs may have similar marginal
premium distributions while charging very different policies.
Example 9.4 (Same distribution, different people). Two tariffs can have the same premium
distribution but assign high premiums to different policyholders. Then
Wp(Law(Π0), Law(Π1)) = 0,
but
E|Π1 − Π0|p
may be large. This is important in tariff governance: distributional stability alone is not enough
to guarantee customer-level stability.
9.12 One-dimensional formulas for premium books
Because premiums are one-dimensional, the quantile formula is especially useful. Let Q0,Q1 be
the quantile functions of Π0 and Π1. Then
Wp p(Law(Π0), Law(Π1)) =
∫ 1
0
|Q1(u)−Q0(u)|p du.
This allows premium distributions to be compared without solving a large linear program. In a
finite portfolio with equal weights, sort the premiums:
Π0,(1) ≤···≤ Π0,(n), Π1,(1) ≤···≤ Π1,(n).
Then
Wp p(ˆµ0,ˆµ1) = 1 n n∑ i=1 |Π1,(i) − Π0,(i)|p.
Computational note
For one-dimensional premium distributions, Wasserstein distances are computationally
cheap. Sorting is enough. This is one reason why OT is practical for tariff governance: a
pricing team can compute premium-book movements for millions of policies using quantiles
rather than a full transport matrix.
9.13 Ground costs and actuarial meaning
The default cost for premiums is often|x−y|p, but other costs may be more meaningful.
A relative premium change can be measured by
c(x,y) =|logy− logx|p,
when premiums are positive. This treats a movement from 100 to 120 similarly to a movement
from 500 to 600, since both are roughly 20% increases.
Page 151/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 152 ===
9 PRICING AND PREMIUM DISTRIBUTIONS
An affordability cost may compare premium-to-income ratios:
c
(
(π,y), (π′,y′) ) = ⏐⏐⏐⏐ π′ y′ − π y ⏐⏐⏐⏐ p .
A customer-disruption cost may be asymmetric:
c(x,y) =a(y−x)p + +b(x−y)p +,
with a>b if increases are viewed as more disruptive than decreases.
Remark 9.2 (Metric versus loss function). The function c need not always be a metric for the
Kantorovich problem to make sense. However, if one wants Wp to be a genuine distance, one
uses a metric cost d(x,y)p. In pricing governance, non-metric costs can still be useful as decision
losses.
9.14 Affordability and welfare distributions
Premiums matter not only in absolute terms. A premium of 1000 may be affordable for one
household and unaffordable for another. Let Y > 0 denote income or an affordability denominator.
Define the burden ratio
A = Π Y .
The induced affordability distribution is
Law(A) = Law
(Π
Y
)
.
A tariff revision changes A0 = Π0/Y into A1 = Π1/Y. Wasserstein distances can compare the
distributions Law(A0) and Law(A1), while risk measures such as TVaRα(A) focus on the most
burdened policyholders.
Definition 9.8 (Affordability transport). Let Y > 0 and let Π0, Π1 be two tariffs. The
affordability displacement is
Wp
(
Law
(Π0
Y
)
, Law
(Π1
Y
))
.
The policy-level affordability displacement is
(
E
⏐⏐⏐⏐ Π1 − Π0 Y ⏐⏐⏐⏐ p)1/p .
This distinction mirrors the earlier distinction between distributional and policy-level premium
changes. A tariff may have limited distributional impact on affordability but still strongly affect
particular households.
9.15 Micro-insurance and affordability distributions
Micro-insurance raises a different actuarial question from standard technical pricing. The
premium must be sufficient to finance expected claims and expenses, but it must also remain
affordable for low-income households or small businesses. Thus the actuarial object is not only
the loss distribution. It is also the joint distribution of premiums, income, wealth, liquidity and
welfare.
Let P denote the premium charged to an insured and let Y denote a measure of ability to
pay, such as income, disposable income or consumption capacity. A simple affordability ratio is
R = P Y .
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 152/416

=== page 153 ===
9.15 Micro-insurance and affordability distributions
An affordability constraint may require
P(R>τ)≤ε,
for a tolerance threshold τ and a small exceedance probability ε. Equivalently, one may study
the distribution of the premium burden R.
Optimal transport enters because pricing reform moves the distribution of premium burdens.
Let R0 be the burden under a baseline tariff and R1 the burden under a new tariff. Then
Wp
(
Law(R0), Law(R1)
)
measures how far the burden distribution is displaced. This is different from comparing average
premiums. Two tariffs can have the same average premium but very different affordability
distributions.
Actuarial takeaway
For micro-insurance, the relevant distribution is often not the premium distribution alone,
but the distribution of premium burden:
R = premium ability to pay.
Optimal transport provides a way to compare tariff designs by asking how they move the
whole affordability distribution, including its upper tail.
A useful benchmark is an affordability target distribution. Let η be a target law for acceptable
premium burdens. For example, η may be concentrated below a threshold τ, or may represent a
socially acceptable burden distribution. The distance
Wp
(
Law(R),η
)
then measures how far a tariff is from the affordability benchmark.
This leads to a constrained pricing problem. Let Pθ be a tariff indexed by parameters θ, and
let L be the loss. One may seek
min
θ
E [ℓ(Pθ,L)] subject to Wp
(
Law(Pθ/Y ),η
)
≤r.
The first term is actuarial or financial. The constraint is distributional and social: it restricts
how far the premium burden can be from an affordability target.
Another formulation uses a barycentric compromise between technical adequacy and afford-
ability. Let µtech be the distribution of technical premiums and µaff a distribution of affordable
premiums. A Wasserstein barycenter
µλ ∈ argmin µ
{
(1−λ)W2 2(µ,µtech) +λW2 2(µ,µaff) }
gives a continuum between actuarial adequacy and affordability. In one dimension, its quantile
function is
Qµλ(t) = (1−λ)Qµtech(t) +λQµaff (t).
Remark 9.3 (Affordability is not average premium alone). An affordability audit compares
premiums to resources. Average premium income can remain unchanged while the upper tail
of the burden distribution increases. For this reason, the distribution of Π/Y is often more
informative than a scalar premium summary.
Page 153/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 154 ===
9 PRICING AND PREMIUM DISTRIBUTIONS
Micro-insurance also involves demand. Let D(p,y) be the probability that an individual
with resources y purchases coverage at premium p. The insured portfolio law is then a selected
distribution:
Law(X,L,Y | purchase).
A tariff reform can improve affordability while changing the composition of the insured pool.
This creates an adverse-selection feedback:
Pθ −→D(Pθ,Y )−→ Law(L| purchase)−→ technical premium.
Transport diagnostics can be used to monitor each movement: premium burden, portfolio
composition and claim distribution.
Computational note
A micro-insurance dashboard may report:
E[P ], E[P/Y ], P(P/Y >τ), TVaRα(P/Y ), Wp(Law(P/Y ),η).
The Wasserstein term summarizes the distributional distance to an affordability target,
while the exceedance and tail-average terms remain directly interpretable.
9.16 Barycentric tariff benchmarks
Suppose several tariffs are available:
Π1,..., ΠK.
They may come from different models, different parameter choices, different regulatory regimes,
or different companies. Let
µk = Law(Πk(X)).
A Wasserstein barycenter of µ1,...,µK solves
¯µ∈ argmin
µ
K∑
k=1
wkW2 2(µ,µk).
The barycenter is a distributional compromise between tariffs.
Proposition 9.5 (One-dimensional barycentric tariff benchmark). Let µ1,...,µK ∈P2(R) be
premium distributions with quantile functions Q1,...,QK, and let wk ≥ 0, ∑ kwk = 1. The
one-dimensional W2-barycenter has quantile function
Q¯µ(u) =
K∑
k=1
wkQk(u), u∈ (0, 1).
Consequently, a barycentric benchmark averages tariff distributions by ranks, not by policy
identities.
Thus a barycentric premium benchmark can be constructed by averaging sorted premium
books. This is different from averaging premiums policy by policy. It averages positions in the
distribution rather than identities.
Example 9.5 (Benchmarking several models). Suppose three pricing models produce premium
distributions µ1,µ2,µ3. Their one-dimensional Wasserstein barycenter has quantile function
Q¯µ(u) = 1 3(Q1(u) +Q2(u) +Q3(u)).
This benchmark may be used to identify which model produces the most extreme premium
distribution at each quantile level.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 154/416

9.17 Discrete implementation
9.17 Discrete implementation
For a finite portfolio i = 1,...,n, let wi be exposure weights and πi the premium. The empirical
premium distribution is
ˆµπ =
n∑
i=1
wiδπi.
If the portfolio is grouped into rating cells k = 1,...,K, with cell weights ak and cell premiums
πk, then
ˆµπ =
K∑
k=1
akδπk.
Comparing two tariffs with old cell weightsa = (a1,...,aK), new cell weightsb = (b1,...,bM),
old premiums π(0) k and new premiums π(1) ℓ leads to the discrete OT problem
min
Γ∈U(a,b)
K∑
k=1
M∑
ℓ=1
|π(0) k −π(1) ℓ |pΓkℓ.
The optimal plan Γ matches old premium cells to new premium cells.
Example 9.6 (Tariff-cell transition table). An insurer can compute a transition matrix between
old and new premium deciles. The empirical transition matrix based on policy identities shows
where policyholders move. The optimal transport matrix between decile distributions shows the
minimal movement needed to transform the old decile distribution into the new one. Comparing
the two matrices reveals whether the tariff revision is mostly a smooth distributional shift or a
large reshuffling of individual policyholders.
9.18 A mini-lab in R
The following code simulates a portfolio, constructs a pooled premium, a coarse class tariff, a
technical individualized tariff and a shrinkage tariff. It then compares their premium distributions
using one-dimensional Wasserstein distances.
set.seed(123)
n <- 5000
age <- runif(n, 18, 80)
veh <- rbinom(n, 1, 0.35)
terr <- sample(1:5, n, replace = TRUE)
# Synthetic conditional pure premium
eta <- 6.5 + 0.015 * (age - 45) + 0.35 * veh + 0.08 * (terr - 3)
m <- exp(eta)
# Pooled tariff
pi _pool <- rep( mean(m), n)
# Coarse class tariff: territory only
pi _ class <- ave(m, terr, FUN = mean)
# Individualized tariff
pi _ind <- m
# Shrinkage tariff
lambda <- 0.5
pi _shrink <- mean(m) + lambda * (pi _ind - mean(m))
Page 155/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 156 ===
9 PRICING AND PREMIUM DISTRIBUTIONS
Wp1d <- function(x, y, p = 2, grid _size = 1000) {
# Equal-size shortcut; otherwise compare empirical quantiles on a
common grid.
if ( length(x) == length(y)) {
return( mean( abs( sort(x) - sort(y))^p)^(1 /p))
}
u <- ( seq _len( grid _size) - 0.5) / grid _size
qx <- quantile(x, probs = u, type = 1, names = FALSE)
qy <- quantile(y, probs = u, type = 1, names = FALSE)
mean( abs(qx - qy)^p)^(1 /p)
}
D _pool _ class <- Wp1d(pi _pool, pi _ class, p = 2)
D _pool _ind <- Wp1d(pi _pool, pi _ind, p = 2)
D _ class _ind <- Wp1d(pi _ class, pi _ind, p = 2)
D _pool _shr <- Wp1d(pi _pool, pi _shrink, p = 2)
c(pool _to _ class = D _pool _ class,
pool _to _individual = D _pool _ind,
class _to _individual = D _ class _ind,
pool _to _shrinkage = D _pool _shr)
# Check the variance identity
sqrt( var(pi _ind) * (n - 1) / n)
Wp1d(pi _pool, pi _ind, p = 2)
The last two quantities differ only by the convention for empirical variance. The Wasserstein
distance to the pooled empirical distribution is the empirical standard deviation around the
mean.
9.19 Pricing governance dashboard
A practical pricing governance dashboard could report several complementary quantities:
(i) average premium and total premium income;
(ii) D1 and D2 from the pooled benchmark;
(iii) W1 and W2 between old and new premium distributions;
(iv) policy-level average absolute and squared changes;
(v) the reallocation gap Gp;
(vi) premium changes by quantile level;
(vii) affordability distances and tail affordability risk measures.
None of these numbers is sufficient alone. Together, they separate different notions: premium
income, dispersion, distributional movement, individual disruption and affordability.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 156/416

9.20 Summary
Further reading
The convex-analytic and optimization viewpoint behind these comparisons is close in spirit
to the general convex optimization framework in Galichon 2016; Peyré and Cuturi 2019.
For actuarial pricing, credibility and GLM-based tariff construction, see Bühlmann 1967;
Bühlmann and Gisler 2005; Ohlsson and Johansson 2010; Wüthrich and Merz 2023. For
adverse selection and insurance-market segmentation, see Rothschild and Stiglitz 1976;
Chiappori et al. 2006. For micro-insurance and affordability, see Churchill 2006; Churchill
and Matul 2012; Biener and Eling 2012. Dependence and actuarial ordering ideas are
treated in Denuit et al. 2005a.
9.20 Summary
This lecture developed a Wasserstein view of pricing and premium distributions. The main
messages are:
(i) A tariff induces a premium distribution by push-forward of the portfolio distribution.
(ii) A constant benchmark corresponds to a Dirac premium distribution; a segmented tariff
corresponds to a more dispersed premium distribution.
(iii) The squared W2 distance to the pooled mean is the variance of premiums.
(iv) Increasing pricing information increases the variance of conditional pure premiums.
(v) Shrinkage paths provide interpretable routes between a constant benchmark and a technical
tariff.
(vi) A tariff revision has both a policy-level displacement and a distributional OT displacement.
(vii) The gap between these quantities reveals reshuffling beyond marginal distributional change.
(viii) Premium affordability can also be studied as a distributional transport problem.

=== page 159 ===
10 Portfolio Drift, Calibration and Domain Adaptation
Learning objectives
The previous lectures described actuarial models as push-forward maps: a function transforms a
portfolio distribution into a distribution of outputs. This lecture studies what happens when the
portfolio itself changes. An actuarial model is rarely used forever on the same population on
which it was trained. The distribution of policyholders changes, the mix of regions and coverages
changes, inflation affects claim severities, climate modifies exposure patterns, and underwriting
or regulatory decisions may alter the book. Optimal transport provides a natural way to compare
the old and new portfolios, to diagnose model drift, and to design calibration or adaptation
procedures.
After this lecture, the reader should be able to:
(i) distinguish covariate shift, label shift, concept drift and portfolio drift in an actuarial
setting;
(ii) represent training and target portfolios as probability measures on covariates, losses, scores
or residuals;
(iii) use Wasserstein distances to monitor drift in covariates, predicted losses, scores and
residuals;
(iv) derive simple stability bounds for expected losses and risk scores under distributional
perturbations;
(v) interpret transport plans as soft matchings between portfolios;
(vi) use a transport plan to construct target-calibrated portfolio weights;
(vii) understand barycentric transport maps and their use in portfolio transfer;
(viii) separate model adaptation from distributional validation;
(ix) design actuarial diagnostics based on Wasserstein distances, transport maps and residual
distributions.
Actuarial takeaway
A pricing or reserving model is not only a function. It is a function used on a portfolio.
When the portfolio changes, the law of the model output changes even if the formula
does not. Optimal transport gives a geometry for monitoring this change and for deciding
whether recalibration is enough or whether the model must be rebuilt.
This lecture uses the push-forward notation of Definition 2.1, the coupling notation of
Definition 2.2, the Wasserstein distance of Definition 5.2, and the empirical viewpoint of Lecture 7.
It also prepares the reserving cash-flow lab in Appendix ?? and the climate-prevention discussion
in Lecture 11. The domain-adaptation perspective is close to the statistical learning literature
on covariate shift, sample selection bias and dataset shift; see, for instance, Shimodaira 2000;
Quiñonero-Candela et al. 2009; Sugiyama et al. 2012; Pan and Yang 2010; Ben-David et al. 2010.
For optimal transport approaches to domain adaptation, see Courty et al. 2014; Courty et al.
2017; Redko et al. 2017; Flamary et al. 2021. For concept drift and monitoring, see Gama et al.
2014; Webb et al. 2016.
Page 159/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 160 ===
10 PORTFOLIO DRIFT, CALIBRATION AND DOMAIN ADAPTATION
10.1 The covariate-shift problem
Let (X,L) denote covariates and future loss. A model is usually estimated on a source or training
portfolio,
Ps = Laws(X,L),
and deployed on a target portfolio,
Pt = Lawt(X,L).
The notation may refer to two time periods, two countries, two distribution channels, two regions,
or two underwriting books. The most common actuarial examples are:
• a motor pricing model trained on last year’s policies and used on this year’s renewals;
• a homeowner model transferred from one province to another;
• a cyber model built on large firms and used on small firms;
• a climate-risk module estimated on historical exposure and used under a future scenario;
• a reserving model trained on mature accident years and used on more recent accident years.
A useful first decomposition, written formally in terms of conditional laws, is
Ps(X,L) =Ps(L|X)Ps(X), Pt(X,L) =Pt(L|X)Pt(X).
The simplest form of domain adaptation assumes that
Ps(L|X) =Pt(L|X), Ps(X)̸=Pt(X).
This is usually called covariate shift. It says that the actuarial relationship between risk
characteristics and losses is stable, but the mix of policyholders has changed. For instance, a
model may remain valid at the individual level but be applied to a portfolio containing more
young drivers, more urban risks, or more high insured values.
A more difficult situation is concept drift:
Ps(L|X)̸=Pt(L|X).
This may occur when repair costs inflate, when claim behavior changes, when climate changes
the distribution of hazards conditional on exposure, or when legal inflation modifies liability
losses. Transport methods can diagnose such changes, but cannot by themselves guarantee that
a model trained under Ps remains valid under Pt.
Definition 10.1 (Source and target portfolios). Let X be the covariate space. A source portfolio
is a probability measure µ∈P(X ), and a target portfolio is a probability measure ν∈P(X ). If
losses are included, the source and target joint laws are probability measures onX× R+.
Definition 10.2 (Covariate shift, label shift and concept drift). Let Ps and Pt denote source
and target joint laws of (X,L). There is covariate shift from Ps to Pt if
Ps(X)̸=Pt(X) but Ps(L|X) =Pt(L|X).
There is label shift if the marginal law of the response changes while the conditional law of
covariates given the response is stable, for example
Ps(L)̸=Pt(L) but Ps(X|L) =Pt(X|L),
when this conditional law is meaningful; see Lipton et al. 2018 for statistical treatments of label
shift. There is concept drift if the actuarial mechanism changes, that is, if the conditional law
P (L|X) changes.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 160/416

=== page 161 ===
10.2 From portfolio drift to output drift
Definition 10.3 (Portfolio drift). A portfolio drift is a change in the law of a chosen actuarial
representation Z: covariates, scores, losses, residuals, cash-flow vectors or hazard variables.
If Zs ∼ µZ and Zt ∼ νZ, a drift diagnostic is any functional of the pair (µZ,νZ), such as a
Wasserstein distance, a sliced Wasserstein distance, a tail quantile difference, or a residual-
distribution comparison.
Remark 10.1 (Drift is scale-dependent). Whether a shift is large or small depends on the
representation of X. A change in a standardized log-income variable is not comparable to a
change in raw income. Before computing a Wasserstein distance, one must choose a meaningful
metric on the covariate space. In actuarial applications, this choice is part of the modelling
problem.
10.2 From portfolio drift to output drift
Let f : X→ R be a fitted actuarial model: a predicted frequency, a predicted severity, a risk
score, a cancellation probability, or a reserve factor. Even if f is unchanged, the distribution of
f(X) changes when Law(X) changes.
Under the source portfolio,
µf =f#µ,
where µ = Laws(X). Under the target portfolio,
νf =f#ν,
where ν = Lawt(X). Thus we can monitor drift at several levels:
covariate drift: Wp(µ,ν),
score drift: Wp(f#µ,f#ν),
loss drift: Wp(Laws(L), Lawt(L)),
residual drift: Wp(Laws(L−f(X)), Lawt(L−f(X))).
The first quantity asks whether the population has changed. The second asks whether the
model sees the new population as different. The remaining quantities ask whether claim behavior
and errors have changed.
Example 10.1 (A stable model with unstable outputs). Suppose f(x) = x, and the source
covariate has distribution µ = N(0, 1), while the target covariate has distribution ν = N(1, 1).
The model formula is unchanged, but
f#µ =N(0, 1), f#ν =N(1, 1).
Thus the score distribution moves by one unit in W2. This means that the book has drifted
toward higher predicted risk, even if the model has not changed.
Example 10.2 (A drift invisible to a model). Let X = (X1,X2), and suppose the fitted score
depends only on X1:
f(X) = exp(β0 +β1X1).
If the portfolio drifts only in X2, then Wp(Laws(X), Lawt(X)) may be large while
Wp(Laws(f(X)), Lawt(f(X))) = 0.
This does not mean the drift is harmless. If X2 is predictive of losses but absent from the model,
residual drift may reveal a problem.
Page 161/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 162 ===
10 PORTFOLIO DRIFT, CALIBRATION AND DOMAIN ADAPTATION
10.3 Wasserstein diagnostics for drift
Let (X,d) be a metric space, and let µ,ν∈Pp(X ). The Wasserstein distance
Wp p(µ,ν) = inf γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
X×X
d(x,y)pγ( dx, dy)
measures the minimal average cost of moving the source portfolio into the target portfolio. If
X⊆ Rd, one often starts with
d(x,y) =∥S(x−y)∥2,
where S standardizes or weights covariates.
Definition 10.4 (Actuarial drift index). Let Zs ∼µZ andZt ∼νZ denote a chosen representation
of the source and target books: covariates, scores, losses or residuals. For p≥ 1, the order-p
Wasserstein drift index is
Dp(Z;s,t) = Wp(µZ,νZ).
When Z is one-dimensional, this becomes
Dp p(Z;s,t) =
∫ 1
0
|Qs(u)−Qt(u)|p du.
The one-dimensional formula is particularly useful in actuarial diagnostics, because many
outputs are scalar: predicted claim frequency, expected severity, loss ratio, reserve residual, or
risk score. The quantile representation also gives interpretable plots: the function
u↦→Qt(u)−Qs(u)
shows where the shift occurs in the distribution.
Proposition 10.1 (Lipschitz propagation of drift). Let f : (X,dX )→ (Y,dY) be K-Lipschitz:
dY(f(x),f(y))≤KdX (x,y).
Then, for every p≥ 1,
Wp(f#µ,f#ν)≤KWp(µ,ν).
Proof. Let γ∈ Π(µ,ν). The push-forward (f,f)#γ is a coupling of f#µ and f#ν. Therefore
Wp p(f#µ,f#ν)≤
∫
dY(f(x),f(y))pγ( dx, dy)≤Kp
∫
dX (x,y)pγ( dx, dy).
Taking the infimum over γ gives the result.
Actuarial takeaway
A large covariate drift may have little output impact if the model is insensitive to the
drifting variables. Conversely, a moderate covariate drift in a highly leveraged variable
may create a large score or residual drift. The relevant diagnostic is often a combination
of covariate drift and output drift.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 162/416

=== page 163 ===
10.4 Stability of expected losses under covariate shift
10.4 Stability of expected losses under covariate shift
Assume covariate shift:
Ps(L|X) =Pt(L|X).
Let
m(x) = E[L|X =x]
be the stable conditional mean. Then the difference between source and target expected losses is
Et[L]− Es[L] =
∫
m(x)ν( dx)−
∫
m(x)µ( dx).
This difference can be controlled by Wasserstein distance if m is regular.
Proposition 10.2 (Expected-loss stability under covariate shift). Let (X,d) be a metric space.
Suppose that covariate shift holds and that m(x) = E[L|X =x] is K-Lipschitz. Then
|Et[L]− Es[L]|≤KW1(µ,ν).
More generally, since W1 ≤ Wp on probability spaces with finite p-moments,
|Et[L]− Es[L]|≤KWp(µ,ν).
Proof. By Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality for W1,
⏐⏐⏐⏐ ∫ m dν− ∫ m dµ ⏐⏐⏐⏐≤KW1(µ,ν).
Under covariate shift, the two integrals are exactly the target and source expected losses.
Remark 10.2 (What the bound does not say). The bound does not validate the conditional model.
It says that if the conditional mean is stable and Lipschitz, then portfolio-level expected loss
cannot change faster than the covariate distribution moves in W1. If inflation, legal environment
or claim behavior changes m, the bound no longer captures the full problem.
A similar bound holds for any Lipschitz functional of the covariates. If h : X → R is
Kh-Lipschitz, then
|Et[h(X)]− Es[h(X)]|≤KhW1(µ,ν).
This is useful for quick impact assessments: a portfolio can drift in many variables, but if the
functional is smooth and the transport distance is small, its mean impact is controlled.
10.5 Transport plans as portfolio matchings
In a finite portfolio, the source book is
ˆµ =
n∑

∑

and the target book is
ˆν =
m∑

bjδyj, bj ≥ 0,
∑

bj = 1.
A transport plan is a nonnegative matrix Γ = (Γij) satisfying
m∑
j=1
Γij =ai,
n∑
i=1
Γij =bj.

Portfolio drift and barycentric projection
Figure 9: Portfolio drift and barycentric projection. The source portfolio is matched to the

The transport cost is

Actuarially, Γij is a soft matching between source policy i and target policy j. If Γij is large, the

Definition 10.5 (Transport matching). A transport matching between two empirical portfolios
is any feasible transport matrix Γ ∈ Rn×m + satisfying the marginal constraints above. An optimal
transport matching minimizes the total cost
∑
i,j
c(xi,yj)Γij.
This matching can be used in three ways:
(i) to explain which source profiles are closest to which target profiles;
(ii) to transfer predictions or residuals from source to target;
(iii) to build calibrated source weights that mimic the target book.
Example 10.3 (Matching an old motor book to a new one). Let xi be standardized rating
variables for last year’s motor policies and yj the same variables for this year’s renewals. An
optimal transport plan identifies which old policies represent the new portfolio. If young urban
drivers increased in the target book, mass is transported toward source profiles with similar
characteristics. The unmatched or expensive movements identify regions of the covariate space
where the old model has little support.

10.6 Transport weights for calibration
10.6 Transport weights for calibration
Classical covariate-shift correction uses importance weights
w(x) = dν dµ(x),
when ν≪µ. These weights transform source expectations into target expectations and are the
standard starting point in covariate-shift correction Shimodaira 2000; Sugiyama et al. 2012:
∫
h(x)ν( dx) =
∫
h(x)w(x)µ( dx).
In high-dimensional or mixed actuarial data, density ratios may be hard to estimate. Transport
provides an alternative: instead of estimating a pointwise ratio, one estimates a matching between
source and target masses, as in OT-based domain adaptation Courty et al. 2014; Courty et al.
2017; Redko et al. 2017.
Given an empirical transport plan Γ, define the target-calibrated version of a source function
h(xi) by
ˆEΓ t [h] =
m∑
j=1
n∑
i=1
Γijh(xi).
Since ∑ j Γij =ai, this is also
ˆEΓ t [h] =
n∑
i=1
aih(xi),
which does not change the total source average if h is kept only at the source points. To obtain
target-specific predictions, one instead uses conditional transport weights for each target point:
ˆhΓ(yj) = 1 bj n∑ i=1 Γijh(xi), bj > 0.
This is a local transport average of source values around the target point yj. The target average
is then m∑
j=1
bjˆhΓ(yj) =
n∑
i=1
aih(xi).
Thus the global mean is preserved, but the values are redistributed over the target book.
Definition 10.6 (Transport-transfer predictor). Let h : {x1,...,xn}→ R be a source quantity,
and let Γ be a coupling between source weights ai and target weights bj. The transport-transfer
predictor at target pointyj is
hΓ(yj) = 1 bj n∑ i=1 Γijh(xi).
Proposition 10.3 (Mean preservation by transport transfer). If bj > 0 for all j, then
m∑
j=1
bjhΓ(yj) =
n∑
i=1
aih(xi).
Proof. By definition,
m∑
j=1
bjhΓ(yj) =
m∑
j=1
n∑

Γijh(xi) =
n∑
i=1


m∑
j=1
Γij

h(xi) =
n∑
i=1
aih(xi).

Proposition 10.4 (Local transfer error for Lipschitz quantities). Assume that h : X→ R is
K-Lipschitz and that the same function can be evaluated on source and target covariates. Then
⏐⏐⏐⏐⏐⏐ m∑ j=1 bjh(yj)− m∑ j=1 bjhΓ(yj) ⏐⏐⏐⏐⏐⏐ ≤K n∑ i=1 m∑ j=1 Γijd(xi,yj).
For a p-cost, Hölder’s inequality gives the weaker but scale-compatible bound
⏐⏐⏐⏐⏐⏐ m∑ j=1 bjh(yj)− m∑ j=1 bjhΓ(yj) ⏐⏐⏐⏐⏐⏐ ≤K  ∑ i,j Γijd(xi,yj)p   1/p .
Proof. Using the marginal constraints and the definition of hΓ,
∑
j
bjh(yj)−
∑
j
bjhΓ(yj) =
∑
i,j
Γij{h(yj)−h(xi)}.
Taking absolute values and using the Lipschitz property gives the first inequality. The second
follows from Hölder’s inequality because ∑ i,j Γij = 1.
Remark 10.3 (Whenmeanpreservationisundesirable). Ifthetargetbookisgenuinelyriskier, mean
preservation may understate target losses. The transport-transfer predictor is a redistribution of
source information, not a substitute for modelling the conditional mean. It is most useful for
comparing like-for-like portfolios or for diagnostic transfer. For final target prediction, it should
be combined with a conditional model.
10.7 Barycentric maps and transferred portfolios
A transport plan can be summarized by a barycentric map. For each source pointxi, define
TΓ(xi) = 1 ai m∑ j=1 Γijyj, ai > 0.
This maps each source point to the weighted average of target points receiving mass from it.
Conversely, the reverse barycentric map is
SΓ(yj) = 1 bj n∑ i=1 Γijxi.
Definition 10.7 (Empirical barycentric projection of a transport plan). Let Γ be a transport
plan between empirical measures ˆµ and ˆν. The source-to-target barycentric projection is the map
TΓ(xi) = 1 ai ∑ j Γijyj.
The target-to-source barycentric projection is
SΓ(yj) = 1 bj ∑ i Γijxi.
Barycentric projections are useful when the transport plan splits mass. They create a
deterministic approximation to a probabilistic matching. In actuarial work, they can be used to:
• visualize how the old book moves toward the new one;
• identify which variables account for the largest shift;
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 166/416
