10.8 Distributional validation
• transfer source residuals to target profiles;
• construct synthetic target books under a changed portfolio mix;
• compare real target exposure to synthetic climate or economic scenarios.
Example10.4 (Barycentricshiftinahouseholdportfolio). Suppose X = (loginsured value,hazard score).
A target book has higher insured values and higher hazard scores. The barycentric displacement
TΓ(xi)−xi
can be averaged componentwise to quantify whether the shift is mainly due to property values,
hazard exposure, or their interaction.
10.8 Distributional validation
A model can have a good average error and still fail distributionally. In actuarial science, the tails
matter. A risk model may be well calibrated on average but underpredict high-risk policyholders.
A reserving model may be unbiased at the aggregate level but produce residuals with a changing
tail. A climate model may match expected annual loss while missing the upper tail of catastrophe
loss.
Let R =L−f(X) be a residual or scaled residual. Distributional validation compares
Laws(R) and Lawt(R).
In one dimension, the diagnostic
W2 2(Laws(R), Lawt(R)) =
∫ 1
0
|Qs(u)−Qt(u)|2 du
can be decomposed visually through the quantile-difference curve.
For count data, one may work with randomized or deviance residuals; randomized quantile
residuals are a useful reference construction Dunn and Smyth 1996. For severity data, one may
work with log-residuals:
R = log(L + 1)− log(f(X) + 1).
For reserving, one may use cell-level Pearson residuals or accident-year residual summaries.
Definition 10.8 (Distributional calibration). A fitted model f is distributionally stable between
source and target if the residual laws are close:
Wp
(
Laws(R), Lawt(R)
)
is small relative to a chosen benchmark, where R is an appropriate residual or transformed
residual.
Remark 10.4 (Choice of residual). There is no universal residual. For Poisson frequency models,
Pearson or deviance residuals are natural. For Gamma severity models, log-ratios may be more
interpretable. For models producing positive fitted values, one may compare ratios L/f(X),
capped ratios, or randomized quantile residuals. The residual must be chosen before the
Wasserstein diagnostic is interpreted.
Page 167/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 168 ===
10 PORTFOLIO DRIFT, CALIBRATION AND DOMAIN ADAPTATION
10.9 A transport view of model recalibration
Suppose a fitted model produces scores S = f(X). The target score distribution may differ from
the source score distribution. Sometimes this is expected because the target portfolio is different.
Sometimes it indicates miscalibration. A recalibration map is a function
r : R→ R
so that r(S) has a desired target distribution.
In one dimension, the monotone transport map from µS to νS is
T (s) =Qν(Fµ(s)),
where Fµ is the source score distribution function and Qν is the target quantile function. Applying
T to source scores transforms the source score distribution into the target score distribution.
This is useful for post-processing, and is closely related to calibration and post-processing
ideas in predictive modelling Zadrozny and Elkan 2002; Guo et al. 2017, but it must be
interpreted carefully. If the target distribution is a benchmark, monotone recalibration can
enforce distributional agreement. If the target distribution reflects new risk, forcing equality to
an old distribution can hide genuine drift.
Proposition 10.5 (Monotone one-dimensional recalibration). Let µ and ν be probability measures
on R, and assume µ is continuous. Define
T (x) =Qν(Fµ(x)).
If X∼µ, then T (X)∼ν. Moreover, T is nondecreasing.
Proof. If µ is continuous, then Fµ(X) is uniform on (0, 1). Therefore Qν(Fµ(X)) has distribution
ν. Since both Fµ and Qν are nondecreasing, their composition is nondecreasing.
Remark 10.5 (Atoms and randomized ranks). If µ has atoms, Fµ(X) is not uniform. One can
either use the generalized inverse construction directly at the level of quantiles, or introduce
randomized ranks Fµ(X−) + U
(
Fµ(X) −Fµ(X−)
)
, where U∼ Unif(0, 1) is independent. This
is the same convention as in the rank–quantile discussion in Lecture ??.
10.10 Support, extrapolation and lack of overlap
Transport distances do not only give a number. An optimal plan identifies where support is
missing. If target points yj are far from all source points, then any transport plan must move
mass over a large distance. This is a warning sign: the model is extrapolating.
A simple diagnostic is the nearest-source distance
ds(yj) = min i d(xi,yj).
A transport-based version uses the reverse barycentric source representative
SΓ(yj) = 1 bj ∑ i Γijxi,
and the displacement
∆j =d(yj,SΓ(yj)).
Large values of ∆j indicate target profiles that are poorly represented in the source book.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 168/416

10.11 Scenario transport and climate adaptation
Definition 10.9 (Transport extrapolation score). Given a transport plan Γ, the transport
extrapolation score of target policy j is
ej =d
(
yj,SΓ(yj)
)
.
The portfolio-level extrapolation score may be summarized by


m∑
j=1
bjep j


1/p
or by upper quantiles of the distribution of ej.
Actuarial takeaway
A high Wasserstein distance is not only evidence that the portfolio changed. The optimal
plan tells where it changed, especially through Definition 10.9. In model governance, the
local transport displacement is often more useful than the scalar distance.
10.11 Scenario transport and climate adaptation
Climate applications often involve a baseline distribution and one or several scenario distributions.
Let H denote a hazard or exposure vector: flood depth, wildfire score, heat stress, wind speed, or
a combination of physical and insured-value variables. A baseline climate-exposure distribution
is µH, and a scenario distribution is νH.
A transport plan from µH to νH can be interpreted as a scenario displacement. If losses are
generated by a vulnerability function
L =g(H,V ),
where V denotes vulnerability or insured value, then the change in loss distribution can be
studied by pushing forward the transported exposures through g.
For example, if a prevention measure modifies the hazard distribution from νH to νprev H , one
can measure the avoided distributional shift by
Wp(µH,νH)− Wp(µH,νprev H ),
or, more directly, by comparing the induced loss laws
Wp
(
g#νH,g#νprev H ) .
Example 10.5 (Avoided drift). A coastal portfolio faces a future flood scenario νH. A prevention
investment changes the scenario distribution to νprev H . If the induced annual-loss laws are µfuture L
and µprev L , then
W1(µfuture L ,µprev L )
measures the average distributional loss displacement avoided by prevention. Tail-specific
diagnostics may use quantile differences or TVaR differences in addition to Wasserstein distance.
Actuarial takeaway
The quantities above compare baseline, scenario and prevention distributions. They do not
identify the loss avoided by a particular insured unit unless a causal or physical intervention
model justifies that interpretation.
Page 169/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 170 ===
10 PORTFOLIO DRIFT, CALIBRATION AND DOMAIN ADAPTATION
10.12 Domain adaptation in reserving
In reserving, drift appears across accident years, development years and calendar years. Let
Cij denote incremental paid or incurred claims for accident year i and development year j. A
reserving model may be trained on older cells and used on recent incomplete triangles. Covariates
include
Xij = (accident year,development year,calendar year,line,exposure,...).
The source distribution is the empirical distribution of observed mature cells. The target
distribution is the empirical distribution of cells to be predicted.
Transport diagnostics can ask:
• Are target cells close to observed cells in accident-development-calendar space?
• Does the distribution of fitted residuals change by calendar year?
• Are recent accident years a transport extrapolation from older years?
• Does inflation create a systematic displacement in severity residuals?
A simple target-cell extrapolation score is
eij =d
(
Xij,SΓ(Xij)
)
,
where SΓ(Xij) is the source representative transported to the target cell. Large eij suggests that
the reserve prediction relies on extrapolation rather than interpolation.
10.13 Algorithmic workflow
A practical Wasserstein drift audit can be organized as follows.
Step 1. Choose the representation. Decide whether to compare covariates, model scores, losses,
residuals, or several of them.
Step 2. Choose the metric. Standardize continuous variables, encode categorical variables, and
choose weights reflecting actuarial relevance.
Step 3. Compute scalar distances. Compute W1, W2, sliced Wasserstein or entropic OT
distances.
Step 4. Inspect local displacements. Use an optimal or regularized plan to identify where mass
moves.
Step 5. Compare output drift. Check whether covariate drift propagates to score and residual
drift.
Step 6. Decide the action. No action, monitoring, recalibration, reweighting, local model update,
or full refit.
Computational note
In high dimension, exact empirical Wp can be unstable and computationally expensive.
For monitoring, sliced Wasserstein distances, random projections, entropic regularization,
or low-dimensional score summaries are often more robust. Exact transport plans are most
useful for small or medium diagnostic samples.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 170/416

=== page 171 ===
10.14 Mini-lab: score drift and residual drift in R
10.14 Mini-lab: score drift and residual drift in R
The following pseudo-code illustrates a minimal workflow. It assumes a fitted model fit, a
source data frame source, a target data frame target, and a scalar response loss. The function
quantile_w2 computes a one-dimensional empirical W2 distance using quantiles.
emp _ q _left <- function(x, probs) {
x <- sort(x[ is.finite(x)])
n <- length(x)
if (n == 0) stop("empty␣sample")
x[ pmax(1, ceiling(probs * n))]
}
quantile _w2 <- function(x, y, grid = seq(0.001, 0.999, length.out = 999)
) {
qx <- emp _ q _left(x, grid)
qy <- emp _ q _left(y, grid)
sqrt( mean((qx - qy)^2))
}
source $score <- predict(fit, newdata = source, type = "response")
target $score <- predict(fit, newdata = target, type = "response")
D _score <- quantile _w2( source $score, target $score)
source $ resid <- log( source $loss + 1) - log( source $score + 1)
target $ resid <- log(target $loss + 1) - log(target $score + 1)
D _ resid <- quantile _w2( source $ resid, target $ resid)
list(score _drift = D _score, residual _drift = D _ resid)
A useful plot is the quantile-difference curve.
grid <- seq(0.01, 0.99, length.out = 99)
qs <- emp _ q _left( source $score, grid)
qt <- emp _ q _left(target $score, grid)
plot( grid, qt - qs, type = "l",
xlab = "Quantile␣level",
ylab = "Target␣score␣quantile␣-␣source␣score␣quantile")
abline(h = 0, lty = 2)
If the curve is positive only at high quantiles, the target book has a heavier high-score tail.
If it is approximately flat and positive, the target distribution is mostly shifted upward. If it
crosses zero, the drift changes the shape rather than only the level.
10.15 Governance interpretation
Transport diagnostics can be attached to model governance thresholds. For example, an insurer
may define:
• a green zone: score and residual drifts are below historical monitoring thresholds;
• an orange zone: score drift is high but residual drift is stable, suggesting portfolio mix
change but no immediate evidence of conditional failure;
• a red zone: residual drift or tail residual drift is high, suggesting model degradation;
• a black zone: target extrapolation scores are high, suggesting the model is used outside its
support.
Page 171/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 172 ===
10 PORTFOLIO DRIFT, CALIBRATION AND DOMAIN ADAPTATION
The thresholds should be calibrated on historical backtests. The absolute value of Wp is
not meaningful without a scale. A distance of 100 monetary units may be small for commercial
property losses and large for small personal-lines payments.
Further reading
For mathematical foundations of Wasserstein distances and empirical transport, see Lec-
tures 5 and 7, together with Villani 2008; Peyré and Cuturi 2019; Panaretos and Zemel 2020.
For domain adaptation and dataset shift, see Quiñonero-Candela et al. 2009; Sugiyama

see Courty et al. 2014; Courty et al. 2017; Redko et al. 2017. For concept drift, see Gama
et al. 2014; Webb et al. 2016. In actuarial applications, the relevant connection is not
the transport algorithm alone but the choice of representation: covariates, scores, losses,
residuals or cash-flow vectors.

11 Reserving, Cash-Flow Distributions and Climate Prevention
Learning objectives
The previous lectures used optimal transport to compare portfolios, to monitor distributional
drift, and to adapt actuarial models across domains. This lecture turns to two actuarial objects
where the same distributional point of view is especially natural: outstanding claims reserves and
climate-related loss distributions. In both cases, the actuary is not only interested in one number.
A best estimate is useful, but it hides timing, tail risk, dependence between future payments,
model uncertainty, and the effect of prevention or adaptation measures. Optimal transport
provides a language for comparing entire predictive distributions, interpolating between reserving
models, and describing how a specified prevention model moves mass in a loss distribution.
After this lecture, the reader should be able to:
(i) represent a reserve as a predictive distribution of future cash-flows rather than as a single
scalar;
(ii) compare reserving models by Wasserstein distances between predictive laws;
(iii) use quantile formulas to compare one-dimensional reserve distributions;
(iv) define barycenters of predictive reserving distributions and interpret them as consensus
forecasts;
(v) distinguish calendar-time inflation, accident-year heterogeneity and development uncertainty
in a transport framework;
(vi) use transport maps to describe the distributional effect of a prevention measure;
(vii) summarize prevention through changes in mean, quantiles, tail risk and Wasserstein
displacement;
(viii) understand why unbalanced transport is relevant when exposure mass changes;
(ix) design diagnostic plots and computational experiments for reserving and climate applica-
tions.
Actuarial takeaway
A reserve is often reported as a number, but it is produced by a distribution. Climate
prevention is often reported as an avoided expected loss, but a prevention model changes
an entire loss distribution. Optimal transport is useful because it keeps the distribution
visible.
This lecture uses the push-forward notation of Definition 2.1, the coupling notation of Defini-
tion 2.2, the Wasserstein distance of Definition 5.2, the barycenter construction of Definition 5.5,
and the empirical viewpoint of Lecture 7. It also prepares the reserving cash-flow lab in Ap-
pendix ?? and the later unbalanced-transport chapter, where changing exposure mass is treated
directly.
Classical and stochastic reserving references include Mack 1993; Renshaw and Verrall 1998;
England and Verrall 2002; W"uthrich and Merz 2008. The accounting language around future
cash flows and uncertainty connects naturally with IFRS 17; see IFRS Foundation 2017. For
climate risk, prevention, catastrophe risk financing and index insurance, useful complements are
Kunreuther and Michel-Kerjan 2009; Kousky 2019; Cummins and Mahul 2009; Barnett et al.
2008; Miranda 1991; Clarke 2016.
Page 173/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 174 ===
11 RESERVING, CASH-FLOW DISTRIBUTIONS AND CLIMATE PREVENTION
11.1 From best estimate reserves to predictive laws
Let R denote the total outstanding liability for a line of business. Classical reserving reports a
best estimate,
BEL = E[R|D],
where D denotes the observed claims development data. The best estimate is central for
accounting and solvency reporting, but it is only one functional of the predictive distribution
µR = Law(R|D).
The law µR contains information about uncertainty, skewness, tail risk and model sensitivity.
Two reserving models can have the same best estimate but very different tail distributions.
In many applications the future liability is not a single payment but a vector of future
cash-flows. Let
R = (R1,...,RT )∈ RT +,
where Rt is the future payment during development or calendar period t. A predictive reserving
model produces a probability measure
µR = Law(R|D) on RT +.
The total outstanding reserve is the push-forward of this distribution by the aggregation map
A(r) =A(r1,...,rT ) =
T∑
t=1
rt.
Thus
µR =A#µR.
This simple identity is useful. It says that the total reserve distribution is obtained by pushing
the cash-flow distribution through a deterministic map. It also makes clear that two models
can agree on µR but disagree on timing, liquidity, calendar-year capital needs or reinsurance
recoveries.
Definition 11.1 (Predictive reserving law). A predictive reserving law is a probability measure
µ∈P(RT +) describing the conditional distribution of future payments (R1,...,RT ) given the
observed claims data. The induced total-reserve law is A#µ, where A(r) = ∑ trt.
Example 11.1 (Same best estimate, different risk). Consider two predictive laws for a total
reserve R. Under model A,
RA = 100 + 10Z,
where Z is centered and moderately dispersed. Under model B,
RB = 100 +Y,
where Y is centered but has a heavy right tail. Both models may have E[RA] = E[RB] = 100,
but TVaR0.995(RB) can be much larger than TVaR0.995(RA). A best-estimate comparison would
miss this difference; a distributional comparison would not.
Remark 11.1 (Cash-flow risk is not only reserve risk). The scalar total R = ∑ tRt loses information
about timing. A model with early payments and a model with late payments may have the
same total reserve law but different liquidity consequences. Conversely, models may have similar
payment timing but different total tail risk. For this reason, it is often useful to compare both
µR and A#µR.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 174/416

=== page 175 ===
11.2 Triangles as empirical measures
Actuarial takeaway
Professional lens: IFRS 17 and distributional cash flows.
IFRS 17 shifts the accounting view of insurance contracts toward current estimates of
future cash flows, discounting, uncertainty and the emergence of insurance service results
over time. In these notes, this is naturally expressed through random cash-flow vectors
rather than through a single deterministic reserve.
Let
C = (C1,...,CT )
denote the future cash-flow vector of a group of contracts, including claims, expenses,
premiums, recoveries or benefits. A best estimate is a functional of Law(C), for example
BEL = E
[ T∑
t=1
vtCt
]
.
A risk adjustment, by contrast, depends on the uncertainty around this estimate. Optimal
transport provides a geometry for comparing two projected cash-flow laws:
Wp
(
Law(Cold), Law(Cnew) ) ,
or for comparing a baseline projection to a stressed projection.
This is useful when model changes, assumption updates or portfolio changes affect not
only the mean cash flow but also the timing, dependence and tail shape of the cash-flow
distribution.
11.2 Triangles as empirical measures
A run-off triangle can be viewed as an array of incremental payments
Ci,j, i = 1,...,I, j = 1,...,J,
where i is accident year and j is development year. The observed region is typically
i +j≤I + 1.
Classical methods impose structure on the conditional expectation of unobserved cells. For
example, a multiplicative development model may write
E[Ci,j] =αiβj,
possibly with an exposure offset or a calendar-year effect. GLM versions replace this by a link
function and a variance function.
The transport viewpoint starts by turning triangle information into a measure. There are
several choices, depending on the question.
A measure over cells. Let xi,j ∈ Rd encode the location and features of cell (i,j): accident
year, development year, calendar year, line of business, inflation index, exposure and possibly
covariates. Define
ˆµC = 1 M ∑ (i,j)∈O Ci,j δxi,j, M = ∑ (i,j)∈O Ci,j,
where O is the observed region. This measure describes where paid mass has appeared in the
triangle.
Page 175/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 176 ===
11 RESERVING, CASH-FLOW DISTRIBUTIONS AND CLIMATE PREVENTION
Definition 11.2 (Triangle payment measure). Let O be the observed part of a run-off triangle
and let xi,j encode the actuarial location of cell (i,j). The normalized triangle payment measure
is
ˆµC = 1∑
(i,j)∈OCi,j
∑
(i,j)∈O
Ci,j δxi,j.
It is a finite measure normalized to total mass one. If the total paid amount itself matters, the
unnormalized version is a finite measure and should be compared with unbalanced OT.
A measure over development patterns. For each accident year, define a normalized
development profile
pi =
(
Ci,1∑
j Ci,j
,..., Ci,J∑
j Ci,j
)
,
whenever the full row is observed or completed. The empirical law
ˆµp = 1 n ∑ i δpi
is a distribution over development patterns. A distance between rows can then measure changes
in reporting or settlement speed.
A measure over predictive samples. If a stochastic reserving model produces simulations
R(1),...,R(N) ∈ RT +,
then the predictive law is represented empirically as
ˆµR = 1 N N∑ k=1 δR(k).
This is the representation most directly connected to Wasserstein distances.
Actuarial takeaway
There is no unique “transport measure” associated with a triangle. The measure must be
chosen according to the actuarial question: movement of paid mass across cells, changes in
development patterns, or uncertainty of future cash-flows.
11.3 Comparing predictive reserve distributions
Suppose two reserving models produce predictive laws µ and ν on RT +. Examples include:
• chain-ladder bootstrap versus Bayesian over-dispersed Poisson;
• lognormal reserving versus Tweedie GLM reserving;
• model with calendar-year inflation versus model without calendar-year inflation;
• model trained on all historical years versus model trained on recent years only;
• baseline climate scenario versus stressed climate scenario.

11.3 Comparing predictive reserve distributions
A Wasserstein distance compares these laws by solving
Wp p(µ,ν) = inf π∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
∥r−s∥pπ( dr, ds).
Here r,s∈ RT + are future cash-flow vectors. The norm should reflect the actuarial meaning of
differences across payment years. The simplest choice is Euclidean:
∥r−s∥2 =
T∑
t=1
(rt −st)2.
A discounted choice is often more meaningful:
∥r−s∥2 v =
T∑
t=1
v2 t (rt −st)2,
where vt is a discount factor or an economic weight. A tail-oriented choice may put more weight
on late liability years or high-volatility payment periods.
Definition 11.3 (Cash-flow ground cost). A cash-flow ground cost is a function c : RT + × RT + →
[0,∞] that measures the actuarial discrepancy between two future payment vectors. Common
choices are
cp(r,s) =
T∑
t=1
|rt −st|p, cp,v(r,s) =
T∑
t=1
vp t|rt −st|p,
with economic weights vt. The choice of c determines whether the comparison emphasizes nominal
amount, discounted value, timing, liquidity or capital relevance.
Definition 11.4 (Discounted Wasserstein distance for cash-flows). Let v = (v1,...,vT ) ∈ RT +.
The discounted Wasserstein distance between predictive cash-flow laws µ,ν∈Pp(RT +) is
Wp p,v(µ,ν) = inf π∈Π(µ,ν)
∫ ( T∑
t=1
vp t|rt −st|p
)
π( dr, ds).
Proposition 11.1 (Aggregation is Lipschitz). Let A(r) = ∑T t=1 rt. If µ,ν∈Pp(RT +), then
Wp(A#µ,A#ν)≤T 1−1/pWp(µ,ν),
with the convention T 1−1/p = 1 for p = 1.
Proof. For anyr,s∈ RT +, Hölder’s inequality gives
|A(r)−A(s)| =
⏐⏐⏐⏐⏐ T∑ t=1 (rt −st) ⏐⏐⏐⏐⏐≤T 1−1/p∥r−s∥p.
Thus A is T 1−1/p-Lipschitz. The contraction property of Wasserstein distances under Lipschitz
maps gives the result.
Proposition 11.2 (Present-value stability). Let v = (v1,...,vT ) ∈ RT + and define the present-
value map
PVv(r) =
T∑
t=1
vtrt.
If µ,ν∈Pp(RT +) and the ground metric on RT + is the ℓp norm, then
Wp
(
(PVv)#µ, (PVv)#ν
)
≤∥v∥q Wp(µ,ν),
where q is the conjugate exponent of p. In particular, discounted best-estimate distributions are
stable under small Wasserstein perturbations of cash-flow laws.
Page 177/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 178 ===
11 RESERVING, CASH-FLOW DISTRIBUTIONS AND CLIMATE PREVENTION
Proof. For allr,s∈ RT +, Hölder’s inequality gives
| PVv(r)− PVv(s)|≤∥v∥q∥r−s∥p.
Thus PVv is ∥v∥q-Lipschitz. The conclusion follows from the contraction property of Wasserstein
distances under Lipschitz maps.
This proposition says that if two predictive cash-flow laws are close, then their induced total
reserve laws are close. Proposition 11.2 gives the analogous statement for discounted present
values. The converse is false: total reserves or present values can be close while the time pattern
of payments differs substantially.
11.4 One-dimensional reserve comparison
For scalar reserve distributions, the Wasserstein distance has a particularly transparent quantile
representation. If R∼µ and S∼ν, then
Wp p(µ,ν) =
∫ 1
0
|F−1 R (u)−F−1 S (u)|p du.
Here, as throughout the notes, F−1 denotes the lower generalized inverse. With atoms or tied
empirical values, the formula should be read with this convention or with an explicit randomized-
rank convention. This identity is useful for communicating model differences. It converts an
abstract optimal coupling into a quantile-by-quantile comparison.
Let qR(u) =Qµ(u) =F−1 R (u) and qS(u) =Qν(u) =F−1 S (u). The function
d(u) =qS(u)−qR(u)
shows where the model difference lies. If d(u) is nearly constant, then one model is essentially a
shifted version of the other. If d(u) is small near the center and large near u = 1, then the models
mostly differ in the tail. If d(u) changes sign, the models differ in shape, not merely in level.
Example 11.2 (Tail disagreement). Suppose two reserving models have the same median and
the same mean, but one has a heavier tail. Then qR(u) ≈qS(u) for central u, but |qR(u) −qS(u)|
becomes large near u = 1. The W2 distance captures this disagreement, but a tail-weighted
diagnostic such as
∫ 1
α
|qR(u)−qS(u)|2 du
may be more directly connected to solvency concerns.
Definition 11.5 (Tail Wasserstein discrepancy). For α∈ (0, 1), define the tail discrepancy
Wp p,α(µ,ν) = 1 1−α ∫ 1 α |Qµ(u)−Qν(u)|p du.
This is not a Wasserstein distance on the full distribution, but it is a useful diagnostic for tail
model uncertainty.
Remark 11.2 (Quantiles are actuarially legible). A quantile plot is often easier to explain than
a transport plan. For scalar reserves, plotting qR(u), qS(u), and qS(u) −qR(u) is one of the
simplest ways to diagnose whether two predictive models disagree in level, dispersion or tail.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 178/416

11.5 Barycenters of reserving models
11.5 Barycenters of reserving models
Reserving often involves several plausible models rather than one objectively correct model. Let
µ1,...,µK
be predictive laws produced by K reserving methods, expert assumptions, inflation scenarios or
climate scenarios. A Wasserstein barycenter is a distribution ¯µ solving
¯µ∈ arg min
η∈P2(RT +)
K∑
k=1
ωkW2 2(η,µk),
where ωk ≥ 0 and ∑ kωk = 1.
Definition 11.6 (Predictive reserve barycenter). A predictive reserve barycenter is a Wasserstein
barycenter of several predictive reserving laws. It represents a consensus distribution, not an
average of best estimates.
In one dimension the barycenter is especially simple. If qk(u) = F−1 k (u), then the W2-
barycenter has quantile function
¯q(u) =
K∑
k=1
ωkqk(u).
Therefore the barycenter preserves the quantile structure of the models. It is not the distribution
of a mixture that first selects a model and then simulates from it. A mixture has quantile function
that is generally not the weighted average of quantile functions.
Example 11.3 (Mixture versus barycenter). Let µ1 and µ2 be two reserve distributions. The
mixture
µmix = 1 2µ1 + 1 2µ2
represents model uncertainty: one of the two models is true, but we do not know which. The
barycenter represents a geometric compromise between the two predictive laws. In scalar problems,
its quantile is the average of the two quantiles. The mixture can be more dispersed than either
model, while the barycenter interpolates between corresponding quantile levels.
Actuarial takeaway
Averaging best estimates gives one number. Mixing predictive distributions gives model
uncertainty. Taking a Wasserstein barycenter gives a consensus predictive distribution.
These are three different operations and should not be confused.
11.6 Model uncertainty and reserve capital
A predictive law can be summarized by risk measures. For a scalar reserve R, one may compute
E[R], VaRα(R), TVaRα(R),
or a cost-of-capital margin based on a future solvency capital requirement. If several predictive
models are available, a distributional approach can separate three effects:
(a) variation in best estimates Eµk[R];
(b) variation in tail risk VaRα(µk), TVaRα(µk);
(c) geometric disagreement between predictive laws W2(µk,µℓ).
Page 179/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

A model-risk diagnostic can be built from pairwise distances
Dkℓ = W2(µk,µℓ).
Large values indicate that models produce substantially different predictive laws. A model may
have a best estimate close to the consensus while still being far in Wasserstein distance because
it has a different tail or timing structure.
Definition 11.7 (Wasserstein model dispersion). Given predictive laws µ1,...,µK and weights
ωk, define the Wasserstein model dispersion around a barycenter ¯µ by
DW =
K∑
k=1
ωkW2 2(µk, ¯µ).
Remark 11.3 (A distributional analogue of variance). The quantity DW plays the role of a
variance of predictive distributions around their barycenter. It is not a variance of losses under
one model. It is a measure of disagreement across models.
11.7 Transporting cash-flow scenarios
Suppose µ is a baseline predictive cash-flow law and ν is a stressed predictive law. An optimal
coupling π∈ Π(µ,ν) pairs baseline scenarios r with stressed scenarios s. If π is induced by a
map T, so that ν =T #µ, then
s =T (r)
may be interpreted as the stressed version of the baseline cash-flow scenarior.
In practice, empirical predictive laws are finite samples:
ˆµ = 1 N N∑ i=1 δri, ˆν = 1 M M∑ j=1 δsj.
A discrete transport plan πij is a soft matching between baseline scenarios and stressed scenarios.
The barycentric projection
ˆT (ri) =
∑M
j=1 πijsj
∑M
j=1 πij
gives a transported stressed scenario associated with baseline scenariori.
This can be used diagnostically. For each baseline scenariori, the displacement
∆i = ˆT (ri)−ri
indicates how stress affects payment timing. If ∆i is concentrated in early development years,
the stress accelerates payments. If it is concentrated in late years, the stress increases long-tail
uncertainty.
Computational note
In finite samples, exact optimal transport can be unstable when N and M are large or
when many scenarios are close. Entropic regularization gives smoother transport plans
and more stable barycentric projections. For scenario diagnostics, this smoothing is often
a feature rather than a defect.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 180/416

11.8 Calendar-year inflation as a transport effect
11.8 Calendar-year inflation as a transport effect
Inflation is one of the main reasons why reserve distributions move over time. Let Rt be a future
payment in calendar year t, and suppose an inflation scenario multiplies payments by factors
at > 0. The inflated cash-flow vector is
Ta(r) =Ta(r1,...,rT ) = (a1r1,...,aTrT ).
The stressed predictive law is
µa =Ta#µ.
If all factors are equal to a, then scalar total reserve satisfies
Ra =aR,
and the Wasserstein distance between Law(R) and Law(aR) can be expressed through quantiles:
Wp p(Law(R), Law(aR)) =
∫ 1
0
|qR(u)−aqR(u)|p du =|a− 1|pE[|R|p].
For nonconstant inflation by payment year, the total reserve is
A(Ta(r)) =
T∑
t=1
atrt,
and timing matters. A long-tailed line of business is more exposed to late inflation factors than
a short-tailed line, even if both have the same undiscounted best estimate.
Example 11.4 (Inflation and payment timing). Consider two portfolios with the same total
reserve R = 100. Portfolio A pays everything next year. Portfolio B pays everything in ten years.
If the annual inflation shock affects only late years, then A and B have the same current best
estimate but different stressed distributions. The cash-flow law detects this; the scalar current
reserve does not.
11.9 Climate loss distributions
Climate risk is naturally distributional. Let L denote annual aggregate loss for a portfolio exposed
to a climate hazard. A baseline climate or exposure scenario produces a loss law
µ0 = Law(L0),
whereas a future scenario produces
µ1 = Law(L1).
Classical summaries include
∆E[L] = E[L1]− E[L0], ∆VaRα = VaRα(L1)− VaRα(L0),
∆TVaRα = TVaRα(L1)− TVaRα(L0).
These summaries are important, but they do not describe how the whole distribution changes.
Wasserstein distance provides a complementary measure:
Wp(µ0,µ1).
In one dimension, the quantile displacement
d(u) =Qµ1(u)−Qµ0(u)
is especially informative. It reveals whether climate change shifts the whole distribution, increases
moderate losses, or mainly thickens the tail.
Page 181/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 182 ===
11 RESERVING, CASH-FLOW DISTRIBUTIONS AND CLIMATE PREVENTION
Definition 11.8 (Climate distributional shift). Let µ0 be a baseline loss law and µ1 a scenario
loss law. The climate distributional shift at order p is Wp(µ0,µ1), possibly supplemented by the
quantile displacement function Qµ1 −Qµ0.
Actuarial takeaway
The same change in expected annual loss can correspond to very different changes in
insurability. A small increase in mean with a large increase in tail risk may matter more for
capital and reinsurance than a larger increase in mean concentrated in attritional losses.
11.10 Prevention as a map between loss distributions
A prevention or adaptation measure changes the loss distribution. Examples include:
• flood barriers reducing the frequency and severity of inundation losses;
• roof strengthening reducing wind losses;
• wildfire defensible space reducing total-loss probability;
• cyber hygiene reducing ransomware frequency and recovery cost;
• road safety interventions reducing bodily injury severity.
Let µbefore and µafter denote loss distributions before and after the measure. A deterministic
prevention model may be written as
Lafter =T (Lbefore),
so that
µafter =T #µbefore.
The map T may reduce large losses more than small losses. For example,
T (x) =x−δ(x),
where δ(x) ≥ 0 is increasing in x, represents a prevention measure whose avoided loss is larger in
severe events.
In many cases the prevention effect is not deterministic. Then the measure is transformed by
a Markov kernel K:
µafter(B) =
∫
K(x,B)µbefore( dx).
The corresponding coupling
π( dx, dy) =K(x, dy)µbefore( dx)
pairs pre-intervention losses with post-intervention losses. It need not be the optimal coupling.
It supports a causal or physical interpretation only when the kernel is derived from a credible
intervention model, experimental design or structural assumption.
Actuarial takeaway
A transport map compares two distributions. It does not by itself identify avoided losses
for the same event or policyholder. Causal avoided-loss claims require a baseline, an
intervention model and evidence linking the intervention to the observed or projected shift.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 182/416

=== page 183 ===
11.11 Measuring prevention
Definition 11.9 (Avoided-loss map). A prevention map is a measurable map T : R+ → R+ such
that T (x)≤x. The avoided-loss function is
a(x) =x−T (x).
The post-prevention loss law is T #µ, where µ is the pre-prevention law.
Proposition 11.3 (Prevention and expected avoided loss). If T (x)≤x and L∼µ, then
E[L]− E[T (L)] = E[a(L)].
Moreover, if T is nondecreasing, then
VaRα(T (L)) =T (VaRα(L))
for all levels α at which the lower quantile convention and the monotone transformation are
compatible, in particular at continuity levels of the distribution of L.
Proof. The identity for expectations follows from linearity. If T is nondecreasing, the quantile
of the push-forward distribution is obtained by applying T to the quantile of the original
distribution at continuity levels, with the usual generalized-inverse convention. This gives the
stated relation.
The condition T (x) ≤x is natural for a loss-reduction intervention, but it is not automatic in
real data. A measure may reduce severe claims but increase small reported claims, for example
because more losses are detected or because deductibles change. In such cases the prevention
map may cross the identity.
11.11 Measuring prevention
A prevention evaluation should not rely on a single metric. The following quantities capture
complementary aspects:
∆mean = E[Lbefore]− E[Lafter],
∆VaR,α = VaRα(Lbefore)− VaRα(Lafter),
∆TVaR,α = TVaRα(Lbefore)− TVaRα(Lafter),
∆W = W2(µbefore,µafter).
The first three are reductions in actuarial risk functionals. The last one measures the size of
the distributional movement. A large ∆W does not necessarily imply a large reduction in risk:
the distribution may move in shape without much mean reduction. Conversely, a large mean
reduction concentrated in a narrow part of the distribution may produce a moderate Wasserstein
distance.
Definition 11.10 (Prevention profile). For scalar loss distributions, the prevention profile is the
quantile-level function
A(u) =Qbefore(u)−Qafter(u), u∈ (0, 1).
Positive values indicate avoided loss at quantile level u. The profile is tail-oriented if A(u) is
increasing near u = 1.
Example 11.5 (Two prevention measures with same mean effect). Suppose two interventions
reduce expected annual loss by the same amount. Measure A reduces many small claims slightly.
Measure B leaves most small claims unchanged but reduces extreme losses. They may have the
same ∆mean, but Measure B may have much larger ∆TVaR,α and a prevention profile concentrated
near u = 1. From a solvency or reinsurance perspective, the two measures are not equivalent.
Page 183/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 184 ===
11 RESERVING, CASH-FLOW DISTRIBUTIONS AND CLIMATE PREVENTION
11.12 Counterfactual prevention and attribution
A prevention claim is always counterfactual: it compares observed or projected losses with losses
that would have occurred without the intervention. Let
L0 and L1
be potential losses without and with prevention. The distributional effect is the pair
Law(L0), Law(L1).
If the two potential losses can be coupled at the event level, one may study avoided losses
A =L0 −L1.
If only marginal laws are available, optimal transport can provide a cost-minimizing coupling,
but it should not automatically be interpreted causally. It is a geometric matching of quantiles
or scenarios, not evidence by itself.
Remark 11.4 (Transport coupling versus causal coupling). The optimal coupling between pre- and
post-prevention distributions minimizes a mathematical cost. A causal coupling pairs the same
underlying event under two potential worlds. These couplings may coincide under monotone
rank preservation, but they need not coincide in general.
This distinction is important in climate-risk accounting. If a prevention project is evaluated
by comparing distributions before and after, the Wasserstein map describes how one distribution
can be moved into the other. To claim avoided losses, one also needs a credible baseline and
evidence that the intervention caused the shift.
11.13 Exposure changes and unbalanced transport
Classical optimal transport assumes equal total mass. In actuarial climate applications, this
assumption may fail. Exposure can be created, destroyed or relocated:
• new properties are built in high-risk zones;
• properties are removed through buyout programs;
• insured values change through inflation or reconstruction cost;
• policyholders enter or leave the insurance market;
• deductibles and limits alter the insured portion of losses.
If µ and ν are finite measures with different total masses, exact marginal constraints are too
rigid. Unbalanced optimal transport relaxes the constraints. A common formulation is
inf
π≥0
∫
c(x,y)π( dx, dy) +λ1D(π1 |µ) +λ2D(π2 |ν),
where π1,π2 are the marginals of π, and D is a divergence such as relative entropy or total
variation. The parameters λ1,λ2 control the cost of creating or destroying mass. The residual-
mass interpretation depends on the chosen divergence: with entropic penalties, for example, the
fitted marginals need not be literal submeasures of the original measures.
Actuarial takeaway
Balanced transport asks: how do we move the same portfolio from one distribution to
another? Unbalanced transport asks: how do we move, create and remove exposure mass?
The second question is often closer to climate adaptation and insurance availability.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 184/416

=== page 185 ===
11.14 Parametric triggers and basis risk
Example 11.6 (Managed retreat). Consider a flood portfolio before and after a buyout program.
Some exposed homes disappear from the insured portfolio. A balanced transport comparison
would be forced to match their mass to other homes. An unbalanced formulation can instead
pay a penalty for removing that mass, making the interpretation closer to the intervention.
11.14 Parametric triggers and basis risk
Traditional indemnity insurance pays according to the realized loss. Parametric insurance pays
according to an index or trigger. For example, a catastrophe contract may pay when wind speed,
rainfall, river height, ground acceleration or drought index crosses a pre-specified threshold. The
advantage is operational: payments can be fast, transparent and less costly to administer. The
difficulty is actuarial: the index payment need not match the policyholder’s actual loss.
Let L denote the economic loss and let I denote the parametric index. A parametric contract
is a measurable function
P =g(I),
where g is the payout function. For example,
g(i) =C (i−u)+ ∧M
for a trigger level u, a payout slope C, and a maximum payment M. The basis risk is the
mismatch between the loss L and the payment P. The signed residual
B =L−P
is useful, but no single scalar residual summarizes basis risk. One usually needs to distinguish
underpayment, overpayment, timing mismatch, spatial mismatch and tail mismatch.
Definition 11.11 (Basis-risk law and shortfall law). Let L be the loss and P = g(I) the
parametric payment. The basis-risk law is the distribution of B = L−P. The shortfall law is
the distribution of
S = (L−P )+.
A distributional basis-risk audit should report both the symmetric mismatch between Law(L)
and Law(P ) and the directional shortfall law Law(S).
Optimal transport provides a useful way to compare the law of actual losses and the law of
parametric payments:
Wp
(
Law(L), Law(P )
)
.
This distance is zero only when the two one-dimensional distributions coincide. In dimension one
it compares quantiles:
Wp p ( Law(L), Law(P ) ) = ∫ 1 0 |QL(t)−QP (t)|p dt.
Thus the discrepancy is not only a mean error. It records where the parametric contract under-
or over-reproduces the loss distribution.
Actuarial takeaway
Parametric insurance can be audited as a comparison between two distributions: the
distribution of actual losses and the distribution of index-based payments. Basis risk is not
only the expectation of L−P; it also includes the geometry and direction of the mismatch
between Law(L) and Law(g(I)).
Page 185/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 186 ===
11 RESERVING, CASH-FLOW DISTRIBUTIONS AND CLIMATE PREVENTION
A useful decomposition separates calibration risk and structural basis risk. Suppose the
actuary can choose the payout function g in a classG. A distributional calibration problem is
inf
g∈G
Wp p ( Law(L), Law(g(I)) ) .
If G contains only simple trigger functions, the infimum may remain large. This remaining
distance is not sampling error; it is the distributional cost of using the index rather than the loss
itself.
One may also focus on underpayment, which is often more important than symmetric basis
risk. Define the shortfall
S = (L−P )+.
A tail-sensitive diagnostic is
TVaRα(S) = 1 1−α ∫ 1 α QS(t) dt.
Alternatively, one can compare the upper tails of L and P through
W tail p,α ( Law(L), Law(P ) )p = 1 1−α ∫ 1 α |QL(t)−QP (t)|p dt.
Remark 11.5 (Basis risk is directional). The distance Wp(Law(L), Law(P )) is symmetric, but
policyholder welfare is not. Underpayment and overpayment have different meanings. For contract
design, one should therefore report both a distributional distance and directional quantities such
as E[(L−P )+], TVaRα((L−P )+), and P(P <L).
In spatial catastrophe insurance, the mismatch may be multivariate. If components are
indexed by locations, write
L = (L1,...,Ld), P = (P1,...,Pd).
A direct vector cost such as
c(l,p) =
d∑
j=1
wj|lj −pj|p
measures amount mismatch at each location. If spatial misallocation matters, it is often better
to represent losses and payments as measures over locations, for example
λL =
d∑
j=1
Ljδzj, λP =
d∑
j=1
Pjδzj,
and to use a ground cost based on geographical or hazard distance between locations. This
separates amount mismatch from the question of whether payment mass is allocated to the right
places.
Computational note
A practical parametric-insurance diagnostic can report:
W1(Law(L), Law(P )), W tail 1,α (Law(L), Law(P )), E[(L−P )+], TVaRα((L−P )+).
The first two are distributional comparisons. The last two are directional policyholder
shortfall measures.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 186/416

11.15 A small computational lab
11.15 A small computational lab
The following pseudo-code illustrates a simple one-dimensional comparison of reserve distributions.
The same structure can be used for climate loss distributions.
set.seed(123)
n <- 5000
R1 <- rlnorm(n, meanlog = log(100), sdlog = 0.25)
R2 <- rlnorm(n, meanlog = log(102), sdlog = 0.40)
u <- ((1:n) - 0.5) / n
q1 <- sort(R1)
q2 <- sort(R2)
W1 <- mean( abs(q1 - q2))
W2 <- sqrt( mean((q1 - q2)^2))
emp _qleft <- function(x, alpha) {
x <- sort(x)
x[ max(1, ceiling(alpha * length(x)))]
}
VaR <- function(x, alpha) emp _qleft(x, alpha)
TVaR <- function(x, alpha) mean(x[x >= VaR(x, alpha)])
alpha <- 0.995
out <- c(
mean _1 = mean(R1),
mean _2 = mean(R2),
VaR _1 = VaR(R1, alpha),
VaR _2 = VaR(R2, alpha),
TVaR _1 = TVaR(R1, alpha),
TVaR _2 = TVaR(R2, alpha),
W1 = W1,
W2 = W2
)
print( round(out, 2))
plot(u, q2 - q1, type = "l",
xlab = "quantile␣level␣u",
ylab = "q2(u)␣-␣q1(u)",
main = "Quantile␣displacement␣between␣reserve␣laws")
abline(h = 0, lty = 2)
For a cash-flow distribution in RT +, one may represent simulations as an N×T matrix and
compute an empirical transport distance using an optimal transport package. Even without
solving full multivariate OT, comparing one-dimensional marginals year by year is a useful
diagnostic:
W2(Law(R(1) t ), Law(R(2) t )), t = 1,...,T.
This does not capture dependence across future years, but it is easy to explain and often reveals
whether model differences are concentrated in early or late payments.
11.16 Practical workflow
A distributional workflow for reserving and prevention can be organized as follows.

=== page 188 ===
11 RESERVING, CASH-FLOW DISTRIBUTIONS AND CLIMATE PREVENTION
Step 1: choose the object. Decide whether the comparison concerns total reserves, cash-flow
vectors, triangle cell mass, development patterns, annual aggregate climate losses or event-level
losses.
Step 2: define the metric. Choose the cost function. For cash-flows, the cost should reflect
discounting, capital relevance and timing. For spatial climate losses, the cost may combine
geographic distance, hazard intensity and insured value.
Step 3: compute scalar summaries. Report changes in best estimate, VaR, TVaR, and
possibly cost-of-capital quantities. These remain essential actuarial outputs.
Step 4: compute distributional summaries. Compute Wasserstein distances, barycenters,
quantile displacement plots or scenario transport maps.
Step 5: separate diagnosis from attribution. A Wasserstein displacement diagnoses a
distributional change. Attribution to a reserving assumption, inflation scenario or prevention
measure requires additional modelling assumptions and evidence.
Further reading
For reserving, this lecture connects naturally to stochastic claims reserving, GLM reserving,
bootstrap reserving and Bayesian predictive reserving. For the transport side, the relevant
tools are one-dimensional quantile transport, empirical Wasserstein distances, barycen-
ters and entropic regularization. For climate prevention, the key distinction is between
distributional comparison and causal avoided-loss attribution.

=== page 190 ===
12 FAIRNESS, DISCRIMINATION AND WASSERSTEIN PROJECTIONS
12 Fairness, Discrimination and Wasserstein Projections
Learning objectives
The previous lectures used optimal transport to compare risks, portfolios, reserving models,
and climate scenarios. This lecture applies the same distributional geometry to fairness and
discrimination in insurance. The central object is again a probability distribution: the distribution
of scores, predicted premiums, residuals, losses, or decisions conditional on a sensitive attribute.
Wasserstein distances provide an audit tool, by quantifying disparities between conditional laws,
and a correction tool, by transporting group-specific distributions toward a common reference
law. This lecture focuses on one-dimensional score distributions.
After this lecture, the reader should be able to:
(i) distinguish individual risk classification, group disparity, proxy discrimination, and distri-
butional fairness;
(ii) formulate demographic parity and related constraints as equalities between conditional
distributions;
(iii) compute one-dimensional Wasserstein distances between group score distributions using
quantile functions;
(iv) define a Wasserstein barycenter of group distributions and interpret it as a fair reference
distribution;
(v) construct monotone group-wise repair maps in dimension one;
(vi) quantify the cost of fairness using Wasserstein displacement, changes in premiums, and
changes in calibration;
(vii) understand the tension between distributional parity and actuarial calibration;
(viii) design empirical diagnostics and simulation exercises for insurance fairness audits.
Actuarial takeaway
Fairness questions in insurance are not only questions about individual predictions. They
are also questions about distributions: who receives which range of premiums, which groups
are concentrated in which tail, and how much mass would have to be moved to remove a
group disparity.
Actuarial takeaway
The transport maps in this lecture align conditional score distributions. They do not
identify individual causal effects of group membership. A causal interpretation requires a
structural model, an intervention design, or other identifying assumptions.
Further reading
For general algorithmic-fairness background, see Dwork et al. 2012; Feldman et al. 2015;
Hardt et al. 2016; Chouldechova 2017; Kleinberg et al. 2017; Barocas et al. 2023. For coun-
terfactual fairness, see Kusner et al. 2017. Wasserstein and optimal-transport approaches
to distributional fairness are developed in Gordaliza et al. 2019; Chzhen et al. 2020. For
insurance-specific discussions of discrimination and pricing, see Lindholm et al. 2022;
Charpentier 2024. The Wasserstein notions used here rely on the quantile and barycenter
tools from Lectures 4 and 5, while statistical uncertainty is discussed in Lecture 7.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 190/416

=== page 191 ===
12.1 The actuarial setting
12.1 The actuarial setting
Let Y denote an insured outcome, such as claim frequency, claim severity, annual aggregate loss,
or a binary claim indicator. Let X be the vector of rating variables available to the insurer and
let S be a sensitive attribute, such as sex, age category, race, immigration status, or another
legally relevant grouping variable. In many jurisdictions, some components of S may be legally
restricted, socially contested, or acceptable only under specific conditions.
A predictive pricing model produces a score or premium
Z = ˆm(X),
where ˆm is typically trained to approximate a risk quantity such as
m(x) = E[Y |X =x].
For a pure premium model, Z is often interpreted as an estimate of expected cost; when the
score is a tariff or a technical premium, we write more specifically Z = Π(X). For a classification
model, Z may be a probability of claim, a fraud score, or an underwriting score.
The actuarial argument for risk classification is conditional accuracy: under a cost-based
pricing principle, different expected losses may lead to different premiums. Fairness concerns
arise because this classification can reproduce, amplify, or hide group-level disparities. Even if S
is excluded from the model, other variables may act as proxies for S. The law of Z conditional
on S =s can therefore differ substantially across groups.
Definition 12.1 (Group-specific score distribution). Let Z be a score or premium and let S
take values in a finite setS. The group-specific score distribution is
µs = Law(Z|S =s), s∈S.
If ps = P(S =s), the marginal score distribution is the mixture
µ =
∑
s∈S
psµs.
The objects µs are the natural starting point for a distributional fairness audit. They describe
whether the score distribution for one group is shifted upward, more dispersed, heavier tailed, or
more concentrated in high-premium regions than the score distribution for another group.
Example 12.1 (A proxy effect). Suppose that S∈{0, 1} is not used directly in the tariff, but
a geographic variable G is strongly associated with S. If Z = ˆm(G,A,V ), where A is age and
V is vehicle value, then the conditional laws Law(Z| S = 0) and Law(Z| S = 1) may differ
even though S never appears in the formula. Fairness through unawareness does not imply
distributional fairness.
Definition 12.2 (Proxy discrimination). A variable or score exhibits proxy discrimination
with respect to a sensitive attribute S when it does not use S explicitly but remains strongly
informative about S, or reproduces a disparity associated with S, through other variables. In
a distributional audit, this is often visible through differences between the conditional laws
Law(Z|S =s).
12.2 From group averages to group distributions
A first audit may compare group averages,
E[Z|S =s], s∈S.
Page 191/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 192 ===
12 FAIRNESS, DISCRIMINATION AND WASSERSTEIN PROJECTIONS
This is useful but incomplete. Two groups can have the same average premium and very different
distributions. One group may have many very low and very high premiums, while another group
may be concentrated around the average. Conversely, two groups can have different averages but
very similar distributions after a small location shift.
Distributional audits retain the full conditional law. For example, one may compare:
mean disparity: E[Z|S =s]− E[Z|S =s′],
tail disparity: VaRα(Z|S =s)− VaRα(Z|S =s′),
spread disparity: Var(Z|S =s)− Var(Z|S =s′),
distributional disparity: Wp(µs,µs′).
The last quantity is especially informative because it is sensitive to the whole distribution,
not only one functional; see Section 5.5 for the one-dimensional formula and Section 5.12 for
barycenters.
Definition 12.3 (Distributional demographic parity). A score Z satisfies distributional demo-
graphic parity with respect to S if
Law(Z|S =s) = Law(Z|S =s′)
for all s,s′∈S. Equivalently, all group-specific score distributions µs are identical.
This condition is strong. In insurance, it should usually not be presented as the only definition
of fairness. It is better understood as one benchmark: it asks what would be needed to remove
all distributional information about S from Z. The answer is useful even when the benchmark is
not ultimately imposed.
Proposition 12.1 (Distributional parity and independence). Assume that S is discrete and that
P(S = s) > 0 for all s∈S. Then Z satisfies distributional demographic parity with respect to S
if and only if Z is independent of S.
Proof. If all conditional laws Law(Z | S = s) are equal to a common law ¯µ, then for every
measurable set B,
P(Z∈B,S =s) = P(S =s)¯µ(B) = P(S =s)P(Z∈B),
so Z and S are independent. The converse follows immediately from independence.
Remark 12.1 (Fairness constraints and fairness audits). A fairness audit may compute a disparity
without recommending that the disparity be set exactly to zero. This distinction is important.
Wasserstein distances can quantify how different groups are treated by a score, while the final
regulatory or ethical decision may involve additional considerations: cost-based pricing, social
acceptability, legal constraints, adverse selection, transparency, and affordability.
12.3 Wasserstein disparity indices
Let µs = Law(Z|S = s) be group score distributions on R. A simple pairwise Wasserstein audit
computes
Dp(s,s′) = Wp(µs,µs′),
or, when p = 2, the squared distance
D2 2(s,s′) = W2 2(µs,µs′).
For one-dimensional distributions with finite second moments,
W2 2(µs,µs′) =
∫ 1
0
{Qs(u)−Qs′(u)}2 du,

12.3 Wasserstein disparity indices
where Qs is the quantile function of µs.
Pairwise distances are useful when there are only two groups. With many groups, it is
more convenient to compare each group with a common reference distribution. This leads to a
barycentric disparity index.
Definition 12.4 (Barycentric Wasserstein disparity). Let ps = P(S = s). A Wasserstein
barycenter of the group distributions is any solution of
¯µ∈ arg min
ν∈P2(R)
∑

psW2 2(µs,ν).
The total disparity around the barycenter is
D2 bar(Z,S) = ∑
s∈S
psW2 2(µs, ¯µ).
The group contribution is
Cs = W2 2(µs, ¯µ).
In dimension one, the barycenter has an explicit quantile representation:
¯Q(u) = ∑

psQs(u), 0<u< 1.
Thus
D2 bar(Z,S) = ∑

∫ 1

{Qs(u)− ¯Q(u)}2 du.
This is the weighted variance, across groups, of the group quantiles at each rank u, integrated
over u.
Proposition 12.2 (Quantile variance identity). Suppose that Z|S = s has finite second moment
for all s. Let Qs be the group quantile functions and let ¯Q = ∑ spsQs. Then
D2 bar(Z,S) =
∫ 1

ps{Qs(u)− ¯Q(u)}2 du.
Moreover, for two groups with weights p and 1−p,
D2 bar(Z,S) =p(1−p)W2 2(µ0,µ1).
Proof. The first identity follows from the one-dimensional representation of the W2 distance and
the quantile formula for the barycenter. For two groups, ¯Q =pQ1 + (1−p)Q0, hence
p{Q1 − ¯Q}2 + (1−p){Q0 − ¯Q}2 =p(1−p)(Q1 −Q0)2.
Integrating over u∈ (0, 1) gives the result.
Actuarial takeaway
The barycentric disparity index has a direct interpretation: it is the average squared move-
ment required to bring each group score distribution to a common reference distribution.
In one dimension, this movement is measured rank by rank.
