12 FAIRNESS, DISCRIMINATION AND WASSERSTEIN PROJECTIONS
12.4 Wasserstein projections and score repair
The previous section described an audit. We now describe a correction. This uses the monotone
rearrangement from Theorem 4.4 and the barycenter construction from Definition 5.5. The
goal is to transform Z into a new score Z⋆ such that the conditional laws Law(Z⋆ |S = s) are
identical across groups. Among all such transformations, the Wasserstein barycentric repair is
the one obtained by transporting each group distribution to the barycenter.
Assume first that Z | S = s has a continuous distribution function Fs. Let ¯F be the
distribution function of the barycenter ¯µ, with quantile function ¯Q. Define
Ts(z) = ¯Q(Fs(z)).
Then, conditionally on S =s,
Z⋆ =Ts(Z)
has distribution ¯µ. Therefore
Law(Z⋆ |S =s) = ¯µ
for all s. This is a distributionally fair score. Any decision rule that depends only on Z⋆ will
then have the same conditional distribution across groups. This conclusion does not apply to
rules that also use S or unrepaired covariates.
Definition 12.5 (Barycentric repair). When the group conditional distribution functions are
continuous, the barycentric Wasserstein repair of a one-dimensional score Z is the group-wise
transformation
Z⋆ =TS(Z), Ts = ¯Q◦Fs,
where Fs is the group conditional distribution function of Z and ¯Q is the barycenter quantile
function. With atoms, the same construction should be read through monotone transport plans,
randomized ranks, or a documented empirical tie convention.
The map Ts is monotone. It preserves ranks within each group: two policyholders in the
same group keep their within-group ordering. This is often an attractive feature in insurance.
The correction does not arbitrarily reshuffle policyholders within a group; it changes the scale on
which group ranks are expressed.
Proposition 12.3 (Optimality of monotone repair). Assume that µs and ¯µ belong to P2(R), and
that either µs is atomless or a randomized-rank convention is used. In dimension one, among all
admissible transformations that send µs to ¯µ, the monotone map Ts = ¯Q◦Fs minimizes
E
[
(T (Z)−Z)2 |S =s ] .
The minimal value is W2 2(µs, ¯µ).
Proof. This is the one-dimensional optimal transport result for the squared cost. The increasing
rearrangement transports µs to ¯µ and minimizes the expected squared displacement. The value
of the minimum is the squared W2 distance.
Remark 12.2 (Ties and empirical scores). Insurance scores often have ties, for example because
tariffs are rounded, binned, or table-based. Then Fs(Z) is not uniform without additional
randomization. In practice one may use randomized ranks inside ties, mid-ranks, or a smoothed
empirical distribution. The choice should be documented because it can affect individual
corrections when many policyholders share the same tariff cell.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 194/416

=== page 195 ===
12.5 Premium balance and mean effects
12.5 Premium balance and mean effects
Let Z be a premium and Z⋆ its repaired version. Since all groups are mapped to the barycenter,
the group mean after repair is
E[Z⋆ |S =s] =
∫ 1
0
¯Q(u) du,
which is the same for all groups. The marginal mean is preserved if the barycenter weights are
the empirical group proportions:
E[Z⋆] = ∑
s
ps
∫ 1
0
¯Q(u) du =
∫ 1
0
∑
s
psQs(u) du =
∑
s
psE[Z|S =s] = E[Z].
Thus the barycentric repair is globally premium-balanced, at least before rounding, expenses,
taxes, and other business constraints.
Proposition 12.4 (Global mean preservation). Assume Z|S = s is integrable for all s. If the
barycenter uses weights ps = P(S =s), then the one-dimensional barycentric repair satisfies
E[Z⋆] = E[Z].
Proof. The proof follows from the quantile formula above and the identity E[Z | S = s] =∫ 1
0 Qs(u) du for integrable random variables.
Mean preservation does not imply that all individual changes are small. Some policyholders
may receive large decreases or increases. It is therefore useful to report the distribution of
displacements
∆ =Z⋆ −Z,
including its mean, standard deviation, quantiles, and tail values by group. In an actuarial
context, one may also impose additional constraints, for example:
no global revenue change: E[Z⋆] = E[Z],
limited individual change: |Z⋆ −Z|≤b,
limited multiplicative change: a≤Z⋆/Z≤A,
minimum premium: Z⋆ ≥zmin.
These constraints may break the simple barycentric formula. They lead to constrained projection
problems, often still convex in discretized form.
Example 12.2 (Two-group location shift). Suppose
Z|S = 0∼N (100, 102), Z|S = 1∼N (120, 102),
with group weights p0 = p1 = 1/2. The barycenter is N (110, 102). The repair decreases group 1
premiums by 10 and increases group 0 premiums by 10. This removes the distributional disparity
while preserving the global average premium.
Example 12.3 (Scale difference). Suppose
Z|S = 0∼N (100, 102), Z|S = 1∼N (100, 302),
again with equal weights. The means are equal, but the distributions are not. The barycenter
has quantile function
¯Q(u) = 100 + 20Φ−1(u),
so group 0 is spread out and group 1 is compressed. A mean-based audit would see no disparity;
a distributional audit would.
Page 195/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 196 ===
12 FAIRNESS, DISCRIMINATION AND WASSERSTEIN PROJECTIONS
12.6 Calibration and the impossibility tension
Distributional parity is not the only desirable property. In insurance, calibration is central. A
score Z is calibrated, in a simplified regression sense, if
E[Y |Z =z] =z.
A group-calibrated score satisfies
E[Y |Z =z,S =s] =z
for all groups and relevant scores. Calibration says that a premium level should correspond to
the same expected loss.
Distributional demographic parity and group calibration can be incompatible when groups
have different base risks. The following proposition gives a simple version of this tension.
Proposition 12.5 (A calibration and parity tension). Assume that Z is integrable and satisfies
group calibration,
E[Y |Z,S] =Z.
If Z also satisfies distributional demographic parity with respect to S, then
E[Y |S =s]
is the same for all groups s.
Proof. By iterated expectation,
E[Y |S =s] = E[E[Y |Z,S =s]|S =s] = E[Z|S =s].
If Z has the same conditional distribution for all groups, then E[Z|S = s] is the same for all
groups. Hence the group expected losses must be equal.
This result does not say that parity is wrong. It says that exact parity and exact group
calibration cannot both hold unless group expected losses are equal. In practice, this creates a
frontier: one can move toward parity at the cost of calibration, or preserve calibration at the
cost of group disparities.
Definition 12.6 (Calibration error after repair). Let Z⋆ be a repaired score. A group calibration
diagnostic is
CalErrs = E
[
(E[Y |Z⋆,S =s]−Z⋆)2 |S =s ] .
Empirically, this can be estimated by binning Z⋆, fitting a calibration curve, or using a flexible
regression of Y on (Z⋆,S).
Actuarial takeaway
A Wasserstein repair can remove a distributional disparity, but it may also change the
actuarial meaning of the score. After any correction, one should check calibration, loss
ratios, residual distributions, and the distribution of premium changes.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 196/416

=== page 197 ===
12.7 Partial repair and fairness frontiers
12.7 Partial repair and fairness frontiers
Exact distributional parity may be too strong. A useful compromise is partial repair. The
following formula is specific to the one-dimensional monotone repair path. Let Ts be the
barycentric repair map and let λ∈ [0, 1]. Define
Z(λ) = (1−λ)Z +λTS(Z).
For λ = 0, there is no correction. For λ = 1, there is full barycentric repair. Intermediate values
trace a path from the original score to the distributionally fair score.
In one dimension, if each group is transformed by
Ts,λ(z) = (1−λ)z +λTs(z),
then the group quantile functions along the path are
Qs,λ(u) = (1−λ)Qs(u) +λ ¯Q(u).
Therefore the distance from group s to the barycenter shrinks linearly in λ, and the squared
distance shrinks quadratically:
W2 2(µs,λ, ¯µ) = (1−λ)2W2 2(µs, ¯µ).
The total barycentric disparity is therefore
D2 bar(Z(λ),S) = (1−λ)2D2 bar(Z,S).
Proposition 12.6 (Quadratic decrease of disparity along the repair path). Under the one-
dimensional barycentric repair path above,
D2 bar(Z(λ),S) = (1−λ)2D2 bar(Z,S).
Proof. The quantile of the repaired group distribution is Qs,λ = (1−λ)Qs +λ ¯Q. Hence
Qs,λ − ¯Q = (1−λ)(Qs − ¯Q).
Squaring, integrating, and summing over groups gives the result.
This path is useful for drawing a fairness and accuracy or fairness and calibration frontier.
For each λ, one can compute:
distributional disparity: Dbar(Z(λ),S),
predictive performance: E[ℓ(Y,Z(λ))],
calibration error: CalErrs(λ),
business disruption: E[|Z(λ) −Z|],
maximum or tail premium change: VaRα(|Z(λ) −Z|).
Remark 12.3 (Choice of loss function). The loss function ℓ should match the actuarial task. For
frequency modeling one may use Poisson deviance; for binary outcomes, log-loss or Brier score;
for pure premium regression, squared error, Gamma deviance, Tweedie deviance, or another
scoring rule. The fairness correction should not be evaluated only by RMSE if the pricing model
was trained under a different actuarial objective.
Page 197/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 198 ===
12 FAIRNESS, DISCRIMINATION AND WASSERSTEIN PROJECTIONS
12.8 Residual distributions and loss-ratio diagnostics
Auditing the score distribution alone can be misleading. Suppose that one group has higher
premiums because it has higher predicted risk, and another group has lower premiums because it
has lower predicted risk. A distributional parity constraint would remove this premium difference,
but it may generate systematic loss-ratio differences.
For this reason, a fairness audit should also examine residuals and loss ratios. Let
R =Y−Z, R⋆ =Y−Z⋆.
Group residual distributions
Law(R|S =s), Law(R⋆ |S =s)
show whether the tariff is systematically high or low for some groups. Similarly, if Z > 0, the
loss ratio
LR =Y/Z
or its aggregated version by group can be examined.
Definition 12.7 (Distributional residual audit). A distributional residual audit compares the
conditional laws
Law(Y−Z|S =s)
across groups, before and after any correction. A Wasserstein residual disparity can be defined as
D2 res = ∑
s
psW2 2 (Law(Y−Z|S =s), ¯ν),
where ¯ν is the Wasserstein barycenter of the residual distributions.
Residual parity is not the same as score parity. A model may have equal score distributions
but unequal residual distributions, or conversely unequal score distributions but well-balanced
residuals. This distinction is important in insurance because fairness may concern both access to
affordable premiums and cost-based adequacy.
12.9 Empirical computation
Suppose the data consist of observations
(zi,si,yi), i = 1,...,n.
For each group s, let zs,(1) ≤···≤zs,(ns) be the ordered scores. The empirical quantile function
is a step function. To compare groups with different sizes, one can evaluate all group quantile
functions on a common grid
uj = j− 1/2 M , j = 1,...,M.
Then approximate
Qs(uj)≈ ˆQs(uj),
compute
ˆ¯Q(uj) = ∑
s
ˆps ˆQs(uj),
and estimate the barycentric disparity by
ˆD2 bar = 1 M M∑ j=1 ∑ s ˆps { ˆQs(uj)− ˆ¯Q(uj) }2 .
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 198/416

=== page 199 ===
12.10 A minimal R lab
The repaired score for an observation in group s can be obtained by replacing its within-group
rank with the barycenter quantile at the same rank. For observation i, define an empirical rank
ˆUi = ˆFsi(zi),
or a mid-rank version. Then
ˆz⋆ i = ˆ¯Q( ˆUi).
Computational note
In empirical work, use the same quantile grid for all groups. This makes Wasserstein
distances, barycenters, partial repairs, and plots all consistent. It also avoids comparing
empirical quantiles at incompatible ranks.
12.10 A minimal R lab
The following code illustrates a one-dimensional barycentric repair. It is the in-chapter counterpart
of the dedicated Wasserstein fairness repair lab in the appendices, and it should be read together
with the statistical cautions in Lecture 7. It is intentionally simple. In a production audit, one
would add sample splitting, bootstrap uncertainty, calibration curves, and careful treatment of
ties.
set.seed(1)
n0 <- 800
n1 <- 600
s <- c( rep(0, n0), rep(1, n1))
z <- c( rlnorm(n0, log(600), .25),
rlnorm(n1, log(760), .35))
# common quantile grid
M <- 1000
u <- ( seq _len(M) - .5) / M
q _emp _left <- function(x, probs) {
x <- sort(x)
n <- length(x)
x[ pmax(1, ceiling(n * probs))]
}
q0 <- q _emp _left(z[s == 0], u)
q1 <- q _emp _left(z[s == 1], u)
p0 <- mean(s == 0)
p1 <- mean(s == 1)
qbar <- p0 * q0 + p1 * q1
# barycentric disparity
D2 <- mean(p0 * (q0 - qbar)^2 + p1 * (q1 - qbar)^2)
sqrt(D2)
# empirical ranks within groups
rank01 <- function(x) rank(x, ties.method = "average") / ( length(x) + 1)
u _ hat <- numeric( length(z))
u _ hat[s == 0] <- rank01(z[s == 0])
u _ hat[s == 1] <- rank01(z[s == 1])
Page 199/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 200 ===
12 FAIRNESS, DISCRIMINATION AND WASSERSTEIN PROJECTIONS
# interpolation of barycenter quantile
z _star <- approx(u, qbar, xout = u _ hat, rule = 2) $y
# partial repair
lambda <- .5
z _half <- (1 - lambda) * z + lambda * z _star
ag gr e ga te( cbind(original = z, repaired = z _star,
half = z _half), by = list(group = s), mean)
# premium changes
delta <- z _star - z
ag gr e ga te(delta, by = list(group = s),
function(x) quantile(x, c(.05, .5, .95)))
This code builds two group-specific lognormal score distributions, computes their quantile
barycenter, repairs the scores by rank matching, and summarizes group mean changes. The same
structure can be used with fitted GLM, GAM, random forest, or gradient boosting predictions.
12.11 A small numerical illustration
Consider two groups with equal weights and scores
Z0 ∼N (600, 1002), Z1 ∼N (750, 1502).
The barycenter is Gaussian with quantile function
¯Q(u) = 1 2{600 + 100Φ−1(u)} + 1 2{750 + 150Φ−1(u)} = 675 + 125Φ−1(u).
Thus the repair maps are affine:
T0(z) = 675 + 125z− 600 100 , T1(z) = 675 + 125z− 750 150 .
The first group is shifted upward and made more dispersed. The second group is shifted downward
and compressed. The squared W2 distance between the two original distributions is
W2 2(µ0,µ1) = (600− 750)2 + (100− 150)2 = 25000.
With equal weights, the barycentric disparity is
D2 bar = 1 4 W2 2(µ0,µ1) = 6250.
A half repair, λ = 1/2, reduces the squared disparity to 6250/4.
Remark 12.4 (Gaussian repairs are special). The affine form above relies on both group distribu-
tions being Gaussian. In general, the repair map is nonlinear. For skewed insurance premiums,
nonlinear maps are common: lower quantiles may be moved little, while upper-tail scores may
be compressed or expanded substantially.
12.12 Fairness as projection
The barycentric repair can be described as a projection onto a fairness constraint. Here projection
means minimization of the chosen transport displacement, not an ordinary Euclidean projection.
LetF be the set of random variables ~Z such that
Law( ~Z|S =s)
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 200/416

=== page 201 ===
12.13 Beyond demographic parity
is the same for all s. Consider the optimization problem
inf
~Z∈F
E[( ~Z−Z)2].
Under the restriction that each group is transformed by an optimal one-dimensional transport
map, the solution is the barycentric repair. More generally, with discrete data, this becomes a
finite-dimensional projection problem.
This projection view clarifies the meaning of the correction. We are not simply forcing all
means to be equal. We are finding the closest score, in squared displacement, whose group
conditional distributions coincide.
Definition 12.8 (Wasserstein fairness projection). A Wasserstein fairness projection of a score
Z is a score Z⋆ satisfying distributional demographic parity and minimizing a transportation
cost from Z to Z⋆, typically
E[(Z⋆ −Z)2].
In one dimension, with group-wise monotone maps, this projection is given by the barycentric
repair.
Proposition 12.7 (One-dimensional barycentric repair as a projection). Assume that each condi-
tional law µs = Law(Z|S = s) belongs to P2(R). Among group-wise monotone transformations
Ts such that (Ts)#µs is the same law for all s, the minimum of
∑
s
ps
∫
|Ts(z)−z|2 µs( dz)
is attained by choosing the common law to be the W2 barycenter ¯µ and Ts to be the monotone
map from µs to ¯µ.
Proof. For any common target law ν, the optimal monotone group-wise transformation has cost
W2 2(µs,ν). Minimizing the weighted sum of these costs over ν is exactly the barycenter problem
from Definition 5.5.
12.13 Beyond demographic parity
Distributional parity of scores is only one possible fairness target. Other targets compare
conditional distributions after controlling for a legitimate risk variable. Let A be an admissible
risk summary, such as a risk tier, exposure class, or predicted loss band. One may ask for
Law(Z|S =s,A =a) = Law(Z|S =s′,A =a)
for each value of a. This is a conditional distributional parity requirement.
Similarly, one may compare residuals conditional on risk tiers:
Law(Y−Z|S =s,A =a).
This type of audit is often more actuarially meaningful than unconditional demographic parity,
because it distinguishes disparities explained by accepted risk factors from disparities that remain
within comparable risk classes.
Definition 12.9 (Conditional Wasserstein disparity). Let A be a finite admissible stratification
variable. A conditional Wasserstein disparity can be defined as
D2 cond = ∑
a
P(A =a)
∑
s
P(S =s|A =a)W2 2 ( Law(Z|S =s,A =a), ¯µa ) ,
where ¯µa is the barycenter of the conditional group laws within stratum a.
The choice of A is normative and actuarial. If A is too rich, it may absorb the sensitive
attribute through proxies. If A is too coarse, it may ignore legitimate risk heterogeneity. The
audit should therefore report which variables are treated as admissible and why.
Page 201/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 202 ===
12 FAIRNESS, DISCRIMINATION AND WASSERSTEIN PROJECTIONS
12.14 Practical audit protocol
A practical Wasserstein fairness audit for an insurance score can be organized as follows.
Step 1. Define the object. Decide whether the audit concerns a predicted frequency,
predicted severity, pure premium, final tariff, underwriting score, residual, or
loss ratio.
Step 2. Define groups. Choose the group variable used in the audit. Record sample
sizes and missingness.
Step 3. Estimate group laws. Compute empirical distributions, quantile functions,
and uncertainty bands.
Step 4. Measure disparity. Report pairwise distances, barycentric contributions,
mean differences, tail differences, and plots.
Step 5. Construct repairs. If correction is considered, compute full and partial
Wasserstein repairs.
Step 6. Check actuarial effects. Evaluate calibration, loss ratios, premium changes,
predictive loss, and business constraints.
Step 7. Document choices. Explain the fairness target, the admissible variables, the
treatment of ties, and uncertainty.
Actuarial takeaway
The output of a fairness audit should not be a single number. A useful report con-
tains at least four objects: group distributions, Wasserstein disparities, premium-change
distributions under possible repairs, and calibration or loss-ratio diagnostics.
12.15 Limitations
Several limitations should be kept in mind.
First, the choice of sensitive variables and admissible controls is normative. Mathematics can
measure a disparity conditional on a chosen set of variables, but it cannot decide by itself which
variables are legitimate.
Second, distributional parity may conflict with actuarial calibration when groups have different
expected losses. This does not invalidate the audit; it clarifies the trade-off.
Third, empirical Wasserstein distances can be noisy, especially in small groups and in the
tails. Bootstrap uncertainty and sensitivity analysis are important.
Fourth, individual-level corrections can be legally and ethically sensitive. A monotone
within-group map preserves ranks, but it still changes premiums partly as a function of group
membership. Depending on the context, this may or may not be acceptable.
Fifth, a score distribution is only one part of the insurance process. Underwriting rules,
marketing, claims handling, cancellation, deductibles, coverage limits, and payment plans can
also produce disparate effects.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 202/416

13 Reinsurance, Capital Allocation and Multi-Marginal Trans-
port
Learning objectives
This lecture brings together several themes developed throughout the course: couplings, push-
forwards, Wasserstein distances, barycenters, duality, computation, and actuarial interpretation.
The guiding observation is that reinsurance and capital allocation are distributional operations.
A treaty transforms a gross loss distribution into retained and ceded distributions. A dependence
model transforms marginal line-of-business distributions into an aggregate loss distribution. A
capital allocation rule transforms aggregate capital into line-level charges. Optimal transport
provides a common language for all three transformations.
After this lecture, the reader should be able to:
(i) represent classical reinsurance treaties as measurable maps and push-forwards of loss
distributions;
(ii) compareretainedandcededdistributionsunderdifferenttreatiesusingWassersteindistances
and tail functionals;
(iii) formulate dependence uncertainty as an optimization problem over couplings with fixed
marginals;
(iv) recognize worst-case and best-case aggregate capital problems as multi-marginal optimal
transport problems;
(v) write the discrete multi-marginal problem and its Kantorovich dual;
(vi) interpret dual potentials as marginal shadow prices of capital or risk contribution;
(vii) connect capital allocation, Euler allocation, and Shapley-style allocation to scenario-level
decompositions;
(viii) design empirical algorithms for reinsurance and capital allocation based on grids, Monte
Carlo scenarios, Sinkhorn regularization, and scenario reduction;
(ix) understand how dependence, treaty design, and allocation rules interact in insurance
portfolios.
Actuarial takeaway
Reinsurance is a distributional transformation. Capital aggregation is a coupling problem.
Capital allocation is a disaggregation problem. Optimal transport links these three
operations by asking what mass is moved, under which constraints, and at which cost.
Further reading
For reinsurance and risk sharing, see the classical actuarial treatments of reinsurance
design and ruin theory, for instance Borch 1960; Gerber 1979; Asmussen and Albrecher
2010; Albrecher et al. 2017. For dependence, comonotonicity and risk aggregation, see
Dhaene et al. 2002b; Denuit et al. 2005b; McNeil et al. 2015b; Embrechts et al. 2013.
Multi-marginal optimal transport is reviewed in Pass 2015; computational and entropic
viewpoints are discussed in Peyré and Cuturi 2019. For capital allocation, see Shapley
1953; Aumann and Shapley 1974; Denault 2001; Tasche 2008. The martingale optimal
transport section is connected to Hobson 1998; Beiglböck et al. 2013; Galichon et al. 2014.

13 REINSURANCE, CAPITAL ALLOCATION AND MULTI-MARGINAL TRANSPORT
Notation used in this lecture. We write X = (X1,...,Xd) for line losses, x = (x1,...,xd)
for deterministic scenarios, and
Σ(x) =x1 +··· +xd
for the aggregation map. The notation Qµ denotes the generalized quantile function of a one-
dimensional law µ, as in Lecture 5. Push-forwards, couplings and Wasserstein distances are used
in the sense of Definitions 2.1, 2.2 and 5.2. Risk functionals use the notation of Lecture 8.
13.1 Why optimal transport appears in reinsurance
Consider an insurer with gross portfolio loss L. A reinsurance treaty decomposes L into a retained
loss and a ceded loss,
L =Lret +Lced.
In the simplest case, both components are deterministic functions of the gross loss:
Lret =r(L), Lced =c(L) =L−r(L).
If µ = Law(L), then the retained and ceded distributions are the push-forwards
µret =r#µ, µced =c#µ.
Thus a treaty is not only a contractual formula. It is also a map between probability distributions.
Definition 13.1 (Treaty map and retained/ceded push-forwards). Let L be a gross loss with
law µ. A deterministic reinsurance treaty is represented by a measurable retained-loss map r
and a ceded-loss map c, usually satisfying c(x) =x−r(x). The retained and ceded laws are
µret =r#µ, µced =c#µ.
When the treaty depends on an event vector Z, the same definition applies with r and c defined
on the event-state space and µ = Law(Z).
This is already useful. Suppose two treaties r0 and r1 are proposed. They induce retained
distributions
µret 0 = (r0)#µ, µret 1 = (r1)#µ.
The quantity
Wp
(
µret 0 ,µret 1 )
measures how much the retained loss distribution is changed by switching treaty. This comparison
is not limited to the mean ceded loss. It compares the whole retained distribution: moderate
losses, tail losses, attachment regions, and exhaustion regions.
The same idea applies to portfolios with several lines of business. Let
X = (X1,...,Xd)
denote line losses, with marginal laws µ1,...,µd. The aggregate loss is
S =X1 +··· +Xd.
The law of S is not determined by the marginal laws alone. It depends on the joint law γ of X,
that is, on the coupling
γ∈ Π(µ1,...,µd).
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 206/416

=== page 207 ===
13.2 Treaties as maps
Definition 13.2 (Multi-marginal coupling). Let µ1,...,µd be probability measures on spaces
X1,...,Xd. A multi-marginal coupling of these laws is a probability measure γ on X1 ×···×Xd
such that
(pk)#γ =µk, k = 1,...,d,
where pk is the projection onto the k-th coordinate. The set of all such couplings is denoted
Π(µ1,...,µd).
For d = 2, this reduces to the two-marginal coupling set introduced in Definition 2.2.
Capital, reinsurance demand, and allocation can therefore vary substantially across admissible
dependence structures.
Actuarial takeaway
In actuarial terms, a coupling is a dependence assumption. A multi-marginal transport
problem is a dependence stress test under fixed marginal line-of-business distributions.
13.2 Treaties as maps
We begin with one-dimensional treaties, where the gross loss L≥ 0 has distribution µ. A treaty
is represented by a retention function
r : [0,∞)→ [0,∞),
with ceded amount c(x) =x−r(x). Usually one assumes
0≤r(x)≤x, r nondecreasing, c nondecreasing or layer-wise nondecreasing.
The monotonicity condition excludes perverse treaties in which a larger gross loss leads to a
smaller retained amount.
Definition 13.3 (Admissible retention function). A retention function is called admissible, in
this chapter, if r : [0,∞)→ [0,∞) is measurable, nondecreasing, and satisfies
0≤r(x)≤x, x≥ 0.
The inequality r(x) ≤x is equivalently the non-negativity of the ceded function c(x) = x−r(x).
Additional contractual restrictions, such as limits, reinstatements or hours clauses, can be added
by restricting the class of admissible maps.
Quota-share treaties
A quota-share treaty with retention proportion q∈ [0, 1] is
rq(x) =qx, cq(x) = (1−q)x.
The retained distribution is
µret q = (x↦→qx)#µ.
If QL is the quantile function of L, then the retained quantile function is
Qrq(L)(u) =qQL(u), 0<u< 1.
For p≥ 1, the Wasserstein distance between two retained distributions with retentions q0 and q1
is therefore
Wp
(
(rq0)#µ, (rq1)#µ
)
=|q0 −q1|
(
E[|L|p] )1/p ,
whenever L∈ Lp. This gives a direct geometric interpretation of changing the quota-share
retention.

Proposition 13.1 (Quota-share scaling in Wasserstein distance). Let L≥ 0 have law µ∈Pp(R),
and let rq(x) =qx for q∈ [0, 1]. Then
Wp
(
(rq0)#µ, (rq1)#µ
)
=|q0 −q1|∥L∥p.
Proof. Since rq(L) = qL and the maps q0x and q1x are increasing functions of the same one-
dimensional variable, the monotone quantile coupling is the coupling induced by the common
rank of L. Hence
Wp p = E [ |q0L−q1L|p] =|q0 −q1|pE[|L|p].
Stop-loss and excess-of-loss treaties
A stop-loss treaty with priority a≥ 0 has retained loss
ra(x) = min(x,a),
and ceded loss
ca(x) = (x−a)+.
The retained distribution has an atom at a whenever P(L>a)> 0. Indeed,
P(ra(L)≤t) =
{
P(L≤t), 0≤t<a,
1, t≥a.
The atom at the retention point is
P(ra(L) =a) = P(L≥a)
when L has no atom at a; more generally, the atom includes the mass of all losses mapped to a.
For an excess-of-loss layer with attachment a and limit ℓ, the ceded amount is
ca,ℓ(x) = min{(x−a)+,ℓ},
and the retained amount is
ra,ℓ(x) =x−ca,ℓ(x).
The layer creates atoms at 0 and ℓ in the ceded distribution, corresponding respectively to losses
below attachment and losses above exhaustion.
Example 13.1 (Layer distribution). Let C =ca,ℓ(L). For 0<t<ℓ,
P(C≤t) = P(L≤a +t).
Moreover,
P(C = 0) = P(L≤a), P(C =ℓ) = P(L≥a +ℓ),
up to endpoint conventions when L has atoms. Thus a smooth gross loss distribution may induce
a ceded distribution with large atoms. This matters when comparing treaties by Wasserstein
distances, because atoms represent mass concentrated at contract thresholds.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 208/416

13.3 Comparing treaties distributionally
Reinstatements, aggregate covers, and non-smooth maps
Real treaties often involve aggregate deductibles, annual aggregate limits, reinstatements, event
definitions, or several layers. Even then, the basic representation remains useful. The treaty
induces a measurable map from the vector of underlying losses to retained and ceded amounts.
For example, if N events generate the loss vector Z = (Z1,...,ZN), an annual aggregate
cover may depend on
L =Z1 +··· +ZN.
The ceded amount could be
c(L) = min{(L−A)+,M},
where A is an aggregate deductible and M an annual aggregate limit. The retained law is again
a push-forward of Law(L).
When the treaty depends on the full event vector, say
c(Z),
the ceded distribution is
c# Law(Z).
This emphasizes a key point: in the presence of event-level dependence, the treaty output depends
on the joint law, not only on the marginal event distributions.
13.3 Comparing treaties distributionally
Classical treaty comparison often uses expected ceded loss, variance reduction, tail reduction, or
impact on a solvency capital requirement. Optimal transport adds a complementary diagnostic:
the distance between whole distributions.
Let r0 and r1 be two retention functions and define
R0 =r0(L), R1 =r1(L).
A natural comparison is
W2 2(Law(R0), Law(R1)).
In dimension one,
W2 2(Law(R0), Law(R1)) =
∫ 1
0
(QR0(u)−QR1(u))2 du.
This makes the comparison easy to compute from simulated samples.
Definition 13.4 (Distributional treaty distance). For two treaties T0,T1, with retained loss
maps r0,r1, define the retained distributional distance
Dret p (T0,T1) = Wp ( (r0)#µ, (r1)#µ ) .
Similarly, the ceded distributional distance is
Dced p (T0,T1) = Wp ( (c0)#µ, (c1)#µ ) .
These two distances need not coincide. Two treaties may have similar retained distributions
but quite different ceded distributions, especially when one treaty removes moderate losses and
another removes rare large losses. The insurer and reinsurer may therefore view the same treaty
switch differently.
Page 209/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

Proposition 13.2 (Map stability and policy-level upper bound). Let r be K-Lipschitz on [0,∞).
For any two gross-loss laws µ,ν∈Pp(R+),
Wp(r#µ,r#ν)≤KWp(µ,ν).
Moreover, if two treaties r0,r1 are applied to the same gross loss L∼µ, then
Wp
(
(r0)#µ, (r1)#µ
)
≤
(
E
[
|r0(L)−r1(L)|p])1/p.
Proof. For the first inequality, push any coupling γ ∈ Π(µ,ν) through (x,y) ↦→ (r(x),r(y)).
Its cost is bounded by Kp|x−y|p. Taking the infimum over γ gives the result. The second
inequality uses the particular coupling (r0(L),r1(L)); the optimal Wasserstein coupling can only
be cheaper.
Remark 13.1 (Paired versus distributional treaty change). The second inequality separates a
paired claim-level movement from an unpaired distributional movement. The paired movement
is an upper bound on the Wasserstein movement between retained laws, and the inequality may
be strict when different pointwise treaty effects produce similar retained distributions.
A retention path
Let θ∈ [0, 1] index a family of treaties, with retention maps rθ. For example, θ may represent an
attachment point, a limit, or a quota-share retention. The path
θ↦→ (rθ)#µ
is a path in Wasserstein space. Its length, curvature, or local derivative can be used to understand
treaty sensitivity.
A simple local diagnostic is
W2((rθ+h)#µ, (rθ)#µ)
|h|
for small h. Large values indicate that small contract changes produce large distributional
changes. For excess-of-loss treaties, such sensitivity is often concentrated near the attachment
and exhaustion points.
Example 13.2 (Moving an attachment point). Let ca(x) = (x−a)+ and Ra = x−ca(x) =
min(x,a). Increasing a by a small amount affects all losses above a, but does not affect losses
below a. Thus the distributional derivative depends on the survival probability P(L>a). The
impact of changing a low attachment point is therefore different from the impact of changing a
high attachment point, even if the numerical change in attachment is the same.
13.4 Premiums, risk measures, and treaty objectives
A treaty is not selected only by comparing retained distributions. It also has a price. Let π(T )
denote the reinsurance premium for treaty T. A stylized insurer objective is
J(T ) =ρ
(
Lret T ) +π(T ),
where ρ is a risk functional, for example VaRα, TVaRα, a distortion risk measure, or an economic
capital functional.
If T is parameterized by θ, this becomes
J(θ) =ρ(rθ(L)) +π(θ).
The first term is a function of the push-forward (rθ)#µ. The second term may be based on
expected ceded loss, a loading, a cost-of-capital principle, or market quotes.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 210/416

=== page 211 ===
13.5 Dependence uncertainty as a coupling problem
Definition 13.5 (Distributional benefit of a treaty). Relative to no reinsurance, the distributional
benefit of treaty T may be summarized by
Bp(T ) = Wp
(
Law(L), Law(Lret T ) ) ,
or by tail reductions such as
∆VaRα(T ) = VaRα(L)− VaRα(Lret T ),
∆TVaRα(T ) = TVaRα(L)− TVaRα(Lret T ).
The Wasserstein benefit and the tail benefit answer different questions. A treaty may have
a modest global distributional effect but a large tail effect, or conversely a large global effect
concentrated on frequent moderate claims but limited solvency benefit.
Actuarial takeaway
Expected ceded loss measures how much is transferred on average. A tail risk reduction
measures how much capital is relieved. A Wasserstein distance measures how much the
entire retained distribution is moved. These are related but not interchangeable.
13.5 Dependence uncertainty as a coupling problem
Now consider d lines of business with line-loss vector X = (X1,...,Xd). Assume that the
marginal laws µ1,...,µd are estimated separately. This is common in practice: frequency
and severity models may be built by line, reserving models may produce line-level predictive
distributions, and catastrophe models may output peril-level or region-level loss distributions.
The aggregate loss is
S =X1 +··· +Xd.
But Law(S) depends on the joint distribution γ of X. The set of all admissible joint laws with
given marginals is
Π(µ1,...,µd).
For any coupling γ, the aggregate distribution is
Lawγ(S) = Σ#γ, Σ(x) =x1 +··· +xd.
A dependence stress test asks how large or small a risk functional can be when the marginals
are fixed:
ρ = sup
γ∈Π(µ1,...,µd)
ρ
(
Σ#γ
)
,
ρ = inf
γ∈Π(µ1,...,µd)
ρ
(
Σ#γ
)
.
This formulation separates two tasks: marginal modeling and dependence stress testing.
Example 13.3 (Two lines). Let X1 be attritional motor loss and X2 be catastrophe property
loss. Their marginal distributions may be estimated from different sources. The aggregate capital
for S = X1 + X2 depends on whether large motor losses and large catastrophe losses tend to
occur together. If the dependence structure is uncertain, optimizing over couplings gives a range
of plausible aggregate capital values.
Page 211/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

13.6 Multi-marginal optimal transport
Definition 13.6 (Multi-marginal optimal transport). Let µ1,...,µd be probability measures and
let c : X1 ×···×Xd → R∪{+∞} be a measurable cost. The multi-marginal optimal transport
problem is
inf
γ∈Π(µ1,...,µd)
∫
c(x) dγ(x).
For actuarial aggregation, natural costs are functions of the sum:
c(x) =h(x1 +··· +xd),
where h may represent capital, penalty, utility loss, or a tail indicator.
If h is convex and increasing, then minimizing or maximizing
Eγ[h(S)]
over γ∈ Π(µ1,...,µd) produces best-case and worst-case aggregation under fixed marginals.
This is a dependence problem, not a marginal problem.
Proposition 13.3 (Comonotonic extremality for convex aggregation). Let Xk ∼µk be integrable
real-valued losses and let h : R→ R be convex and increasing, with finite expectations. Among
all couplings of µ1,...,µd, the comonotonic coupling
Xcom k =Qµk(U), U∼ Unif(0, 1),
maximizes E[h(X1 +···+Xd)]. In particular, it is a conservative benchmark for convex increasing
aggregate penalties.
Proof idea. The function (x1,...,xd) ↦→ h(x1 + ··· + xd) is supermodular when h is convex.
Classical rearrangement and supermodular-order results imply that the comonotonic coupling is
maximal for expectations of supermodular functions among couplings with fixed marginals; see,
for example, Dhaene et al. 2002b; Denuit et al. 2005b; Rüschendorf 1982b.
Discrete formulation
Suppose each marginal is supported on a grid:
µk =
nk∑
i=1
a(k) i δx(k) i .
A multi-marginal coupling is an array
γi1,...,id ≥ 0.
The marginal constraints are
∑
i1,...,ik−1,ik+1,...,id
γi1,...,id =a(k) ik , k = 1,...,d.
The objective is ∑
i1,...,id
c
(
x(1) i1 ,...,x(d) id ) γi1,...,id.
This is a linear program, but its dimension grows quickly with d. For d = 2, the coupling is
a matrix. For d = 5, it is a five-dimensional array. This curse of dimensionality is the main
computational obstacle.

13.7 Worst-case capital over couplings
Dual formulation
The Kantorovich dual is
sup
φ1,...,φd
d∑
k=1
∫
φk(x) dµk(x)
subject to
φ1(x1) +··· +φd(xd)≤c(x)
for allx = (x1,...,xd). The functions φk are dual potentials.
In an actuarial interpretation, φk(x) is a marginal shadow price assigned to line k when that
line realizes loss level x. The inequality says that the sum of line-level shadow prices cannot
exceed the aggregate scenario cost. At optimality, equality holds on the support of the optimal
coupling.
Actuarial takeaway
The primal problem searches over dependence structures. The dual problem searches over
line-level price functions that support the aggregate capital functional.
13.7 Worst-case capital over couplings
Let ρ be a risk measure applied to aggregate loss S. For expectations of transformed aggregate
losses,
ρ(S) = E[h(S)],
the worst-case problem is directly a multi-marginal OT problem with cost
c(x) =h(x1 +··· +xd).
For quantile-based capital, such as VaRα, the problem is more delicate because VaR is nonlinear in
the distribution. Nevertheless, it can often be reformulated or approximated using tail indicators,
expected shortfall, or optimization over thresholds.
For example, expected shortfall, also called tail value-at-risk in actuarial notation, has the
representation
TVaRα(S) = inf t∈R
{
t + 1 1−α E[(S−t)+] } .
Thus, for a fixed threshold t, the dependence-sensitive term is
Eγ[(Σ(X)−t)+],
which is a multi-marginal OT objective. One can therefore study worst-case TVaR by combining
threshold optimization and coupling optimization.
Remark 13.2 (Aminimaxcautionfortailrisk). Because TVaRα(S) = inft{t+(1−α)−1E[(S−t)+]},
optimizing over couplings and over thresholds does not automatically commute:
sup
γ
inf
t
F (γ,t)≤ inf
t
sup
γ
F (γ,t).
Thus fixed-threshold multi-marginal OT problems give useful building blocks and upper bounds
for worst-case TVaR; equality requires additional compactness, convexity and minimax conditions,
or a more careful reformulation.

=== page 214 ===
13 REINSURANCE, CAPITAL ALLOCATION AND MULTI-MARGINAL TRANSPORT
Comonotonic upper bound
In one-dimensional aggregation, the comonotonic coupling is obtained by taking a common
uniform random variable U∼ Unif(0, 1) and setting
Xk =Qk(U), k = 1,...,d,
where Qk is the quantile function of µk. The aggregate is
Scom =Q1(U) +··· +Qd(U).
For many convex increasing functionals of the aggregate, this coupling gives a conservative
dependence structure. It represents perfect positive rank dependence: large losses occur together
across lines.
The comonotonic model is actuarially important because it is transparent and easy to compute.
It is not always the exact worst case for every functional and every constraint set, but it is a
natural benchmark for stress testing.
Diversification as movement away from comonotonicity
Diversification may be viewed as a movement from a concentrated, highly aligned coupling toward
a less aligned coupling. If γcom is the comonotonic coupling and γ is a selected dependence model,
one can compare them using a distance between joint laws, for example
W2(γ,γcom),
when both are distributions on Rd. A large distance indicates a substantial departure from
perfect positive dependence.
Another diagnostic compares aggregate distributions:
W2
(
Σ#γ, Σ#γcom) .
This measures the distributional diversification benefit at the aggregate level.
Definition 13.7 (Distributional diversification benefit). Given a reference conservative coupling
γref, define the distributional diversification benefit of γ by
DDBp(γ;γref) = Wp ( Σ#γ, Σ#γref) .
If γref = γcom, this compares the selected aggregate distribution to the comonotonic aggregate
distribution.
13.8 Reinsurance under dependence uncertainty
Reinsurance effectiveness depends on dependence. Consider a treaty T applied to the aggregate
loss
S =X1 +··· +Xd.
The retained loss is
RT =rT (S).
For a coupling γ, its distribution is
Lawγ(RT ) = (rT ◦ Σ)#γ.
A robust assessment of the treaty considers
sup
γ∈Π(µ1,...,µd)
ρ
(
(rT ◦ Σ)#γ
)
.

This is the worst retained capital under fixed line marginals. Similarly,
sup
γ∈Π(µ1,...,µd)
Eγ[cT (Σ(X))]
quantifies the worst-case expected ceded loss for the reinsurer.
Example 13.4 (Aggregate excess-of-loss). Let T be an aggregate excess-of-loss treaty with

rT (s) =s− min{(s−a)+,ℓ}.
For fixed marginals, the expected ceded loss is

This is a multi-marginal OT objective with cost
c(x) = min{(Σ(x)−a)+,ℓ}.
Thus dependence uncertainty directly affects the reinsurance price.
Actuarial takeaway
Professional lens: cyber accumulation and dependence uncertainty.
Cyber risk is an accumulation risk. Marginal loss models for individual firms are not
enough, because many insureds may depend on the same cloud provider, software library,
payment processor, authentication service, managed-service provider or security product.
A single incident can therefore generate losses across many policies at once.
Let
L = (L1,...,Ld)
be the vector of cyber losses across insured firms, sectors or coverage components. The
marginal laws Law(Lj) may be reasonably calibrated from claims, scenarios or expert
judgement. The aggregate loss
S =
d∑
j=1
Lj
depends crucially on the coupling of these marginals. Transport language makes this
explicit:
γ∈ Π(µ1,...,µd)
is not a technical detail; it is the accumulation assumption.
A cyber accumulation stress can therefore be written as an optimization over couplings:
sup
γ∈Π(µ1,...,µd)
ρ


d∑
j=1
Lj

,
possibly with constraints reflecting known common vulnerabilities, vendor concentration,
sectoral dependence or geographical exposure. Reinsurance, limits, exclusions and aggregate
covers then act on the transported aggregate distribution.
13.9 Capital allocation
Let S =X1 +··· +Xd and let aggregate capital be
K =ρ(S).
A capital allocation rule assigns amounts K1,...,Kd to lines, business units, or risk cells, with
K1 +··· +Kd =K.

13 REINSURANCE, CAPITAL ALLOCATION AND MULTI-MARGINAL TRANSPORT
Definition 13.8 (Capital allocation rule). Let S = X1 + ··· + Xd and K = ρ(S). A full capital
allocation is a vector (K1,...,Kd) such that ∑d k=1 Kk = K. It is called dependence-sensitive
if (K1,...,Kd) depends on the coupling γ = Law(X1,...,Xd), not only on the marginal laws
µ1,...,µd.
The allocation should reflect both marginal distributions and dependence. This dependence
on the coupling is the main point for allocation.
Scenario-level allocation
Definition 13.9 (Scenario-level allocation). A scenario-level allocation is a family of functions
ak : Rd → R, k = 1,...,d,
such that
a1(x) +··· +ad(x) =A(x),
where A(x) is a scenario-level aggregate capital or loss quantity. For example, one may take
A(x) = Σ(x),
or
A(x) = (Σ(x)−t)+.
Then line-level allocations are expectations under the selected coupling γ:
Kk =
∫
ak(x) dγ(x).
For tail-based allocation, a common choice is based on tail scenarios. If S = Σ(X) and
t = VaRα(S), then one may allocate expected shortfall / TVaR using
Kk = E[Xk |S≥t]
or a centered variant. This depends crucially on the joint law ofX.
Actuarial takeaway
A capital allocation rule is not only a formula. It is a way of assigning scenario-level
aggregate risk back to coordinates. The selected coupling determines which line-level losses
appear in tail aggregate scenarios.
Euler allocation
Suppose ρ is positively homogeneous and differentiable in line weights. Define
S(w) =
d∑
k=1
wkXk,
and
K(w) =ρ(S(w)).
Definition 13.10 (Euler allocation). The Euler allocation is
Kk = ∂K(w) ∂wk
⏐⏐⏐⏐
w=1
.
If ρ = TVaRα and appropriate regularity conditions hold, this often leads to
Kk = E[Xk |S≥ VaRα(S)].
This expression reveals the dependence issue: the allocation depends on the conditional distribu-
tion of Xk in aggregate tail scenarios.

13.10 Transport-based allocation diagnostics
Shapley-style allocation
Another approach allocates capital by averaging marginal contributions across coalitions. For a
coalition A⊂{1,...,d}, let
SA =
∑
k∈A
Xk, v(A) =ρ(SA).
Definition 13.11 (Shapley allocation). The Shapley allocation to line k is
ϕk =
∑
A⊆N\{k}
|A|!(d−|A|− 1)!
d! (v(A∪{k})−v(A)),
where N ={1,...,d}.
This allocation depends on the laws of all coalition sums SA, hence on the dependence
structure within every coalition.
Optimal transport can be used to stress-test Shapley allocations by optimizing coalition risk
measures over admissible couplings. It can also compare allocations under different dependence
models.
13.10 Transport-based allocation diagnostics
Suppose two dependence models γ0 and γ1 have the same marginals but induce different capital
allocations
K(0) = (K(0) 1 ,...,K(0) d ), K(1) = (K(1) 1 ,...,K(1) d ).
A simple diagnostic is the Euclidean difference
∥K(0) −K(1)∥.
But a distributional diagnostic is also possible. If allocation is scenario-level, it induces for each
line a distribution of allocated scenario charges,
ν(j) k = Lawγj(ak(X)), j = 0, 1.
Then
Wp(ν(0) k ,ν(1) k )
measures how the whole distribution of allocated charges changes for line k, not only its average.
Similarly, if an allocation rule produces a random vector of line charges
A(j) = (A(j) 1 ,...,A(j) d ),
then the joint allocation distributions can be compared by
Wp
(
Law(A(0)), Law(A(1)) ) .
This is useful when allocations are later used as internal prices, performance targets, or business-
unit risk budgets.

13.11 Optimal risk sharing and infimal convolution
Capital allocation starts from a total risk and splits it across units. Optimal risk sharing asks
the reverse question: how should a total loss be split among agents?
Let L be a total loss. A risk sharing rule is a vector of functions
f1,...,fm
such that
f1(x) +··· +fm(x) =x.
Agent j receives loss fj(L) and evaluates it using risk functional ρj. A stylized optimal risk
sharing problem is
inf
f1+···+fm=Id
m∑
j=1
λjρj(fj(L)).
This is a functional optimization problem over loss-sharing maps.
In convex analysis, a related object is the infimal convolution of risk functionals; see also the
convex-analysis insert in Appendix A.7:
Definition 13.12 (Infimal convolution of risk functionals). The infimal convolution of risk
functionals ρ1,...,ρm is
(ρ1□··· □ρm)(L) = inf



m∑
j=1
ρj(Lj) :L1 +··· +Lm =L


.
This expression resembles a transport problem because it optimizes over decompositions of a
total mass or total loss into components. In insurance, those components correspond to retained
loss, ceded loss, retroceded loss, or capital-bearing entities.
Example 13.5 (Insurer and reinsurer). Let L1 be retained by the insurer and L2 ceded to a
reinsurer, with L1 + L2 = L. If the insurer evaluates L1 using ρ1 and the reinsurer evaluates L2
using ρ2, the efficient frontier of treaty design can be studied through
inf
L1+L2=L
{ρ1(L1) +ρ2(L2)}.
Contractual constraints restrict the admissible decompositions, for example by requiring L2 =
(L−a)+ ∧ℓ.
13.12 Multi-marginal transport for market clearing
A reinsurance market can also be viewed as matching ceded risks with reinsurer capacities.
Suppose an insurer has ceded-loss scenarios C, while reinsurers have capital or appetite profiles.
A stylized market-clearing problem assigns pieces of ceded loss to reinsurers subject to capacity
constraints.
Let Yj be the loss assigned to reinsurer j, with
Y1 +··· +Ym =C.
If each reinsurer has a cost ρj(Yj), market clearing may be represented by
inf
Y1+···+Ym=C
m∑
j=1
ρj(Yj).
When scenario distributions are prescribed or when matching constraints are imposed, the
problem becomes multi-marginal. The cost combines aggregate feasibility, risk appetite, and
capital cost.
This abstract formulation is not meant to replace detailed reinsurance pricing. Rather, it
gives a geometric language for understanding why reinsurance allocation, retrocession, and capital
relief are intrinsically linked to transport-like constraints.

13.13 Advanced topic: martingale optimal transport
13.13 Advanced topic: martingale optimal transport
Classical optimal transport fixes two marginal distributions and optimizes over all couplings
between them:
Π(µ,ν) ={γ :γX =µ, γY =ν}.
Martingale optimal transport adds a financial or actuarial consistency constraint. When X and
Y represent values of a market-consistent quantity at two dates, one may require
Eγ[Y |X] =X.
Definition 13.13 (Martingale coupling). A coupling γ∈ Π(µ,ν) is a martingale coupling if,
under (X,Y )∼γ,
Eγ[Y |X] =X.
The set of martingale couplings is denoted
ΠM(µ,ν) ={γ∈ Π(µ,ν) : Eγ[Y |X] =X}.
The martingale optimal transport problem is then
inf
γ∈ΠM(µ,ν)
∫
c(x,y) dγ(x,y),
or, depending on the application,
sup
γ∈ΠM(µ,ν)
∫
Φ(x,y) dγ(x,y).
Actuarial takeaway
Martingale optimal transport is optimal transport with a no-arbitrage-type constraint. It
is useful when the marginals are calibrated at different dates but the coupling must be
compatible with a martingale dynamics.
The martingale constraint is restrictive. It requires that µ and ν be in convex order.
Definition 13.14 (Convex order). For integrable probability measures µ and ν on R, we write
µ⪯cx ν
if ∫
φ dµ≤
∫
φ dν
for every convex function φ for which the integrals exist. Convex order also requires equality of
means, obtained by testing both φ(x) =x and φ(x) =−x.
Intuitively, ν must be more spread out than µ while having the same mean. This is consistent
with a martingale that accumulates uncertainty over time.
Remark 13.3 (Why convex order appears). If Y is a martingale successor of X, then for any
convex function φ,
φ(X) =φ(E[Y |X])≤ E[φ(Y )|X]
by Jensen’s inequality. Taking expectations gives
E[φ(X)]≤ E[φ(Y )].
Thus convex order is necessary for the existence of a martingale coupling. In the one-dimensional
integrable setting, it is also sufficient for existence of a martingale coupling. Outside that standard
setting, the precise existence statement must be checked with the appropriate version of the
theorem.
Page 219/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 220 ===
13 REINSURANCE, CAPITAL ALLOCATION AND MULTI-MARGINAL TRANSPORT
Martingale optimal transport is most developed in mathematical finance, where it is used for
model-independent bounds on derivative prices. The marginal laws at different maturities may
be inferred from option prices, while the unknown coupling between maturities represents model
uncertainty. The martingale constraint enforces absence of arbitrage.
In actuarial science, the same idea arises when liabilities contain financial guarantees or
market-consistent components. Examples include:
• variable annuities with guarantees;
• participating life insurance contracts;
• equity-linked insurance;
• pension guarantees;
• asset-liability management under market-consistent valuation;
• robust bounds for embedded options.
Suppose S0 and S1 are asset values at two dates under a risk-neutral measure. A guarantee
payoff may be
Φ(S0,S1) = (K−S1)+
or a path-dependent payoff involving both dates. If only the marginal laws of S0 and S1 are
trusted, a robust upper price is
sup
γ∈ΠM(µ0,µ1)
∫
Φ(s0,s1) dγ(s0,s1).
This is a model-independent bound over all martingale models consistent with the given marginals.
Example 13.6 (Embedded guarantee). Consider a simplified equity-linked insurance contract
with payoff
Φ(S1) = max(G,S1)−S1 = (G−S1)+.
If the marginal law of S1 is fixed, the expectation of Φ(S1) is fixed. For more complex guarantees
depending on intermediate values, such as ratchets or cliquet-type benefits, the joint law across
dates matters. Martingale optimal transport gives robust bounds over all martingale couplings
consistent with the calibrated marginals.
There is a useful contrast with the dependence uncertainty problems discussed earlier in this
chapter. In a classical Fréchet problem, one fixes marginals and optimizes over all couplings:
γ∈ Π(µ1,...,µd).
In a martingale problem, one fixes marginals across time and imposes dynamic consistency:
E[Xt+1 |X0,...,Xt] =Xt.
Thus martingale transport is not only about dependence; it is about admissible dependence
under a time-consistency constraint.
Computational note
Classical OT asks:
How should mass move from µ to ν?
Martingale OT asks:
How should mass move from µ to ν without creating predictable gains?
The second question is central when actuarial liabilities interact with financial markets.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 220/416

=== page 221 ===
13.14 Computational approaches
The dual formulation also has an actuarial interpretation. A typical martingale OT dual
writes a robust payoff bound as the cost of a semi-static hedge: static positions in instruments
that determine the marginals, plus a dynamic trading strategy in the underlying. In schematic
form,
sup
γ∈ΠM(µ,ν)
Eγ[Φ(X,Y )] = inf φ,ψ,h
{∫
φ dµ +
∫
ψ dν
}
,
subject to
φ(x) +ψ(y) +h(x)(y−x)≥ Φ(x,y).
The term h(x)(y−x) is the gain from dynamic trading. In actuarial language, it represents
the part of the guarantee risk that can be hedged dynamically, while φ and ψ represent static
calibration instruments.
Remark 13.4 (Scope). Martingale optimal transport is an advanced topic. It is not needed
for ordinary loss modelling or distributional comparison. It becomes relevant when insurance
liabilities are linked to tradable assets, when valuation is market-consistent, or when one wants
model-independent bounds under no-arbitrage constraints.
For multi-period problems, the admissible set contains laws of processes
(X0,...,XT )
such that
E[Xt+1 |X0,...,Xt] =Xt, t = 0,...,T− 1.
This connects martingale OT with adapted transport. Both impose temporal structure on
couplings. The martingale constraint imposes financial consistency; adapted transport imposes
non-anticipativity.
13.14 Computational approaches
The exact multi-marginal problem can be computationally expensive. In actuarial applications,
one typically uses approximations adapted to the data structure.
Grid discretization
For small d, discretize each marginal on a grid and solve the linear program. This is transparent
and useful for teaching or benchmarking, but it scales poorly.
Computational note
For d marginals with n grid points each, the full coupling array has nd entries. Even n = 50
and d = 4 gives more than six million variables. This motivates structure, regularization,
or simulation-based approximations.
Entropic regularization
Add entropy to the objective:
inf
γ∈Π(µ1,...,µd)
∫
c dγ +ε
∫
log
( dγ
dm
)
dγ,
wheremisareferenceproductmeasure. Thisproducessmoothercouplingsandenablesgeneralized
Sinkhorn scaling. The price is regularization bias, controlled by ε.
Entropic methods are especially useful when the goal is not an exact worst case but a stable
dependence stress test.
Page 221/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

13 REINSURANCE, CAPITAL ALLOCATION AND MULTI-MARGINAL TRANSPORT
Scenario optimization
Another approach starts from simulated scenarios
x(1),...,x(N).
One can reweight scenarios to match target marginals or stress constraints. If wi is the weight
assigned to scenario i, a reweighted stress test solves
max
wi≥0
N∑
i=1
wih
( d∑
k=1
x(i) k
)
subject to
N∑
i=1
wi = 1
and calibration constraints ensuring that the reweighted line-level distributions remain close to
target marginals. This is a practical compromise between full coupling optimization and fixed
scenario analysis.
Sliced and pairwise approximations
For high-dimensional portfolios, one may use pairwise dependence diagnostics or random projec-
tions. For example, compare projected aggregate losses
θ⊤X
for several directions θ. Sliced Wasserstein distances can then summarize how joint dependence
changes across models. This is less exact than full multi-marginal OT but often more scalable.
13.15 Mini-lab: comparing aggregate dependence models
The following pseudo-code illustrates a simple simulation-based comparison of aggregate dis-
tributions under independent and comonotonic dependence. It extends the one-dimensional
empirical Wasserstein computations of Lab ?? and the discrete coupling ideas from Lecture 3. It
is deliberately minimal and can be adapted to any marginal model.
set.seed(1)
N <- 20000
alpha <- 0.995
# Marginal quantile functions
Q1 <- function(u) qlnorm(u, meanlog = 10, sdlog = 0.6)
Q2 <- function(u) qlnorm(u, meanlog = 9.5, sdlog = 1.0)
Q3 <- function(u) qgamma(u, shape = 2, rate = 1 /50000)
# Independent coupling
U _ind <- matrix( runif(3 * N), ncol = 3)
X _ind <- cbind(Q1(U _ind[,1]), Q2(U _ind[,2]), Q3(U _ind[,3]))
S _ind <- rowSums(X _ind)
# Comonotonic coupling
U <- runif(N)
X _com <- cbind(Q1(U), Q2(U), Q3(U))
S _com <- rowSums(X _com)
emp _ quantile _left <- function(x, a){
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 222/416

=== page 223 ===
13.16 A small worked example
xs <- sort(x)
xs[ max(1, ceiling(a * length(xs)))]
}
VaR <- function(x, a) emp _ quantile _left(x, a)
ES <- function(x, a){
v <- VaR(x, a)
mean(x[x >= v])
}
c(VaR _ind = VaR(S _ind, alpha),
VaR _com = VaR(S _com, alpha),
ES _ind = ES(S _ind, alpha),
ES _com = ES(S _com, alpha))
# Wasserstein-2 distance between aggregate distributions
w2 _empirical <- function(x, y){
sqrt( mean(( sort(x) - sort(y))^2))
}
w2 _empirical(S _ind, S _com)

c(s) = min{(s−a)+,ℓ},
then comparing retained and ceded distributions under the two couplings.
13.16 A small worked example
Suppose two lines have marginal losses X1 andX2, each taking values in {0, 1, 2}with probabilities
a = (0.6, 0.3, 0.1), b = (0.7, 0.2, 0.1).
A coupling is a matrix γ = (γij) satisfying
∑
j
γij =ai,
∑
i
γij =bj, γij ≥ 0.
If the cost is
c(i,j) =h(xi +yj),
with h(s) = (s− 2)+, then the objective
∑
i,j
h(xi +yj)γij
is expected excess loss above threshold 2. Maximizing it over γ finds the dependence structure
that creates the largest expected layer loss. Minimizing it finds the dependence structure that
creates the smallest expected layer loss.
The dual problem is
max
φ,ψ
∑
i
aiφi +
∑
j
bjψj
subject to
φi +ψj ≤h(xi +yj)
for all i,j. The dual variables can be interpreted as line-level marginal prices that support the
layer loss cost.
Page 223/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 224 ===
13 REINSURANCE, CAPITAL ALLOCATION AND MULTI-MARGINAL TRANSPORT
13.17 Interpretation for internal models
Internal capital models often combine line-level distributions, dependence assumptions, rein-
surance programs, and allocation methodologies. These components are sometimes calibrated
separately. Transport notation makes the chain of operations explicit.
(1) Marginals. Line-level or cell-level distributions define the constraints.
(2) Dependence. Couplings define admissible joint laws.
(3) Aggregation. The sum map pushes joint laws to aggregate laws.
(4) Reinsurance. Treaty maps push aggregate or event-level laws to retained and ceded laws.
(5) Capital. Risk functionals act on retained aggregate laws.
(6) Allocation. Scenario-level decompositions push capital back to lines.
This sequence is a chain of maps and optimizations. Each step can be audited by asking:
What is fixed? What is transported? What cost is minimized or maximized?
13.18 Limitations
The transport formulation is powerful but not automatic. Several limitations should be kept in
mind.
First, optimizing over all couplings can be too conservative if some dependence information is
available. In practice, one may constrain the coupling set by rank correlations, tail dependence
coefficients, common factor structures, or expert judgment.
Second, finite samples make extreme tail estimates unstable. Worst-case coupling optimization
can amplify this instability. Regularization and sensitivity analysis are therefore essential.
Third, contractual details matter. Real reinsurance treaties include reinstatement provisions,
event definitions, hours clauses, exclusions, aggregate limits, and legal wording. A push-forward
representation remains possible, but the map may be complex.
Fourth, capital allocation is partly normative. Different allocation rules answer different
questions: marginal contribution, standalone risk, diversification benefit, equity among lines, or
managerial incentives. Optimal transport clarifies the geometry but does not by itself decide
which allocation principle is appropriate.
13.19 Summary
This lecture has presented reinsurance, dependence stress testing, and capital allocation as
transport problems. The main lessons are as follows.
• A treaty maps gross losses to retained and ceded losses; therefore it pushes forward loss
distributions.
• Comparing treaties by Wasserstein distances gives a distributional view beyond expected
ceded loss and capital reduction.
• Aggregating line losses with fixed marginals but uncertain dependence is a coupling problem.
• Worst-case and best-case aggregate risk can often be expressed as multi-marginal optimal
transport problems.
• Dual potentials can be interpreted as line-level shadow prices supporting aggregate capital
costs.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 224/416

14 Counterfactual Worlds and Transport
Learning objectives
This lecture introduces counterfactual reasoning as a bridge between optimal transport and
actuarial applications. After this lecture, the reader should be able to:
(i) distinguish philosophical, causal, algorithmic and distributional meanings of counterfactuals;
(ii) represent counterfactual worlds through couplings and push-forwards;
(iii) explain why optimal transport selects one plausible counterfactual matching among many
possible couplings;
(iv) separate individual causal counterfactuals from distributional and actuarial counterfactuals;
(v) apply transport-based counterfactuals to recourse, catastrophe modelling, cyber risk,
prevention and stochastic scenario generation;
(vi) state precisely what is identified by marginals, what is selected by a cost, and what requires
causal assumptions.
Further reading
Counterfactuals have several intellectual origins. The possible-world semantics is classically
associated with Lewis 1973. The causal view is developed through potential outcomes
and structural causal models; see Rubin 1974; Holland 1986; Pearl 2009; Imbens and
Rubin 2015; Hernán and Robins 2020; Peters et al. 2017. Counterfactual explanations and
recourse in machine learning are discussed in Wachter et al. 2018; Verma et al. 2020; Karimi
et al. 2021. Transport-based counterfactual models are developed in De Lara et al. 2024.
Actuarial examples include counterfactual disaster risk analysis Woo 2018, cyberinsurance
and causal attribution Farahmand 2020. For Schrödinger bridges and stochastic transport
paths, see Léonard 2014; Chen et al. 2021; Peyré and Cuturi 2019. The optimal transport
background is standard in Villani 2003; Villani 2008; Figalli and Glaudo 2021; Ambrosio
et al. 2021; Peyré and Cuturi 2019.
14.1 Why counterfactual worlds in actuarial science?
Actuaries reason counterfactually all the time. A technical premium is a prediction of what
would happen under a contract. A reserve is a valuation of future payments under possible claims
developments. A catastrophe model evaluates losses under events that have not occurred. A
solvency capital requirement asks what would happen under an adverse scenario. A model audit
asks how a score would change under another statistical environment. A prevention analysis asks
what losses would have been observed without the protection measure, or what losses would be
observed with a new one.
The word “counterfactual” therefore hides several questions:
• what would have happened to the same policyholder under another tariff?
• what would the loss distribution have been under another climate, another mitigation
policy, or another dependence structure?
• what would the portfolio have looked like if its covariate distribution had matched another
market?
• what small perturbation of a historical event would have generated a catastrophe-level loss?
Page 227/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 228 ===
14 COUNTERFACTUAL WORLDS AND TRANSPORT
These questions are not identical. Some concern the same individual under another interven-
tion. Some concern a whole population under another distribution. Some concern explanations
of a black-box decision. Some concern stress scenarios. This lecture develops a common mathe-
matical language for these questions: a counterfactual world is often represented by a probability
measure, and a relation between the observed world and the counterfactual world is often repre-
sented by a coupling. The static constructions below point back to the couplings and Wasserstein
maps of Lectures 2–5; the stochastic-path version in Section 14.17 points forward to dynamic
scenario generation.
Actuarial takeaway
In actuarial science, the most stable and useful counterfactual object is often not an
individual statement of the form “this person’s loss would have been y”, but a distributional
statement of the form “the loss distribution would have been ν”. Optimal transport then
supplies a principled way to connect the observed distribution µ and the counterfactual
distribution ν.
14.2 Three languages of counterfactuals
Counterfactuals appear in philosophy, causal inference, machine learning and insurance practice.
The same words are used, but the mathematical objects are different.
14.2.1 Possible-world counterfactuals
A possible-world counterfactual is a statement of the form
A □→ B,
read as “if A had been the case, then B would have been the case”. The Lewisian idea is that
this statement is true if B is true in the closest possible worlds in which A is true. The key
mathematical difficulty is the word “closest”. It asks for a notion of similarity between worlds.
The connection with optimal transport is suggestive. Optimal transport also requires a
notion of closeness, but it makes it explicit through a cost function c(x,y). In a possible-world
semantics, closeness is a semantic primitive. In optimal transport, closeness is a modelling choice
and can be audited.
14.2.2 Causal counterfactuals
Incausalinference, counterfactualsareusuallyformalizedthroughpotentialoutcomesorstructural
causal models. For a binary treatment T∈{0, 1}, the two potential outcomes are
Yi(1), Yi(0).
The individual causal effect would be
Yi(1)−Yi(0),
but only one of Yi(1) and Yi(0) is observed. This is the fundamental problem of causal inference.
A structural causal model gives more structure. It writes variables as functions of their
parents and exogenous noises:
Xj =fj
(
Xpa(j),Uj
)
, j = 1,...,d.
A counterfactual is then computed by the abduction–action–prediction recipe: infer the latent
noise compatible with the observation, modify the structural equations by an intervention, and
propagate the resulting system.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 228/416

=== page 229 ===
14.3 Observed and counterfactual distributions
Definition 14.1 (Structural causal model). Let G be a directed acyclic graph on variables
X1,...,Xd. A structural causal model consists of exogenous random variables U1,...,Ud, usually
jointly independent, and measurable functions fj such that
Xj =fj
(
Xpa(j),Uj
)
, j = 1,...,d.
An intervention do(Xk = x) replaces the equation for Xk by the constant equation Xk = x,
leaving the other structural equations unchanged.
Causal counterfactuals are powerful because they aim at the same unit under another
intervention. They are demanding because they require structural information that is usually
not identified from the observational law alone.
14.2.3 Algorithmic counterfactuals
In machine learning, a counterfactual explanation is often a nearby point x′ that changes a
decision. If m :X→ R is a score and y⋆ is the desired decision, a typical formulation is
x⋆ ∈ arg min
z∈A(x)
{
d(x,z) +λℓ(m(z),y⋆) } ,
where A(x) encodes feasibility and actionability constraints. In insurance, x⋆ may be interpreted
as a recourse recommendation: what should change for a premium to decrease, for a policy to be
accepted, or for a risk score to cross a threshold?
This is useful, but potentially misleading. A closest point in Euclidean distance may be out of
distribution. It may recommend changing non-actionable features. It may alter a variable while
leaving implausible values of correlated variables unchanged. Transport-based counterfactuals
are one way to keep counterfactual profiles distributionally plausible.
14.3 Observed and counterfactual distributions
Let X be a Polish space. We write µ∈P(X ) for the observed distribution and ν∈P(X ) for a
counterfactual distribution. The notation follows the measure-theoretic conventions introduced
in Definition 2.1 and Definition 2.2. The two measures may correspond to two populations, two
time periods, two climates, two legal regimes, two prevention states or two portfolios.
Definition 14.2 (Distributional counterfactual). A distributional counterfactual for an observed
law µ∈P(X ) is an alternative law ν∈P(X ) representing the population, portfolio or scenario
distribution under another regime. It is a statement about a law, not yet about the same
individual unit under another intervention.
A distributional counterfactual is not yet a statement about individuals. It says what the
alternative population law is. To make statements of the form “this observed profile x is matched
to that counterfactual profile x†”, we need a joint law, that is, a coupling in the sense of
Definition 2.2.
Definition 14.3 (Counterfactual coupling). Let µ,ν∈P(X ). A counterfactual coupling from µ
to ν is an element
γ∈ Π(µ,ν).
If (X,X†)∼γ, thenX∼µ is the observed profile andX†∼ν is the counterfactual profile.
The dagger notation is deliberate. It denotes a profile constructed by a selected distributional
matching. It does not, by itself, denote a structural potential outcome.
Page 229/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 230 ===
14 COUNTERFACTUAL WORLDS AND TRANSPORT
Proposition 14.1 (Couplings as distributional counterfactual models). Let µ,ν∈P(X ). Any
coupling γ∈ Π(µ,ν) defines a distributional counterfactual model by specifying the joint law of an
observed profile X∼µ and an alternative profile X†∼ν. Conversely, any joint law of (X,X†)
with marginals µ and ν is an element of Π(µ,ν).
Proof. This is exactly Kantorovich’s definition of a transference plan; see Chapter 1 of Villani
2003, Chapter 4 of Villani 2008, and the introductory chapters of Figalli and Glaudo 2021. Sketch
of the proof: if γ∈ Π(µ,ν), then by definition its first marginal is µ and its second marginal
is ν. Thus a random vector (X,X†) ∼γ has X∼µ and X†∼ν. Conversely, the law of any
pair (X,X†) with these two marginals satisfies the two marginal constraints in the definition of
Π(µ,ν).
Remark 14.1 (A coupling is not a causal model). A coupling specifies how two marginal distribu-
tions are matched. It does not specify why one world changes into the other. A causal reading
requires assumptions that select the coupling as the structural relation between observed and
counterfactual units.
14.4 Non-identification from marginals
The two marginals µ and ν do not identify the joint law γ. This is the first warning in any
transport-based counterfactual analysis. If we know only the current distribution and the target
distribution, we do not know which individual in the first distribution corresponds to which
individual in the second.
Proposition 14.2 (Non-identification of individual counterfactuals). Let µ,ν∈P(X ). Unless
additional assumptions select a unique element of Π(µ,ν), the joint law of (X,X†) is not
identified by the two marginal laws µ and ν. In particular, individual counterfactual quantities
depending on the pair (X,X†) are generally not identified by µ and ν alone.
Proof. This is the elementary non-uniqueness of Kantorovich couplings; compare the discussion
of transport plans in Villani 2003; Villani 2008 and the discrete formulation in Peyré and Cuturi
2019. Sketch of the proof: it is enough to give a two-point example. Let µ = ν = 1 2 δ0 + 1 2 δ1. Both
γ1 = 1 2δ(0,0) + 1 2δ(1,1) and γ2 = 1 2δ(0,1) + 1 2δ(1,0)
belong to Π(µ,ν). Under γ1, X = X† almost surely, whereas under γ2, |X−X†| = 1 almost
surely. The marginal laws are identical, but the implied individual counterfactual displacements
are different.
Actuarial takeaway
A before/after distribution is not an audit trail. The audit trail is the coupling: it
tells which units, or which pieces of mass, moved from which observed states to which
counterfactual states.
14.5 Transport maps as deterministic counterfactuals
A counterfactual coupling may be deterministic. If T :X→X satisfies
T#µ =ν,
then X† = T (X) has law ν. The associated coupling is (Id,T )#µ. In this case every observed
point is assigned a unique counterfactual point.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 230/416

=== page 231 ===
14.6 Optimal transport as a selection rule
Definition 14.4 (Transport-based counterfactual map). Let µ,ν∈P(X ). A measurable map
T : X→X is a transport-based counterfactual map from µ to ν if T#µ = ν. The counterfactual
profile associated withX∼µ is
X† =T (X).
Proposition 14.3 (Maps are graph couplings). Let T : X→X be measurable and let µ∈P(X ).
Then
T#µ =ν ⇐⇒ (Id,T )#µ∈ Π(µ,ν).
Moreover, the coupling (Id,T )#µ is concentrated on the graph of T.
Proof. This is the standard graph-plan construction of Monge transport; see Chapter 1 of Villani
2003, Chapter 1 of Figalli and Glaudo 2021, and Ambrosio et al. 2021. Sketch of the proof: for
any measurable A⊆X,
(Id,T )#µ(A×X ) =µ(A),
so the first marginal is µ. For any measurable B⊆X,
(Id,T )#µ(X×B) =µ(T−1(B)) =T#µ(B).
Hence the second marginal is ν exactly when T#µ = ν. The graph concentration follows because
(x,T (x)) lies on the graph of T for µ-almost every x.
Deterministic maps are attractive for explanation because they provide a single answer. But
they can be too restrictive: discrete mass may need to be split, and in many applications a
stochastic counterfactual rule is more realistic.
14.6 Optimal transport as a selection rule
The set Π(µ,ν) contains many counterfactual matchings. Optimal transport selects one by
minimizing an explicit cost. Let c : X×X→ R∪{+∞} be a lower semicontinuous cost. The
optimal transport problem is
OTc(µ,ν) = inf γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
X×X
c(x,x†)γ( dx, dx†).
In counterfactual language, c(x,x†) is the cost of declaring x† to be the alternative version
of x. This cost may measure physical distance, financial effort, behavioural change, social
plausibility, causal implausibility, legal inadmissibility or statistical displacement.
Theorem 14.1 (Existence of an optimal counterfactual coupling). Let X be Polish. Let
µ,ν∈P(X ), and let c : X×X→ [0, +∞] be lower semicontinuous. If there exists at least one
coupling γ∈ Π(µ,ν) with finite cost, then there exists an optimal coupling γ⋆ ∈ Π(µ,ν) such that
∫
c dγ⋆ = inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
c dγ.
Proof. This is the standard existence theorem for Kantorovich’s problem; see Theorem 4.1 in
Villani 2008, Chapter 1 of Villani 2003, and Ambrosio et al. 2021. Sketch of the proof: take a
minimizing sequence (γn) ⊂ Π(µ,ν). The set of couplings is tight and closed, hence compact
for weak convergence on Polish spaces. Extract a weakly convergent subsequence γnk ⇒ γ⋆.
Closedness of the marginal constraints gives γ⋆ ∈ Π(µ,ν). Lower semicontinuity of the integral
functional γ↦→
∫
c dγ gives ∫
c dγ⋆ ≤ lim inf
k
∫
c dγnk,
which proves optimality.
Page 231/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0
