14 COUNTERFACTUAL WORLDS AND TRANSPORT
Actuarial takeaway
Optimaltransportdoesnotidentifythetruecounterfactualworld. Itselectsacounterfactual
world by a declared criterion. This is a strength for governance: the cost function can be
discussed, challenged and documented.
14.7 One-dimensional counterfactuals
On the real line, the monotone transport gives an explicit deterministic counterfactual map. Let
µ,ν∈P(R), with distribution functions Fµ,Fν and quantile functions
Qµ =F−1 µ , Qν =F−1 ν .
This notation is the same as in the Wasserstein and risk-measure chapters. If X∼µ, define
T (x) =Qν(Fµ(x))
when µ is continuous. More generally, the increasing rearrangement is represented by
X =Qµ(U), X† =Qν(U), U∼ Unif(0, 1).
Proposition 14.4 (Quantile counterfactual on the real line). Let µ,ν∈Pp(R) and let U∼
Unif(0, 1). Then
(X,X†) = ( Qµ(U),Qν(U) )
defines a coupling of µ and ν. Moreover, for the cost c(x,y) = |x−y|p, this coupling is optimal
and
Wp p(µ,ν) =
∫ 1
0
⏐⏐Qµ(u)−Qν(u) ⏐⏐p du.
Proof. This is the classical one-dimensional monotone rearrangement theorem; see Chapter 2 of
Villani 2003, Chapter 2 of Figalli and Glaudo 2021, and the computational account in Peyré and
Cuturi 2019. Sketch of the proof: the quantile transform gives F−1 µ (U) ∼µ and F−1 ν (U) ∼ν,
hence the pair is a coupling. Optimality follows from monotonicity: if two pairs are crossed,
replacing (x1,y2), (x2,y1) by (x1,y1), (x2,y2), with x1 ≤ x2 and y1 ≤ y2, cannot increase the
convex cost |x−y|p. Iterating this uncrossing argument yields the increasing coupling, and
integration over quantiles gives the formula.
Example 14.1 (Severity ranks). Let µ be the distribution of baseline claim severities and
ν the distribution under a stress scenario. The monotone counterfactual map sends each
baseline severity quantile to the same stressed severity quantile. It preserves ranks. This is
often interpretable, but it may be inappropriate if the stress changes the ordering of risks in a
meaningful way.
14.8 Gaussian counterfactuals
In multivariate actuarial examples, Gaussian approximations often appear: log-loss factors after
transformation, latent scores, reserving residuals or climate-index perturbations. For quadratic
cost, the optimal map between non-degenerate Gaussian distributions is affine.
Proposition 14.5 (Gaussian quadratic transport). Let µ = N (m0, Σ0) and ν = N (m1, Σ1) on
Rd, where Σ0 and Σ1 are positive definite. For the quadratic cost c(x,y) = ∥x−y∥2 (equivalently,
for W 2 2 ), the optimal transport map from µ to ν is
T (x) =m1 +A(x−m0),
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 232/416

=== page 233 ===
14.9 Individual, distributional and actuarial counterfactuals
where
A = Σ−1/2 0 ( Σ1/2 0 Σ1Σ1/2 0 )1/2Σ−1/2 0 .
In particular, T#µ =ν, and
W2 2(µ,ν) =∥m0 −m1∥2 + tr ( Σ0 + Σ1 − 2(Σ1/2 0 Σ1Σ1/2 0 )1/2 ) .
Proof. This is the standard Gaussian formula for quadratic optimal transport; see Chapter 2 of
Villani 2003, Chapter 7 of Villani 2008, and the Gaussian examples in Peyré and Cuturi 2019.
Sketch of the proof: if X∼N (m0, Σ0), then T (X) is Gaussian with mean m1 and covariance
AΣ0A. The displayed matrix A is symmetric positive definite and satisfies AΣ0A = Σ1, hence
T#µ = ν. Brenier’s theorem identifies the quadratic optimal map as the gradient of a convex
function; the affine map above has symmetric positive definite linear part, so it is such a gradient.
Uniqueness of the Brenier map gives optimality.
14.9 Individual, distributional and actuarial counterfactuals
It is helpful to separate three levels.
Individual causal counterfactual. For unit i, an expression such as Yi(1) −Yi(0) compares
two potential outcomes of the same unit. It requires a causal model or an identification design.
Distributional counterfactual. For a population, a counterfactual law ν describes the
distribution of outcomes in an alternative world. We may compare µ and ν by expectations,
quantiles, Wasserstein distances or stochastic orders.
Actuarial counterfactual. For a loss variable L, a risk functional ρ produces quantities such
as
ρ(L†)−ρ(L), ρ∈{E, VaRα, TVaRα, ESα, SCR}.
This is the natural scale for pricing, capital, reserving, prevention and reinsurance.
Definition 14.5 (Transport-induced actuarial counterfactual). Let L = ℓ(X) be a loss generated
by an observed profile X∼µ. Let γ∈ Π(µ,ν) and (X,X†) ∼γ. Given a loss function ℓ† in the
counterfactual world, define
L† =ℓ†(X†).
For a risk functional ρ, the transport-induced actuarial counterfactual effect is
∆ρ(γ) =ρ(L†)−ρ(L).
Proposition 14.6 (Dependence of pathwise effects on the coupling). Let L = ℓ(X) and L† =
ℓ†(X†). Distributional quantities depending only on Law(L) and Law(L†), such as E[L†] − E[L],
are determined by the two marginal laws. Pathwise quantities depending on (L,L†), such as
E[|L†−L|] or P(L†<L), depend on the coupling.
Proof. This is the elementary distinction between marginal functionals and joint functionals; it
is the same distinction underlying the Fréchet class in actuarial dependence theory and the set
of couplings in Villani 2003; Villani 2008. Sketch of the proof: if a functional can be written
as
∫
h(l) Law(L)( dl) and
∫
h†(l) Law(L†)( dl), then it is determined by the marginal laws. In
contrast, E[|L†−L|] = ∫ |l†−l|η( dl, dl†), where η = Law(L,L†). The joint law η is induced by
the coupling γ, and different couplings with the same marginals can give different values, as in
Proposition 14.2.
Page 233/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

14.10 Counterfactual explanations and recourse in insurance
Counterfactual explanations are often individual and local. Given a model m, a policyholder
profilex, and a target premium p⋆, one may solve
x⋆ ∈ arg min
z∈A(x)
d(x,z) subject to m(z)≤p⋆.
The set A(x) is crucial. It encodes which changes are feasible, actionable, legal and meaningful.
In insurance, the following distinction matters.
Type of variable Counterfactual recourse interpretation
Contract choices Deductible, limit, coverage, usage-based options; often
actionable
Behavioural variables Mileage, prevention, maintenance, security practices;
potentially actionable but costly
Historical variables Past claims, previous defaults, prior incidents; not di-
rectly changeable
Contextual variables Territory, occupation, climate exposure; sometimes
changeable, often socially constrained
Protected attributes Sex, race, disability, age in some contexts; not legitimate
recourse variables
Proxy variables May be technically actionable but normatively problem-
atic
Proposition 14.7 (Linear premium recourse). Let m(x) = β0 + β⊤x on Rd. Suppose all
coordinates are actionable and distance is Euclidean. If m(x) >p⋆ and β̸= 0, the closest point
satisfying m(z)≤p⋆ is
z⋆ =x− m(x)−p⋆
∥β∥2 β.
If m(x)≤p⋆, thenz⋆ =x.
Proof. This is the Euclidean projection onto a closed half-space; see the projection theorem
in Hilbert spaces in Bauschke and Combettes 2017 and the convex optimization background
in Boyd and Vandenberghe 2004. Sketch of the proof: the feasible set is the half-space H =
{z : β⊤z≤ p⋆ −β0}. If x∈ H, the projection is x. Otherwise, the projection lies on the
boundary β⊤z = p⋆ −β0 and has the form z = x−tβ. Enforcing the boundary condition gives
t = (m(x)−p⋆)/∥β∥2.
Actuarial takeaway
A recourse recommendation is not neutral. Telling a policyholder to increase a deductible
is different from telling them to move neighborhood, change occupation, or alter a proxy
for a protected attribute. Counterfactual explanations need actuarial, legal and ethical
constraints, not only a distance function.
14.11 Catastrophe and disaster counterfactuals
Catastrophe risk is naturally counterfactual. The observed historical record is only one realization.
Many near misses did not enter loss databases because no large loss occurred. Counterfactual
disaster analysis asks what perturbation of a historical event would have generated a severe loss,
and how plausible that perturbation was.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 234/416

=== page 235 ===
14.12 Cyber counterfactuals and causal attribution
A simple representation is the following. For each historical event j, define possible counter-
factual losses
C(j) 1 ,C(j) 2 ,...,C(j) K
with probabilities
p(j) 1 ,p(j) 2 ,...,p(j) K .
The event generates a local counterfactual loss distribution
νj =
K∑
k=1
p(j) k δC(j) k .
Aggregating across historical events produces a counterfactual catalogue. This is close to
catastrophe modelling, but with a past event as the anchor and perturbations as the scenario
generator.
Definition 14.6 (Counterfactual catastrophe catalogue). Let historical events be indexed by
j = 1,...,J. For each event, let νj ∈P(R+) be a distribution of counterfactual losses generated
by perturbing the event. Given event weights wj ≥ 0, ∑ j wj = 1, the counterfactual catalogue
distribution is
νcat =
J∑
j=1
wjνj.
Proposition 14.8 (Risk metrics from a counterfactual catalogue). Let L†∼νcat. Then expected
annual loss, value-at-risk and tail value-at-risk are computed from the single distribution νcat:
EAL† = E[L†], VaR† α =F−1 νcat(α),
and, when integrable,
TVaR† α = 1 1−α ∫ 1 α F−1 νcat(u) du.
Proof. This is the standard quantile representation of VaR and TVaR; see actuarial risk-measure
references such as Denuit et al. 2005a and the quantile notation in Charpentier 2024. Sketch of
the proof: by definition, VaR is the generalized inverse of the distribution function. For integrable
nonnegative losses, TVaR is the average of upper quantiles. Applying these definitions to the
distribution νcat gives the formulas.
14.12 Cyber counterfactuals and causal attribution
Cyber risk is particularly sensitive to counterfactual thinking because historical data are unstable.
A vulnerability may be patched after an incident. Attackers adapt. Reporting changes. Legal
liability depends on whether a loss would have happened under reasonable security practices.
Let A denote a security action, such as multi-factor authentication, segmentation, patching,
backup isolation or incident response. Let L(A) be the loss under action A. A causal cyber
question is
L(A = 1)−L(A = 0),
where A = 1 may mean that a control is implemented. A distributional cyber question is
Law(L|A = 1) versus Law(L|A = 0),
possibly after controlling for firm size, sector and threat environment.
Transport can help describe how a loss distribution moves under a security intervention:
T# Law(Lwithout) = Law(Lwith).
But legal attribution usually needs more than this distributional movement. It asks whether
the intervention would have prevented this incident, not only whether it improves the portfolio
distribution.
Page 235/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 236 ===
14 COUNTERFACTUAL WORLDS AND TRANSPORT
Remark 14.2 (Cyberinsurance and non-stationarity). Cyber counterfactuals often mix causal and
distributional questions. Pricing requires a distribution under future threat conditions. Liability
requires a causal statement about a specific incident. Governance requires documenting the
assumptions connecting the two.
14.13 Prevention counterfactuals
Prevention is a central actuarial counterfactual. Let L(0) denote the loss without prevention and
L(1) the loss with prevention. A prevention benefit can be expressed at several levels:
∆E = E[L(0)]− E[L(1)],
∆VaRα = VaRα(L(0))− VaRα(L(1)),
∆TVaRα = TVaRα(L(0))− TVaRα(L(1)).
These quantities may tell different stories. A prevention action can reduce frequent small losses
while leaving the tail unchanged; another can leave the mean almost unchanged while cutting
the extreme tail.
Definition 14.7 (Distributional prevention value). Let µ0 = Law(L(0)) and µ1 = Law(L(1)).
For a risk functional ρ, the distributional prevention value is
Vρ(µ0,µ1) =ρ(µ0)−ρ(µ1),
where ρ(µ) denotes the value of ρ(L) for L∼µ.
Proposition 14.9 (Mean prevention value is marginal). Let (L(0),L(1)) be any coupling of µ0
and µ1, with both means finite. Then
E[L(0) −L(1)] =
∫
lµ0( dl)−
∫
lµ1( dl),
so the mean prevention value does not depend on the coupling. By contrast, P(L(1) <L(0)) and
E[(L(0) −L(1))+] generally depend on the coupling.
Proof. This is the linearity of expectation under couplings; compare the marginal-versus-joint
distinction in Villani 2008 and actuarial dependence discussions in Denuit et al. 2005a. Sketch of
the proof: for any coupling η∈ Π(µ0,µ1),
∫
(l0 −l1)η( dl0, dl1) =
∫
l0 µ0( dl0)−
∫
l1 µ1( dl1),
because the marginals of η are µ0 and µ1. The final claim follows because nonlinear functions of
the pair depend on its joint law, as shown by the two-point example in Proposition 14.2.
Actuarial takeaway
For prevention accounting, it is often safer to report marginal distributional benefits such
as ∆E, ∆VaR and ∆TVaR. Statements such as “this specific loss was avoided” require a
stronger coupling or causal model.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 236/416

14.14 Sequential and graph-aligned counterfactuals
14.14 Sequential and graph-aligned counterfactuals
A global transport map can be difficult to interpret when covariates have an internal structure.
Suppose the variables satisfy a graph
X1 −→X2 −→X3 −→Y.
If we transport (X1,X2,X3) as a single vector, we may align the joint distribution while obscuring
how each variable changes conditionally on its parents. A graph-aligned counterfactual transports
variables sequentially.
Let G be a directed acyclic graph on X = (X1,...,Xd). For two groups A = 0 and A = 1,
write the conditional factorization
P (X|A =a) =
d∏
j=1
P
(
Xj |Xpa(j),A =a
)
for a topological order. A sequential transport constructsX† one coordinate at a time:
X† 1 =T1(X1),
X† j =Tj ( Xj |X† pa(j) ) , j = 2,...,d,
where each Tj transports a conditional law in group 0 toward a conditional law in group 1.
Definition14.8 (Graph-alignedtransportcounterfactual). Let GbeaDAGonX = (X1,...,Xd).
A graph-aligned transport counterfactual from group 0 to group 1 is a sequential construction of
X† such that, for each node j, the conditional law of X† j given its already transported parents is
aligned with the target conditional law
Law(Xj |Xpa(j),A = 1).
Proposition 14.10 (Sequential factorization preserves the target law). Assume that, in a
topological order, each step of a sequential construction samples or transports X† j from the target
conditional kernel
P1( dxj |x† pa(j)) = Law(Xj ∈ dxj |Xpa(j) =x† pa(j),A = 1).
ThenX†∼ Law(X|A = 1).
Proof. This is the standard chain-rule construction of a joint distribution from conditional
kernels on a DAG; compare the disintegration theorem in Villani 2008, the causal factorization
used in Pearl 2009, and graph-aligned transport constructions such as Fernandes Machado and
Charpentier 2026. Sketch of the proof: in a topological order, the joint law generated by the
sequential kernels is
d∏
j=1
P1( dxj |xpa(j)),
which is precisely the conditional factorization of Law(X|A = 1) under the DAG. Therefore the
generated vector has the target group law.
Remark 14.3 (Sequential transport is still not automatically causal). The graph may encode
a statistical ordering, a causal ordering, or an audit ordering. Only in the causal case, with
appropriate structural assumptions, should the sequentially transported profile be interpreted as
a causal counterfactual.
Page 237/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

14 COUNTERFACTUAL WORLDS AND TRANSPORT
14.15 What the cost means
The cost function is where mathematical transport becomes modelling. In actuarial counterfac-
tuals, common choices include:
• geometric costs, such as∥x−y∥2, for numerical covariates;
• rank costs, based on quantiles, for one-dimensional scores and losses;
• economic costs, such as the financial effort required to change a deductible, install
protection, or reduce exposure;
• plausibility costs, penalizing moves outside the support of the data;
• legal costs, assigning infinite cost to prohibited changes;
• causal costs, penalizing changes that violate a known structural ordering.
A cost is therefore not only a numerical device. It encodes a theory of what changes are
small, plausible, admissible or actionable.
Definition 14.9 (Actionability-constrained transport cost). Let A⊆X×X be the set of
admissible counterfactual moves. Given a base cost c0, define
cA(x,y) =
{
c0(x,y), (x,y)∈A,
+∞, (x,y) /∈A.
The corresponding optimal transport problem selects the cheapest admissible counterfactual
coupling.
Proposition 14.11 (Hard constraints as infinite costs). Let cA be defined as above. A coupling
γ∈ Π(µ,ν) has finite cA-cost only if γ(A) = 1. Conversely, if γ(A) = 1 and
∫
c0 dγ <∞, then
γ has finite cA-cost.
Proof. This is the standard way of encoding constraints in variational problems and optimal
transport; see the constrained formulations in Villani 2008 and the optimization background
in Boyd and Vandenberghe 2004. Sketch of the proof: if γ(Ac) > 0, then cA = +∞ on a set
of positive γ-mass, so
∫
cA dγ = +∞. If γ(A) = 1, then cA = c0 γ-almost surely, so the two
integrals coincide.
14.16 Mini-lab: transport counterfactuals in R
The following code illustrates three counterfactual matchings between two score distributions:
rank-preserving, independent and random permutation. The marginals are the same; the implied
individual displacements differ.
set.seed(123)
n <- 2000
# observed and counterfactual score distributions
x <- rnorm(n, mean = 0.0, sd = 1.0)
y <- rnorm(n, mean = 0.8, sd = 1.4)
# 1. rank-preserving / monotone transport
x _ord <- sort(x)
y _ord <- sort(y)
disp _ rank <- y _ord - x _ord

# 2. independent coupling
x _ind <- sample(x, n, replace = FALSE)
y _ind <- sample(y, n, replace = FALSE)
disp _ind <- y _ind - x _ind
# 3. reverse-rank coupling
x _ rev <- sort(x)
y _ rev <- sort(y, decreasing = TRUE)
disp _reverse <- y _ rev - x _ rev
out <- rbind(
rank _preserving = c( mean = mean(disp _ rank),
mean _ abs = mean( abs(disp _ rank)),
q95 _ abs = quantile( abs(disp _ rank), .95)),
independent = c( mean = mean(disp _ind),
mean _ abs = mean( abs(disp _ind)),
q95 _ abs = quantile( abs(disp _ind), .95)),
reverse _ rank = c( mean = mean(disp _reverse),
mean _ abs = mean( abs(disp _reverse)),
q95 _ abs = quantile( abs(disp _reverse), .95))
)
round(out, 3)
# all three have approximately the same marginal means difference,
# but the pathwise displacement metrics differ.
Computational note
This lab is deliberately simple. It shows the distinction between marginal effects and
coupling-dependent effects. The mean displacement is approximately the difference between
marginal means. The mean absolute displacement depends heavily on the coupling.
14.17 Schrödinger bridges and stochastic counterfactual paths
Optimal transport gives a static counterfactual rule. Given an observed law µ0 and a target law
µ1, one selects a coupling
γ⋆ ∈ Π(µ0,µ1)
that minimizes a cost. If the optimal plan is induced by a map T, the counterfactual version of
an observation x is T (x). This is a powerful construction, but it is static: it says where mass
starts and where it ends, not how it travels from one distribution to the other.
In many actuarial problems, the path matters. A loss distribution may evolve over several
years under climate change. A portfolio may drift gradually as new business enters and older
contracts lapse. A prevention policy may reduce risk progressively. A model intervention may be
phased in over renewal cycles. In such cases, a static counterfactual endpoint is not enough. We
may want a stochastic path
(Xt)0≤t≤1
such that
X0 ∼µ0, X1 ∼µ1,
while the whole process remains as close as possible to a reference dynamics.
This is the idea behind Schrödinger bridges; it connects the present lecture with dynamic OT
in Lecture ?? and entropic regularization in Lecture 6.
Page 239/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 240 ===
14 COUNTERFACTUAL WORLDS AND TRANSPORT
Definition 14.10 (Reference dynamics). Let R be a probability measure on path space
Ω =C([0, 1], Rd)
or, more generally, on a measurable space of trajectories. We write Xt(ω) = ω(t) for the canonical
process. The law R represents a reference dynamics, for example an uncontrolled diffusion, a
baseline portfolio evolution, or a prior scenario generator.
Definition 14.11 (Schrödinger bridge problem). Let µ0,µ1 ∈P(Rd). The Schrödinger bridge
from µ0 to µ1, relative to the reference path law R, is the solution of
inf
P
{
KL(P∥R) :P◦X−1 0 =µ0, P◦X−1 1 =µ1
}
,
where P ranges over probability measures on path space and KL(P∥R) is the relative entropy
of P with respect to R.
Thus the Schrödinger bridge is the least informative change of dynamics that forces the
prescribed initial and terminal laws. It does not choose the shortest path in a deterministic sense.
It chooses the path law closest to a reference stochastic model, under marginal constraints.
Actuarial takeaway
Optimal transport asks: what is the cheapest way to move the distribution µ0 into µ1?
A Schrödinger bridge asks: what is the least surprising stochastic evolution, relative to a
baseline model, that starts from µ0 and ends at µ1?
14.17.1 Static entropic transport as a two-time bridge
The link with entropic optimal transport appears when one looks only at the joint law of (X0,X1).
Let
R01 =R◦ (X0,X1)−1
be the two-time marginal of the reference dynamics. Then any path law P has a two-time
marginal
γ =P◦ (X0,X1)−1.
The static two-time projection of the Schrödinger bridge solves the entropy projection problem
inf
γ∈Π(µ0,µ1)
KL(γ∥R01).
If the reference two-time law has the Gibbs form
R01( dx, dy)∝ exp
(
−c(x,y) ε ) m( dx, dy),
for a base measure m, then this is equivalent, up to constants depending on the endpoints and
the base measure, to an entropic optimal transport problem of the form
inf
γ∈Π(µ0,µ1)
{∫
c(x,y)γ( dx, dy) +εKL(γ∥m)
}
.
A common discrete choice is m =µ0 ⊗µ1, but the reference law should be stated explicitly.
For Brownian reference dynamics on Rd, the transition density is Gaussian:
rε(x,y) = (2πε)−d/2 exp
(
−∥x−y∥2
2ε
)
.
The negative log-density is proportional to the quadratic transport cost. Thus the Schrödinger
bridge can be viewed as a stochastic, entropy-regularized version of quadratic optimal transport.

14.17 Schrödinger bridges and stochastic counterfactual paths
Remark 14.4 (Zero-noise limit). As the noise parameter ε tends to zero, the Schrödinger bridge
concentrates around optimal transport trajectories. In this sense, optimal transport is the
small-noise limit of Schrödinger bridge problems. Conversely, for positive ε, the bridge keeps
stochastic dispersion around the deterministic OT path.
14.17.2 The dynamic formulation
For a Brownian reference process, the Schrödinger bridge has a controlled diffusion representation.
Consider the reference dynamics
dXt = √ε dBt,
and allow a progressively measurable drift vt. The controlled process is
dXt =vt(Xt) dt + √ε dBt.
If ρt denotes the density of Xt, the pair (ρt,vt) must satisfy the Fokker–Planck equation
∂tρt +∇· (ρtvt) = ε 2∆ρt,
with boundary conditions
ρ0 =µ0, ρ1 =µ1.
With the convention above, Girsanov’s formula gives the following control cost. The dynamic
Schrödinger problem can be written formally as
infρ,v
{ 1
2ε
∫ 1
0
∫
Rd
∥vt(x)∥2ρt(x) dx dt
}
subject to the Fokker–Planck equation and the endpoint constraints.
This should be compared with the Benamou–Brenier formulation of quadratic optimal
transport:
W 2 2 (µ0,µ1) = infρ,v
{∫ 1
0
∫
Rd
∥vt(x)∥2ρt(x) dx dt
}
,
subject to the deterministic continuity equation
∂tρt +∇· (ρtvt) = 0.
The only difference is the diffusion term
ε
2∆ρt.
Thus Schrödinger bridges are to stochastic paths what Benamou–Brenier transport is to deter-
ministic displacement paths.
Actuarial takeaway
Benamou–Brenier gives a least-action deterministic trajectory of distributions. The
Schrödinger bridge gives a least-action stochastic trajectory, allowing random fluctuations
around the transported path. This distinction is important when actuarial trajectories
are not deterministic: claim processes, climate losses, lapses, portfolio renewal and cyber
events all include genuine noise.
Page 241/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

14 COUNTERFACTUAL WORLDS AND TRANSPORT
14.17.3 Counterfactual interpretation
Suppose that µ0 is the observed baseline distribution and µ1 is a counterfactual target distribution.
A static transport map answers:
if the observed state wasx, what target state T (x) should correspond to it?
A Schrödinger bridge answers a different question:
what stochastic evolution from baseline to target is closest to the reference dynamics?
This is useful when the counterfactual is not a one-shot change but a process. Examples
include:
• the counterfactual path of annual losses under a prevention policy;
• the path of portfolio composition under a different underwriting strategy;
• the evolution of claim severity under a climate adaptation scenario;
• the trajectory of scores under a phased model adjustment;
• the cyber-loss process under a different security investment trajectory.
The bridge therefore provides a stochastic counterfactual path, not merely a counterfactual
endpoint.
Definition 14.12 (Stochastic counterfactual path). Let R be a baseline path law and let µ0,µ1
be prescribed endpoint distributions. A stochastic counterfactual path is a path law P⋆ solving
P⋆ ∈ arg min
P
{KL(P∥R) :P0 =µ0, P1 =µ1}.
The intermediate laws
µ⋆ t =P⋆ ◦X−1 t , 0<t< 1,
are the counterfactual trajectory of distributions.
14.17.4 Why entropy is the right penalty
Relative entropy measures how much the counterfactual dynamics departs from the baseline
dynamics. If P≪R, then
KL(P∥R) = EP
[
log dP dR ] .
A small value means that the counterfactual path law is difficult to distinguish from the reference
path law. A large value means that the counterfactual dynamics requires a strong modification
of the baseline model.
This gives a useful modelling principle:
counterfactual path = baseline dynamics +minimal information update to match endpoints.
In actuarial language, the endpoint constraints encode the counterfactual facts we want to
impose. The entropy objective keeps everything else as close as possible to the reference model.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 242/416

=== page 243 ===
14.17 Schrödinger bridges and stochastic counterfactual paths
14.17.5 Example: prevention as a stochastic bridge
Let Lt denote the annual loss distribution over a time interval t∈ [0, 1]. Suppose µ0 is the current
loss distribution and µprev 1 is a target loss distribution after a prevention policy. A deterministic
OT map gives a direct transformation
Lprev 1 =T (L0).
A Schrödinger bridge instead constructs a path law
P⋆ = arg min
P
{KL(P∥R) :P0 =µ0, P1 =µprev 1 },
where R is a baseline stochastic loss evolution.
The intermediate laws
µ⋆ t = LawP⋆(Lt)
describe how the loss distribution changes during the prevention period. This matters when
benefits are not instantaneous. For instance, flood adaptation may gradually reduce moderate
losses before affecting tail losses; fire prevention may reduce frequency before reducing severity;
building-code changes may affect new exposures before old exposures.
Actuarial takeaway
A prevention policy is often evaluated by comparing a baseline loss law and a post-
prevention loss law. Schrödinger bridges add a dynamic layer: they describe a plausible
stochastic path between the two, constrained by the baseline dynamics.
14.17.6 Example: cyber counterfactuals
Cyber risk is dynamic and path-dependent. The loss distribution after a breach depends on
detection time, containment, propagation, backup restoration, liability notification and regulatory
response. A static counterfactual such as “what if the firm had invested in better security?”
hides this temporal structure.
Let R be a baseline incident-response dynamics calibrated from scenarios or expert judgement.
Let µ0 be the distribution of initial cyber states, and let µsecure 1 be a target distribution of
terminal losses under a stronger security posture. The Schrödinger bridge
arg min
P
{KL(P∥R) :P0 =µ0, P1 =µsecure 1 }
gives the least informative modification of the incident path that achieves the target terminal
law.
This can be read as a stochastic counterfactual scenario tool: rather than claiming that a
specific breach would have produced a specific alternative loss, the bridge produces a distribution
over alternative incident paths.
14.17.7 Discrete-time formulation
For actuarial applications, a discrete-time version is often more practical. Let
X0,X1,...,XT
be a Markov chain with reference transition kernels
Rt(xt−1, dxt).
Page 243/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 244 ===
14 COUNTERFACTUAL WORLDS AND TRANSPORT
The reference path law is
R( dx0:T ) =µR 0 ( dx0)
T∏
t=1
Rt(xt−1, dxt).
Given endpoint laws µ0 and µT, the discrete Schrödinger bridge solves
inf
P
{KL(P∥R) :P0 =µ0, PT =µT}.
Under standard positivity assumptions, the solution has the form
dP⋆(x0:T ) =f0(x0)gT (xT ) dR(x0:T ),
where f0 and gT are nonnegative scaling functions chosen so that the endpoint constraints hold.
This is the path-space analogue of Sinkhorn scaling.
Computational note
In a finite state space, the Schrödinger bridge can be computed by iterative scaling. One
alternately enforces the initial marginal and the terminal marginal, exactly as Sinkhorn
alternately enforces row and column sums in entropic optimal transport.
14.17.8 Finite-state actuarial example
Suppose a portfolio has three risk states:
X ={low,medium,high}.
The baseline transition matrix over one year is
K =

 0.85 0.12 0.03 0.10 0.78 0.12
0.04 0.16 0.80

.
Let µ0 be the current portfolio composition and let µcf 1 be a counterfactual target composition
after a prevention or underwriting policy. A static coupling describes how much mass moves
from each current risk state to each terminal risk state. A Schrödinger bridge instead modifies
the transition dynamics as little as possible, in relative entropy, while matching the target
composition.
For one period, this becomes the entropic coupling problem
min
γ∈Π(µ0,µcf 1 ) KL(γ∥µ0 ⊗K),
where
(µ0 ⊗K)(i,j) =µ0(i)Kij.
The solution has the scaling form
γ⋆ ij =aiµ0(i)Kij bj,
with positive scaling vectorsa andb chosen to match the two marginals.
Proposition 14.12 (One-period Schrödinger bridge as KL projection). Let Q be a strictly
positive prior joint law on a finite space X×X. Let µ0 and µ1 be endpoint laws with strictly
positive masses. Then the problem
min
γ∈Π(µ0,µ1)
KL(γ∥Q)

has a unique solution. It has the multiplicative scaling form
γ⋆ ij =aiQijbj,
where the positive scaling vectors a = (ai) and b = (bj) are chosen so that γ⋆ has row sums µ0
and column sums µ1.
Proof. The feasible set is a compact convex polytope and the relative entropy is strictly convex
on the positive orthant. Since Qij > 0 and the marginals are strictly positive, the optimum is
unique. The Lagrangian for the marginal constraints is
∑
i,j
γij log γij Qij + ∑ i αi

µ0(i)− ∑
j
γij

 + ∑
j
βj
(
µ1(j)−
∑
i
γij
)
.
The first-order condition gives log(γij/Qij) = αi + βj − 1. Setting ai = exp(αi − 1/2) and
bj = exp(βj − 1/2) gives the scaling form. The scaling factors are determined by the row and
column constraints, up to the usual common multiplicative normalization.
14.17.9 A minimal R lab
The following code computes the one-period finite-state bridge. It is just Sinkhorn scaling applied
to a prior joint law.
# One-period finite-state Schrodinger bridge
mu0 <- c(low = 0.50, medium = 0.35, high = 0.15)
K <- matrix( c(
0.85, 0.12, 0.03,
0.10, 0.78, 0.12,
0.04, 0.16, 0.80
), nrow = 3, byrow = TRUE)
colnames(K) <- rownames(K) <- names(mu0)
# Counterfactual terminal distribution:
# fewer high-risk policies after prevention
mu1 <- c(low = 0.58, medium = 0.34, high = 0.08)
# Prior joint law under baseline dynamics
Q <- diag(mu0) % * % K
sinkhorn _bridge <- function(Q, mu0, mu1, n _iter = 1000, tol = 1e-12) {
A <- Q
for (it in 1:n _iter) {
A <- A * (mu0 / rowSums(A))
A <- t( t(A) * (mu1 / colSums(A)))
err <- max( abs(rowSums(A) - mu0), abs(colSums(A) - mu1))
if (err < tol) break
}
A
}
Gamma <- sinkhorn _bridge(Q, mu0, mu1)
Gamma
rowSums( Gamma)
Page 245/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 246 ===
14 COUNTERFACTUAL WORLDS AND TRANSPORT
colSums( Gamma)
# Baseline and counterfactual transition matrices
K _baseline <- K
K _cf <- Gamma / mu0
K _baseline
K _cf
The matrix Kcf is the counterfactual transition matrix closest to the baseline transition
matrix K, subject to the target terminal composition. It can be interpreted as the least disruptive
transition adjustment achieving the counterfactual scenario.
14.17.10 Interpretation for actuarial governance
Schrödinger bridges are useful because they separate three ingredients:
1. the baseline dynamics R;
2. the counterfactual endpoint constraints µ0,µ1;
3. the entropy-minimizing adjustment from R to P⋆.
This separation is valuable in governance. The baseline dynamics can be documented as an
internal model or scenario generator. The endpoint constraints can be justified by a prevention
target, regulatory constraint, stress scenario or policy intervention. The entropy objective
provides a transparent rule for selecting the least informative path law compatible with these
constraints.
Actuarial takeaway
A Schrödinger bridge is not a causal model by itself. It is a principled selection rule for
stochastic counterfactual paths. Causal interpretation still requires assumptions about the
intervention, the structural mechanism and the meaning of the endpoint constraints.
14.17.11 Limitations
Schrödinger bridges should be used with care.
• They depend on the reference dynamics R. A poorly chosen baseline process leads to a
poorly justified counterfactual path.
• They enforce marginal constraints, but do not identify structural causal effects without
additional assumptions.
• The entropy criterion favours minimal information change, not necessarily economic realism
or legal admissibility.
• In high dimension, estimation of path laws can be difficult. Practical implementations
usually require Markov structure, finite-state approximations, Gaussian approximations or
entropic numerical methods.
• Endpoint constraints may be too weak. Many different path-level phenomena can share
the same initial and terminal laws.
Thus Schrödinger bridges should be presented as stochastic counterfactual scenario generators,
not as automatic causal truth machines.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 246/416

=== page 247 ===
14.18 A small dictionary for actuarial use
14.17.12 What to remember
• Optimal transport gives static counterfactual couplings or maps.
• Benamou–Brenier transport gives deterministic distributional paths.
• Schrödinger bridges give stochastic distributional paths.
• The bridge is selected by minimizing relative entropy with respect to a reference dynamics.
• In finite state spaces, the bridge is computed by Sinkhorn-type scaling.
• For actuarial science, this is useful for prevention, climate transition, portfolio drift, phased
model adjustment, cyber incident paths and dynamic scenario generation.
14.18 A small dictionary for actuarial use
Counterfactual object Actuarial interpretation
ν Alternative distribution: target group, future cli-
mate, post-prevention losses
γ∈ Π(µ,ν) Matching between observed and alternative worlds

distribution∫
c dγ Total plausibility, effort or displacement cost
Quantile transport Rank-preserving counterfactual for scores, premi-
ums or losses
Gaussian transport Linear distributional counterfactual for multivari-
ate normal approximations
Recourse pointx⋆ Individual explanation or recommended action
ρ(L†)−ρ(L) Actuarial value of a counterfactual scenario
Sequential transport Graph-alignedmediatororcovariatecounterfactual
14.19 What to remember
The main lesson of this lecture is not that optimal transport solves causality. It does not. The
lesson is that many actuarial counterfactuals are distributional, and optimal transport provides
a rigorous way to construct and audit distributional counterfactual worlds.
• Marginals describe the observed and alternative worlds.
• Couplings describe how those worlds are matched.
• Costs select which matching is preferred.
• Maps provide deterministic counterfactual profiles.
• Causal interpretation requires additional structural assumptions.

Exercise 14.1 (Two-point counterfactual worlds). Let µ = Bernoulli(p) and ν = Bernoulli(q).
Write all couplings as 2×2 matrices. Determine the admissible interval for a = P(X = 1,X† = 1).
Compute E[|X−X†|] as a function of a.

Exercise 14.2 (Non-identification). Let µ = ν = 1 2 δ0 + 1 2 δ1. Construct two couplings with the
same marginals but different values of P(X = X†), E[|X−X†|] and E[(X−X†)2]. Explain the
implication for individual counterfactual interpretation.
Exercise 14.3 (Premium recourse under a linear tariff). Let m(x) = β0 + β⊤x and suppose
m(x) >p⋆. Derive the closest Euclidean counterfactual z⋆ such that m(z⋆) ≤p⋆. Repeat the
calculation when only a subset of coordinates J⊂{1,...,d} is actionable.
Exercise 14.4 (Quantile transport between normal laws). Let µ = N (m0,σ2 0) and ν = N (m1,σ2 1)
on R. Show that the monotone transport map is
T (x) =m1 + σ1 σ0 (x−m0).
Compute W2 2(µ,ν).
Exercise 14.5 (Counterfactual catastrophe catalogue). A historical event has three counterfac-
tual losses C = (10, 50, 200) with probabilities p = (.8,.15,.05). Another event has counterfactual
losses (5, 30, 300) with probabilities (.7,.25,.05). Give equal weight to the two events. Compute

is the same under both, but P(L(1) <L(0)) differs.

corresponding infinite cost cA. Explain what happens if the target distribution ν has a different
marginal distribution of xN than µ.

Construct a sequential Gaussian transport from A = 0 to A = 1. Compare it with a global
Gaussian transport on (X1,X2).

=== page 249 ===
15 Dynamic Optimal Transport and Actuarial Trajectories
Learning objectives
The previous lectures treated optimal transport mostly as a static problem: compare two
distributions, compute a distance, find a coupling, or construct a barycenter. Many actuarial
questions, however, are dynamic. A portfolio ages, claim inflation evolves over calendar time, a
climate scenario modifies loss distributions gradually, a prevention policy changes losses over
many years, and a reserving model produces a future cash-flow trajectory rather than a single
scalar reserve.
This lecture introduces the dynamic viewpoint on optimal transport. The main idea is to
replace a single coupling between two distributions by a path of distributions and a velocity field
that moves probability mass through time.
After this lecture, the reader should be able to:
(i) distinguish a static transport plan from a dynamic transport trajectory;
(ii) describe a time-indexed family of actuarial laws (µt)0≤t≤1;
(iii) state the continuity equation for moving probability measures;
(iv) interpret a velocity field as the infinitesimal movement of risk mass;
(v) state the Benamou–Brenier dynamic formulation of W2;
(vi) compute one-dimensional Wasserstein geodesics through quantile interpolation;
(vii) write the Gaussian W2-geodesic and interpret mean and covariance movements;
(viii) define dynamic drift, action, path length and kinetic energy for actuarial trajectories;
(ix) formulate discrete-time dynamic transport problems for yearly portfolios, reserving scenarios
and climate trajectories;
(x) connect dynamic OT to climate adaptation, prevention accounting and scenario generation.
Further reading
The dynamic formulation of optimal transport goes back to the fluid-dynamical formulation
of Benamou and Brenier 2000; see also Villani 2003; Villani 2008; Ambrosio et al. 2005;
Ambrosio et al. 2021; Santambrogio 2015; Figalli and Glaudo 2021. Computational
aspects, including dynamic viewpoints, entropic variants and Sinkhorn-type algorithms,
are discussed in Peyré and Cuturi 2019. Statistical aspects of Wasserstein geometry are
treated in Chewi et al. 2025. Adapted and causal versions of transport for stochastic
processes are related to Lassalle 2018; Backhoff-Veraguas et al. 2020. Schrödinger bridges
and stochastic transport paths are surveyed in Léonard 2014; Chen et al. 2021. The
actuarial interpretation of moving loss, reserve, cash-flow and scenario distributions builds
on the distributional viewpoint developed throughout these notes and on the insurance
examples in Charpentier 2024; Wüthrich and Merz 2023; Denuit et al. 2005a.
Actuarial takeaway
Static OT compares two actuarial distributions. Dynamic OT describes how one distribu-
tion can move into another through time. It is therefore a useful language for portfolio
drift, climate transition, prevention effects, reserving cash-flow evolution and stochastic
scenario generation.
Page 249/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 250 ===
15 DYNAMIC OPTIMAL TRANSPORT AND ACTUARIAL TRAJECTORIES
Actuarial takeaway
A dynamic transport path is a distributional construction. It should not be read as an
identified causal trajectory for an individual policyholder, claim or asset unless a causal
model or intervention design has been added separately.
15.1 From static comparison to dynamic movement
Let µ0 and µ1 be two probability measures on Rd. In the static formulation of Lectures 3–5, an
optimal coupling
γ⋆ ∈ Π(µ0,µ1)
solves
inf
γ∈Π(µ0,µ1)
∫
Rd×Rd
∥x−y∥2 γ( dx, dy).
This tells us which source states x are matched to which target states y. It does not, by itself,
describe the intermediate states betweenx andy.
The dynamic viewpoint asks for a curve
(µt)0≤t≤1
with endpoints
µt=0 =µ0, µt=1 =µ1,
and for a velocity field vt(x) such that probability mass moves according to this velocity. Instead
of paying directly for the jumpx→y, one pays kinetic energy along the path.
This distinction matters in insurance. Suppose µ0 is the current loss distribution and µ1 is a
stressed climate loss distribution. A static Wasserstein distance answers:
how far are the two loss distributions?
A dynamic trajectory asks:
through which intermediate loss distributions could the portfolio move?
For adaptation, prevention and long-term solvency, the second question is often closer to the
actuarial problem.
Example 15.1 (Three actuarial trajectories).
(a) Portfolio ageing. Let Xt be the vector of covariates of a representative policyholder at
time t. Then µt = Law(Xt) is a path of portfolio distributions.
(b) Loss inflation. Let L0 ∼ µ0 and let Lt = (1 + it)L0, where it is cumulative inflation.
Then µt = Law(Lt) is a deterministic push-forward path.
15.2 Curves of probability measures
We work on Rd, equipped with the Euclidean norm. A curve of probability measures is a map
t∈ [0, 1]↦−→µt ∈P(Rd).
For dynamic W2-geometry, one usually assumes µt ∈P2(Rd), so that second moments are finite.

15.2 Curves of probability measures
Definition 15.1 (Actuarial distributional trajectory). An actuarial distributional trajectory is a
time-indexed family
t↦−→µt
where each µt is the law of the same type of actuarial object at time t: losses, covariates, reserves,
cash-flow vectors, mortality surfaces or exposure-weighted measures. The time scale must be
specified: calendar time, development time, underwriting year, climate horizon, valuation date or
intervention strength.
Definition 15.2 (Wasserstein absolutely continuous curve). A curve (µt)0≤t≤1 ⊂P2(Rd) is
absolutely continuous for W2 if there exists an integrable function m∈L1(0, 1) such that, for all
0≤s<t≤ 1,
W2(µs,µt)≤
∫ t
s
m(r) dr.
The smallest admissible instantaneous bound, when it exists, is the metric derivative
| ˙µt| = lim h→0
W2(µt+h,µt)
|h| .
This is the speed of the distributional trajectory.
Example 15.2 (Deterministic push-forward path). LetX0 ∼µ0, and define
Xt =Tt(X0),
where (Tt)0≤t≤1 is a family of measurable maps. Then
µt = (Tt)#µ0.
If Tt(x) = (1−t)x +tT (x), then
µt = ((1−t)Id +tT )#µ0.
This is the displacement interpolation associated with the map T, formalized in Definition 15.5
and Proposition 15.2.
Example 15.3 (Calendar-year severity inflation). Let L0 ∼µ0, and let cumulative inflation be
represented by a differentiable function at > 0, with a0 = 1. Set
Lt =atL0, µt = (x↦→atx)#µ0.
Then the trajectory is deterministic. A loss amount xt at time t evolves according to
˙xt = ˙at at xt.
The velocity field is therefore
vt(x) = ˙at at x.
This is a simple actuarial velocity: large claims move faster in absolute monetary units under
proportional inflation.

15.3 The continuity equation
A velocity fieldvt(x)∈ Rd describes how mass at locationx moves at time t. The evolution of
the probability law µt is governed by the continuity equation
∂tµt +∇· (vtµt) = 0.
This equation is understood weakly, because µt may not have a density.
Definition 15.3 (Continuity equation). Let (µt)0≤t≤1 ⊂P(Rd) and let (vt)0≤t≤1 be a Borel
vector field such that ∫ 1
0
∫
Rd
∥vt(x)∥µt( dx) dt<∞.
We say that (µt,vt) solves the continuity equation if, for every smooth compactly supported test
function φ∈C∞ c ((0, 1)× Rd),
∫ 1
0
∫
Rd
{∂tφ(t,x) +∇xφ(t,x)·vt(x)}µt( dx) dt = 0.
If µt has density ρt(x), the equation becomes the usual PDE
∂tρt(x) +∇·{ρt(x)vt(x)} = 0.
It says that probability mass is conserved as it flows.
Proposition 15.1 (Push-forward flows solve the continuity equation). Let Xt solve the ordinary
differential equation
˙Xt =vt(Xt), X0 ∼µ0,
where the flow is sufficiently regular. Let µt = Law(Xt). Then (µt,vt) solves the continuity
equation.
Proof. This is the standard weak form of mass conservation for a flow; see the dynamic formulation
in Villani 2008; Ambrosio et al. 2021; Figalli and Glaudo 2021. Sketch of the proof: for a smooth
test function ϕ, the chain rule gives
d
dtϕ(Xt) =∇ϕ(Xt)·vt(Xt).
Taking expectations yields
d
dt
∫
ϕ(x)µt( dx) =
∫
∇ϕ(x)·vt(x)µt( dx).
This is the weak form of ∂tµt + ∇· (vtµt) = 0. A time-dependent test function gives the displayed
distributional formulation.
Actuarial takeaway
The continuity equation says that risk mass is not created or destroyed along the trajectory.
This is appropriate for balanced transport: the portfolio distribution changes shape, but
total probability remains one. When exposure enters or leaves the portfolio, one needs
unbalanced transport or a source term.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 252/416

=== page 253 ===
15.4 The dynamic action
15.4 The dynamic action
Given a curve (µt,vt), define its kinetic action by
A(µ,v) =
∫ 1
0
∫
Rd
∥vt(x)∥2 µt( dx) dt.
This is the expected squared speed accumulated over time. It is the dynamic analogue of the
static quadratic transport cost.
Definition 15.4 (Admissible dynamic transport). A pair (µt,vt)0≤t≤1 is dynamically admissible
between µ0 and µ1 if:
(i) µt ∈P2(Rd) for each t;
(ii) (µt,vt) solves the continuity equation;
(iii) the endpoint constraints hold: µt=0 =µ0 and µt=1 =µ1;
(iv) A(µ,v)<∞.
The central theorem is that, for quadratic cost, the minimum dynamic action equals the
static squared Wasserstein distance.
Theorem 15.1 (Benamou–Brenier formula). Let µ0,µ1 ∈P2(Rd). Then
W2 2(µ0,µ1) = inf (µt,vt)
∫ 1
0
∫
Rd
∥vt(x)∥2 µt( dx) dt,
where the infimum is over all dynamically admissible pairs (µt,vt) joining µ0 to µ1.
Proof. This is the classical dynamic formulation of quadratic optimal transport; see Villani 2008;
Ambrosio et al. 2021; Figalli and Glaudo 2021; Peyré and Cuturi 2019. Sketch of the proof: first
assume an optimal Monge map T exists. Define
Xt = (1−t)X0 +tT (X0), X0 ∼µ0.
Then µt = Law(Xt) and the particle velocity is constant along each trajectory:
˙Xt =T (X0)−X0.
The action is ∫ 1
0
E∥T (X0)−X0∥2 dt = E∥T (X0)−X0∥2 = W2 2(µ0,µ1).
This proves that the dynamic infimum is at most W2 2. Conversely, for any admissible curve, a
particle representation or superposition principle gives trajectories whose expected squared length
is bounded by the action. By Cauchy–Schwarz, the squared endpoint displacement is bounded
by the time-integrated squared velocity. Taking endpoint laws yields a coupling of µ0 and µ1
with cost at most the action. Hence W2 2 is at most any admissible action. The two inequalities
give equality. The general case follows by approximation and the superposition principle.
Remark 15.1 (Why quadratic cost?). The kinetic-energy form is naturally quadratic. There are
dynamic formulations for other costs, but the cleanest Riemannian-type geometry appears for
W2. This is why W2 is central in Wasserstein geodesics, barycenters and gradient flows.
Actuarial takeaway
The Benamou–Brenier formula gives a physical interpretation of W2 2: it is the least kinetic
energy required to move a portfolio, loss or cash-flow distribution from its initial law to its
final law.
Page 253/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

15.5 Displacement interpolation as the least-action path
Assume that the optimal transport from µ0 to µ1 is induced by a map T. The least-action path
is
µt = ((1−t)Id +tT )#µ0.
This is the W2-geodesic between µ0 and µ1.
Definition 15.5 (Wasserstein geodesic). A curve (µt)0≤t≤1 is a constant-speed W2-geodesic
from µ0 to µ1 if
W2(µs,µt) =|t−s| W2(µ0,µ1)
for all s,t∈ [0, 1].
Proposition 15.2 (Displacement interpolation is a geodesic). Suppose T is an optimal W2-
transport map from µ0 to µ1. Define
µt = ((1−t)Id +tT )#µ0.
Then (µt)0≤t≤1 is a constant-speed W2-geodesic.
Proof. This is the standard displacement interpolation result in quadratic optimal transport; see
Villani 2003; Villani 2008; Figalli and Glaudo 2021. Sketch of the proof: let
Tt(x) = (1−t)x +tT (x).
For 0≤s<t≤ 1, the coupling induced by (Ts(X),Tt(X)),X∼µ0, gives
W2 2(µs,µt)≤ E∥Tt(X)−Ts(X)∥2 = (t−s)2E∥T (X)−X∥2.
Since T is optimal, the last expectation is W2 2(µ0,µ1). The reverse inequality follows from the
triangle inequality:
W2(µ0,µ1)≤ W2(µ0,µs) + W2(µs,µt) + W2(µt,µ1),
and the analogous upper bounds on the other segments. Equality must hold, so
W2(µs,µt) =|t−s|W2(µ0,µ1).
15.6 One-dimensional dynamic transport
Dimension one is again explicit. Let Q0 = F−1 µ0 and Q1 = F−1 µ1 be the quantile functions. The
W2-geodesic has quantile function
Qt(u) = (1−t)Q0(u) +tQ1(u), 0<u< 1.
Thus each rank u moves linearly from its initial quantile to its final quantile. Throughout this
section, quantiles use the lower generalized inverse convention; with atoms or ties, the path
should be read through quantile functions or randomized ranks rather than through a pointwise
unique transport map.
Proposition 15.3 (One-dimensional geodesic). Let µ0,µ1 ∈P2(R). Define µt by the quantile
function
F−1 µt (u) = (1−t)F−1 µ0 (u) +tF−1 µ1 (u).
Then (µt)0≤t≤1 is a constant-speed W2-geodesic.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 254/416

Proof. This follows from the one-dimensional quantile embedding of (P2(R), W2) into L2(0, 1);

Example 15.4 (Climate severity trajectory). Suppose µ0 is the baseline annual loss distribution
and µ1 is a 2050 climate-stressed distribution. The quantile path
Qt(u) = (1−t)Q0(u) +tQ1(u)
creates an interpretable family of intermediate scenarios. The 99.5% capital quantile along the
path is simply
VaR0.995(µt) = (1−t)VaR0.995(µ0) +tVaR0.995(µ1),
provided the same quantile convention is used. This is a rank-preserving stress trajectory.
Proposition 15.4 (Derivative of tail averages along a one-dimensional geodesic). Let Qt =
(1−t)Q0 +tQ1, and assume the relevant integrals are finite. Then for 0<α< 1,
d
dtTVaRα(µt) = 1 1−α ∫ 1 α {Q1(u)−Q0(u)} du.
Proof. Using the integrated-quantile representation,
TVaRα(µt) = 1 1−α ∫ 1 α Qt(u) du.
Since Qt is affine in t, differentiation under the integral sign gives the result.
Actuarial takeaway
Along a one-dimensional Wasserstein geodesic, VaR and TVaR evolve linearly in quantile
coordinates. This is often the simplest way to build interpretable capital trajectories
between baseline and stressed distributions.

Let
µ0 =N (m0, Σ0), µ1 =N (m1, Σ1),
where Σ0 and Σ1 are positive definite. The optimal W2-map from µ0 to µ1 is affine:
T (x) =m1 +A(x−m0),
with
A = Σ−1/2 0 ( Σ1/2 0 Σ1Σ1/2 0 )1/2 Σ−1/2 0 .
The displacement interpolation is
Xt = (1−t)X0 +tT (X0), X0 ∼N (m0, Σ0).

Therefore µt =N (mt, Σt), where

Σt = ((1−t)I +tA)Σ0((1−t)I +tA).
Proposition 15.5 (Gaussian W2-geodesic). The curve µt = N (mt, Σt) defined above is the
W2-geodesic betweenN (m0, Σ0) andN (m1, Σ1).
Proof. This is the standard Gaussian formula for quadratic optimal transport; see Villani 2008;
Chewi et al. 2025. Sketch of the proof: the optimal map between two non-degenerate Gaussian
laws is affine and given by T (x) = m1 +A(x−m0). Applying the interpolation map (1−t)Id+tT
to a Gaussian random vector preserves Gaussianity. Its mean and covariance are exactly the
displayedmt and Σt.
Example 15.5 (Dynamic capital model comparison). Suppose a multivariate capital model
represents line-of-business losses by a Gaussian vector. A stress scenario changes both the mean
vector and the covariance matrix. The Wasserstein geodesic decomposes the movement into:
(i) a linear movement of expected line losses;
(ii) a nonlinear movement of the covariance matrix;
(iii) an induced change in diversification along the path.
This can be more informative than jumping directly from baseline SCR to stressed SCR.
15.8 Actuarial speed, path length and action
A dynamic path (µt) can be summarized by several quantities.
Definition 15.6 (Distributional speed). If the metric derivative exists, the instantaneous
W2-speed is
s(t) =| ˙µt|.
For a smooth velocity representation, the speed satisfies
s(t)2 ≤
∫
∥vt(x)∥2 µt( dx),
with equality for the minimal velocity field.
Definition 15.7 (Path length and action). The W2-length of a curve is
L(µ) =
∫ 1
0
| ˙µt| dt,
and its quadratic action is
A(µ) =
∫ 1
0
| ˙µt|2 dt,
when these quantities are defined.
The path length measures cumulative distributional movement. The action penalizes rapid
movement more strongly.
Proposition 15.6 (Direct displacement is no larger than path length). For any absolutely
continuous path (µt)0≤t≤1,
W2(µ0,µ1)≤L(µ).
If the curve is a constant-speed geodesic, equality holds.

Proof. This is a direct consequence of the definition of metric derivative in a metric space. For a

W2(µ0,µ1)≤

Actuarial takeaway
A portfolio may end close to where it started but experience large intermediate movement.
The endpoint distance W2(µ0,µ1) misses this churn. The path length L(µ) captures
cumulative distributional movement.
15.9 Discrete-time actuarial trajectories
In practice, we often observe distributions at discrete dates:
µ0,µ1,...,µT.
For example, µt may be the distribution of claim severities in calendar year t, the distribution of

valuations.
A simple dynamic diagnostic is the sequence of one-step Wasserstein distances
Dt = W2(µt,µt+1), t = 0,...,T− 1.

15.10 Dynamic couplings over several dates
The pairwise distances W2(µt,µt+1) compare adjacent dates. Sometimes one wants a joint
scenario path across all dates. This requires a multi-time coupling.
Definition 15.8 (Multi-time coupling). Given probability measures µ0,...,µT on Rd, a multi-
time coupling is a probability measure
Γ∈ Π(µ0,...,µT )⊂P((Rd)T +1)
such that the t-th marginal of Γ is µt for each t = 0,...,T.
A path sampled from Γ is
(X0,X1,...,XT ), Xt ∼µt.
The natural dynamic cost is
EΓ
[T−1∑
t=0
∥Xt+1 −Xt∥2
]
.
This leads to the discrete-time dynamic OT problem
inf
Γ: (Xt)∼µt
EΓ
[T−1∑
t=0
∥Xt+1 −Xt∥2
]
.
Proposition 15.9 (Pairwise Markov construction). Suppose, for each t, γt ∈ Π(µt,µt+1). Under
standard regularity assumptions, the pairwise couplings can be glued into a multi-time law Γ such
that (Xt,Xt+1)∼γt for each t. If each γt is optimal, then
EΓ
[T−1∑
t=0
∥Xt+1 −Xt∥2
]
=
T−1∑
t=0
W2 2(µt,µt+1).
Proof. This is an application of the gluing lemma for probability measures; see Villani 2008;
Ambrosio et al. 2021. Sketch of the proof: disintegrate each pairwise coupling as
γt( dxt, dxt+1) =µt( dxt)Kt(xt, dxt+1).
Starting from X0 ∼µ0, generate Xt+1 from Kt(Xt,·). This defines a Markov law Γ with the
desired pairwise marginals. The cost identity follows by summing expectations.
Remark 15.2 (Pairwise optimal is not always globally meaningful). Pairwise optimal couplings
minimize adjacent costs. They need not preserve individual identity, contractual continuity or
causal consistency across all dates. For policyholder-level modelling, additional constraints may
be necessary: same policyholder tracking, no impossible jumps in covariates, monotonic ageing,
renewal behaviour or lapse.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 258/416

=== page 259 ===
15.11 Adapted transport and non-anticipative actuarial paths
Actuarial takeaway
A multi-time coupling is a scenario generator. It turns marginal distributions at several
dates into full sample paths. This is useful for climate scenarios, reserve cash-flow paths,
portfolio migration and staged interventions.
15.11 Adapted transport and non-anticipative actuarial paths
The dynamic transport constructions introduced above compare trajectories of distributions.
In many actuarial applications, however, trajectories carry information over time. A coupling
between two stochastic processes should not use future information when matching the present.
This leads to the idea of adapted, or non-anticipative, transport.
Let
X = (X0,...,XT ), Y = (Y0,...,YT )
be two stochastic processes with laws µ and ν on a path space XT +1. A usual coupling
λ∈ Π(µ,ν) is a joint law of (X,Y ) with the prescribed marginals. It may, however, match
Xt using information from Y t+1,...,Y T, or match Y t using future values of X. This is not
acceptable when the coupling is meant to represent a feasible scenario transformation over time.
An adapted coupling imposes a causality constraint.
Definition 15.9 (Causal and bicausal couplings). A coupling λ∈ Π(µ,ν) is causal from X to
Y if, for each date t, the conditional law of Y 0:t given the whole path X0:T depends only on the
past and presentX0:t:
Lawλ(Y 0:t |X0:T ) = Lawλ(Y 0:t |X0:t), t = 0,...,T.
It is bicausal if the same condition holds in both directions, from X to Y and from Y to X. We
write Πad(µ,ν) or Πbc(µ,ν) for the corresponding adapted or bicausal feasible sets, depending
on the convention used.
The corresponding adapted transport problem is
inf
λ∈Πad(µ,ν)
Eλ
[ T∑
t=0
ct(Xt,Y t)
]
.
Actuarial takeaway
Ordinary transport compares distributions. Adapted transport compares stochastic pro-
cesses without allowing the coupling to look into the future. This is the right notion when
trajectories represent reserving paths, cash-flow projections, epidemic states, mortality
scenarios or portfolio evolution.
15.11.1 Why non-anticipativity matters
Consider two reserving scenarios
R0 = (R0 0,...,R0 T ), R1 = (R1 0,...,R1 T ),
where Rk t is the outstanding liability at valuation date t. A static coupling of the full paths may
match an early reserve under one model with a path whose later development is already known
under the other model. Such a coupling can be useful for comparing laws of paths, but it cannot
be interpreted as a feasible online transformation.
Page 259/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 260 ===
15 DYNAMIC OPTIMAL TRANSPORT AND ACTUARIAL TRAJECTORIES
For model monitoring, the actuary often wants a diagnostic of the following kind: given the
information available at time t, how much must the reserve process move to resemble the target
process? This is a non-anticipative question.
Similarly, for mortality and longevity projections, one may compare two stochastic mortality
surfaces
q0 x,t, q1 x,t.
A pathwise coupling should not match the mortality rate at date t using future realizations of
the mortality trend. The same issue arises in climate trajectories, cyber accumulation scenarios
and epidemic state processes.
15.11.2 A discrete-time formulation
Let FX t = σ(X0,...,Xt) and FY t = σ(Y 0,...,Y t). A bicausal coupling may be described by
the condition that, under λ,
Law(Y t+1:T |X0:T,Y 0:t) = Law(Y t+1:T |X0:t,Y 0:t),
and symmetrically with X and Y exchanged. Equivalently, the matching rule at time t cannot
depend on future observations.
For a finite state space, this becomes a dynamic programming problem. Suppose Xt,Yt ∈E,
with E finite. A path coupling is a probability mass function
λ(x0:T,y0:T ).
The marginal constraints impose
∑
y0:T
λ(x0:T,y0:T ) =µ(x0:T ),
∑
x0:T
λ(x0:T,y0:T ) =ν(y0:T ).
The adapted constraints restrict how the conditional kernels factor over time. A typical bicausal
factorization is
λ( dx0:T, dy0:T ) =λ0( dx0, dy0)
T−1∏
t=0
Kt( dxt+1, dyt+1 |x0:t,y0:t),
with kernels compatible with the marginal transition laws.
15.11.3 Actuarial path costs
The choice of path cost should reflect the actuarial object being compared. For cash-flow vectors,
one may use
c(X,Y ) =
T∑
t=0
vt|Xt −Yt|p,
where vt are discount factors or reporting weights. For reserving paths, changes near the valuation
date may matter more:
c(X,Y ) =
T∑
t=0
wt|RX t −RY t |p, w0 ≥w1 ≥···≥wT.
For epidemic or health-state trajectories, the cost may be a state-transition cost:
c(X,Y ) =
T∑
t=0
κt(Xt,Y t),

=== page 261 ===
15.12 Dynamic OT for reserving cash-flow trajectories
where κt penalizes mismatches between health states such as susceptible, infected, hospitalized,
disabled or recovered.
Computational note
For actuarial monitoring, adapted transport should be reported together with the ordinary
path Wasserstein distance. A large difference between the two indicates that the apparent
similarity of two path distributions relies on anticipative matching.
15.11.4 Connection with martingale and causal transport
Adapted transport is close in spirit to martingale optimal transport, but the constraints are
different. Martingale optimal transport imposes
E[Y t+1 |Y 0:t] =Y t,
or a similar financial consistency constraint. Adapted transport imposes non-anticipativity of the
coupling. In insurance applications, both may be relevant: market-consistent guarantees require
martingale constraints, while cash-flow, mortality and reserving paths require adaptedness.
Remark 15.3 (Governance interpretation). A non-anticipative transport diagnostic is useful
for governance because it respects the information structure of actuarial work. At valuation
date t, the actuary has observed the past, not the future. A path comparison that uses future
information may be mathematically valid, but it should not be interpreted as an implementable
scenario transformation.
15.12 Dynamic OT for reserving cash-flow trajectories
Reserving is naturally dynamic. A stochastic reserving model produces a random vector
R = (R1,...,RH),
where Rh is the future payment in development or calendar period h. A model gives a law
µ = Law(R)∈P(RH).
Comparing two reserving models can be done statically through W2(µ0,µ1), as in Lecture 11.
But there is also a dynamic viewpoint across valuation dates. At valuation date t, the predictive
law of future cash-flows is
µt = Lawt(Rt+1,...,Rt+H).
As claims develop, µt should evolve. A large distributional speed W2(µt,µt+1) may indicate:
(i) new information from reported claims;
(ii) changes in inflation assumptions;
(iii) calendar-year effects;
(iv) model instability;
(v) large payments or case reserve revisions.
Example 15.7 (Reserve revaluation shock). Let µt be the predictive distribution of total unpaid
claims at quarter t. If
W2(µt,µt+1)
is much larger than usual, the reserve distribution has moved significantly. This may happen
even if the best estimate changes only moderately, because the tail or dispersion of the reserve
distribution may have shifted.
Page 261/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

15 DYNAMIC OPTIMAL TRANSPORT AND ACTUARIAL TRAJECTORIES
15.13 Dynamic OT for climate and prevention
Climate risk is not a one-step perturbation. A baseline climate distribution, an intermediate
transition distribution and a long-run stressed distribution should be linked by a trajectory. Such
a trajectory is a distributional scenario device. It is not, by itself, an identification argument for
the causal effect of a prevention policy on an individual loss.
Let µt denote the annual aggregate loss distribution at climate time t, where t = 0 is current
climate and t = 1 is a long-run scenario. A dynamic OT path can be used to define:
(i) a smooth climate stress trajectory;
(ii) a prevention trajectory from no intervention to intervention;
(iii) a sequence of adaptation benefits over time;
(iv) a cumulative prevented-loss measure.
If Lt ∼µt, the expected annual loss is
E[Lt] =
∫
xµt( dx).
Definition 15.10 (Dynamic prevention profile). Let (µ0 t )0≤t≤1 be a baseline loss trajectory and
(µ1 t )0≤t≤1 a post-prevention trajectory. A dynamic prevention profile is the family of distributional
differences
t↦−→
(
µ0 t,µ1 t,γt, ∆mean t , ∆tail t
)
,
where γt ∈ Π(µ0 t,µ1 t ) is a chosen comparison coupling, ∆mean t = E[L0 t ] − E[L1 t ], and ∆tail t denotes
a tail functional difference such as TVaRα(µ0 t )− TVaRα(µ1 t ).
A prevention effect may be summarized by
∆t = E[Lbaseline t ]− E[Lprevention t ],
but dynamic OT also measures how the full distributions move:
W2(µbaseline t ,µprevention t ).
Example 15.8 (Tail-focused prevention). Two prevention measures may reduce expected annual
loss by the same amount. The first reduces frequent small claims; the second reduces rare large
claims. Their expected benefits may match, but their dynamic quantile paths differ. In one
dimension, this is visible through
Qbaseline t (u)−Qprevention t (u), u∈ (0, 1).
A tail-focused prevention measure has larger differences near u = 1.
Actuarial takeaway
Prevention accounting should not only ask how much expected loss was avoided. It should
also ask which parts of the loss distribution were moved, when they were moved, and how
persistent the movement is. Dynamic OT provides a language for this comparison.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 262/416

=== page 263 ===
15.14 When total mass changes: toward unbalanced dynamics
15.14 When total mass changes: toward unbalanced dynamics
The continuity equation conserves total mass. Probability measures always have mass one, but
actuarial exposure may change. A portfolio can grow, shrink, lapse or acquire new business. A
climate model can change the number of exposed assets. A reserving triangle can gain or lose
observed cells as time passes.
To model changing exposure explicitly, one introduces a source term:
∂tηt +∇· (vtηt) =σt,
where ηt is now a finite exposure measure and σt creates or destroys mass.
Definition 15.11 (Source-term dynamics). A source-term dynamic transport model is a triple
(ηt,vt,σt) where ηt is a finite measure, vt is a velocity field, and σt is a signed measure satisfying
∂tηt +∇· (vtηt) =σt.
Positive mass in σt represents new exposure, while negative mass represents lapse, exit, death,
settlement or other disappearance of exposure.
In probability-normalized applications, the source term is hidden by renormalization. In
exposure-weighted applications, it is essential.
Example 15.9 (Lapse and new business). Let ηt be an exposure measure rather than a probability
measure. If policies lapse, some mass disappears. If new business enters, new mass appears.
Balanced OT can compare normalized distributions µt = ηt/ηt(Rd), but it cannot distinguish
portfolio composition drift from total exposure growth. Unbalanced transport is needed for that
distinction.
Remark 15.4 (Practical rule). If the actuarial question is about composition, normalize to
probability measures and use balanced OT. If the actuarial question is about both composition
and volume, keep exposure weights and use unbalanced OT or a source term.
15.15 Epidemic trajectories and state-dependent insurance risk
Epidemic risk creates time-varying heterogeneity in an insured population. The risk profile of
an individual depends on an evolving health state, and the aggregate claim process depends on
the changing composition of the portfolio. This is not well represented by a single static loss
distribution.
Let the health state space be
E ={Sus, Inf, Rec},
where Sus denotes susceptible, Inf infected, and Rec recovered or removed. At time t, let
µt ∈P(E)
be the distribution of the insured population across states. In a more detailed model, E may
include hospitalization, disability, long-term complications, death, return to work, or vaccination
status.
The insurance loss at time t is state-dependent. If ct(e) denotes the expected claim cost for
an insured in state e, then the expected portfolio cost is
E[Ct] =
∑
e∈E
ct(e)µt(e).
The full distribution of claims depends not only on the marginal state distribution µt, but also
on the dependence between individuals, common shocks and transition dynamics.
Page 263/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

15 DYNAMIC OPTIMAL TRANSPORT AND ACTUARIAL TRAJECTORIES
Dynamic optimal transport provides a language for comparing epidemic scenarios. Let
(µ0 t )T t=0 and (µ1 t )T t=0
be two trajectories of population state distributions, for example a baseline scenario and a severe
epidemic scenario. A simple trajectory discrepancy is
T∑
t=0
wtWp p (µ0 t,µ1 t ).
This compares state distributions date by date. A more dynamic comparison also penalizes the
movement between states over time.
Actuarial takeaway
Epidemic insurance risk is distributional and dynamic. The relevant object is not only a
claim severity law, but a trajectory of population compositions:
t↦→µt.
Transport compares how much mass must move between health states, calendar dates or
cost states to transform one epidemic scenario into another.
Suppose that the state distribution evolves according to a transition matrix Kt:
µt+1 =µtKt.
In an SIR-type model, the transition probabilities may depend on the current infected mass. For
example, in discrete time,
P(Sus→ Inf|µt) =βµt(Inf), P(Inf→ Rec) =γ.
Thus the transition kernel is endogenous. Two epidemic scenarios can differ because they have
different initial states, different transition intensities, different intervention policies, or different
reporting conventions.
A transport-based stress can be described at the level of trajectories:
µ0 −→µ1 −→···−→µT.
If the state space is finite, a dynamic coupling consists of a joint law over paths
(E0,...,ET ).
The cost of moving from one path to another can encode actuarial meaning:
c(e0:T,f0:T ) =
T∑
t=0
κt(et,ft) +λ
T−1∑
t=0
1{et̸=et+1},
where the first term compares health states and the second term penalizes transition events.
Remark 15.5 (Static versus dynamic comparison). A date-by-date comparison of µ0 t and µ1 t may
miss the timing of the epidemic wave. Two scenarios can have similar total infection counts
but very different peaks, durations and hospitalization timing. A path-level transport cost can
penalize these differences directly.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 264/416
