15.16 Entropic dynamics and stochastic paths
Insurance cash flows are obtained by pushing the state trajectory through a cost map. Let
ht :E→ R+
be the claim cost at time t. The cumulative discounted claim is
L =
T∑
t=0
vtht(Et).
Therefore
Law(L) =
(
e0:T ↦→
T∑
t=0
vtht(et)
)
#
Law(E0:T ).
This connects epidemic dynamics to the push-forward viewpoint used throughout these notes.
The same framework applies to multiple insurance lines:
Lhealth, Llife, Ldisability, Lbusiness interruption.
An epidemic can increase claims in some lines while reducing or delaying claims in others. The
aggregate effect depends on the joint law of the line-specific losses.
Computational note
A practical epidemic stress test can report:
T∑
t=0
W1(µbase t ,µstress t ), max t µstress t (Inf), Law
( T∑
t=0
vtht(Et)
)
,
and a capital measure of the resulting aggregate loss. The Wasserstein term measures
population-state displacement; the loss distribution measures financial impact.
Finally, epidemic trajectories are a natural example where balanced transport may be
insufficient. Entry, exit, death, lapse, delayed reporting and changes in exposure can alter the
total insured mass. In such cases, unbalanced transport or source-term dynamics are more
appropriate:
∂tρt +∇· (ρtvt) =σt,
where σt represents creation or destruction of exposure mass, as in Definition 15.11.
15.16 Entropic dynamics and stochastic paths
Exact dynamic OT produces least-action paths. Entropic regularization produces smoother,
more diffuse path measures. This is connected to the Schrödinger bridge viewpoint: among
stochastic processes matching prescribed endpoint distributions, choose the one closest to a
reference diffusion or random walk in relative entropy.
For these notes, the key idea is simple. Static OT selects a cheapest endpoint coupling.
Dynamic entropic OT selects a stochastic evolution that balances:
(i) endpoint constraints;
(ii) movement cost;
(iii) closeness to a reference stochastic dynamics;
(iv) computational smoothness.
Page 265/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 266 ===
15 DYNAMIC OPTIMAL TRANSPORT AND ACTUARIAL TRAJECTORIES
Example 15.10 (Stochastic climate scenario paths). A deterministic displacement path may be
too rigid for climate risk. A stochastic path allows individual scenarios to diffuse around a trend
while still matching endpoint distributions. Entropic dynamic OT provides a principled way to
construct such paths.
Computational note
Dynamic entropic transport is computationally more involved than pairwise Sinkhorn,
but the intuition is the same: exact deterministic motion is replaced by a smoother
probabilistic motion. For many actuarial dashboards, a practical first step is to compute
pairwise entropic couplings between consecutive dates and glue them into Markov scenario
paths.
15.17 Transport flows and synthetic scenario generation
Optimal transport gives deterministic maps and geodesic interpolations between probability
distributions. Modern generative modelling extends this idea: instead of only computing a
map between two known distributions, one learns a mechanism that transforms a simple latent
distribution into a complex target distribution.
LetZ0 ∼µ0 be a simple random vector, for example a standard Gaussian, and letZ1 ∼µ1
represent actuarial scenarios: claim amounts, catastrophe losses, reserve cash-flows, cyber
accumulation losses, mortality paths or economic risk factors. A generative transport model
seeks a transformation
Tθ : Rd → Rd
such that
(Tθ)#µ0 ≈µ1.
Once learned, the map can generate synthetic scenarios by sampling Z0 and computing Tθ(Z0).
Actuarial takeaway
Synthetic scenario generation is a transport problem: move a tractable latent law to a
realistic actuarial scenario law. The actuarial difficulty is not only to generate samples,
but to preserve tails, dependence, constraints and interpretability.
15.17.1 Deterministic flows
A continuous deterministic flow is defined by an ordinary differential equation
dZt
dt =vt(Zt), t∈ [0, 1],
with initial lawZ0 ∼µ0. If ρt = Law(Zt), then ρt solves the continuity equation
∂tρt +∇· (ρtvt) = 0.
Learning the vector field vt is one way to learn a generative transport from µ0 to µ1. This
viewpoint connects continuous normalizing flows, flow matching and dynamic optimal transport;
see, for example, Chen et al. 2018a; Rezende and Mohamed 2015; Dinh et al. 2017; Lipman et al.
2023a.
For actuarial applications, the vector field may be constrained to respect properties of the
scenario:
Zt ∈ Rd +,
d∑
j=1
Zt,j ≤M, Zt,1 ≤Zt,2 ≤···≤Zt,T,
depending on whether the scenario represents non-negative losses, limited coverages, or cumulative
paid losses.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 266/416

=== page 267 ===
15.17 Transport flows and synthetic scenario generation
15.17.2 Stochastic flows and diffusion models
A stochastic generative flow is defined by a stochastic differential equation
dZt =bt(Zt) dt +σt dWt.
Diffusion-based generative models use noise to move between data and latent distributions; see
Song et al. 2021a; Ho et al. 2020. In a forward diffusion, the data distribution is gradually noised
until it becomes close to a simple reference distribution. A reverse process is then learned to
transform noise back into data-like samples.
The deterministic and stochastic viewpoints are closely related. Deterministic flows provide
direct scenario maps. Diffusions provide stochastic scenario generation and may better represent
uncertainty, multimodality and model ambiguity.
Remark 15.6 (Map versus generator). A deterministic map sends the same latent point to the
same scenario. A diffusion generator adds stochasticity along the path. For actuarial stress
testing, the first is useful for traceability; the second is useful for rich scenario generation.
15.17.3 Scenario generation tasks
Transport-based generators are useful for several actuarial tasks.
Catastrophe and climate scenarios. A generator can produce spatial loss fields, rainfall
maps, wind fields or portfolio loss vectors. The transport structure can help align generated
scenarios with a baseline distribution, a climate-stressed distribution or an event catalogue.
Reserving cash-flow paths. A cash-flow scenario is a vector
C = (C1,...,CT ).
A generative model can learn the joint law of paid or incurred development paths, including
timing and tail dependence. Transport diagnostics can then compare synthetic and historical
cash-flow distributions.
Mortality and longevity paths. A mortality scenario may be a surface
q = (qx,t)x,t.
A generator can produce stochastic mortality improvements while preserving smoothness over
ages, cohort effects and plausible long-run trends.
Cyber accumulation. Cyber scenarios require dependence across firms, vendors and coverages.
A transport generator can learn or impose common shock structures, vendor concentration effects
and heavy-tailed accumulation losses.
15.17.4 Validation by transport diagnostics
Generated scenarios should be validated distributionally. If ˆµreal is the empirical law of observed
or benchmark scenarios and ˆµgen is the law of generated scenarios, one may report
Wp(ˆµreal,ˆµgen),
or sliced Wasserstein distances in high dimension:
SWp(ˆµreal,ˆµgen).
Page 267/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 268 ===
15 DYNAMIC OPTIMAL TRANSPORT AND ACTUARIAL TRAJECTORIES
For actuarial work, these global diagnostics should be complemented by tail and business metrics:
VaRα, TVaRα, E[(L−d)+ ∧ℓ], Corr(Li,Lj).
Computational note
A generative actuarial model should not be validated only by visual realism or average
error. A minimum validation report should include Wasserstein or sliced Wasserstein
distance, marginal calibration, tail calibration, dependence diagnostics and layer-specific
errors.
15.17.5 Stress generation by interpolation
A simple use of transport flows is to interpolate between a baseline law µ0 and a stressed law µ1.
If T transports µ0 to µ1, the displacement interpolation is
Zt = (1−t)Z0 +tT (Z0), t∈ [0, 1].
Then
µt = Law(Zt)
is a path of scenarios from baseline to stress. This gives a transparent stress ladder:
µ0,µ0.25,µ0.5,µ0.75,µ1.
For more complex settings, a learned flow can replace the explicit map T.
Remark 15.7 (Synthetic does not mean plausible). A generated scenario is not automatically a
plausible actuarial scenario. It must satisfy accounting identities, policy conditions, exposure
constraints, monotonicity constraints, tail behavior and expert review. Transport gives a geometry
for generation and validation, but governance remains essential.
15.17.6 Connection with Schrödinger bridges
Schrödinger bridges provide a stochastic transport between two endpoint distributions. They
can be viewed as the most likely stochastic evolution between µ0 and µ1 relative to a reference
diffusion. In scenario generation, this is useful when one wants both endpoint calibration and
stochastic path variability.
For example, suppose µ0 is a current reserve distribution and µ1 is a one-year stressed reserve
distribution. A deterministic map gives a single transformation of each scenario. A Schrödinger
bridge gives a distribution over intermediate paths, allowing stochastic transition scenarios
between the endpoints.
Actuarial takeaway
Transport flows, diffusion models and Schrödinger bridges are different ways of turning
distributional information into scenarios. The actuarial choice depends on the objective:
traceable stress maps, rich synthetic generation, or stochastic paths between calibrated
endpoints.
15.18 A mini-lab: dynamic severity stress in R
The following lab, which complements the statistical labs in Lecture 7, constructs a one-
dimensional Wasserstein geodesic between a baseline severity distribution and a stressed severity
distribution. It then tracks mean, VaR and TVaR along the path.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 268/416

=== page 269 ===
15.18 A mini-lab: dynamic severity stress in R
set.seed(123)
n <- 50000
u <- (1:n) / (n + 1)
# Baseline and stressed severity quantile functions
q0 <- function(u) qlnorm(u, meanlog = 8.0, sdlog = 0.75)
q1 <- function(u) qlnorm(u, meanlog = 8.25, sdlog = 1.00)
Q0 <- q0(u)
Q1 <- q1(u)
# Wasserstein-2 distance between endpoints
W2 <- sqrt( mean((Q1 - Q0)^2))
W2
# Integrated tail functional
TVaR _from _quantiles <- function(Q, u, alpha = 0.99) {
mean( Q[u >= alpha])
}
VaR _from _quantiles <- function(Q, u, alpha = 0.995) {
Q[ which. min( abs(u - alpha))]
}
# Dynamic path indexed by t
grid _ t <- seq(0, 1, by = 0.05)
out <- data. frame(
t = grid _t,
mean = NA _ real _,
VaR995 = NA _ real _,
TVaR99 = NA _ real _,
W2 _from _ start = NA _ real _,
W2 _to _target = NA _ real _
)
for (k in seq _along( grid _ t)) {
t <- grid _ t[k]
Qt <- (1 - t) * Q0 + t * Q1
out $ mean[k] <- mean(Qt)
out $VaR995[k] <- VaR _from _quantiles(Qt, u, alpha = 0.995)
out $TVaR99[k] <- TVaR _from _quantiles(Qt, u, alpha = 0.99)
out $W2 _from _ start[k] <- sqrt( mean((Qt - Q0)^2))
out $W2 _to _target[k] <- sqrt( mean((Qt - Q1)^2))
}
print(out)
par(mfrow = c(1, 3))
plot(out $t, out $ mean, type = "l", lwd = 2,
xlab = "stress␣time␣t", ylab = "mean␣loss")
plot(out $t, out $VaR995, type = "l", lwd = 2,
xlab = "stress␣time␣t", ylab = "VaR␣99.5%")
plot(out $t, out $W2 _from _ start, type = "l", lwd = 2,
xlab = "stress␣time␣t", ylab = "W2␣from␣baseline")
Along this geodesic, W2(µ0,µt) = tW2(µ0,µ1). The mean, VaR and TVaR evolve linearly
in quantile coordinates, but their monetary interpretation depends on the tail region under
Page 269/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

15 DYNAMIC OPTIMAL TRANSPORT AND ACTUARIAL TRAJECTORIES
consideration.
15.19 Practical workflow
A practical dynamic OT workflow for actuarial applications can be organized as follows.
Step 1. Define the moving object. Decide whether µt represents losses, covariates, reserves,
cash-flow vectors, climate scenarios or exposure-weighted measures.
Step 2. Choose the time scale. Calendar year, underwriting year, development period, quarterly
monitoring or climate horizon.
Step 3. Choose the ground metric. Monetary distance, standardized covariate distance, geo-
graphic distance, cash-flow vector distance, or a mixed actuarial cost.
Step 4. Compute one-step distances. Estimate W2(µt,µt+1) or a regularized approximation.
Step 5. Compute cumulative movement. Track path length, action and direct distance from
baseline.
Step 6. Interpret velocity. Identify which regions of the distribution are moving: low losses, tail
losses, high-exposure regions or long-tail reserves.
Step 7. Stress or intervene. Construct geodesic paths to target distributions for scenario analysis
or prevention.
Step 8. Report uncertainty. Use bootstrap, sensitivity to ground cost, and alternative radii or
regularization parameters.
15.20 A small dictionary for dynamic actuarial OT
Mathematical object Actuarial interpretation
(µt)t∈[0,1] Path of loss, reserve, cash-flow or scenario laws
vt Instantaneous velocity of risk mass
Continuity equation Conservation of normalized portfolio mass
Benamou–Brenier action Least kinetic energy of distributional movement
W2-geodesic Shortest path between two actuarial laws
Path length Cumulative distributional movement or churn
Multi-time coupling Γ Joint scenario law across dates
Adapted coupling Non-anticipative comparison of stochastic processes
Source term σt Entry, lapse, settlement, death or exposure growth
Schrödinger bridge Stochastic path calibrated to endpoint laws
Transport generator Map or kernel from latent scenarios to actuarial scenarios
15.21 Summary
Dynamic optimal transport turns Wasserstein distance into a theory of trajectories. A path of
probability measures (µt) evolves under a velocity field vt satisfying the continuity equation.
The Benamou–Brenier formula states that the squared W2 distance is the least kinetic action
needed to move µ0 into µ1. In one dimension, the geodesic is obtained by linear interpolation of
quantile functions. For Gaussian laws, the geodesic has explicit mean and covariance formulas.
For actuarial science, dynamic OT provides a language for portfolio drift, reserving revalua-
tions, climate transition, prevention accounting and scenario generation. Static OT tells us how
far two distributions are. Dynamic OT tells us how they move.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 270/416

=== page 272 ===
16 UNBALANCED TRANSPORT, EXPOSURE AND PORTFOLIO GROWTH
16 Unbalanced Transport, Exposure and Portfolio Growth
Further reading
Balanced optimal transport compares probability measures with the same total mass.
This is the right model when we compare two normalized loss distributions, two severity
distributions, or two reserving scenario distributions after rescaling to total mass one.
Many actuarial objects, however, are finite measures rather than probability measures.
A portfolio grows or shrinks; exposures enter and leave; claims are created by frequency
variation; insured values increase; climate change changes both the shape and the mass of
loss distributions. In such cases, forcing all mass to be matched is artificial.
This lecture introduces unbalanced optimal transport. The key idea is to relax the marginal
constraints of Kantorovich transport. Instead of requiring the first and second marginals
of a plan to be exactly equal to the source and target measures, we penalize deviations
from them. This allows mass to be transported, created or destroyed. The mathematical
references are the unbalanced and entropic transport chapters in Peyré and Cuturi 2019,
together with the measure-theoretic and dynamic background in Villani 2003; Villani 2008;
Ambrosio et al. 2021. For the modern theory of unbalanced, partial and Wasserstein–
Fisher–Rao transport, see Figalli 2010; Chizat et al. 2018b; Liero et al. 2018; Kondratyev
et al. 2016; Piccoli and Rossi 2014. The computational generalized Sinkhorn viewpoint
is closely related to Frogner et al. 2015; Chizat et al. 2018a. The dynamic source-term
formulation should be read together with Lecture 15, while the numerical implementation
below extends the entropic ideas of Lecture 6.
16.1 Learning objectives
After this lecture, the reader should be able to:
(i) distinguish probability measures from finite exposure measures;
(ii) explain why balanced OT can be misleading when total exposure changes;
(iii) formulate static unbalanced optimal transport with marginal penalties;
(iv) interpret the Kullback–Leibler marginal penalty for finite measures;
(v) derive and implement generalized Sinkhorn iterations for entropic unbalanced OT;
(vi) separate shape displacement, transported mass, created mass and destroyed mass;
(vii) connect unbalanced OT to lapse, new business, portfolio growth, claim frequency and
climate exposure;
(viii) understand the dynamic continuity equation with a source term;
(ix) use unbalanced transport as an actuarial diagnostic rather than only as a distance.
Actuarial takeaway
Balanced OT answers: how should one unit of mass be rearranged into one unit of mass?
Unbalanced OT answers: how much of the source mass should be transported, how much
should disappear, and how much target mass should be newly created? This is often the
actuarial question when portfolios, exposures or claim counts change.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 272/416

=== page 273 ===
16.2 Why balanced transport is sometimes the wrong model
all mass must be matched
Balanced OT
transport + lapse + new business
Unbalanced OT
Figure 10: Balanced and unbalanced transport for exposure changes. Balanced transport
forces all source mass to be matched to target mass. Unbalanced transport separates three effects:
transported exposure, disappearing exposure such as lapses, and newly created exposure such as
new business.
Actuarial takeaway
An unbalanced plan is a distributional diagnostic. It can suggest which parts of a source
measure are plausibly matched to a target measure and which parts are better read as
disappearing or newly created mass. It does not identify a causal migration mechanism for
a specific policy, exposure or claim without additional assumptions.
16.2 Why balanced transport is sometimes the wrong model
Let µ and ν be probability measures. Balanced optimal transport solves
inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
c(x,y)γ( dx, dy),
where the two marginal constraints are
(p1)#γ =µ, (p2)#γ =ν.
This is appropriate when the total amount of mass is the same and must be conserved.
But many actuarial objects are not naturally normalized to mass one. For instance:
• a portfolio exposure measure may have total mass equal to the number of vehicle-years;
• a territorial risk measure may have mass equal to total insured value in each region;
• a claim measure may have mass equal to expected claim count rather than one;
• a loss measure may have mass equal to total expected loss volume;
• a reserving cash-flow measure may have mass equal to expected future payments;
• a climate exposure measure may gain mass because more assets enter high-risk regions.
IfthetotalmasschangesfromM0 toM1, abalancedcomparisonhastwocommonworkarounds.
The first is to normalize both measures into probability measures and compare only their shapes.
Page 273/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 274 ===
16 UNBALANCED TRANSPORT, EXPOSURE AND PORTFOLIO GROWTH
The second is to compare total masses separately, for example through |M1 −M0|. Both are
useful, but they miss the interaction between shape change and mass change. Figure 10 illustrates
the difference between forcing all mass to move and allowing some mass to disappear or be
created.
Example 16.1 (A growing motor portfolio). Suppose that in year 0 an insurer has 100,000
vehicle-years distributed over territories, and in year 1 it has 130,000 vehicle-years. If we normalize
both territorial exposure measures to probability measures, we ignore the fact that the book grew
by 30%. If we compare total exposure only, we ignore where the growth occurred. Unbalanced
OT combines both questions: mass can be matched geographically where the same type of
exposure persists, but additional mass must be created in the territories where the portfolio
grows.
Example 16.2 (Frequency versus severity). A severity distribution is often normalized: condi-
tional on a claim occurring, the severity has law µ. A claim-cost measure may instead be
α =λµ,
where λ is expected claim frequency. If frequency changes from λ0 to λ1 and severity changes
from µ0 to µ1, then comparing only µ0 and µ1 ignores frequency. Unbalanced OT can compare
the finite measures α0 =λ0µ0 and α1 =λ1µ1 directly.
16.3 Finite measures as actuarial objects
Let X be a Polish space. We denote by M+(X ) the set of finite nonnegative Borel measures on
X. For α∈M+(X ), its total mass is
|α| =α(X ).
When|α| = 1, α is a probability measure. In unbalanced transport, we allow|α|̸=|β|.
Definition 16.1 (Finite positive measure). A finite positive measure on X is a nonnegative Borel
measure α such that |α| = α(X ) <∞. Its normalized shape, when |α|> 0, is the probability
measure α/|α|. In this lecture, |α| carries actuarial information and should not be silently
normalized away.
A finite exposure measure may be written as
α =
n∑
i=1
aiδxi,
where ai ≥ 0 is the exposure volume in cell i and xi describes the cell. The total mass ∑ iai is
not a nuisance: it is part of the actuarial object.
Definition 16.2 (Exposure measure). An exposure measure on a state space X is a finite
measure α∈M+(X ) whose mass α(A) represents the amount of exposure in states A⊆X. The
state x may encode territory, age, vehicle type, insured value, coverage, climate zone or a vector
of rating variables.
Definition 16.3 (Claim-intensity measure). Let µ be a probability distribution of claim amounts
and let λ> 0 be an expected number of claims. The finite measure
α =λµ
is called a claim-intensity measure. Its total mass is the expected claim count. Its normalized
shape is the conditional severity distribution.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 274/416

=== page 275 ===
16.4 Three ways to compare finite measures
Actuarial takeaway
A probability law answers a conditional question: what does a randomly selected claim,
policy or scenario look like? A finite measure answers an unconditional portfolio question:
how much mass is there, and where is it located? This distinction will reappear in the
reserving volume application in Section 16.19.
16.4 Three ways to compare finite measures
Let α,β∈M+(X ) and write
Mα =|α|, Mβ =|β|.
If both masses are positive, define normalized shapes
¯α = α Mα , ¯β = β Mβ .
There are at least three approaches.
Shape-only comparison. Compute Wp(¯α, ¯β). This compares the distribution of mass but
ignores the total change Mβ −Mα.
Mass plus shape comparison. Report both |Mβ −Mα| and Wp(¯α, ¯β). This is transparent
and often sufficient for a first report, but it does not say which part of the mass change is spatially
or actuarially aligned with the shape change.
Unbalanced comparison. Optimize over partial transport plans and penalize deviations from
the original marginals. This lets the model decide how much mass should be transported, created
or destroyed.
Example 16.3 (A localized exposure increase). Suppose that total exposure grows by 10%, all
in flood-prone coastal cells. A mass-plus-shape summary reports growth and distributional drift,
but it does not identify how much of the year-1 coastal exposure corresponds to transported
year-0 mass and how much should be treated as new business. An unbalanced plan gives such a
distributional attribution.
16.5 Partial transport as a first unbalanced idea
Before introducing marginal penalties, it is useful to consider partial transport. Suppose
α,β∈M+(X ) and choose a mass level m≤|α|∧|β|. A partial transport plan of mass m is a
measure γ∈M+(X×X ) such that
(p1)#γ≤α, (p2)#γ≤β, |γ| =m.
The partial transport problem is
infγ
{∫
c(x,y)γ( dx, dy) : (p1)#γ≤α, (p2)#γ≤β,|γ| =m
}
.
This transports only m units of mass. Unused source mass is left behind; unmatched target mass
is not explained by transport.
Remark 16.1 (Partial transport versus unbalanced transport). Partial transport fixes the trans-
ported mass m externally. Unbalanced transport typically lets the transported mass be chosen
endogenously through a trade-off: transporting mass has a cost, while destroying or creating
mass has a penalty. Partial transport is useful when the analyst has an externally meaningful
retained mass, whereas KL-penalized unbalanced OT is useful when the retained mass itself is
part of the diagnostic.
Page 275/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 276 ===
16 UNBALANCED TRANSPORT, EXPOSURE AND PORTFOLIO GROWTH
16.6 Relative entropy for finite measures
A standard way to penalize marginal deviations is through a divergence between finite measures.
The most common choice in computational unbalanced OT is the finite-measure Kullback–Leibler
divergence.
Definition 16.4 (Finite-measure KL divergence). Let ρ,α∈M+(X ). If ρ≪α and f = dρ/ dα,
define
KL(ρ|α) =
∫
X
(
f logf−f + 1
)
α( dx).
If ρ is not absolutely continuous with respect to α, set
KL(ρ|α) = +∞.
For probability measures, this coincides with the usual relative entropy, because both total
masses are equal to one. For finite measures, the terms−f + 1 are essential: they make
KL(α|α) = 0
and allow different total masses.
If ρ =qδx and α =mδx, then
KL(qδx|mδx) =q log
(q
m
)
−q +m,
with the convention 0 log 0 = 0.
Proposition 16.1 (Basic properties of finite KL). Let ρ,α∈M+(X ). Then
KL(ρ|α)≥ 0,
with equality if and only if ρ =α.
Proof. This is the standard convexity argument for relative entropy; see the entropy regularization
and unbalanced transport discussion in Peyré and Cuturi 2019. Sketch: the function h(t) =
t logt−t + 1 is convex and satisfies h(t)≥ 0, with equality only at t = 1. Therefore
∫
h(f) dα≥ 0.
Equality implies f = 1 α-almost surely, hence ρ =α.
Remark 16.2 (The cost of deleting all mass). If ρ = 0, then f = 0 and
KL(0|α) =α(X ) =|α|.
Thus deleting one unit of source mass costs one unit of KL penalty before multiplication by the
penalty parameter. This gives a simple interpretation of the penalty scale.
16.7 Static unbalanced optimal transport
Let α∈M+(X ) and β∈M+(Y). Let c : X×Y → [0,∞] be a lower semicontinuous cost.
A transport plan is now any finite measure γ∈M+(X×Y), not necessarily with prescribed
marginals. Its marginals are
γ1 = (p1)#γ, γ2 = (p2)#γ.
Unbalanced OT penalizes the fact that γ1 may differ from α and γ2 may differ from β.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 276/416

=== page 277 ===
16.7 Static unbalanced optimal transport
Definition 16.5 (KL-penalized unbalanced OT). For parameters τ1,τ2 > 0, define
UOTc,τ1,τ2(α,β) = inf γ∈M+(X×Y)
{∫
c(x,y)γ( dx, dy) +τ1KL(γ1 |α) +τ2KL(γ2 |β)
}
.
Proposition 16.2 (Existence, standard KL-penalized form). Assume that X and Y are Polish
spaces, c is lower semicontinuous and nonnegative, and α,β are finite positive measures. Under
the usual tightness or coercivity conditions ensuring that transported mass cannot escape to
infinity at finite cost, the problem in Definition 16.5 admits an optimal finite plan.
Remark 16.3 (Unbalanced value versus unbalanced metric). The KL-penalized value UOTc,τ1,τ2
is an optimization criterion, not automatically a metric. It may be asymmetric when τ1 ̸= τ2, and
even in symmetric cases it does not have the same status as Wp. Metric unbalanced geometries
require additional structure, as in Hellinger–Kantorovich or Wasserstein–Fisher–Rao distances.
This distinction is useful when the quantity is used as a governance diagnostic rather than as a
mathematical distance.
The parameters τ1 and τ2 tune the cost of violating the source and target marginals. Large τ
makes marginal deviations expensive; small τ allows more deletion and creation.
Actuarial takeaway
The plan γ represents mass that is explained by transport. The residual measure α−γ1,
when meaningful, represents source exposure that disappears, lapses or is not matched.
The residual β−γ2 represents new target exposure or newly created mass. With KL
penalties, residuals need not be simple positive differences everywhere, but the same
intuition remains: the marginals of γ indicate the mass actually transported.
Definition 16.6 (Transported, unused and unexplained mass). For an unbalanced plan γ, the
transported mass is |γ|. Its source usage and target explanation marginals are γ1 = (p1)#γ and
γ2 = (p2)#γ. When γ1 ≤α and γ2 ≤β, the unused source mass and unexplained target mass
are α−γ1 and β−γ2. For KL penalties, these signed residual interpretations may fail locally,
but γ1 and γ2 still identify where the plan uses source mass and explains target mass.
Proposition 16.3 (Balanced OT as a limiting case). Assume |α| = |β| and suppose the balanced
problem has finite value. As τ1,τ2 →∞, unbalanced transport increasingly enforces
γ1 =α, γ2 =β,
so that the limiting problem is the balanced Kantorovich problem.
Proof. This is the usual penalty-method interpretation of constrained optimization, as developed
in unbalanced OT references such as Peyré and Cuturi 2019. Sketch: if a sequence of plans has
objective values bounded independently of τ1,τ2, then the terms KL(γ1 |α) and KL(γ2 |β) must
tend to zero as the penalties diverge. By the non-negativity and definiteness of KL, this forces
γ1 →α and γ2 →β in the appropriate weak sense, under tightness assumptions. The remaining
term is then the balanced transport cost.
Remark 16.4 (Why two penalties?). There is no reason the cost of deleting old mass should equal
the cost of creating new mass. In portfolio management, lapse and acquisition may have different
economic meanings. In climate applications, disappearing exposure may be an adaptation success,
while newly created exposure in a high-risk zone may be a concern. Therefore τ1 and τ2 need
not be equal.
Page 277/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 278 ===

16.8 A one-point calculation
The simplest example explains the trade-off.
Proposition 16.4 (One-point KL–UOT solution). Let
α =mδx, β =nδy,
with m,n> 0 and cost c(x,y) =c0 ≥ 0. Any plan is of the form
γ =qδ(x,y), q≥ 0.
For the KL-penalized unbalanced objective of Definition 16.5, the unique minimizer is
q∗ = (mτ1nτ2 exp(−c0))1/(τ1+τ2) =mτ1/(τ1+τ2)nτ2/(τ1+τ2) exp
(
− c0
τ1 +τ2
)
.
Proof. The objective is
J(q) =qc0 +τ1
{
q log q m −q +m } +τ2 { q log q n −q +n } .
For q > 0, the first-order condition is
c0 +τ1 log q m +τ2 log q n = 0.
Solving gives the displayed expression. Strict convexity in q gives uniqueness.
Remark 16.5 (Interpretation). If the transport cost c0 is small, the transported mass is close to
a weighted geometric average of m and n. If c0 is large, less mass is transported: it is cheaper
to delete source mass and create target mass than to force a costly match. This is exactly the
actuarial trade-off in a portfolio where some new exposure is not plausibly a migration of old
exposure.

16.10 Entropic unbalanced OT and generalized Sinkhorn
Proposition 16.5 (Existence in the finite case). If all entries of C are finite and nonnegative
anda,b are finite nonnegative vectors, the discrete unbalanced problem admits a minimizer.
Proof. This is a finite-dimensional convex optimization argument. Sketch: the objective is lower
semicontinuous and convex on Rn×m + . Because the KL terms grow superlinearly in the row and
column masses, level sets are bounded as soon as the zero-mass rows and columns forced by
finite KL are removed. A minimizing sequence therefore has a convergent subsequence, and lower
semicontinuity gives a minimizer.
Remark 16.6 (Strict convexity and numerical stability). Without entropic regularization, the
KL marginal penalties regularize the marginal masses but do not necessarily make the whole
matrix Γ strictly identifiable when several routes have the same cost and the same row or column
masses. The entropic term in Section 16.10 removes this degeneracy and gives a smoother plan.
Remark 16.7 (No equality constraints). The balanced transportation polytope is replaced by the
full nonnegative orthant. The marginal constraints are no longer hard constraints; they enter
through the penalty. This is one reason why unbalanced OT is flexible in actuarial applications
with exposure changes.
16.10 Entropic unbalanced OT and generalized Sinkhorn
For computation, one often adds an entropy penalty to the plan itself.
Definition 16.8 (Entropic unbalanced OT). For ε > 0, the entropically regularized discrete
unbalanced problem is
min
Γ≥0


⟨C, Γ⟩ +ε
∑
i,j
γij(logγij − 1) +τ1KL(Γ1m|a) +τ2KL(Γ⊤1n|b)


,
where the KL divergences between vectors are understood componentwise.
Set
Kij = exp
(
−Cij
ε
)
.
The minimizer has a scaling form
Γ = diag(u)K diag(v)
with nonnegative scaling vectors u and v, positive on active rows and columns. Unlike balanced
Sinkhorn, the updates are damped by exponents.
Theorem 16.1 (Generalized Sinkhorn updates). For entropic unbalanced OT with KL marginal
penalties, define
θ1 = τ1 τ1 +ε, θ2 = τ2 τ2 +ε.
A standard generalized Sinkhorn iteration is
u←
( a
Kv
)θ1
, v←
( b
K⊤u
)θ2
,
where powers and divisions are componentwise. The resulting plan is
Γ = diag(u)K diag(v).
Rows or columns with zero prescribed mass should be removed before the iteration, or handled
with the corresponding scaling factor fixed at zero.
Page 279/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 280 ===
16 UNBALANCED TRANSPORT, EXPOSURE AND PORTFOLIO GROWTH
Proof. This is the first-order condition for the entropic unbalanced problem; see the computational
presentation of unbalanced Sinkhorn in Peyré and Cuturi 2019. Sketch: differentiating the
regularized objective yields equations in the row sums, column sums and entries of Γ. Writing
Γ = diag(u)K diag(v) reduces these equations to two nonlinear scaling relations. Solving each
relation alternately gives the displayed updates. When τ1,τ2 →∞, the exponents tend to one
and the balanced Sinkhorn updates are recovered.
Remark 16.8 (Log-domain implementation). For small ε or large costs, the kernel Kij =
exp(−Cij/ε) may underflow. The same warning as in Lecture 6 applies: production code should
use log-domain updates or stabilized scaling rather than dense exponentiation of−C/ε.
Computational note
The parameter ε smooths the coupling. The parameters τ1 and τ2 soften the marginal
constraints. These are different roles. A small ε with small τ gives a sharp but highly
unbalanced matching. A large ε with large τ gives a smooth approximation to balanced
OT.
16.11 Interpreting the parameters
The three main parameters in entropic unbalanced OT are:
• ε: smoothing of the transport plan;
• τ1: penalty for not using source mass;
• τ2: penalty for not explaining target mass.
The ratios
θk = τk τk +ε
are informative. If τk ≫ε, then θk ≈ 1, and the corresponding marginal is nearly enforced. If
τk ≪ε, then θk ≈ 0, and the corresponding marginal is weakly enforced.
Example 16.4 (Acquisition versus lapse). In a portfolio-growth comparison from year 0 to year
1, τ1 may represent the cost of declaring old exposure unmatched, while τ2 represents the cost of
declaring year-1 exposure new. If new-business acquisition is expected and cheap to explain, one
may choose a smaller τ2. If unexpected lapse is more concerning, one may choose a larger τ1 to
force source mass to be matched whenever plausible.
Example 16.5 (Climate exposure). If exposure in a coastal region grows because of construction,
a low target penalty τ2 allows the model to say: this is genuinely new exposure. If τ2 is high,
the model is forced to match that new exposure to some old exposure, possibly producing a
misleading migration story.
16.12 Diagnostics from an unbalanced plan
Given a plan γ, recall from Definition 16.6 that the transported mass is
|γ| =γ(X×Y).
The source marginal γ1 indicates which part of α is used. The target marginal γ2 indicates which
part of β is explained by transported mass.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 280/416

.
When the KL penalty is used, the difference a−r(Γ) need not be nonnegative componentwise,
because a row marginal may locally exceed ai if that is beneficial. Still, the ratios above remain
useful summaries of how much mass is explained through transport.
Actuarial takeaway
For governance, the transport value alone is rarely enough. A useful unbalanced OT report
should include at least: total source mass, total target mass, transported mass, transport
cost, source usage, target explanation, largest disappearing cells and largest newly created
cells.
16.13 Balanced normalization versus unbalanced comparison
Suppose α = Mµ and β = Nν with M,N > 0 and µ,ν∈P(X ). A normalized comparison
computes Wp(µ,ν). This is invariant to multiplying M or N. Therefore it cannot detect growth.
Unbalanced comparison is not invariant to total mass. This is a feature, not a bug, when
total exposure is meaningful. But it also means that one must be careful when interpreting
values across portfolios of different sizes.
Remark 16.9 (Scale matters). If all exposure weights are multiplied by 10, the unbalanced
objective typically scales by 10. Thus, for comparison across books, it is often useful to report
both the total value and a per-unit value, such as value divided by average exposure mass. The
denominator should be stated explicitly: source mass, target mass, average mass (M + N)/2 and
insured value lead to different per-unit interpretations.
Example 16.6 (Two portfolios with the same shape). Let α = Mµ and β = Nµ have the
same shape but different total masses. Balanced OT between normalized shapes gives zero.
Unbalanced OT gives a positive value unless M = N, because mass must be created or destroyed.
This is appropriate if the question is portfolio growth, but not if the question is only geographic
composition.
16.14 Unbalanced dynamic transport
The dynamic formulation of balanced W2 uses the continuity equation
∂tρt +∇· (ρtvt) = 0.
This expresses conservation of mass along a trajectory. For unbalanced transport, we add a
source term:
∂tρt +∇· (ρtvt) =ζtρt.
Page 281/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 282 ===
16 UNBALANCED TRANSPORT, EXPOSURE AND PORTFOLIO GROWTH
Here vt moves mass, while ζt creates or destroys mass locally. Positive ζt means growth; negative
ζt means decay.
Definition 16.9 (Unbalanced dynamic trajectory). An unbalanced dynamic trajectory between
finite measures α and β is a curve of finite measures (ρt)0≤t≤1, a velocity field vt and a growth-rate
field ζt satisfying
∂tρt +∇· (ρtvt) =ζtρt, ρ0 =α, ρ1 =β.
A common dynamic action has the form
∫ 1
0
∫
X
(
∥vt(x)∥2 +κ2ζt(x)2
)
ρt( dx) dt,
subject to the source continuity equation and endpoint constraints
ρ0 =α, ρ1 =β.
The parameter κ> 0 controls the cost of mass variation relative to spatial motion. This is one
route to Hellinger–Kantorovich or Wasserstein–Fisher–Rao type geometries.
Remark 16.10 (Dynamic meaning). Balanced dynamic OT says: the portfolio mass moves through
state space. Unbalanced dynamic OT says: the portfolio mass moves, grows and shrinks. This is
much closer to actual insurance portfolios, where new business, lapse, renewal, cancellation and
exposure inflation occur at the same time as changes in the covariate distribution.
Actuarial takeaway
For actuarial trajectories, the source term ζt is the key new object. It can represent lapse,
new business, migration into coverage, migration out of coverage, claim frequency growth,
insured value growth, or exposure reduction due to prevention.
16.15 Application: portfolio growth and new business
Let Xt denote policyholder characteristics at time t, and let Et be exposure volume. A probability
law µt = Law(Xt) describes the composition of the portfolio conditional on being in the book.
A finite measure
αt =Etµt
describes both composition and size.
Balanced OT between µ0 and µ1 measures composition drift. Unbalanced OT between α0
and α1 measures exposure drift. The latter can distinguish:
• stable exposure transported from old to similar new states;
• lapsed exposure not matched to the target;
• new exposure not explained by the source;
• exposure that moved to different risk states.
Example 16.7 (Motor insurer expanding into a new territory). Suppose a motor insurer enters
a new province. The normalized portfolio distribution changes, but a balanced transport map
may misleadingly match old policies from existing provinces to new policies in the new province.
Unbalanced OT can instead declare part of the target mass as newly created, which is the correct
business interpretation.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 282/416

=== page 283 ===
16.16 Application: lapse and retention
16.16 Application: lapse and retention
Consider a renewal portfolio. Let α0 be the exposure measure at the start of the year and α1 the
exposure measure among policies renewed at the next year, possibly including new business. An
unbalanced plan can be used to quantify retention by region of state space.
For each source cell i, define the transported row mass
ri =
∑
j
γij.
The ratio
ℓi = 1− ri ai
can be read as an unbalanced-transport lapse indicator, when ri ≤ai. With KL penalties, local
ratios may need smoothing or truncation, but the diagnostic remains meaningful.
Example 16.8 (Lapse after a premium increase). Let xi include old premium, age, territory
and claim history. If high-premium cells have low row usage ratios ri/ai, the unbalanced plan
suggests that these cells are not well matched to the renewal portfolio. This can be interpreted
as a distributional lapse signal.
16.17 Application: exposure growth and climate risk
Climate risk is not only a change in hazard intensity. It is also a change in exposure: more assets
may be built in high-risk zones, property values may increase, and adaptation may remove or
protect some exposure.
Letx encode location and vulnerability. Let
α0( dx) = insured value at baseline in statex,
and
α1( dx) = insured value under a future or stressed scenario.
Unbalanced OT separates three mechanisms:
(i) existing exposure moves in risk space, for example because vulnerability or hazard changes;
(ii) exposure disappears, for example because of withdrawal, relocation or protection;
(iii) exposure appears, for example because of new construction or portfolio growth.
Actuarial takeaway
A prevention policy can reduce loss by moving mass to safer states, by shrinking vulnerable
mass, or both. Balanced OT can describe the first mechanism. Unbalanced OT is needed
for the second.
16.18 Application: claim frequency and severity jointly
Suppose losses are generated by a frequency-severity model. Let λt be expected claim frequency
at time t, and let µt be the conditional severity distribution. The finite measure
αt =λtµt
encodes the unconditional claim-intensity distribution.
If λ1 >λ0 but µ1 = µ0, then the severity shape is unchanged but the expected number of
claims has increased. If λ1 = λ0 but µ1 has a heavier tail, then frequency is unchanged but
severity shape has changed. If both change, unbalanced OT compares both effects in one object.
Page 283/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 284 ===
16 UNBALANCED TRANSPORT, EXPOSURE AND PORTFOLIO GROWTH
Example 16.9 (Frequency growth without severity inflation). Let α0 = 10µ and α1 = 12µ.
Balanced OT between normalized measures gives zero. Unbalanced OT gives a positive creation
cost corresponding to two additional expected claims. This is the correct conclusion if the
actuarial question concerns aggregate expected claim volume.
16.19 Application: reserving cash-flow volume
A predictive reserving model may produce a probability law of future cash-flow vectors, but it
may also produce a finite payment measure across development years. For instance,
α =
T∑
t=1
mtδt,
where mt is expected payment volume in future year t. Comparing two reserving models through
balanced OT after normalization compares the timing shape of payments. Comparing finite
measures through unbalanced OT also accounts for differences in total reserve volume. This
complements the cash-flow distribution comparison in Lecture 11.
Example 16.10 (Same timing, different best estimate). Two reserving models may have the
same payment pattern but different total best-estimate reserve. Balanced OT between normalized
payment patterns is zero. Unbalanced OT is positive and reflects the difference in total future
payment mass.
16.20 Cost engineering in unbalanced OT
The ground cost c(x,y) and the marginal penalties τ1,τ2 must be chosen together. They answer
different questions:
• c(x,y): how expensive is it to interpret target state y as a transformation of source state
x?
• τ1: how expensive is it to leave source mass unexplained?
• τ2: how expensive is it to declare target mass newly created?
A useful calibration principle is to compare the cost of transporting one unit from x to y with
the cost of deleting it and recreating it. In the one-point calculation, the relevant comparison is
between c0 and τ1 +τ2. If
c(x,y)≫τ1 +τ2,
then matchingx toy is strongly discouraged; the model prefers destruction and creation. If
c(x,y)≪τ1 +τ2,
then transport is encouraged.
Actuarial takeaway
The penalty parameters define the boundary between “migration” and “new/lost exposure”.
They are therefore governance parameters, not merely numerical tuning constants.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 284/416

=== page 285 ===
16.21 Mini-lab: generalized Sinkhorn in R
16.21 Mini-lab: generalized Sinkhorn in R
The following code implements a simple generalized Sinkhorn algorithm for unbalanced entropic
transport. It is deliberately written in transparent form.
unbalanced _sinkhorn <- function(a, b, C, epsilon = 1, tau1 = 10, tau2 =
10,
n _iter = 1000, tol = 1e-10) {
a <- as. numeric(a)
b <- as. numeric(b)
C <- as. matrix( C)
n <- length(a)
m <- length(b)
if (epsilon <= 0 || tau1 <= 0 || tau2 <= 0) stop("epsilon,␣tau1␣and␣
tau2␣must␣be␣positive")
if ( any(a < 0) || any(b < 0)) stop("a␣and␣b␣must␣be␣nonnegative")
K <- exp(- C / epsilon)
u <- rep(1, n)
v <- rep(1, m)
theta1 <- tau1 / (tau1 + epsilon)
theta2 <- tau2 / (tau2 + epsilon)
tiny <- 1e-300
for (it in seq _len(n _iter)) {
u _ old <- u
v _ old <- v
Kv <- as. numeric(K % * % v)
u <- (a / pmax(Kv, tiny))^theta1
Ktu <- as. numeric( t(K) % * % u)
v <- (b / pmax(Ktu, tiny))^theta2
err <- max( abs( log( pmax(u, tiny)) - log( pmax(u _ old, tiny))),
abs( log( pmax(v, tiny)) - log( pmax(v _ old, tiny))))
if (err < tol) break
}
Gamma <- diag(u, nrow = n) % * % K % * % diag(v, nrow = m)
row _mass <- rowSums( Gamma)
col _mass <- colSums( Gamma)
list(plan = Gamma,
row _mass = row _mass,
col _mass = col _mass,
transported _mass = sum( Gamma),
transport _cost = sum( C * Gamma),
iterations = it,
theta = c(theta1, theta2))
}
# Example: exposure by territory-risk score
x <- c(0, 1, 2, 3, 4)
y <- c(0, 1, 2, 3, 4)
Page 285/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

# Year 0 exposure: concentrated in low-risk cells
# Year 1 exposure: larger total mass, shifted toward higher-risk cells
a <- c(40, 35, 20, 5, 0)
b <- c(30, 30, 25, 20, 15)
C <- outer(x, y, function(u, v) (u - v)^2)
fit <- unbalanced _sinkhorn(a, b, C, epsilon = 0.5, tau1 = 5, tau2 = 5)
fit $transported _mass
fit $transport _cost
round(fit $plan, 2)
round(fit $ row _mass, 2)
round(fit $ col _mass, 2)
# Diagnostics
source _usage <- fit $ row _mass / pmax(a, 1e-12)
target _explained <- fit $ col _mass / pmax(b, 1e-12)
data. frame(cell = x,
source _exposure = a,
used _ source _mass = round(fit $ row _mass, 2),
source _usage = round( source _usage, 3))
data. frame(cell = y,
target _exposure = b,
explained _target _mass = round(fit $ col _mass, 2),
target _explained = round(target _explained, 3))
The target has more total mass than the source. Balanced OT cannot compare a and b
directly without normalization. The unbalanced plan transports a substantial part of the old
exposure and creates additional target mass where needed. In a full report, the diagnostics in
Section 16.12 should be displayed together with the plan.
16.22 Mini-lab: sensitivity to the marginal penalty
The next code varies τ = τ1 = τ2. Small values of τ allow more mass creation and destruction.
Large values force more mass to be transported.
taus <- c(0.5, 1, 2, 5, 10, 50)
summary _tau <- lapply(taus, function(tt) {
fit <- unbalanced _sinkhorn(a, b, C, epsilon = 0.5, tau1 = tt, tau2 =
tt)
data. frame(tau = tt,
transported _mass = fit $transported _mass,
transport _cost = fit $transport _cost,
source _mass = sum(a),
target _mass = sum(b),
source _usage = fit $transported _mass / sum(a),
target _explained = fit $transported _mass / sum(b))
})
summary _tau <- do. call( rbind, summary _tau)
summary _tau
plot( summary _tau $tau, summary _tau $transported _mass, type = "b", log = "x
",
xlab = "marginal␣penalty␣tau",
ylab = "transported␣mass")

16.23 Practical workflow
abline(h = sum(a), lty = 2)
abline(h = sum(b), lty = 3)
The plot typically shows transported mass increasing with τ. When marginal deviations are
cheap, the algorithm avoids costly long-distance moves. When marginal deviations are expensive,
it transports more mass even if the ground cost is higher.
16.23 Practical workflow
A practical unbalanced OT workflow for actuarial portfolios can be organized as follows.
Step 1: choose the mass. Decide what the measure represents: policy count, vehicle-years,
insured value, expected claim count, expected loss amount or reserve cash-flow volume.
Step 2: choose the state space. Decide what the locations x represent: geography, rating-
cell centroid, severity, claim-count intensity, vulnerability index, cash-flow time, or multivariate
covariates.
Step 3: choose the ground cost. Encode what counts as a plausible migration. Moving
from one adjacent risk class may be cheap; moving across territories or across product types may
be expensive.
Step 4: choose marginal penalties. Calibrate τ1 and τ2 to the cost of declaring mass
disappeared or newly created. Use sensitivity analysis rather than a single arbitrary value.
Step 5: compute the plan. Use generalized Sinkhorn for large problems, exact convex
optimization for small problems, or problem-specific approximations for one-dimensional or
gridded settings.
Step 6: report diagnostics. Report transported mass, cost per transported unit, source
usage, target explanation, disappearing cells and newly created cells.
Step 7: interpret actuarially. Translate mass creation/destruction into business concepts:
lapse, new business, exposure growth, portfolio withdrawal, frequency change, reserve strength-
ening, prevention or adaptation.
16.24 Limitations and cautions
Unbalanced OT is useful, but it can be misused.
Penalty calibration is normative. The choice of τ1 and τ2 determines whether a difference
is interpreted as migration or creation/destruction. This is not purely statistical.
Mass has units. A measure of policy count, a measure of insured value and a measure of
expected loss are different objects. Their unbalanced OT values are not directly comparable
unless units are made explicit.
KL is asymmetric. The penalty KL(γ1 |α) is not symmetric. The two penalties have
directional meanings: source usage and target explanation. This is often useful, but it should be
documented.
Page 287/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 288 ===
16 UNBALANCED TRANSPORT, EXPOSURE AND PORTFOLIO GROWTH
Entropic smoothing can hide sharp changes. A large ε spreads mass over many matches.
This may improve numerical stability, but it can blur localized migration, lapse or new-business
signals.
Unbalanced transport is not causality. A plan can suggest a plausible matching between
exposures in two periods, but it does not prove that a specific policy migrated causally from
one state to another. As in the counterfactual chapter, causal interpretation requires additional
design or structural assumptions.
16.25 Summary
Balanced OT is a geometry for probability measures with conserved mass. Unbalanced OT
extends this geometry to finite measures, allowing mass to be transported, created or destroyed.
The most common static formulation penalizes deviations from the source and target marginals
using finite-measure KL divergences. Entropic regularization leads to generalized Sinkhorn
iterations, which are scalable and easy to implement.
For actuarial science, unbalanced OT is especially natural for exposure analysis. It can
compare portfolios with different sizes, distinguish composition drift from growth, analyze lapse
and new business, compare claim-intensity measures, quantify climate exposure growth, and
compare reserving payment volumes. The output should not be reduced to a single number:
transported mass, source usage and target explanation are often as important as the transport
cost itself.
16.26 Exercises
Exercise 16.1 (Finite-measure KL). Let ρ = qδx and α = mδx with q,m≥ 0 and m > 0.
Derive
KL(ρ|α) =q log(q/m)−q +m.
Show that this is nonnegative and minimized at q =m.
Exercise 16.2 (One-point unbalanced OT). Let α = mδx, β = nδy and c(x,y) = c0. Derive the
minimizer q∗ of the one-point problem. Study the limits c0 → 0, c0 →∞, τ1 →∞ and τ2 →∞.
Exercise 16.3 (Balanced limit). Assume |α| = |β| and consider KL-penalized UOT with
τ1 = τ2 = τ. Give a formal argument showing that, as τ→∞, any sequence of nearly optimal
plans must have marginals close to α and β.
Exercise 16.4 (Normalization can hide growth). Let α = 100δ0 and β = 120δ0. What is the
Wasserstein distance between the normalized probability measures? What does unbalanced OT
detect that normalization removes?
Exercise 16.5 (Two-cell exposure). Let
α = 50δ0 + 50δ1, β = 30δ0 + 90δ1.
Use squared distance cost on {0, 1}. Compare: (i) normalized balanced OT, (ii) total mass
change, (iii) a qualitative unbalanced interpretation. Which part of the change is shape drift and
which part is mass growth?
Exercise 16.6 (Generalized Sinkhorn). Starting from the entropic unbalanced objective, derive
the scaling equations for Γ = diag(u)K diag(v). Show why the exponents are τk/(τk +ε).
Exercise 16.7 (Penalty sensitivity). Run the mini-lab with τ∈{0.1, 0.5, 1, 5, 20, 100}. Plot
transported mass and transport cost as functions of τ. Explain the actuarial meaning of the
curve.

17 LIFE, MORTALITY AND LONGEVITY TRANSPORT
17 Life, Mortality and Longevity Transport
Further reading
Life insurance mathematics starts from the distribution of a future lifetime. A life table,
a mortality projection, an annuity portfolio or a longevity stress is therefore already a
probability measure, or a family of probability measures indexed by age, calendar time
and cohort. This lecture uses optimal transport to compare and stress such laws. Classical
references on life contingencies include Bowers et al. 1997; Gerber 1990; Dickson et al. 2009;
De Vylder 1997. For multi-state life insurance and long-term care models, see Haberman
and Pitacco 1999; Pitacco 1999; Andersen et al. 1993. For stochastic mortality, longevity
dynamics and annuity applications, see Pitacco et al. 2009; Lee and Carter 1992; Brouhns
et al. 2002; Cairns et al. 2006; Renshaw and Haberman 2006; Plat 2009; Cairns et al. 2009.
The optimal-transport tools used below are those introduced in Lectures 5, 8, 15 and 16.
17.1 Learning objectives
After this lecture, the reader should be able to:
(i) represent a life table as a probability measure on age at death or residual lifetime;
(ii) compute Wasserstein distances between residual lifetime laws;
(iii) interpret quantile transport as a rank-preserving longevity stress;
(iv) write annuity and death-benefit present values as push-forwards of lifetime laws;
(v) use Wasserstein barycenters to combine mortality scenarios;
(vi) compare dynamic mortality trajectories and cohort-specific laws;
(vii) formulate longevity capital and prudent pricing with Wasserstein ambiguity sets;
(viii) connect tail-aware transport with old-age mortality and extreme longevity;
(ix) use multi-state and joint-life models as transport problems on richer state spaces;
(x) understand how quantile sketches can support distributed mortality or longevity audits.
Actuarial takeaway
In life insurance, the basic distributional object is often not a loss severity law but a
lifetime law. Optimal transport compares how lifetime mass moves across ages, cohorts,
health states, mortality scenarios or life-contingent present values.
Actuarial takeaway
A transport map between two lifetime laws is a distributional alignment. It is not, by itself,
a statement about how long a particular person would have lived under another mortality
regime. Individual counterfactual claims require a structural model or an intervention
design in addition to the coupling.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 290/416

17.2 Future lifetime as a probability measure
17.2 Future lifetime as a probability measure
Let Tx denote the future lifetime of an individual aged x. In continuous time, Tx is a non-negative
random variable with survival function
tpx = P(Tx >t), t≥ 0.
Its distribution is a probability measure on R+, denoted
µx = Law(Tx).
In discrete time, let Kx be the curtate future lifetime,
Kx =⌊Tx⌋.
Then Kx is a probability measure on N. If qx+k is the one-year death probability at age x + k,
then, for an open-ended table,
P(Kx =k) = kpxqx+k, k = 0, 1, 2,...,
where
kpx =
k−1∏
j=0
(1−qx+j), 0px = 1.
For a table closed at terminal age ω, one usually sets qω = 1, or equivalently puts the remaining
survival mass at Kx =ω−x. Thus a life table induces a collection of probability measures
x↦−→µx.
Definition 17.1 (Life table as a lifetime measure). Fix an initial age x and a terminal age ω. A
discrete life table{qy :x≤y≤ω}, closed at ω, induces the probability measure
µcurt x =
ω−x∑
k=0
px,kδk,
where
px,k = kpxqx+k, 0≤k<ω−x, px,ω−x = ω−xpx.
The latter convention places at the terminal curtate age all mass surviving to ω.
Definition 17.2 (Residual lifetime law). For a life aged x, the residual lifetime law is
µx = Law(Tx)∈P1(R+)
when the mean residual lifetime is finite. In discrete time, the curtate residual lifetime law is
µcurt x = Law(Kx)∈P(N).
When p-th moments are finite, we view µx as an element ofPp(R+).
This viewpoint separates two questions. The actuarial table gives death probabilities. Optimal
transport compares the resulting lifetime distributions. The same distinction already appeared
for severity distributions in Lecture 5; here the state space is measured in years rather than in
monetary units.
Example 17.1 (Two mortality tables). Let µ0 x and µ1 x be residual lifetime laws at age x under
two mortality tables. The quantity
W1(µ0 x,µ1 x)
measures the average number of residual years by which lifetime mass must be moved to transform
one table into the other. It is expressed in years and is therefore directly interpretable.
Page 291/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

17 LIFE, MORTALITY AND LONGEVITY TRANSPORT
17.3 Transport between age-at-death distributions
Instead of residual lifetime Tx, one may work with age at death
Ax =x +Tx.
If αx = Law(Ax), then αx is the push-forward of µx by the translation map
τx :t↦−→x +t.
Thus
αx = (τx)#µx.
For two populations or two mortality scenarios, a∈{0, 1}, write
αa x = Law(Aa x), µa x = Law(Ta x ).
Proposition 17.1 (Residual lifetime and age-at-death distances). Let µ0 x,µ1 x ∈Pp(R+), and let
αa x = (τx)#µa x, where τx(t) =x +t. Then
Wp(α0 x,α1 x) = Wp(µ0 x,µ1 x).
Proof. Translations by the same vector are isometries for the ground distance on R. If γ∈
Π(µ0 x,µ1 x), then (τx,τx)#γ∈ Π(α0 x,α1 x) and |(x + s) − (x + t)| = |s−t|. Taking infima in both
directions gives the result.
The choice between age at death and residual lifetime is therefore mostly interpretive. For a
single starting age, both contain the same geometry. For comparisons across several starting ages,
residual lifetimes are often easier to compare because all measures live on the same support R+.
In one dimension, the optimal transport map is the quantile map. We use the lower generalized
inverse convention throughout this lecture:
Qa x(u) =Qµax(u) =F−1 µax (u), u∈ (0, 1).
Then
Wp p(µ0 x,µ1 x) =
∫ 1
0
|Q0 x(u)−Q1 x(u)|p du.
The corresponding rank-preserving lifetime stress is
Tx(t) =Q1 x
(
Fµ0x(t)
)
,
when the source law is continuous. With atoms, Fµ0x(Tx) is not uniform; one should use the lower
inverse together with a deterministic tie convention, or randomized ranks inside atoms. A life
whose baseline lifetime lies at percentile u is moved to the target lifetime at the same percentile.
Actuarial takeaway
A quantile longevity stress preserves mortality rank. It does not say that an individual
would causally live exactly Tx(t) years. It says that the baseline lifetime distribution is
transformed into the stressed lifetime distribution by matching percentiles. This is the
same interpretive caution as in Lectures 2 and 14.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 292/416

=== page 293 ===
17.4 Longevity stresses by quantile maps
17.4 Longevity stresses by quantile maps
A longevity stress often shifts mass toward older ages. Let µ0 x be a baseline lifetime law and µ1 x a
stressed law with lower mortality. In one dimension, the monotone transport map
Tx(t) =Q1 x(Fµ0x(t))
gives a transparent scenario map.
Definition 17.3 (Longevity quantile displacement). For two residual lifetime laws µ0 x and µ1 x,
the quantile displacement curve is
∆x(u) =Q1 x(u)−Q0 x(u), u∈ (0, 1).
Positive values of ∆x(u) mean that the stressed scenario increases residual lifetime at rank u.
If Tx(t) >t for most t, the stress increases residual lifetimes. If the increase is concentrated
in the upper tail, then the stress mainly affects annuity tail risk and old-age capital. If it affects
the body of the distribution, it changes expected annuity costs more broadly. The curve ∆x is
therefore often more informative than a single scalar distance.
Proposition 17.2 (Expected lifetime stability). Let µ and ν be two residual lifetime laws with
finite first moments. Then
⏐⏐⏐⏐ ∫ t dµ(t)− ∫ t dν(t) ⏐⏐⏐⏐≤ W1(µ,ν).
Proof. The function t↦→t is 1-Lipschitz on R+. The Kantorovich–Rubinstein dual for W1 gives
the inequality.
Remark 17.1 (Mean lifetime is not enough). Two mortality stresses may have the same effect on
expected lifetime but different effects on old-age survival probabilities. Wasserstein distances
and quantile displacement curves show where the lifetime distribution moves.
17.5 Annuities as push-forwards of lifetime laws
Let v = (1 + i)−1 be a discount factor. In discrete time, the present value of a life annuity-due
paying one unit at the beginning of each year while alive is
gann(k) =
k∑
j=0
vj = 1−vk+1
1−v , k∈ N.
Thus the random present value is a measurable function of the curtate future lifetime:
Ax =gann(Kx).
Its law is the push-forward
Law(Ax) = (gann)#µcurt x .
Similarly, the present value of a death benefit of one paid at the end of the year of death is
Bx =gdeath(Kx) =vKx+1,
so
Law(Bx) = (gdeath)#µcurt x .
Page 293/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 294 ===
17 LIFE, MORTALITY AND LONGEVITY TRANSPORT
Definition 17.4 (Life-contingent present-value law). Let µ be a lifetime law and let g be a
measurable present-value map. The associated life-contingent present-value law is
λg =g#µ.
For a single-life annuity, g =gann; for a death benefit, g =gdeath.
Actuarial takeaway
Life-contingent present values are push-forwards of lifetime laws. Mortality transport
becomes financial transport once the lifetime is mapped through annuity or insurance
present-value functions.
This gives a direct stability bound. The annuity present-value map satisfies, for k,ℓ∈ N,
|gann(k)−gann(ℓ)|≤|k−ℓ|.
Indeed, the difference is a sum of at most|k−ℓ| terms, each bounded by one. Therefore
W1 ((gann)#µ, (gann)#ν)≤ W1(µ,ν).
Proposition 17.3 (Push-forward stability of life-contingent values). Let g : R+ → R be Lg-
Lipschitz and let µ,ν∈Pp(R+). Then
Wp(g#µ,g#ν)≤Lg Wp(µ,ν).
In particular, the discrete annuity-due map gann is 1-Lipschitz on N with the usual distance.
Proof. If γ∈ Π(µ,ν), then (g,g)#γ∈ Π(g#µ,g#ν). Hence
∫
|a−b|p d(g,g)#γ(a,b) =
∫
|g(s)−g(t)|p dγ(s,t)≤Lp g
∫
|s−t|p dγ(s,t).
Taking infima over γ gives the result.
For death benefits, the map k↦→vk+1 is decreasing. Longer lifetimes decrease the present
value of death benefits but increase annuity present values. Thus the same longevity stress has
opposite financial effects for life annuities and death insurance.
Proposition 17.4 (Opposite monotonicity of annuity and death-benefit values). Assume K1
first-order stochastically dominates K0. Then
E[gann(K1)]≥ E[gann(K0)], E[gdeath(K1)]≤ E[gdeath(K0)].
Proof. The map gann is increasing and gdeath is decreasing. The claim follows from the standard
characterization of first-order stochastic dominance by expectations of increasing functions,
applied to gann and to−gdeath.
Example 17.2 (Annuity versus death benefit). A stress that transports lifetime mass to
older ages increases the distribution of annuity present values but decreases the distribution of
immediate death-benefit present values. The same Wp(µ0 x,µ1 x) therefore has different business
meanings depending on the contract map applied after the lifetime law.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 294/416

17.6 Barycenters of mortality scenarios
17.6 Barycenters of mortality scenarios
Suppose M mortality models produce residual lifetime laws
µ(1) x ,...,µ(M) x
for a fixed age x. A Wasserstein barycenter is a consensus lifetime distribution
¯µx ∈ argmin
µ∈Pp(R+)
M∑
m=1
ωmWp p(µ,µ(m) x ),
where ωm ≥ 0 and ∑ mωm = 1. This is the same Fréchet-mean construction as in Lecture 5, now
applied to mortality scenarios.
Definition 17.5 (Mortality-scenario barycenter). A mortality-scenario barycenter at age x is
any minimizer
¯µx ∈ argmin
µ∈Pp(R+)
M∑
m=1
ωmWp p(µ,µ(m) x ).
It represents a central residual lifetime law in Wasserstein geometry, not a mixture of mortality
models.
Proposition 17.5 (One-dimensional W2-barycenter of lifetime laws). Assume µ(1) x ,...,µ(M) x ∈
P2(R+). In one dimension, a W2-barycenter has quantile function
Q¯µx(u) =
M∑
m=1
ωmQ(m) x (u), u∈ (0, 1).
Proof. The one-dimensional W2 objective is
M∑
m=1
ωm
∫ 1
0
|Q(u)−Q(m) x (u)|2 du.
For each u, the pointwise minimizer over real values is the weighted average ∑ mωmQ(m) x (u),
which is nondecreasing because it is a weighted average of nondecreasing functions. It is therefore
a quantile function.
Remark 17.2 (Barycenter versus mixture). A mixture mortality law first selects a model and
then samples a lifetime. A Wasserstein barycenter averages quantiles. The two operations are
different. Mixtures preserve model heterogeneity; barycenters produce a central scenario in the
geometry of lifetimes.
For a portfolio with ages x1,...,xn, one can compute age-specific barycenters ¯µxi. Alterna-
tively, one may first form the portfolio liability law under each mortality model and then take
a barycenter of the resulting liability distributions. These two operations need not commute
because aggregation and Wasserstein barycentering are nonlinear.
17.7 Mortality surfaces and dynamic trajectories
Mortality modelling often describes a surface
qx,t,
where x is age and t is calendar time. A stochastic mortality model produces random future
surfaces or paths
(qx,t+s)x,s≥0.
Page 295/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 296 ===
17 LIFE, MORTALITY AND LONGEVITY TRANSPORT
Each future path induces a law of lifetimes for each starting age and calendar date. Thus a
mortality model is not only a table but a distribution over future lifetime laws.
Lee–Carter type models describe log mortality rates by a low-dimensional time factor, whereas
Cairns–Blake–Dowd type models focus on older ages and annuity-relevant dynamics. Cohort
extensions add dependence along birth cohorts. From the transport viewpoint, all these models
define trajectories in a space of probability measures.
Let µx,t denote the residual lifetime law of a life aged x at calendar time t. A dynamic
mortality comparison between two scenarios a∈{0, 1} may use
T∑
s=0
wsWp p
(
µ0 x,t+s,µ1 x,t+s
)
.
This compares the projected residual lifetime law date by date. A stronger comparison works
on whole paths of mortality rates, survival curves or annuity present values. This is the same
static-versus-dynamic distinction as in Lecture 15.
Definition 17.6 (Mortality trajectory in Wasserstein space). For a fixed starting age x, a
mortality trajectory is a curve
t↦−→µx,t ∈Pp(R+),
where µx,t is the residual lifetime law at calendar date t. Its discrete path length over t0,...,tT is
T−1∑
s=0
Wp(µx,ts,µx,ts+1).
Actuarial takeaway
Longevity risk is dynamic. A single distance between two tables may miss the timing of
mortality improvements. Dynamic OT compares the path of lifetime laws, not only the
endpoint.
17.8 Cohorts and transport along diagonals
A cohort is identified by year of birth b = t−x. Mortality for a cohort evolves along the diagonal
(x +s,t +s), s = 0, 1, 2,....
Thus the residual lifetime law at age x and time t depends on the future mortality rates along
this diagonal.
Let µb x,t denote a cohort-specific lifetime law. Comparing two cohorts b0 and b1 may be
expressed as
Wp
(
µb0 x,t,µb1 x,t
)
,
or, for several starting ages, ∑
x
ωxWp p
(
µb0 x,t,µb1 x,t
)
.
This gives a distributional measure of cohort improvement. Quantile displacement curves can
show whether differences occur around median lifetime or in the old-age tail.
Remark 17.3 (Cohort effects are not only mean shifts). A cohort effect may increase survival
mostly at advanced ages, or it may shift the whole lifetime distribution. Wasserstein comparisons
reveal the shape of the cohort displacement.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 296/416

17.9 Annuity portfolios and liability laws
17.9 Annuity portfolios and liability laws
Consider a portfolio of annuitants i = 1,...,n, with ages xi, annual payments bi and future
curtate lifetimes Kxi. Write b = (b1,...,bn) and K = (Kx1,...,Kxn). Conditional on a mortality
scenario Θ, the portfolio present value is
L(Θ) =
n∑
i=1
bi
Kxi∑
k=0
vk.
The law of L depends on both idiosyncratic lifetime risk and systematic longevity risk. For large
annuity portfolios, idiosyncratic risk diversifies, but systematic longevity risk remains.
Definition 17.7 (Annuity portfolio liability law). Let Γ be a joint law of lifetimes K =
(Kx1,...,Kxn). The portfolio present-value map is
G(k) =
n∑
i=1
bi
ki∑
j=0
vj, k = (k1,...,kn).
The associated liability law is
λ =G#Γ = Law{G(K)}.
A mortality model therefore induces a liability law λ = Law(L). Comparing two mortality
models can be done either before or after aggregation:
Wp(µ0 x,µ1 x) for each age x,
or
Wp(λ0,λ1) for the portfolio liability law.
Both are useful. Age-specific distances diagnose where mortality changes. Liability distances
measure financial impact.
Proposition 17.6 (Lipschitz stability of annuity portfolio liabilities). Equip Nn with the weighted
ℓ1 distance
db(k,l) =
n∑
i=1
bi|ki −ℓi|.
The annuity portfolio map G in Definition 17.7 is 1-Lipschitz from (Nn,db) to R. Consequently,
for any two joint lifetime laws Γ0, Γ1,
W1(G#Γ0,G#Γ1)≤ Wdb 1 (Γ0, Γ1),
where the right-hand side is W1 computed with ground distance db.
Proof. For each life i, the map ki ↦→bi
∑ki
j=0 vj is bi-Lipschitz on N. Summing over i gives the
stated Lipschitz bound. The Wasserstein inequality is then Proposition 17.3 with p = 1.
Example 17.3 (Portfolio-level distance). Two mortality projections may differ substantially at
young ages but have little effect on an old annuity portfolio. Conversely, small differences after
age 90 may matter greatly for a portfolio concentrated at retirement ages. Liability push-forwards
translate demographic transport into financial transport.

17.10 Prudent pricing and longevity capital
Let ˆµx be a baseline estimated residual lifetime law. A Wasserstein ambiguity set is
Bp(ˆµx,r) ={ν∈Pp(R+) : Wp(ν,ˆµx)≤r}.
A prudent annuity price may be written as
Πann r = sup
ν∈Bp(ˆµx,r)
Eν[gann(T )].
Similarly, a prudent capital diagnostic may use
Capr = sup
ν∈Bp(ˆµx,r)
ρ ((gann)#ν),
where ρ is a risk measure such as VaR, TVaR or a spectral risk measure. This is the longevity
version of the Wasserstein-DRO construction in Lecture 8.
Proposition 17.7 (Simple robust annuity-price bound). Assume g : R+ → R is Lg-Lipschitz
and ˆµ∈P1(R+). Then
sup
ν: W1(ν,ˆµ)≤r
Eν[g(T )]≤ Eˆµ[g(T )] +Lgr.
If g(t) =t, the bound is sharp whenever the ambiguity set allows translation by r to the right.
Proof. For every feasible ν, the Kantorovich–Rubinstein dual implies
Eν[g(T )]− Eˆµ[g(T )]≤LgW1(ν,ˆµ)≤Lgr.
Taking the supremum over ν gives the result.
Actuarial takeaway
A Wasserstein radius in longevity modelling represents uncertainty about the lifetime
law. It can be calibrated from sampling error, model disagreement, historical backtesting,
expert stress or regulatory prudence. The radius is a modelling decision and should be
reported.
For a death benefit, the prudent direction may be opposite. A mortality improvement
increases annuity values but may reduce death-benefit present values. A robust framework should
therefore be contract-specific.
17.11 Old-age mortality and tail-aware transport
For annuities, old-age survival matters disproportionately. A global Wasserstein distance between
lifetime laws may hide differences after age 95 or 100. Tail-aware comparisons are therefore
useful; they parallel the tail-aware severity tools of Lecture 8.
Definition 17.8 (Tail-aware lifetime Wasserstein discrepancy). For α ∈ (0, 1), define the
upper-tail lifetime discrepancy
Wtail p,α(µ,ν)p = 1 1−α ∫ 1 α |Qµ(u)−Qν(u)|p du.
It is the Lp-distance between quantiles restricted to the longest 1−α fraction of lifetimes.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 298/416

=== page 299 ===
17.12 Multi-state health and long-term care
This focuses on the longest lifetimes. It is especially relevant for extreme longevity, late-life
annuity cash-flows and longevity reinsurance. One may also compare survival probabilities at
advanced ages,
tpx = P(Tx >t),
and report tail displacement curves
u↦−→Qν(u)−Qµ(u), u∈ [α, 1).
Remark 17.4 (Extreme longevity). Extreme-value thinking is not limited to non-life severity. In
longevity risk, the upper tail of the lifetime distribution corresponds to very old ages. Tail-aware
transport makes explicit whether a mortality improvement affects ordinary retirement ages or
extreme survival.
17.12 Multi-state health and long-term care
Life insurance and pensions often require more than alive/dead states. Health, disability and
long-term care risks can be represented by a finite state space
E ={healthy,disabled,long-term care,dead}.
A trajectory is the vector
Z = (Z0,Z1,...,ZT )∈ET +1.
A multi-state model defines a law on paths ET +1. Payments are push-forwards of this path law.
For example,
L =
T∑
t=0
vtht(Zt),
where ht is the state-dependent benefit at time t.
Definition 17.9 (Multi-state life path law). Let E be a finite health-state space. A multi-state
life path law over horizon T is a probability measure
Ξ∈P(ET +1)
on paths z0:T = (z0,...,zT ). A benefit schedule ht : E → R+ induces the present-value
push-forward
Law(L) =G#Ξ, G(z0:T ) =
T∑
t=0
vtht(zt).
Transport between multi-state models should use a state cost κ(e,f). For instance, matching
healthy to disabled should cost less than matching healthy to dead, depending on the application.
A path cost may be
c(z0:T,z′ 0:T ) =
T∑
t=0
wtκ(zt,z′ t).
This turns multi-state actuarial comparison into a dynamic transport problem on path space.
Computational note
For multi-state life and health models, the modelling work lies in the state cost. A good
cost should reflect benefit levels, transition severity, recovery possibilities and timing. This
is an instance of the cost-engineering principles collected in Appendix B.
Page 299/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

17.13 Joint lives and dependence
Many life products depend on more than one lifetime. Let Tx and Ty be future lifetimes of two
lives. A joint-life annuity may pay while both are alive; a last-survivor annuity may pay until
the second death. The relevant variables are
min(Tx,Ty), max(Tx,Ty).
The marginal lifetime laws do not determine these distributions. The dependence coupling
matters.
Definition 17.10 (Joint-life coupling). Let µx and µy be marginal residual lifetime laws. A
joint-life model is a coupling
γ∈ Π(µx,µy).
For a last-survivor present value g(max(Tx,Ty)), the law of the benefit is the push-forward of γ
by the map (s,t)↦→g(max(s,t)).
Worst-case or prudent values can be formulated as optimization over couplings:
sup
γ∈Π(µx,µy)
Eγ
[
g
(
max(Tx,Ty)
)]
.
This is a dependence-uncertainty problem analogous to the non-life aggregation problems of
Lecture 13.
Proposition 17.8 (Marginal lifetime laws do not identify survivor benefits). The law of
max(Tx,Ty), and therefore the law of a last-survivor benefit, is generally not determined by the
marginal laws µx and µy.
Proof. Take Tx and Ty with the same Bernoulli law on {0, 1}. Under the comonotonic coupling,
Tx = Ty, hence max(Tx,Ty) = Tx. Under the countermonotonic coupling, Ty = 1 −Tx, hence
max(Tx,Ty) = 1 almost surely. The marginal laws are the same, but the last-survivor distribution
differs.
Actuarial takeaway
Joint-life products are coupling problems. Marginal mortality tables determine individual
lifetime distributions, but not the dependence between lives. Transport provides the
feasible set and a language for prudent dependence analysis.
For more than two lives, one obtains a multi-marginal problem. If T = (T1,...,Td) has fixed
marginal lifetime laws µ1,...,µd, admissible joint models are
γ∈ Π(µ1,...,µd).
The aggregate liability may depend on all lifetimes, for example through survivor pensions, group
annuities or spouse benefits.
17.14 Federated quantile sketches for mortality comparison
Mortality and longevity data may be distributed across insurers, countries or pension schemes.
Privacy, governance or competition may prevent sharing individual records. One can still share
summary information such as quantile sketches of residual lifetime distributions and annuity
present values. This mirrors the one-dimensional statistical-OT shortcuts from Lecture 7; the
full longevity lab uses the same quantile representation in Lab ??.
