17.15 Mini-lab: longevity transport in R
Definition 17.11 (Mortality quantile sketch). A mortality quantile sketch for a one-dimensional
law µ is a finite collection
{(uk, ˆQ(uk)) :k = 1,...,K},
where 0 <u1 <···<uK < 1 and ˆQ(uk) approximates Qµ(uk). It is a compressed representation
of the residual lifetime, age-at-death or present-value distribution.
Suppose institution m holds a residual lifetime sample and reports approximate quantiles
ˆQm(u1),..., ˆQm(uK).
A central analyst can approximate one-dimensional Wasserstein distances by
ˆWp p(m,m′) =
K∑
k=1
ωk
⏐⏐⏐ˆQm(uk)− ˆQm′(uk) ⏐⏐⏐ p .
The same idea applies to subportfolio-specific longevity distributions, annuity present values or
country-specific mortality laws.
Remark 17.5 (What sketches do and do not contain). Quantile sketches are well suited for
one-dimensional lifetime laws and present-value laws. They do not fully describe multivariate
dependence, joint-life dependence or path dependence. For those objects, richer summaries are
required.
17.15 Mini-lab: longevity transport in R
This lab builds synthetic mortality tables, converts them into residual lifetime laws, computes
Wasserstein distances, and maps lifetimes through annuity present values. A fuller version can
be used as Lab ??.
# Synthetic longevity transport lab
set.seed(123)
ages <- 60:120
omega <- max(ages)
# Gompertz-Makeham style one-year death probabilities
qx _ table <- function(ages, A = 0.0005, B = 0.00003, c = 1.095) {
q <- A + B * c^ages
pmin(q, 0.999)
}
q _base <- qx _ table(ages, A = 0.0005, B = 0.000030, c = 1.095)
q _longev <- qx _ table(ages, A = 0.0004, B = 0.000022, c = 1.090)
curtate _pmf <- function(qx) {
n <- length(qx)
surv <- cumprod( c(1, 1 - qx[-n]))
pmf <- surv * qx
pmf[n] <- pmf[n] + prod(1 - qx) # close the table at omega
pmf / sum(pmf)
}
pmf _base <- curtate _pmf( q _base)
pmf _longev <- curtate _pmf( q _longev)
support <- 0:( length(ages) - 1)
Page 301/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 302 ===
17 LIFE, MORTALITY AND LONGEVITY TRANSPORT
# Lower generalized inverse for a discrete law on a fixed support
q _discrete <- function(pmf, probs, support) {
cdf <- cumsum(pmf)
vapply(probs, function(u) support[ which(cdf >= u)[1]], numeric(1))
}
u <- seq(0.001, 0.999, length.out = 2000)
Q _base <- q _discrete(pmf _base, u, support)
Q _longev <- q _discrete(pmf _longev, u, support)
W1 <- mean( abs( Q _base - Q _longev))
W2 <- sqrt( mean(( Q _base - Q _longev)^2))
life _ exp _base <- sum(support * pmf _base)
life _ exp _longev <- sum(support * pmf _longev)
# Annuity and death benefit present values
v <- 1 / 1.03
annuity _pv <- function(k) (1 - v^(k + 1)) / (1 - v)
death _pv <- function(k) v^(k + 1)
A _base <- annuity _pv( Q _base)
A _longev <- annuity _pv( Q _longev)
D _base <- death _pv( Q _base)
D _longev <- death _pv( Q _longev)
ans <- rbind(
lifetime = c(W1 = W1, W2 = W2,
mean _base = life _ exp _base,
mean _longev = life _ exp _longev),
annuity _pv = c(W1 = mean( abs(A _base - A _longev)),
W2 = sqrt( mean((A _base - A _longev)^2)),
mean _base = mean(A _base),
mean _longev = mean(A _longev)),
death _pv = c(W1 = mean( abs( D _base - D _longev)),
W2 = sqrt( mean(( D _base - D _longev)^2)),
mean _base = mean( D _base),
mean _longev = mean( D _longev))
)
round(ans, 4)
# Quantile displacement curve
plot(u, Q _longev - Q _base, type = "l",
xlab = "lifetime␣percentile",
ylab = "longevity␣stress␣in␣years",
main = "Quantile␣longevity␣displacement")
abline(h = 0, lty = 2)
The output separates three layers: movement in residual lifetimes, movement in annuity
present values, and movement in death-benefit present values. The same longevity stress moves
these financial objects in different directions.
17.16 Practical workflow
A transport workflow for longevity risk can be organized as follows:
1. choose the object: residual lifetime, age at death, annuity present value, death-benefit
present value, health-state path or portfolio liability;
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 302/416

=== page 303 ===
17.17 What to remember
2. construct the probability law under each mortality table or stochastic mortality model;
3. choose the cost: years of lifetime, discounted cash-flow distance, state-transition cost or
portfolio present-value distance;
4. compute Wasserstein distances, quantile displacement curves and tail-aware diagnostics;
5. map mortality differences through contract cash-flow functions;
6. report both demographic and financial distances;
7. perform sensitivity analysis with respect to mortality scenario, discount rate, tail focus and
dependence assumptions.
17.17 What to remember
Life, mortality and longevity problems are naturally distributional. A mortality table induces
residual lifetime laws. A contract maps those lifetime laws into financial present-value laws.
Optimal transport compares mortality scenarios, builds longevity stresses, averages mortality
models through barycenters and defines prudent ambiguity sets. The actuarial interpretation
comes from the chosen state space: years of life, age at death, health states, cash-flow vectors or
portfolio liabilities.
17.18 Exercises
Exercise 17.1 (Life table as a measure). Given one-year death probabilities qx,qx+1,...,qω,
derive the probability mass function of the curtate future lifetime Kx. Check that the probabilities
sum to one when the table is closed at ω.
Exercise 17.2 (Residual lifetime Wasserstein distance). Let K0 x and K1 x have discrete distribu-
tions on{0, 1, 2}:
µ0 = (0.3, 0.4, 0.3), µ1 = (0.2, 0.3, 0.5).
Compute W1(µ0,µ1) using the quantile formula.
Exercise 17.3 (Quantile longevity stress). Let T 0 x ∼ Exp(λ0) and T 1 x ∼ Exp(λ1), with λ1 <λ0.
Derive the monotone transport map from T 0 x to T 1 x. Interpret it as a longevity stress.
Exercise 17.4 (Annuity push-forward). Let Kx be curtate future lifetime and let
g(k) =
k∑
j=0
vj.
Write explicitly the distribution of g(Kx) in terms of the distribution of Kx. Explain why this is
a push-forward.
Exercise 17.5 (Opposite effects). Show that a first-order stochastic increase in lifetime increases
the present value of a life annuity but decreases the present value of a death benefit paid at the
end of the year of death.
Exercise 17.6 (Wasserstein barycenter). Let two residual lifetime laws have quantile functions
Q0 and Q1. For weights 1 −λ and λ, derive the one-dimensional W2 barycenter quantile function.
Interpret the result as a blended mortality scenario.
Exercise 17.7 (Liability push-forward). Consider a portfolio of independent annuitants with
payments bi and lifetime vector K = (K1,...,Kn). Write the portfolio present value as a
function ofK. Explain how a joint law of lifetimes is pushed forward to a liability law.

Exercise 17.8 (Joint-life dependence). Let Tx and Ty have fixed marginal lifetime laws. Explain

Exercise 17.9 (Tail-aware longevity). Define Wtail 1,0.95 between two residual lifetime laws. Explain

A MATHEMATICAL TOOLBOX FOR OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
A Mathematical Toolbox for Optimal Transport
This appendix collects the mathematical notions used repeatedly throughout the notes. It is a
reference chapter on push-forwards, couplings, dual potentials, convexity, projections, Wasserstein
geodesics, barycenters, dynamic formulations, entropic regularization, unbalanced transport and
Schrödinger bridges. The aim is not to give a complete treatment of optimal transport, but to
fix notation and to state the assumptions behind the objects used in the actuarial chapters.
Further reading
Standard references for optimal transport include Villani 2003; Villani 2008; Santambrogio
2015; Ambrosio et al. 2008; Peyré and Cuturi 2019; Figalli and Glaudo 2021. For convex
analysis, see Rockafellar 1970; Rockafellar and Wets 1998. For computational and entropic
OT, see Cuturi 2013; Benamou et al. 2015; Peyré and Cuturi 2019. For unbalanced
transport and Schrödinger bridges, see Chizat et al. 2018b; Liero et al. 2018; Léonard 2014;
Chen et al. 2016.
Throughout the appendix, (X,dX ) and (Y,dY) denote Polish spaces, usually subsets of
Euclidean spaces in actuarial applications. We write P(X ) for the set of probability measures on
X. For p≥ 1,
Pp(X ) =
{
µ∈P(X ) :
∫
X
dX (x,x0)pµ( dx)<∞ for some x0 ∈X
}
.
The choice of x0 is immaterial. Finite positive measures are denoted by M+(X ). Push-forwards
are written T#µ, and couplings are written Π(µ,ν), consistently with the main lectures.
Actuarial takeaway
Throughout the notes, one-dimensional quantiles are lower generalized inverses, Qµ(u) =
inf{x : Fµ(x) ≥ u}. With atoms or ties, formulas involving Fµ(X) should be read
either through the quantile coupling (Qµ(U),Qν(U)) or through randomized ranks. This
convention matters for empirical VaR, TVaR, one-dimensional barycenters and rank-
preserving maps.
A.1 Measures, random variables and push-forwards
A probability measure is the distributional object behind a random variable. In these notes,
losses, premiums, reserves, capital scenarios and climate losses are often treated as probability
measures rather than only as scalar summaries.
Let X : Ω →X be a random variable defined on a probability space (Ω,F, P). Its law is the
push-forward of P by X:
Law(X) =X#P.
Equivalently, for any measurable A⊆X,
Law(X)(A) = P(X∈A).
Definition A.1 (Moment space). Let (X,d) be a metric space and p≥ 1. The Wasserstein
moment space Pp(X ) is the set of probability measures µ∈P(X ) with finite p-th moment,
namely ∫
d(x,x0)pµ( dx)<∞
for some, hence every, x0 ∈X.

A.2 Couplings, marginal projections and stochastic kernels
Definition A.2 (Push-forward). Let T : X → Y be measurable and let µ ∈ P(X ). The
push-forward of µ by T, denoted T#µ, is the probability measure onY defined by
T#µ(B) =µ(T−1(B)), B∈B(Y).
Equivalently, if X∼µ, then T (X)∼T#µ.
Proposition A.1 (Change of variables for push-forwards). Let T : X→Y be measurable and
let µ∈P(X ). For every nonnegative measurable function h :Y→ [0,∞],
∫
Y
h(y) (T#µ)( dy) =
∫
X
h(T (x))µ( dx).
The same identity holds for any integrable real-valued h.
Proof. First prove the identity for indicators h = 1B, where it is exactly the definition of T#µ.
Extend by linearity to nonnegative simple functions, by monotone convergence to nonnegative
measurable functions, and by decomposition into positive and negative parts to integrable
functions.
Discrete case. If µ = ∑n i=1 aiδxi, then
T#µ =
n∑
i=1
aiδT (xi).
A deterministic map moves locations while preserving their masses.
Smooth density case. If X = Y = Rd, T is a diffeomorphism, and µ and ν = T#µ have
densities ρµ and ρν, then
ρµ(x) =ρν(T (x))| detDT (x)|.
This formula is useful for intuition, but most actuarial applications in the notes allow atoms,
discrete grids or empirical measures, and therefore do not rely on smoothness.
Pull-back versus push-forward. The push-forward acts on measures. The pull-back acts on
functions. If h :Y→ R, then T∗h =h◦T, and
∫
Y
h d(T#µ) =
∫
X
T∗h dµ.
Actuarial takeaway
If Π : X → R+ is a tariff rule and X ∼ µX, the induced premium distribution is
Law(Π(X)) = Π#µX. If T (l) = (l−d)+ ∧u is a deductible-limit transformation, the
payment law is T# Law(L). The same notation covers stress maps, recalibrations and
reserving aggregations.
A.2 Couplings, marginal projections and stochastic kernels
Optimal transport is optimization over joint distributions with prescribed marginals.
Definition A.3 (Coupling). Let µ∈P(X ) and ν∈P(Y). A coupling between µ and ν is a
probability measure γ∈P(X×Y) such that
(p1)#γ =µ, (p2)#γ =ν,
where p1(x,y) =x and p2(x,y) =y. The set of all such couplings is denoted by
Π(µ,ν).
Page 317/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 318 ===
A MATHEMATICAL TOOLBOX FOR OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
Definition A.4 (Product coupling). The product coupling of µ and ν is the product measure
µ⊗ν∈ Π(µ,ν). If (X,Y )∼µ⊗ν, then X and Y are independent.
Proposition A.2 (Non-emptiness and convexity). For any µ∈P(X ) and ν∈P(Y), the set
Π(µ,ν) is non-empty and convex. If X and Y are Polish, then Π(µ,ν) is compact for weak
convergence.
Proof. Non-emptiness follows from µ⊗ν. Convexity follows because marginalization is linear.
Compactness follows from tightness of the fixed marginals and Prokhorov’s theorem; closedness
follows because weak convergence preserves marginals when tested against bounded continuous
functions.
Discrete case. For µ = ∑n i=1 aiδxi and ν = ∑m j=1 bjδyj, a coupling is a nonnegative matrix
Γ = (γij)∈ Rn×m + satisfying
m∑
j=1
γij =ai,
n∑
i=1
γij =bj.
This is the transportation polytope introduced in Definition 3.2.
Definition A.5 (Markov kernel and disintegration). A Markov kernel from X to Y is a map
K : X×B(Y) → [0, 1] such that K(x,·) is a probability measure for every x, and x↦→K(x,B)
is measurable for every measurable B. A coupling γ∈ Π(µ,ν) admits a disintegration
γ( dx, dy) =µ( dx)K(x, dy)
under the usual standard-Borel assumptions.
A deterministic map T corresponds to the special kernel K(x, dy) = δT (x)( dy). Thus Monge
maps are deterministic couplings, whereas Kantorovich plans allow randomization or mass
splitting.
Definition A.6 (Graph coupling). If T : X→Y is measurable and T#µ = ν, the graph coupling
induced by T is
(Id,T )#µ∈ Π(µ,ν).
It is concentrated on{(x,T (x)) :x∈X}.
Actuarial takeaway
A coupling is a dependence model with fixed marginals. It is also an audit trail: the kernel
K(x, dy) records how mass starting at actuarial state x is redistributed to target states
y. This interpretation is used in portfolio drift, reinsurance dependence uncertainty and
scenario design.
Actuarial takeaway
A coupling aligns two marginal laws. It does not by itself identify the causal effect of an
intervention on an individual unit. Such an interpretation requires additional structure,
such as an intervention design, a structural causal model, ignorability assumptions or rank
preservation justified outside the transport problem.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 318/416

A.3 Monge maps, Kantorovich plans and optimal transport
A.3 Monge maps, Kantorovich plans and optimal transport
Let c :X×Y→ R∪{+∞} be a lower semicontinuous cost.
Definition A.7 (Monge problem). The Monge problem from µ to ν is
Mc(µ,ν) = inf T :T#µ=ν
∫
X
c(x,T (x))µ( dx),
with value +∞ if no admissible map exists.
This formulation is deterministic: each source state x is sent to one target state T (x). It may
fail to have feasible maps, especially with atoms.
Definition A.8 (Kantorovich problem). The Kantorovich relaxation is
OTc(µ,ν) = inf γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
X×Y
c(x,y)γ( dx, dy).
An optimizer γ⋆, when it exists, is called an optimal transport plan.
The Kantorovich problem is linear in γ. In finite spaces, it is a linear program. This is the
source of both its computational tractability and its dual interpretation.
Proposition A.3 (Existence of optimal plans). Let X and Y be Polish spaces and let c : X×Y→
[0, +∞] be lower semicontinuous. Then the Kantorovich problem admits an optimal plan, possibly
with infinite value. If at least one coupling has finite cost, the optimal value is finite and attained.
Proof. The set Π(µ,ν) is compact for weak convergence. The functional γ↦→
∫
c dγ is lower
semicontinuous by the portmanteau theorem. A minimizing sequence therefore has a weakly
convergent subsequence, and the limit is optimal.
Cost as modelling choice. The cost c decides which changes are small or large. For claim
amounts, c(x,y) = |x−y|p measures monetary displacement. For premium changes, a relative
cost such as | logx− logy|p may be more meaningful. For cash-flow vectors, c may combine
amount, timing and discounting. For geographical exposures, c may be based on physical distance,
hazard distance or administrative distance. Appendix B is devoted to these choices.
A.4 Wasserstein distances and convergence
WhenX =Y and c(x,y) =d(x,y)p, optimal transport defines the p-Wasserstein distance.
Definition A.9 (Wasserstein distance). Let (X,d) be a metric space and let p ≥ 1. For
µ,ν∈Pp(X ),
Wp(µ,ν) =
(
inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
X×X
d(x,y)pγ( dx, dy)
)1/p
.
The distance Wp lifts the ground geometry d from points to probability measures. For Dirac
masses,
Wp(δx,δy) =d(x,y).
Thus Wasserstein distance recognizes that moving a loss from 1000 to 1001 is small, while moving
it from 1000 to 100000 is large.
Proposition A.4 (Metric and convergence). For p≥ 1, Wp defines a metric on Pp(X ). On
Polish spaces,
Wp(µn,µ)→ 0
if and only if µn ⇒µ weakly and the p-th moments converge.
This proposition is the background for the statistical discussion in Lecture 7: empirical
Wasserstein distances estimate population distances, but the rate depends strongly on dimension
and moment assumptions.
Page 319/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

A.5 Quantile maps and monotone rearrangement
In dimension one, the optimal transport map has an explicit form. For a distribution function F,
define the generalized inverse
Qµ(u) =F−1 µ (u) = inf{x∈ R :Fµ(x)≥u}, u∈ (0, 1).
The notation Qµ is used throughout the notes.
Definition A.10 (Quantile map). Let µ,ν∈P(R). A rank-preserving quantile map from µ to
ν is
T (x) =Qν(Fµ(x))
when Fµ is continuous. In the presence of atoms, one may use a randomized rank
U =Fµ(X−) +V{Fµ(X)−Fµ(X−)}, V ∼ Unif(0, 1),
and map X to Qν(U).
Theorem A.1 (Monotone rearrangement). Let µ,ν∈Pp(R). For p≥ 1,
Wp p(µ,ν) =
∫ 1
0
|Qµ(u)−Qν(u)|p du.
The coupling (Qµ(U),Qν(U)), with U∼ Unif(0, 1), is optimal. More generally, it is optimal for
costs c(x,y) =h(x−y) with h convex.
Actuarial takeaway
Quantile transport preserves ranks. A claim, premium or other one-dimensional actuarial
quantity at percentile u in the source distribution is sent to the value at percentile u in the
target distribution. This is used for severity stresses, premium transformations, longevity
stresses and one-dimensional barycenters.
A.6 Dual potentials and the c-transform
Duality gives a second view of optimal transport. It introduces potentials, which can be
interpreted as shadow prices, sensitivity functions or certificates of optimality.
Definition A.11 (Kantorovich potentials). Under the usual assumptions ensuring strong duality,
OTc(µ,ν) = sup
φ,ψ
{∫
φ dµ +
∫
ψ dν :φ(x) +ψ(y)≤c(x,y)
}
.
A feasible pair (φ,ψ) is called a pair of Kantorovich potentials. If it attains the supremum, it is
called a pair of optimal Kantorovich potentials.
In the discrete problem with cost matrixC = (Cij), the dual is
maxu,v



∑
i
aiui +
∑
j
bjvj :ui +vj ≤Cij


.
Definition A.12 (c-transform). For a function φ :X→ R∪{−∞}, its c-transform is
φc(y) = inf
x∈X
{c(x,y)−φ(x)}.
Similarly, for ψ :Y→ R∪{−∞}, define
ψ¯c(x) = inf
y∈Y
{c(x,y)−ψ(y)}.

A.7 Convex analysis for quadratic optimal transport
At the optimum, one can often take ψ = φc and φ = ψ¯c. The c-transform is the analogue,
for a general transport cost, of the Legendre–Fenchel transform.
Definition A.13 (Contact set). For a feasible pair (φ,ψ), the contact set is
Cφ,ψ ={(x,y) :φ(x) +ψ(y) =c(x,y)}.
Proposition A.5 (Complementary slackness). If γ⋆ is primal optimal and (φ⋆,ψ⋆) is dual
optimal, then
supp(γ⋆)⊆Cφ⋆,ψ⋆.
In finite form,
γ⋆ ij > 0 =⇒ u⋆ i +v⋆ j =Cij.
Proof. The slack c(x,y) −φ⋆(x) −ψ⋆(y) is nonnegative. Equality of primal and dual values
implies that its integral under γ⋆ is zero, hence it is zero γ⋆-almost surely.
Definition A.14 (c-cyclical monotonicity). A set S⊆X×Y is c-cyclically monotone if, for
every finite family (x1,y1),..., (xN,yN)∈S and every permutation σ of{1,...,N},
N∑
k=1
c(xk,yk)≤
N∑
k=1
c(xk,yσ(k)).
Under standard assumptions, optimal plans are concentrated on c-cyclically monotone sets.
In one dimension with c(x,y) =|x−y|p, this reduces to no-crossing monotone matching.
Remark A.1 (Potentials versus Brenier potentials). The word “potential” is used in two related
but distinct ways. Kantorovich potentials are dual variables. Brenier potentials are convex
functions whose gradients define optimal maps for quadratic transport. They are related through
convex conjugacy, but they are not the same object.
A.7 Convex analysis for quadratic optimal transport
Quadratic optimal transport relies on elementary convex analysis. This subsection is placed after
duality because the link becomes clearest once Kantorovich potentials and contact sets have been
introduced.
Definition A.15 (Convex function and subdifferential). Let C⊆ Rd be convex. A function
u :C→ R∪{+∞} is convex if
u((1−t)x +ty)≤ (1−t)u(x) +tu(y), x,y∈C, t∈ [0, 1].
For a proper convex function u : Rd → R∪{+∞}, the subdifferential atx is
∂u(x) ={y∈ Rd :u(z)≥u(x) +y· (z−x) for allz∈ Rd}.
If u is differentiable atx, then ∂u(x) ={∇u(x)}.
Definition A.16 (Legendre–Fenchel transform). The convex conjugate of a proper function
u : Rd → R∪{+∞} is
u∗(y) = sup
x∈Rd
{x·y−u(x)}.
The Fenchel inequality is
u(x) +u∗(y)≥x·y.
For proper closed convex functions, equality holds if and only if y ∈ ∂u(x), equivalently
x∈∂u∗(y).
Page 321/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 322 ===
A MATHEMATICAL TOOLBOX FOR OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
Proposition A.6 (Quadratic OT duality and Fenchel equality). Let c(x,y) = 1 2 ∥x−y∥2. If
(φ,ψ) is dual feasible for the Kantorovich problem, and
u(x) = 1 2∥x∥2 −φ(x), v(y) = 1 2∥y∥2 −ψ(y),
then u(x) +v(y)≥x·y. On the contact set
φ(x) +ψ(y) = 1 2∥x−y∥2,
one has equality in the Fenchel inequality. Thus, when v = u∗, the contact set is contained in the
graph of ∂u.
Proof. The dual inequality is
φ(x) +ψ(y)≤ 1 2∥x−y∥2 = 1 2∥x∥2 + 1 2∥y∥2 −x·y.
Rearranging gives u(x) + v(y) ≥x·y. Equality is exactly the contact condition. If v = u∗,
equality in Fenchel’s inequality is equivalent toy∈∂u(x).
Theorem A.2 (Brenier theorem, informal statement). Let µ,ν∈P2(Rd), and assume that µ is
absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure. For the quadratic cost c(x,y) = 1 2 ∥x−y∥2,
there exists a convex function Φ : Rd → R∪{+∞} such that
T (x) =∇Φ(x), T#µ =ν,
and T is the optimal transport map from µ to ν. The map is unique µ-almost surely.
If ϕ is a Kantorovich potential for the cost 1 2 ∥x−y∥2, then one may write
Φ(x) = 1 2∥x∥2 −ϕ(x), T (x) =x−∇ϕ(x) =∇Φ(x),
where differentiability holds.
Definition A.17 (Monge–Ampère equation, formal). In the smooth positive-density setting, if
T =∇Φ pushes ρ0(x) dx to ρ1(y) dy, then formally
ρ0(x) =ρ1(∇Φ(x)) det(D2Φ(x)).
This nonlinear PDE is the Monge–Ampère equation associated with quadratic optimal transport.
Actuarial takeaway
For quadratic OT, convex analysis explains why the optimal map is not an arbitrary
regression-like transformation. Under Brenier’s hypotheses, it is a gradient of a convex
function. This imposes a monotonicity structure on multivariate stresses and scenario maps.
In empirical actuarial data, however, atoms, ties and categorical covariates often lead to
non-unique plans, mass splitting or barycentric summaries rather than true deterministic
maps.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 322/416

A.8 Projections in optimal transport
A.8 Projections in optimal transport
The word “projection” appears in several different ways in the notes. The distinctions below
avoid a common source of confusion.
Definition A.18 (Marginal projection). For γ∈P(X×Y), the marginal projections are (p1)#γ
and (p2)#γ. This is marginalization, not orthogonal projection.
Definition A.19 (Barycentric projection of a plan). Assume that Y⊆ Rd, γ∈ Π(µ,ν), and
γ( dx, dy) =µ( dx)K(x, dy). The barycentric projection of γ is
¯Tγ(x) =
∫
Y
yK(x, dy),
whenever the integral is well-defined. In the discrete case,
¯TΓ(xi) = 1 ai m∑ j=1 γijyj, ai = m∑ j=1 γij.
The barycentric projection is a deterministic summary of a plan. It generally does not push
µ exactly to ν. It loses conditional dispersion.
Definition A.20 (Metric projection in Wasserstein space). Let C⊆Pp(X ). A Wasserstein
projection of µ ontoC is any solution of
ΠWp C (µ)∈ arg min η∈C Wp p(µ,η).
The projection may not be unique.
Definition A.21 (Bregman divergence). Let Φ be a differentiable convex function on a convex
set D. The Bregman divergence generated by Φ is
DΦ(x,y) = Φ(x)− Φ(y)−⟨∇Φ(y),x−y⟩.
It is nonnegative by convexity of Φ, but it is generally not symmetric and does not satisfy the
triangle inequality.
For the entropy generator ϕ(t) =t logt−t, the associated Bregman divergence is
Dϕ(s,t) =s log s t −s +t.
Summing over entries gives the generalized Kullback–Leibler divergence
KL(P|Q) =
∑
i,j
{
pij log pij qij −pij +qij
}
.
Sinkhorn iterations can be interpreted as alternating KL/Bregman projections onto marginal
constraint sets; this is different from Wasserstein metric projection and from Euclidean projection.
A.9 Displacement interpolation and Wasserstein geodesics
A Wasserstein distance turns probability measures into points in a metric space. It is therefore
natural to ask what a shortest path between two probability measures is.
Definition A.22 (Displacement interpolation). Let γ⋆ ∈ Π(µ0,µ1) be an optimal plan for W2
on Rd. For t∈ [0, 1], define
et(x,y) = (1−t)x +ty, µt = (et)#γ⋆.
The curve (µt)t∈[0,1] is a displacement interpolation between µ0 and µ1.

A MATHEMATICAL TOOLBOX FOR OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
If γ⋆ = (Id,T )#µ0, then
µt = ((1−t)Id +tT )#µ0.
In the discrete case,
µt =
∑
i,j
γ⋆ ijδ(1−t)xi+tyj.
Definition A.23 (Constant-speed Wasserstein geodesic). A curve (µt)t∈[0,1] ⊂P2(Rd) is a
constant-speed W2-geodesic if
W2(µs,µt) =|t−s|W2(µ0,µ1), 0≤s,t≤ 1.
Displacement interpolations built from optimal plans are W2-geodesics McCann 1997. They
should not be confused with linear mixtures (1 −t)µ0 + tµ1: mixtures average mass vertically,
while displacement interpolations move mass through the state space.
A.10 Barycenters and Fréchet means
Barycenters generalize averages from Euclidean points to probability measures.
Definition A.24 (Wasserstein barycenter). Let µ1,...,µK ∈Pp(X ), and let λ = (λ1,...,λK)
be weights in the simplex. A p-Wasserstein barycenter is any solution of
¯µ∈ arg min
µ∈Pp(X )
K∑
k=1
λkWp p(µ,µk).
For one-dimensional distributions, the quadratic Wasserstein barycenter satisfies
Q¯µ(u) =
K∑
k=1
λkQµk(u), u∈ (0, 1).
This formula is central for one-dimensional severity, premium, output and longevity applications.
Mixture versus barycenter. The mixture ∑ kλkµk keeps the original components and av-
erages probabilities. The Wasserstein barycenter averages locations through transport. This
distinction matters when constructing consensus reserve distributions, mortality-scenario sum-
maries or climate benchmarks.
A.11 Dynamic transport, velocities and action
The static Kantorovich problem compares two endpoint distributions. Dynamic optimal transport
represents the movement between them through a time-indexed path.
Let (µt)t∈[0,1] be a curve of probability measures on Rd, and let (vt)t∈[0,1] be a velocity field.
The continuity equation is
∂tµt +∇· (µtvt) = 0,
understood in the weak sense. It expresses conservation of mass along the path.
Theorem A.3 (Benamou–Brenier formula, informal statement). For µ0,µ1 ∈P2(Rd) Benamou
and Brenier 2000,
W2 2(µ0,µ1) = inf (µt,vt)
∫ 1
0
∫
Rd
∥vt(x)∥2 µt( dx) dt,
where the infimum is taken over curves satisfying the continuity equation with endpoints µ0 and
µ1.
The integral is the kinetic action of the path. This formulation gives mathematical meaning
to distributional speed, actuarial trajectory and least-action path.

A.12 Entropic regularization and Sinkhorn potentials
Adapted transport. When distributions live on paths, a coupling may use future information
unless non-anticipativity is imposed. Adapted, causal or bicausal transport restricts couplings so
that matching up to time t depends only on information up to time t. This distinction matters
in reserving, sequential pricing and scenario generation; see Lecture 15.
Mass variation. If total mass changes, the continuity equation is replaced by a source-term
equation
∂tηt +∇· (ηtvt) =σt,
where ηt is a finite positive measure and σt records creation or removal of mass. This is the
dynamic counterpart of unbalanced transport.
A.12 Entropic regularization and Sinkhorn potentials
Entropic regularization smooths the transport problem and leads to scalable algorithms. In the
finite case, let a∈ ∆n, b∈ ∆m, and let C∈ Rn×m be a cost matrix. For ε > 0, the entropic
problem is
OTε,C(a,b) = min Γ∈U(a,b)


⟨C, Γ⟩ +ε

i,j
Γij(log Γij − 1)


.
The solution has the scaling form
Γε = diag(u)K diag(v), Kij = exp(−Cij/ε),
whereu∈ Rn + andv∈ Rm + enforce the two marginal constraints.
Proposition A.7 (Entropic transport as KL projection). Let Kij = exp(−Cij/ε). The entropic
optimal transport plan is the solution of
min

Equivalently, it is the KL/Bregman projection of the Gibbs kernel K onto the marginal constraint

are entropic dual potentials, up to additive constants. They are smooth analogues of Kantorovich

The log-sum-exp function replaces the hard c-transform by a smooth c-transform. This is
why the entropic dual is differentiable.
Proposition A.8 (Envelope derivative). Assume the entropic optimal plan Γε(C) is unique.
Then ∂

OTε,C(a,b) = Γε,ij(C).
Consequently, ifC =C(θ) is differentiable, then

Bias and Sinkhorn divergence. Small ε gives a plan close to a sparse OT plan, but can be
numerically unstable. Large ε gives a smoother plan but introduces entropic bias. The Sinkhorn
divergence corrects part of this bias by subtracting self-transport terms, with the appropriate
self-cost matrices on the source and target supports.
A.13 Unbalanced transport and mass variation
Balanced OT compares measures with the same total mass and forces all mass to be matched.
Unbalanced OT compares finite positive measures and allows mass to be created, destroyed or
left unmatched at a penalty. This is natural for exposure growth, lapse, new business, changing
portfolio volume, claim count variation and climate exposure changes.
Let µ∈M+(X ) and ν∈M+(Y) be finite positive measures, not necessarily of the same
total mass. A generic unbalanced formulation is
inf
γ≥0
{∫
c(x,y)γ( dx, dy) +τ1D((p1)#γ|µ) +τ2D((p2)#γ|ν)
}
,
where D is a divergence between finite positive measures, often a relative entropy, and τ1,τ2 > 0
control the cost of changing mass.
Remark A.2 (Unbalanced OT is not automatically a distance). The KL-penalized value above is
a useful comparison criterion, but it is not automatically a metric. Metric structures such as
Hellinger–Kantorovich or Wasserstein–Fisher–Rao require specific dynamic or static formulations.
The plan γ describes the mass actually transported. The deviations between its marginals
and µ,ν describe source mass that is removed and target mass that is created. In actuarial
terms, these terms may represent lapses, new policies, portfolio expansion, claim-count changes
or unmatched exposure.
A.14 Schrödinger bridges and stochastic transport paths
A Schrödinger bridge is an entropic dynamic analogue of optimal transport. Instead of choosing
a least-cost deterministic displacement between two endpoint laws, one chooses the most likely
stochastic path measure, relative to a reference process, subject to endpoint constraints.
Definition A.26 (Schrödinger bridge). Let P be a reference path measure on trajectories
(Xt)t∈[0,1]. Given endpoint laws µ0 and µ1, the problem is
min
Q
KL(Q| P) subject to (X0)#Q =µ0, and (X1)#Q =µ1
The static two-time marginal of this problem is first a KL projection onto the set of couplings
with marginals µ0 and µ1. When the two-time reference law has a Gibbs form, this projection
becomes an entropic OT problem. The resulting bridge produces a stochastic transition from µ0

to µ1, unlike displacement interpolation, which gives a deterministic least-action path once an

Actuarial takeaway
Schrödinger bridges are useful when a stochastic path is more realistic than a deterministic
map: climate trajectories, reserving scenario evolution, epidemic risk and cyber incidents.

=== page 328 ===
B COST ENGINEERING FOR ACTUARIAL OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
B Cost Engineering for Actuarial Optimal Transport
Further reading
This appendix is about a modelling choice that is sometimes hidden behind optimal
transport notation: the ground cost. General references on optimal transport explain how
a cost selects a coupling; see Villani 2008; Peyré and Cuturi 2019; Galichon 2016. In
actuarial applications, the cost is not only geometric. It encodes money, time, regulation,
actionability, contractual constraints and governance. Choosing it is therefore an actuarial
modelling task. For compositional or categorical states, the ground geometry may be the
geometry of a simplex rather than a Euclidean coordinate system; see Aitchison 1982;
Egozcue et al. 2003; Pal and Wong 2020; Baxendale and Wong 2022; Fernandes Machado
et al. 2025.
B.1 Why cost engineering matters
An optimal transport problem has the form
inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
X×Y
c(x,y) dγ(x,y).
The two marginals µ and ν describe the distributions to be matched. The coupling γ describes
how mass is paired. The cost c(x,y) says which pairings are cheap, expensive, allowed or
forbidden.
In a mathematical introduction, one often takes
c(x,y) =∥x−y∥p.
This is a good default when the state space is Euclidean and all coordinates have comparable
meaning. But actuarial objects are rarely that simple. A state may contain monetary losses,
premium levels, categorical rating variables, territory codes, legal contract terms, policyholder
scores, payment calendars and exposure measures. A single Euclidean norm can then be
misleading.
Actuarial takeaway
Cost engineering is the step where the actuary translates the question “how different are
these two distributions?” into the question “what is the actuarial cost of matching state x
with state y?” The answer depends on the application.
There are three distinct roles for the cost.
(i) Geometry. The cost defines what it means for two risks, two claims, two policies, two
territories or two scenarios to be close.
(ii) Governance. The cost can encode constraints: legally fixed variables should not be
changed, illegal contract transformations are forbidden, large contractual jumps are penal-
ized, and non-actionable covariates receive infinite or very large cost.
(iii) Economics. The cost determines the unit of the Wasserstein value: dollars, squared
dollars, log-points, kilometres, duration-weighted cash-flow movement, or a dimensionless
normalized index.
The same pair of distributions can lead to very different transport plans under different costs.
This is not a defect. It reflects different actuarial questions.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 328/416

B.2 A general template
Let a state be written as
x = (xloss,xprem,xcov,xgeo,xscore,xcontract,xcf),
where the components may represent losses, premiums, covariates, geography, scores, contract
terms and cash-flows. A general composite cost can be written as
c(x,y) =
K∑
k=1
λkck(xk,yk) +ιA(x,y),
where ck are component costs, λk ≥ 0 are weights, and ιA is a feasibility penalty:
ιA(x,y) =
{
0, (x,y)∈A,
+∞, (x,y) /∈A.
The set A contains admissible transformations. For example, it may forbid changing a legally
fixed or non-actionable attribute, matching policies across incompatible contract types, or moving
a claim scenario to an impossible geographical state.
Definition B.1 (Composite actuarial cost). A composite actuarial cost is a cost of the form
c(x,y) =
K∑
k=1
λkck(xk,yk) +ιA(x,y),
where each component cost ck measures movement along one actuarial coordinate, λk ≥ 0 is the
exchange rate between that coordinate and other coordinates, and A is the set of admissible
pairs. The weights and the admissibility set are part of the model specification.
Definition B.2 (Actuarial ground cost). An actuarial ground cost is a measurable function
c :X×Y→ [0, +∞]
chosen to encode the actuarial meaning of moving mass from state x to state y. It may be
asymmetric, may take the value +∞, and need not be a metric.
The cost does not always need to be symmetric. Increasing an insured amount, a coverage
limit or a contribution by 20% may have a different commercial or regulatory cost from decreasing
it by 20%. Moving a policyholder from a high-risk to a low-risk class may have a different
interpretation from the reverse. Creating exposure in a region may not be the same as removing
exposure from that region.
Remark B.1 (Metric versus cost). A Wasserstein distance requires a metric-like ground cost
d(x,y)p if one wants metric properties. Many actuarial applications require only an optimal
transport cost. For scenario design, stress testing or contract comparison, asymmetry and infinite
penalties can be useful.
Page 329/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 330 ===
B COST ENGINEERING FOR ACTUARIAL OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
B.3 Loss amounts
Let x,y≥ 0 denote two loss amounts or severity levels. Several costs are natural.
Absolute monetary displacement.
c1(x,y) =|x−y|.
This cost is measured in monetary units. It is appropriate when the question is average claim
amount displacement. It is robust and directly interpretable.
Squared monetary displacement.
c2(x,y) = (x−y)2.
This cost penalizes large movements more strongly. It is useful when tail mismatches should
dominate the comparison, and it leads to the W2 geometry.
Relative or log-scale displacement. For heavy-tailed severities, absolute displacement may
be dominated by a few large claims. A log-scale cost is
clog(x,y) = (log(x +a)− log(y +a))2 ,
where a> 0 avoids singularity at zero. This cost compares multiplicative changes. Moving from
100 to 200 is then similar to moving from 10, 000 to 20, 000.
Tail-weighted displacement. If one wants to emphasize large losses, one may use
ctail(x,y) =w(x,y)|x−y|p,
with, for instance,
w(x,y) = 1 +η 1{x>u0 or y >u0},
where u0 is a high loss threshold and η > 0. This makes movements in the tail more expensive
than movements in the body of the distribution.
Capped displacement. Sometimes extremely large claim differences should not completely
dominate the comparison. A capped cost is
ccap(x,y) = min{|x−y|p,M},
where M > 0. This gives a bounded stress-diagnostic cost, but the resulting transport value is
no longer a standard Wp p distance.
Example B.1 (Inflation versus tail deformation). Suppose Y = (1+i)X. Under |x−y|, the cost is
proportional to the average monetary inflation effect. Under the log cost, the same transformation
has constant displacement log(1 + i) for all positive losses. The first cost emphasizes large claims;
the second emphasizes proportional change.
Actuarial takeaway
For losses, the first modelling decision is scale. Absolute costs answer a monetary question.
Log costs answer a multiplicative question. Tail-weighted costs answer a capital or stress
question.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 330/416

=== page 331 ===
B.4 Mixed actuarial covariates
B.4 Mixed actuarial covariates
Policyholder states often contain continuous, ordinal, binary and categorical variables. Let
x = (xcont,xord,xcat,xbin).
A useful cost is a weighted sum of normalized component distances:
c(x,y) =
∑
j∈C
λj
(
xj −yj
sj
)2
+
∑
j∈O
λjdj(xj,yj)2 + ∑
j∈K
λj1{xj ̸=yj}.
Here C, O, and K index continuous, ordinal and categorical variables. The constants sj are scale
parameters, such as robust standard deviations or actuarial materiality units.
Continuous variables. For variables such as age, insured value or claim history, one may use
cj(xj,yj) =
(
xj −yj
sj
)2
.
The scale sj should not be chosen mechanically. It can be a standard deviation, an interquartile
range, or a business unit such as 10 years of age or 10,000 dollars of insured value.
Ordinal variables. For bonus-malus level, credit-score band or risk tier, use the distance
between ordered levels:
cj(xj,yj) = (rank(xj)− rank(yj))2 .
Categorical variables. For vehicle body type, occupation class or coverage type, a simple
cost is
cj(xj,yj) = 1{xj ̸=yj}.
A richer version uses a substitution matrix Dj(a,b):
cj(xj,yj) =Dj(xj,yj).
For example, moving between two similar vehicle classes may be cheaper than moving from a
private passenger car to a commercial truck.
Actionabilityandfixedvariables. Somecovariatesshouldnotbechangedbyacounterfactual
construction or by a constrained matching rule. If A is a non-actionable, legally fixed or otherwise
preserved variable, impose
cA(xA,yA) =
{
0, xA =yA,
+∞, xA ̸=yA.
This forces transport to preserve that variable.
Actuarial takeaway
Mixed-variable costs are never purely statistical. They encode what the insurer regards
as comparable, actionable, legally admissible and economically material. The cost matrix
should therefore be documented like a model assumption.

B.5 Simplex-valued states and categorical probabilities
Categorical variables often enter actuarial models through predicted probability vectors. For
instance, a policyholder may have probabilities over vehicle body classes, occupation groups,
coverage choices or claim-type categories. These vectors live in a simplex rather than in an
unconstrained Euclidean space.
For d≥ 2, write the open probability simplex as
∆◦ d =
{
x∈ (0, 1)d :
d∑
r=1
xr = 1
}
.
The closed simplex ∆d allows zero components. The distinction matters: log-ratio and Dirichlet-
transport costs are naturally defined in the interior, whereas empirical category probabilities
may lie on or near the boundary.
Definition B.3 (Simplex-valued actuarial state). A simplex-valued actuarial state is a vector
x = (x1,...,xd) ∈ ∆d whose components represent probabilities or shares over mutually exclusive
categories. Examples include class-probability outputs from a multinomial classifier, exposure
shares by peril, payment-pattern shares over development years, or territorial composition vectors.
Why not use only Euclidean cost? The Euclidean cost ∥x−y∥p is easy to compute, but it
treats the simplex as an ordinary subset of Rd. This may be misleading because compositional
information is relative. Moving a component from .01 to .02 is small in absolute terms but
doubles the corresponding share; moving from .40 to .41 has the same Euclidean size but a very
different relative meaning. This is the central message of compositional data analysis Aitchison
1982; Pawlowsky-Glahn and Egozcue 2001; Egozcue et al. 2003.
Closure and log-ratio coordinates. Forz∈ Rd + \{0}, define the closure operation
C(z) = z ∑d
r=1 zr
∈ ∆d.
Log-ratio transforms move the simplex into a Euclidean space. Common choices are
alr(x) =
(
log x1
xd
,..., log xd−1
xd
)
∈ Rd−1,
clr(x)r = log xr g(x), g(x) =
( d∏
r=1
xr
)1/d
,
and the isometric log-ratio transform ilr, which chooses an orthonormal basis of the clr hyperplane.
One can then compute a Euclidean OT problem in transformed coordinates and map the result
back to the simplex.
Definition B.4 (Log-ratio transport cost). Let h : ∆◦ d → Rd−1 be a log-ratio coordinate map,
for example alr or ilr. The induced log-ratio cost is
ch(x,y) =∥h(x)−h(y)∥p, x,y∈ ∆◦ d.
This cost compares categorical probability vectors through relative proportions rather than
through raw coordinate differences.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 332/416

=== page 333 ===
B.5 Simplex-valued states and categorical probabilities
Aitchison geometry. The Aitchison inner product on ∆◦ d may be written as
⟨x,y⟩A = 1 d ∑ r<s log xr xs log yr ys ,
with associated Aitchison distance dA(x,y) = ∥ clr(x) − clr(y)∥, up to the chosen normalization.
This is useful when category probabilities should be compared by ratios rather than by absolute
gaps.
Dirichlet or multiplicative transport cost. An alternative is to work directly on the
simplex. Following the multiplicative transport geometry of Pal and Wong 2020 and related
Dirichlet transport ideas Baxendale and Wong 2022; Pistone and Shoaib 2024, one can use the
cost
cD(x,y) = log
(
1
d
d∑
r=1
yr
xr
)
− 1 d d∑ r=1 log (yr xr ) , wherex,y∈ ∆◦ d.
By Jensen’s inequality, cD(x,y) ≥ 0, with equality when y/x is constant componentwise, hence,
on the simplex, when x = y. This cost penalizes relative composition changes and is asymmetric
in general.
Definition B.5 (Simplex transport cost). A simplex transport cost is a ground cost on ∆d or
∆◦ d chosen to respect the compositional meaning of category probabilities. Typical examples are
Euclidean costs, log-ratio costs, Aitchison costs, Hellinger costs, Jensen–Shannon costs, KL-type
costs and Dirichlet transport costs.
Remark B.2 (Boundary handling). Costs involving logarithms require strictly positive components.
In empirical work, one must specify a boundary convention: add a small pseudo-count, group
rare categories, trim observations too close to the boundary, or use a bounded alternative such
as Hellinger or Jensen–Shannon cost. This is not a numerical detail; it changes the meaning of
categorical counterfactuals and rare-class transport.
From categories to the simplex. One practical route is to replace a categorical variable
C∈{1,...,d} by a predicted probability vector
ˆp(x) =
(
ˆP(C = 1|x),...,ˆP(C =d|x)
)
∈ ∆d.
A transport problem can then be run on ˆp(X) rather than on arbitrary integer labels. This
avoids imposing an artificial order on the categories and is particularly useful for counterfactual
and categorical-stability analyses of categorical variables; see the methodology and ternary
illustrations in Fernandes Machado et al. 2025.
Actuarial takeaway
For categorical variables, the cost should not depend on arbitrary label names. A sim-
plex representation lets the actuary compare uncertainty over categories: occupation
mixes, vehicle-body probabilities, claim-type probabilities, payment shares or territorial
compositions. The modelling choice then becomes the geometry of the simplex.
Simplex cost matrix in R. The following small helper computes three cost matrices for
simplex-valued data: Euclidean, Aitchison/clr, and Dirichlet transport. The parameter eps
implements a pseudo-count for boundary stability.
Page 333/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 334 ===
B COST ENGINEERING FOR ACTUARIAL OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
closure <- function(Z, eps = 1e-8) {
Z <- pmax( as. matrix(Z), eps)
Z / rowSums(Z)
}
clr <- function(Z, eps = 1e-8) {
Z <- closure(Z, eps)
logZ <- log(Z)
sweep(logZ, 1, rowMeans(logZ), "-")
}
simplex _cost <- function(X, Y, type = c("euclidean", "aitchison", "
dirichlet"),
eps = 1e-8) {
type <- match.arg(type)
X <- closure(X, eps)
Y <- closure(Y, eps)
if (type == "euclidean") {
D <- as. matrix(dist( rbind(X, Y)))
return( D[ seq _len( nrow(X)), nrow(X) + seq _len( nrow(Y))]^2)
}
if (type == "aitchison") {
Xc <- clr(X, eps)
Yc <- clr(Y, eps)
D <- as. matrix(dist( rbind(Xc, Yc)))
return( D[ seq _len( nrow(X)), nrow(X) + seq _len( nrow(Y))]^2)
}
# Dirichlet / multiplicative cost
outer( seq _len( nrow(X)), seq _len( nrow(Y)), Vectorize( function(i, j) {
r <- Y[j, ] / X[i, ]
log( mean(r)) - mean( log(r))
}))
}
B.6 Geographical costs
Geographyappearsinpropertyinsurance, climaterisk, motorinsurance, healthaccess, catastrophe
modelling and territorial pricing. The choice of geographical cost should reflect the mechanism
being modelled.
Euclidean or great-circle distance. If locations are coordinatess,t∈ R2, one may take
cgeo(s,t) =∥s−t∥p.
On the sphere, replace Euclidean distance by great-circle distance. This is appropriate when
physical distance is the relevant notion of closeness.
Administrative adjacency. If locations are postal codes, municipalities or rating territories,
physical distance may be less relevant than adjacency in a graph. Let G = (V,E) be a graph of
territories. Define
cgraph(i,j) = distG(i,j)p,
where distG is shortest-path distance. This cost respects the territorial structure used by the
insurer.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 334/416

=== page 335 ===
B.7 When spaces do not align: Gromov–Wasserstein costs
Hazard-based distance. Two territories can be geographically far but actuarially similar,
or close but hazard-wise different. Let h(s) ∈ Rq be a vector of hazard features: flood depth,
wildfire score, wind speed, earthquake intensity, heat exposure. Then
chaz(s,t) =∥h(s)−h(t)∥2.
This compares territories by risk profile rather than by location.
Hybrid distance. A hybrid geographical cost is
c(s,t) =λkmdkm(s,t)2 +λhaz∥h(s)−h(t)∥2 +λadmin1{region(s)̸= region(t)}.
This is useful when both physical proximity and administrative boundaries matter.
Example B.2 (Climate scenario matching). Let µ be the distribution of insured exposure by
location today and ν the exposure distribution under a future climate scenario. A geographical
OT problem can match current and future exposure masses. If the cost is based on hazard
vectors, the transport plan answers: which current risk profiles are closest to future risk profiles?
B.7 When spaces do not align: Gromov–Wasserstein costs
The transport problems considered so far compare measures living on the same space, or at least
on spaces that can be embedded into a common ground-cost framework. This is often reasonable:
two loss distributions live on R+, two premium distributions live on R+, two cash-flow vectors
live on RT, and two model-output distributions live on the same output scale.
In practice, however, actuarial objects are sometimes defined on different spaces. Two
portfolios may use different rating variables. Two countries may have different geographical
partitions. Two insurers may encode coverages differently. Two cyber portfolios may involve
different dependency networks. In such cases, it is not obvious how to define a pointwise cost
c(x,y), because x and y do not belong to the same type of space.
Gromov–Wasserstein transport addresses this problem by comparing internal geometries
rather than points directly; see Mémoli 2011; Peyré et al. 2016; Vayer et al. 2019. Let (X,dX,µ)
and (Y,dY,ν) be two metric measure spaces. Instead of specifying a cost between x∈X and
y∈Y, one compares pairwise distances:
dX(x,x′) and dY (y,y′).
A Gromov–Wasserstein discrepancy is defined by
GWp p(µ,ν) = inf γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫∫ ⏐⏐dX(x,x′)−dY (y,y′) ⏐⏐p dγ(x,y) dγ(x′,y′)
The coupling γ does not match points because their coordinates are directly comparable. It
matches points because their relational structure is similar.
Actuarial takeaway
Ordinary Wasserstein transport asks whether two distributions place mass in similar
locations. Gromov–Wasserstein transport asks whether two portfolios have similar internal
structures. This is useful when the variables, territories, coverages or networks are not
directly aligned.
Page 335/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 336 ===
B COST ENGINEERING FOR ACTUARIAL OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
B.7.1 Examples in actuarial modelling
Different geographical partitions. Suppose one catastrophe model uses postal codes and
another uses municipalities. There is no one-to-one correspondence between cells. Still, each
space has an internal distance, for example travel distance, exposure-weighted distance, or hazard
correlation distance. Gromov–Wasserstein compares the two spatial exposure distributions
through these internal distances.
Different rating structures. One insurer may classify motor policies by age, vehicle type and
region, while another uses telematics clusters and claims-history summaries. A direct cost between
classes is arbitrary. But within each portfolio, one can define a distance between classes based
on claim frequency, severity, lapse, exposure level or reserve response. A Gromov–Wasserstein
coupling then matches classes with similar relational roles.
Cyber dependency networks. Cyber risk is often structured by networks: firms share cloud
providers, software dependencies, managed-service providers or authentication systems. Two
cyber portfolios may not contain the same firms, but they may have similar dependency graphs.
A graph distance or diffusion distance can be used as dX and dY, and Gromov–Wasserstein
compares the network structures.
Contract and guarantee structures. Life or P&C contracts may differ across markets. One
space may contain deductibles, limits and exclusions; another may contain riders and embedded
guarantees. If an internal distance between contracts can be defined through cash-flow behavior or
risk response, then Gromov–Wasserstein provides a way to compare portfolios without requiring
identical contract features.
B.7.2 Discrete formulation
For finite spaces X ={x1,...,xn} and Y ={y1,...,ym}, let
DX ii′ =dX(xi,xi′), DY jj′ =dY (yj,yj′).
Leta∈ Rn + andb∈ Rm + be probability weights. A coupling is a matrixP∈ Rn×m + such that
P 1m =a, P⊤1n =b.
The discrete Gromov–Wasserstein objective is
inf
P
n∑
i,i′=1
m∑
j,j′=1
⏐⏐⏐DX ii′−DY jj′ ⏐⏐⏐ p PijPi′j′.
This is no longer a linear program in P. The objective is quadratic in the coupling, which makes
the problem computationally more difficult than classical discrete OT.
Computational note
In a finite actuarial application, the key inputs for Gromov–Wasserstein are not only
portfolio weights a,b. They are the two internal distance matrices DX and DY. Most of
the actuarial modelling work lies in constructing and validating these matrices.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 336/416

=== page 337 ===
B.8 Scores and predicted probabilities
B.7.3 Fused Gromov–Wasserstein
Sometimes part of the structure is aligned and part is not. For example, two portfolios may both
have claim frequency and exposure level, but use different geographical or contractual features.
One can then combine an ordinary transport cost with a Gromov–Wasserstein structural term.
A fused objective has the form
inf
γ∈Π(µ,ν)
∫
c(x,y) dγ(x,y) +λ
∫∫
|dX(x,x′)−dY (y,y′)|p dγ(x,y) dγ(x′,y′),
where c(x,y) compares aligned features and the second term compares internal structures.
Remark B.3 (Cost engineering warning). Gromov–Wasserstein does not remove modelling choices.
It moves them from a cross-space cost c(x,y) to within-space distances dX and dY. These
distances must be justified actuarially: geographical proximity, similarity of loss behavior,
similarity of contract response, or network dependence.
B.7.4 When to use it
Gromov–Wasserstein is useful when:
• the two portfolios have no common covariate representation;
• the relevant object is a graph, network or relational structure;
• geography or coverage categories differ across countries;
• contract features are not directly comparable;
• one wants to align clusters rather than individual observations.
It is less appropriate when the variables already live in a common, meaningful metric space. In
that case, ordinary Wasserstein transport is simpler, more stable and easier to interpret.
Actuarial takeaway
Use Wasserstein when locations are comparable. Use Gromov–Wasserstein when relation-
ships are comparable.
B.8 Scores and predicted probabilities
Let s,t denote two model scores, predicted conditional means, claim probabilities or risk rankings.
The appropriate cost depends on the scale of the score.
Raw-score cost.
c(s,t) =|s−t|p.
This is natural if the score is already on a meaningful monetary or risk scale. For a predicted
conditional mean,|s−t| is a displacement on the scale of the target variable.
Probability-score cost. If s,t∈ (0, 1) are probabilities, raw differences may underweight
changes near 0 or 1. A logit-scale cost is
clogit(s,t) =
⏐⏐⏐⏐log s 1−s − log t 1−t ⏐⏐⏐⏐ p .
This compares odds rather than probabilities.
Page 337/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 338 ===
B COST ENGINEERING FOR ACTUARIAL OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
Vector predicted probabilities. When a model outputs a full probability vector over
categories, the state is s,t ∈ ∆d, not s,t ∈ (0, 1). In that case, use the simplex costs of
Section B.5: Euclidean cost for absolute probability changes, Aitchison or log-ratio cost for
relative category composition, Hellinger or Jensen–Shannon cost for bounded comparisons, and
Dirichlet transport cost for a multiplicative simplex geometry.
Rank cost. Sometimes only ranking matters. If Rs and Rt denote empirical ranks, use
crank(s,t) =|Rs −Rt|p.
This cost is useful for audits focused on ordering rather than score calibration.
Calibration-aware cost. If the score s is interpreted as a conditional mean, it may be useful
to penalize changes in both score and calibration residual:
c((s,r), (t,u)) =|s−t|2 +λ|r−u|2,
where r = L−s and u = L′−t are residual-type quantities. This is useful when score repair
should not destroy loss-ratio diagnostics.
B.9 Contracts and coverage terms
Insurance contracts are not only points in Rd. A contract is a function that maps a gross loss ℓ
to an indemnity payment. For example, a deductible d and limit M define
Id,M(ℓ) = min{(ℓ−d)+,M}.
A natural distance between two contracts a and b is a distance between their indemnity functions:
ccontract(a,b) =
∫ ∞
0
|Ia(ℓ)−Ib(ℓ)|pω( dℓ),
where ω is a reference loss measure. It may be a fitted severity law, an exposure-weighted severity
law, or a grid measure emphasizing relevant loss levels.
Deductibles and limits. For two contracts a = (d,M) and b = (d′,M′), a simpler parameter
cost is
cparam(a,b) =λd|d−d′|p +λM|M−M′|p.
The functional cost is often more actuarial because it compares actual payments rather than
contract labels.
Coverage compatibility. Some transformations should be forbidden. A liability contract
should not be matched to a property contract unless the application permits this. Write
c(a,b) =ccontract(a,b) +ι{type(a) = type(b)}.
Example B.3 (Comparing product portfolios). Let µ be the distribution of contract terms in an
old book and ν the distribution in a new book. Using the indemnity-function cost, OT compares
portfolios by the payments their contracts generate across a loss grid. This is more meaningful
than comparing deductibles and limits separately when contract features interact.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 338/416

B.10 Reinsurance treaties
B.10 Reinsurance treaties
A reinsurance treaty is also a transformation of losses. If L is the gross loss and T is the ceded-loss
function, then
Lceded =T (L), Lnet =L−T (L).
A cost between treaties Ta and Tb may be defined by
ctreaty(a,b) = EL∼ω [|Ta(L)−Tb(L)|p],
where ω is a reference gross-loss law.
For layer treaties, the cost should reflect attachment points, limits and tail importance. For
example,
clayer(a,b) =
∫ ∞
0
|Ta(ℓ)−Tb(ℓ)|2w(ℓ)ω( dℓ),
with w(ℓ) increasing in ℓ if tail treaty differences are more important.
Actuarial takeaway
For contracts and reinsurance, costs defined on parameters can be misleading. Two treaties
with different parameters may have similar indemnity functions on the relevant loss range.
Conversely, small parameter changes near the tail can have large capital effects.
B.11 Cash-flow vectors
Let
x = (x1,...,xT ) andy = (y1,...,yT )
be two future cash-flow vectors, for instance reserve payments by development year or liability
cash-flows by calendar year.

cdisc(x,y) =

This compares cash-flows in present-value units.

A cumulative cost is

This penalizes persistent differences in payment development.

Example B.4 (Reserving model comparison). Let µ and ν be predictive distributions of future
reserve cash-flow vectors from two stochastic reserving models. A componentwise Euclidean cost
compares payment amounts year by year. A cumulative cost compares development paths. A
level-shape cost separates reserve level uncertainty from payment timing uncertainty.
B.12 Choosing weights
Composite costs require weights λk. These weights are not innocuous. They determine the
exchange rate between actuarial dimensions. There are several reasonable calibration principles.
Statistical scaling. Set λk = 1/sp k, where sk is a standard deviation, median absolute deviation
or interquartile range. This makes all coordinates roughly comparable.
Materiality scaling. Choose sk as a business materiality unit: 10,000 dollars of loss, one risk
tier, five years of age, one development year or another documented threshold. Then
ck(xk,yk) =
⏐⏐⏐⏐ xk −yk sk ⏐⏐⏐⏐ p
means “distance measured in materiality units.”
Economic scaling. Choose λk so that each component has an approximate monetary impact.
For instance, if a one-unit change in variable xk changes expected loss by bk, set
λk ∝b2 k.
This turns covariate movement into expected-loss movement.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 340/416

=== page 341 ===
B.13 Cost diagnostics
Governance scaling. Some weights reflect policy rather than data. For example, one may
assign a large weight to changes in legally fixed or non-actionable variables, or set the cost to
+∞ to forbid such changes entirely.
Computational note
A practical rule is to report results under at least three cost specifications: a baseline cost,
a tail-sensitive cost, and a governance-constrained cost. If conclusions change dramatically,
the cost is driving the result and should be part of the actuarial discussion.
B.13 Cost diagnostics
A good OT analysis should not report only the optimal value. It should also inspect how the
cost shaped the coupling.
Distribution of matched costs. Given an optimal plan γ⋆, examine the distribution of
c(X,Y ), (X,Y )∼γ⋆.
Large quantiles identify expensive parts of the matching.
Component contributions. If
c(x,y) =
∑
k
λkck(xk,yk),
report
Ck =
∫
λkck(xk,yk) dγ⋆(x,y).
This decomposes the transport cost by actuarial dimension.
Dual potentials. When available, Kantorovich potentials identify where the source and target
marginals exert pressure. In portfolio drift, high source potentials may identify profiles that are
expensive to remove; high target potentials may identify profiles that are expensive to create.
Sensitivity to weights. Compute the optimal value and key actuarial quantities for a grid of
weights λk. For example,
λtail ∈{0, 1, 5, 10}
shows how sensitive the plan is to tail emphasis.
Boundary and feasibility diagnostics. For log, logit and simplex costs, check how many
observations are close to the boundary. Report the pseudo-count, clipping rule or grouping rule
used to make the cost finite. If a cost contains infinite penalties, report the fraction of forbidden
pairs in the cost matrix and verify that the feasible transportation polytope is non-empty.
B.14 Mini-lab: building a mixed actuarial cost matrix in R
The following code builds a cost matrix between two small portfolios with continuous, categorical,
geographical and model-output components.
Page 341/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 342 ===
B COST ENGINEERING FOR ACTUARIAL OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
set.seed(123)
n <- 200
m <- 180
A <- data. frame(
age = rnorm(n, 45, 12),
value = rlnorm(n, 10, .4),
region = sample( c("urban", "suburban", "rural"), n, replace = TRUE),
score = runif(n),
lat = rnorm(n, 45.5, .3),
lon = rnorm(n, -73.6, .3)
)
B <- data. frame(
age = rnorm(m, 48, 13),
value = rlnorm(m, 10.1, .45),
region = sample( c("urban", "suburban", "rural"), m, replace = TRUE),
score = pmin( pmax( rbeta(m, 2.2, 4), 0), 1),
lat = rnorm(m, 45.6, .35),
lon = rnorm(m, -73.5, .35)
)
# robust scales
s _age <- IQR( c(A $age, B $age))
s _logvalue <- IQR( log( c(A $value, B $value)))
s _score <- IQR( c(A $score, B $score))
s _geo <- 0.25 # roughly a chosen materiality unit in degrees
# pairwise squared differences helper
outer _diff2 <- function(x, y, scale = 1) {
outer(x, y, function(a, b) ((a - b) / scale)^2)
}
C _age <- outer _diff2(A $age, B $age, s _age)
C _value <- outer _diff2( log(A $value), log(B $value), s _logvalue)
C _score <- outer _diff2(A $score, B $score, s _score)
C _geo <- outer _diff2(A $lat, B $lat, s _geo) +
outer _diff2(A $lon, B $lon, s _geo)
C _region <- outer(A $region, B $region, function(a, b) as. numeric(a ! = b))
lambda <- list(age = .5, value = 1.0, score = 1.5,
geo = .25, region = 2.0)
C <- lambda $age * C _age +
lambda $value * C _value +
lambda $score * C _score +
lambda $geo * C _geo +
lambda $region * C _region
summary( as. vector( C))
image(C, main = "Mixed␣actuarial␣ground␣cost")
This matrix can then be used in a discrete OT solver or an entropic Sinkhorn solver. The
key point is that the cost has been documented component by component.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 342/416

B.15 Mini-lab: cash-flow cost between reserving scenarios
B.15 Mini-lab: cash-flow cost between reserving scenarios
The following code compares two sets of cash-flow scenarios using discounted, cumulative and
level-shape components.
set.seed(1)
n <- 150
m <- 150
T <- 8
simulate _cf <- function(n, level _shift = 0, speed = 1) {
base _pattern <- dgamma(1:T, shape = 2 * speed, rate = .6)
base _pattern <- base _pattern / sum(base _pattern)
total <- rlnorm(n, meanlog = 12 + level _shift, sdlog = .35)
noise <- matrix( rgamma(n * T, shape = 20, rate = 20), n, T)
cf <- total * sweep(noise, 2, base _pattern, " *")
cf / rowSums(cf) * total
}
X <- simulate _cf(n, level _shift = 0, speed = 1.0)
Y <- simulate _cf(m, level _shift = .05, speed = 1.2)
cashflow _cost <- function(x, y, i = .03,
lambda _level = 1,
lambda _disc = 1,
lambda _cum = .5,
lambda _dur = .5) {
T <- length(x)
v <- (1 + i)^(-(1:T))
Sx <- sum(x); Sy <- sum(y)
qx <- x / Sx; qy <- y / Sy
Dx <- sum((1:T) * x) / Sx
Dy <- sum((1:T) * y) / Sy
level <- ( log(Sx) - log(Sy))^2
disc <- sum(v * (qx - qy)^2)
cum <- sum(( cumsum(qx) - cumsum( qy))^2)
dur <- ((Dx - Dy) / T)^2
lambda _level * level +
lambda _disc * disc +
lambda _cum * cum +
lambda _dur * dur
}
C _cf <- outer(1:n, 1:m, Vectorize( function(i, j) {
cashflow _cost(X[i, ], Y[j, ])
}))
summary( as. vector( C _cf))
image( C _cf, main = "Cash-flow␣scenario␣ground␣cost")
The cost separates total reserve level, discounted payment pattern, cumulative development
and duration. These components answer different actuarial questions.
Page 343/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 344 ===
B COST ENGINEERING FOR ACTUARIAL OPTIMAL TRANSPORT
B.16 Checklist for cost engineering
Before using an OT cost in an actuarial report, it is useful to answer the following questions.
(1) What is the state space? Losses, covariates, territories, model outputs, contracts, treaties,
cash-flows or scenarios?
(2) What does it mean for two states to be close? Monetary closeness, statistical closeness,
legal admissibility, policyholder impact, or physical proximity?
(3) Are all variables actionable? Should some coordinates be preserved with infinite cost?
(4) Are changes symmetric? Is an increase as acceptable as a decrease?
(5) What are the units of the transport value? Dollars, squared dollars, log-points, normalized
units, kilometres or discounted cash-flow units?
(6) How are component weights calibrated? Statistical scale, materiality, economic impact,
expert judgement or regulation?
(7) Does the cost emphasize the body or the tail of the distribution?
(8) Does the cost respect contractual constraints and product definitions?
(9) If the state is a probability vector or composition, does the cost respect the simplex
geometry and the boundary convention?
(10) If spaces do not align, are the internal distances for a Gromov–Wasserstein comparison
actuarially meaningful?
(11) How sensitive are the conclusions to reasonable alternative costs?
(12) Can the optimal coupling be explained to an actuarial, regulatory or business audience?
Actuarial takeaway
The cost function is part of the actuarial model. It should be versioned, justified, stress-
tested and documented. In many OT applications, the main source of model risk is not
the solver but the ground cost.
B.17 What to remember
The ground cost is not a technical nuisance to be chosen after the actuarial question has been
stated. It is the way the actuarial question is stated. A reasonable cost should specify the
state space, the units, the allowed moves, the relative weights, the treatment of tails, and the
treatment of categorical or simplex-valued components. When these choices are unclear, the
correct response is not to search for a universal cost, but to report sensitivity across several
credible costs.

Exercise B.1 (Loss scale). Let µ be the law of X and let ν be the law of (1 + i)X, with X > 0.
Compare the interpretation of W1(µ,ν) using absolute loss cost and the transport cost based
on | logx− logy|. Which cost is more appropriate for inflation monitoring? Which one is more
appropriate for capital impact?
Exercise B.2 (Categorical substitution matrix). Consider vehicle classes {sedan,SUV,truck}.
