=== page 346 ===
C DETERMINISTIC MAPPING VERSUS DIFFUSION PROCESSES
C Deterministic Mapping versus Diffusion Processes
Further reading
This appendix compares two ways of generating actuarial scenarios from a baseline dis-
tribution. The first uses a deterministic map or a deterministic flow. The second uses a
stochastic process, typically a diffusion or an entropy-minimizing bridge. The distinction
is central in recent generative AI: normalizing flows and neural ODEs learn deterministic
transformations, whereas score-based diffusion models, Schrödinger bridges, stochastic
interpolants and flow-matching formulations work with stochastic paths, conditional proba-
bility paths or velocity fields attached to distributional curves; see Rezende and Mohamed
2015; Dinh et al. 2017; Chen et al. 2018b; Sohl-Dickstein et al. 2015; Ho et al. 2020; Song
et al. 2021b; Anderson 1982; Hyvärinen 2005; Lipman et al. 2023b; Liu et al. 2023; Albergo
and Vanden-Eijnden 2023; De Bortoli et al. 2021; Léonard 2014. The actuarial question is
not which paradigm is more fashionable, but which one gives the right form of uncertainty,
traceability, tail behaviour and governance for losses, reserves, mortality tables or climate
scenarios.
C.1 Why this distinction matters
Throughout this appendix, t∈ [0, 1] denotes an artificial interpolation or modelling time unless
stated otherwise. It need not be calendar time. We write T for a static transport map, ϕt for
an ODE flow, K(x, dy) for a Markov kernel, and P for a law on paths. This keeps separate
four objects that are easily confused: endpoint map, marginal curve, conditional transition, and
complete path law.
Optimal transport is often introduced through a map. Given two laws µ0 and µ1, one searches
for a transformation
T :X→X, T#µ0 =µ1,
which is optimal for a chosen cost. IfX0 ∼µ0, the generated target sample is
X1 =T (X0).
This is a deterministic construction. Conditional on the starting point X0 = x, the endpoint is
fixed.
Generative AI has made a closely related distinction prominent. One may learn a single
invertible map from a simple latent law to the data law, as in normalizing flows. Or one may learn
a path of distributions, either through an ordinary differential equation or through a stochastic
differential equation. In the first case, randomness enters only through the initial latent variable.
In the second, additional randomness can enter along the path.
For actuarial science, this is not a technical detail. It changes the meaning of scenario
generation.
• A deterministic transport map is appropriate when one wants a reproducible rank-preserving
stress, a policy-transformation audit trail or a counterfactual matching rule.
• A stochastic diffusion is appropriate when the path itself contains genuine uncertainty:
claim arrival, cyber contagion, climate evolution, mortality improvement, reserve runoff,
lapse behaviour, or economic scenarios.
• A deterministic flow can still create complex distributions, but it does not represent process
noise conditional on the current state.
• A diffusion can represent uncertainty along the path, but it is less immediately traceable
at the individual observation level.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 346/416

=== page 347 ===
C.2 Three levels: map, ODE flow, diffusion
Actuarial takeaway
A deterministic map answers: given this baseline scenario, what is its matched target
scenario? A diffusion process answers: what random evolution, starting from this baseline
state, can lead to the target distribution while respecting a chosen stochastic dynamics?
C.2 Three levels: map, ODE flow, diffusion
It is useful to separate three objects.
Definition C.1 (Deterministic generator and stochastic generator). Let µ0 and µ1 be probability
measures on a state space X. A deterministic generator is a measurable map T : X→X such
that T#µ0 =µ1. A stochastic generator is a Markov kernel K :X×B(X )→ [0, 1] such that
µ1(B) =
∫
K(x,B)µ0( dx), B∈B(X ).
The deterministic case corresponds to the degenerate kernel KT (x, dy) =δT (x)( dy).
Proposition C.1 (Maps as degenerate kernels). Let X0 ∼ µ0. If T#µ0 = µ1, then γT =
(Id,T )#µ0 is a coupling in Π(µ0,µ1) and can be written as γT ( dx, dy) = µ0( dx)δT (x)( dy).
Conversely, a kernel-induced coupling µ0( dx)K(x, dy) is induced by a map if and only if K(x,·)
is a Dirac mass for µ0-almost every x.
Proof. The first marginal of (Id,T )#µ0 is µ0 and its second marginal is T#µ0 = µ1. The
disintegration with respect to the first coordinate is δT (x). Conversely, if K(x,·) = δT (x) almost
surely for a measurable selector T, then the joint law is (Id,T )#µ0.
Static deterministic map. A map T sends µ0 directly to µ1:
X1 =T (X0), T#µ0 =µ1.
When T is the optimal transport map, it is selected by minimizing
∫
c(x,T (x)) dµ0(x).
In dimension one, for continuous distributions,
T (x) =F−1 1 (F0(x)).
This is the usual quantile map.
Deterministic ODE flow. Instead of jumping directly from X0 to X1, one may define a
trajectory
d
dtϕt(x) =vt(ϕt(x)), ϕ0(x) =x,
and set
Xt =ϕt(X0), µt = (ϕt)#µ0.
The marginal laws satisfy the continuity equation
∂tµt +∇· (µtvt) = 0.
This is the deterministic language behind Benamou–Brenier dynamic optimal transport, continu-
ous normalizing flows and much of flow matching.
Page 347/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 348 ===
C DETERMINISTIC MAPPING VERSUS DIFFUSION PROCESSES
Stochastic diffusion process. A diffusion adds pathwise noise:
dXt =bt(Xt) dt +σt(Xt) dBt.
If ρt is the density ofXt, it satisfies the Fokker–Planck equation
∂tρt =−∇· (btρt) + 1 2 ∑ i,j ∂ij ( aij,tρt ) , at =σtσ⊤ t .
The diffusion term makes the path stochastic even after conditioning on the initial state. Score-
based diffusion models and diffusion Schrödinger bridges belong to this family.
Proposition C.2 (Weak Fokker–Planck equation). Let Xt solve dXt = bt(Xt) dt+σt(Xt) dBt
and let at =σtσ⊤ t . For every smooth compactly supported f,
d
dt E[f(Xt)] = E
[
bt(Xt)·∇f(Xt) + 1 2 tr{at(Xt)∇2f(Xt)} ] .
When Xt has a smooth density ρt, this weak equation is equivalent to the Fokker–Planck equation
displayed above.
Proof. Apply Itô’s formula to f(Xt) and take expectations. The martingale term has mean zero.
Integrating by parts yields the density form.
Computational note
The same endpoint law µ1 can be produced by many mechanisms: a quantile map, an
ODE flow, a stochastic diffusion, a jump process, a mixture model, or a Markov chain.
The endpoint distribution alone does not identify the generating mechanism. This is why
path assumptions matter.
C.3 A dictionary from generative AI to actuarial OT
The following table gives a useful translation.
Generative AI object Mathematical object Actuarial interpretation
Latent law µ0 baseline, simple prior, neutral scenario law
Data law µ1 observed portfolio, target stress, empirical law
Generator map T#µ0 =µ1 scenario transformer or counterfactual map
Normalizing flow invertible Tθ reversible stress or density model
Neural ODE ˙Xt =vt(Xt) deterministic scenario trajectory
Flow matching learned velocity field learned distributional motion
Score model ∇ logρt local direction of denoising or concentration
Diffusion model stochastic path law noisy scenario generator
Schrödinger bridge entropy-minimal path law least-surprising stochastic stress path
Guidance / conditioning conditional path law scenario under constraints or observations
The actuarial analogue is clear. A generative model does not merely generate pictures or
text. Abstractly, it generates samples from a probability law. In actuarial applications, that
law may be the distribution of annual losses, a vector of future reserve cash-flows, a portfolio of
policyholder profiles, a mortality surface, a cyber incident path, or a climate loss scenario.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 348/416

=== page 349 ===
C.4 Deterministic maps as actuarial audit trails
C.4 Deterministic maps as actuarial audit trails
A deterministic map has a strong audit-trail interpretation. If X0 = x, then the target scenario
is T (x). This is useful when one wants to explain how a baseline object was transformed.
Example C.1 (Severity stress). Let µ0 be a baseline severity distribution and µ1 a stressed
severity distribution. The monotone transport map
T (x) =F−1 1 (F0(x))
keeps the rank of every claim scenario. A claim at the 95th percentile remains a 95th percentile
claim after stress, but its amount is replaced by the stressed 95th percentile. This is simple,
transparent and easy to document.
The main strength of a deterministic map is accountability. The main limitation is that it
gives a single endpoint for each starting point. If the intervention or future evolution is genuinely
random, the map may understate uncertainty.
C.5 Normalizing flows: invertible deterministic generators
Normalizing flows learn an invertible map
Tθ : Rd → Rd, Z∼µ0, X =Tθ(Z)∼µθ.
The invertibility allows likelihood computation through the change-of-variables formula
logpθ(x) = logp0(T−1 θ (x)) + log ⏐⏐⏐detDT−1 θ (x) ⏐⏐⏐.
This is attractive when density evaluation matters. In actuarial terms, one could fit a flow to a
vector of losses or cash-flows and then simulate new scenarios.
Definition C.2 (Normalizing flow). A normalizing flow is an invertible differentiable map Tθ that
transports a tractable base law µ0 to a model law µθ = (Tθ)#µ0 and allows density evaluation
through the Jacobian change-of-variables formula. A continuous normalizing flow replaces Tθ by
the time-one map of an ODE.
However, invertibility is also a constraint. It can make it difficult to represent distributions
with very different geometry unless the architecture is sufficiently expressive. For actuarial state
spaces with atoms, zero masses, categorical variables, constraints and extreme tails, a naive
normalizing flow may be a poor model unless the state representation is engineered carefully.
Actuarial takeaway
A normalizing flow is a deterministic generator with a likelihood. This is useful for scenario
simulation and density estimation, but actuarial data often contain mixed variables, atoms,
truncation, censoring, contractual constraints and heavy tails. These features should be
modelled explicitly rather than hidden inside a black-box map.
C.6 ODE flows and flow matching
Continuous normalizing flows replace a single map by the time-one solution of an ODE:
˙Xt =vθ(t,Xt), X0 ∼µ0.
The endpoint X1 is still deterministic conditional on X0, but the map is constructed gradually.
The marginal laws satisfy
∂tµt +∇· (µtvθ) = 0.
Page 349/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 350 ===
C DETERMINISTIC MAPPING VERSUS DIFFUSION PROCESSES
Flow matching starts from a chosen family of probability paths t↦→µt and learns a velocity
field that realizes or approximates that path. The path can be induced by a coupling between µ0
and µ1, by a conditional probability path, by a Markov kernel, or by a stochastic process. Once
the velocity field is learned, one samples by solving an ODE from latent noise to data.
This is close in spirit to dynamic optimal transport. In Benamou–Brenier, the path and
velocity solve
inf
(µt,vt)
∫ 1
0
∫
∥vt(x)∥2 dµt(x) dt, ∂tµt +∇· (µtvt) = 0.
Flow matching does not necessarily minimize this exact kinetic energy, but it uses the same
geometric idea: distributions are moved through a velocity field. This should be read together
with Lecture 15: there,vt is an actuarial velocity field; here, it is also a learnable generator.
Example C.2 (Cash-flow scenario generation). Let X∈ RT be a vector of future reserve cash-
flows. A deterministic ODE flow can transform a simple latent distribution, such as N(0,IT ), into
a model for realistic cash-flow vectors. The velocity field can be constrained or post-processed so
that cash-flows are nonnegative, discounted present values are reasonable, and tail scenarios are
not smoothed away.
C.7 Diffusion models: generating by denoising
Score-based diffusion models use a different idea. The forward process gradually corrupts data
into noise. A simple version is
dXt =ft(Xt) dt +gt dBt,
chosen so that X1 is close to a simple distribution, often Gaussian. The reverse process then
transports noise back to data. Under suitable conditions, the reverse-time dynamics involve the
score
∇x logρt(x),
where ρt is the density of the noised data at time t. A neural network is trained to approximate
this score or an equivalent denoising vector field.
The important point is that the learned object is local in time. The model learns how to
remove a little noise at each step. This often scales better than learning a single global map.
Definition C.3 (Score and score-based generator). If a distribution at noise level t has density
ρt, its score is the vector field st(x) = ∇x logρt(x). A score-based generator estimates st and
uses it to construct a reverse-time dynamics from a simple noisy law to the target law. In
actuarial terms, the score is not a risk score; it is a density-gradient field.
Actuarial takeaway
For actuarial scenario generation, a diffusion model says: start from a simple noisy scenario
and iteratively denoise it until it resembles a realistic actuarial scenario. This can be useful
for high-dimensional objects such as spatial climate loss fields, reserve triangles, mortality
surfaces or cyber incident paths.
C.8 Probability flow ODE versus reverse SDE
Score-based diffusion models often come with two related samplers.
The first is a reverse-time stochastic differential equation:
dXt = ~bt(Xt) dt + ~σt dBt,
which generates random paths from noise to data.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 350/416

=== page 351 ===
C.9 Schrödinger bridges as controlled diffusions
The second is a probability flow ODE:
dXt =ut(Xt) dt,
whose one-time marginals match those of the stochastic reverse process under the ideal score.
Thus the same distributional evolution can sometimes be represented by either a stochastic
sampler or a deterministic ODE sampler.
This creates an important distinction.
• The ODE sampler gives deterministic trajectories conditional on the latent starting point.
It is more convenient for inversion, interpolation and audit trails.
• The SDE sampler keeps stochasticity along the path. It can explore more variation and
may be more robust when the learned score is imperfect.
In actuarial terms, one should not confuse equality of marginal laws with equality of path
laws. Two processes may produce the same distribution at each date while having different
conditional dynamics, different path volatility and different scenario interpretation.
Proposition C.3 (Marginal curves do not identify path laws). A curve of one-time marginals
(µt)0≤t≤1 does not determine the law of the full path (Xt)0≤t≤1.
Proof. Let Z be a Rademacher random variable and set Xt = Z for all t. Let ε be an independent
Rademacher random variable and define Yt = Z for t< 1/2 and Yt = εZ for t≥ 1/2. For every
fixed t, both Xt and Yt are Rademacher, so the one-time marginals coincide. But X0 = X1
almost surely, whereas Y0 = Y1 only with probability 1/2. Hence the path laws are different.
C.9 Schrödinger bridges as controlled diffusions
Schrödinger bridges provide a particularly clean bridge between optimal transport and diffusion
models. Let R be a reference path law, for instance a Brownian motion or a baseline actuarial
scenario generator.
Definition C.4 (Schrödinger bridge). Given a reference path law R and endpoint distributions
µ0,µ1, the Schrödinger bridge is the path law, among all path laws with endpoints µ0 and µ1,
that minimizes relative entropy with respect to R.
Given endpoint laws µ0 and µ1, the Schrödinger bridge solves
inf
P
{KL(P∥R) :P0 =µ0, P1 =µ1}.
It is the path law closest to the reference dynamics while matching the two endpoint marginals.
Proposition C.4 (Entropy projection interpretation). If the feasible set {P : P0 = µ0,P1 = µ1}
is non-empty and the KL projection exists, the Schrödinger bridge is the information projection of
the reference path law R onto the affine set of path laws with prescribed endpoints. In particular,
changing R changes the meaning of the bridge.
This proposition is often the most important modelling point: the bridge is not “the” stochastic
path from µ0 to µ1; it is the least surprising one relative to a reference dynamics.
For Brownian reference dynamics with noise level ε, the bridge has a controlled diffusion
representation
dXt =vt(Xt) dt + √ε dBt,
and the density satisfies
∂tρt +∇· (ρtvt) = ε 2∆ρt.
Page 351/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 352 ===
C DETERMINISTIC MAPPING VERSUS DIFFUSION PROCESSES
Compare this with deterministic dynamic OT:
∂tρt +∇· (ρtvt) = 0.
The difference is the diffusion term. When ε is small, the bridge is close to deterministic OT.
When ε is positive, the path keeps stochastic dispersion.
Actuarial takeaway
A Schrödinger bridge is a natural model for a stochastic counterfactual stress: match the
baseline law and the target law, but otherwise stay as close as possible to a documented
reference dynamics.
Example C.3 (Climate loss bridge). Let µ0 be the current annual loss distribution and µ1 a
target climate-stressed distribution. A deterministic OT path gives the least-action movement
of losses. A Schrödinger bridge gives the least-informative stochastic evolution from current to
stressed losses relative to a baseline climate-loss process.
Example C.4 (Longevity bridge). Let µ0 be the current distribution of future lifetimes and
µ1 a longevity-improved target distribution. A deterministic quantile map shifts every rank of
lifetime. A diffusion bridge can represent uncertainty around the transition path, which is more
appropriate when mortality improvement itself is a stochastic trend.
C.10 Deterministic counterfactuals versus stochastic counterfactuals
Counterfactual language makes the distinction especially clear.
A deterministic transport counterfactual has the form
Xcf =T (X).
It says that the observation X = x is matched with a unique counterfactual state T (x). This is
useful for recourse, audit and rank-preserving stress testing.
A stochastic counterfactual has the form
Xcf ∼K(x, dy),
where K is a Markov kernel. Conditional on X = x, several counterfactual endpoints are possible.
A diffusion path gives an even richer object:
(Xcf t )0≤t≤1 ∼Px.
It describes a distribution over complete counterfactual trajectories.
Actuarial takeaway
Deterministic counterfactuals are useful when the counterfactual rule is a policy trans-
formation. Stochastic counterfactuals are useful when the counterfactual world contains
residual uncertainty after the intervention is specified.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 352/416

=== page 353 ===

C.12 What can go wrong?
Both paradigms can fail actuarially.
Failure modes of deterministic maps. A deterministic map may be too rigid. It can preserve
ranks when ranks should not be preserved. It can create an artificial one-to-one matching between
baseline and target scenarios. It can understate uncertainty by replacing a range of plausible
outcomes with one matched outcome. In high dimension, the chosen cost may impose an arbitrary
geometry.
Failure modes of diffusions. A diffusion may generate plausible-looking but actuarially
invalid scenarios. It may violate accounting identities, cash-flow timing, positivity constraints,
contractual rules or regulatory restrictions. It may smooth extremes too much, or generate tails
that are visually rare but financially material. It may also be hard to explain why a specific
simulated scenario was produced.
Failure modes shared by both. Both maps and diffusions can learn the wrong distribution.
Both can hide data bias, model misspecification, insufficient tail information, changing exposure,
or an inappropriate cost. In actuarial applications, the learned generator must be audited as a
model, not merely used as a sampler.
C.13 Tail behaviour and extreme scenarios
Generative models are often judged by visual or average fit. Actuarial models must also be
judged in the tail. Suppose L is a generated loss and u a high threshold. The tail law
Law(L−u|L>u)
may be more important for capital than the fit of the body of the distribution.
Definition C.5 (Tail validation law). For a generated loss L and threshold u, the conditional
excess law is Law(L−u|L>u). A generator is tail-valid at level u only if this excess law, and
not merely the unconditional law of L, is acceptably close to the reference or target tail law.
A deterministic quantile map can preserve tail ranks exactly in one dimension. This is valuable
for transparent stress testing. A diffusion model may generate more diverse tail scenarios, but
only if the training procedure gives sufficient attention to extremes. Otherwise, repeated denoising
can underrepresent rare but material losses.
Practical diagnostics include:
Page 353/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 354 ===
C DETERMINISTIC MAPPING VERSUS DIFFUSION PROCESSES
• comparison of high quantiles and expected shortfall;
• comparison of threshold exceedance counts;
• comparison of tail-index estimates when relevant;
• Wasserstein distances restricted to upper-tail conditional laws;
• stress tests on generated scenarios rather than only likelihood or average fit.
Actuarial takeaway
For generative actuarial modelling, a model that looks good in the body but fails in the
tail may be worse than a simpler model whose limitations are transparent. Tail validation
is not optional.
C.14 Conditioning and guidance
Recent generative models often allow conditional generation. One specifies a condition C = c,
and samples from an approximation to
Law(X|C =c).
In actuarial science, conditions may include:
• territory, line of business, policy form or coverage layer;
• climate pathway or macroeconomic scenario;
• target loss ratio or capital constraint;
• mortality improvement scenario;
• regulatory or accounting constraint;
• observed partial cash-flow development.
In deterministic flow models, conditioning may be incorporated by learning a map
Tθ(z,c).
In diffusion models, conditioning can modify the reverse dynamics, either through a conditional
score or through guidance terms. In both cases, actuarial conditioning must be checked for
coherence. A model should not generate a reserve triangle that violates cumulative development
constraints simply because it satisfies a high-level conditioning variable.
C.15 Scenario generation versus causal interpretation
Generative models produce samples. They do not automatically identify causal effects. This
warning is the path-level version of the warning in Lecture 14: a transport plan, map or bridge
is not automatically a structural causal model. A deterministic map from µ0 to µ1 is a coupling,
not a structural causal model. A diffusion bridge from µ0 to µ1 is a path law, not proof that the
path would occur under an intervention.
To interpret generated paths causally, one needs additional assumptions:
• what intervention is being applied;
• which variables are manipulable;
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 354/416

=== page 355 ===
C.16 Actuarial governance checklist
• which variables are only descriptive;
• which mechanisms remain invariant;
• what information is available at each time;
• how hidden confounding is ruled out or bounded.
This is particularly important for prevention and climate adaptation. A transport or diffusion
path can be a useful counterfactual scenario generator, but causal claims require more than
endpoint matching.
C.16 Actuarial governance checklist
Before using a deterministic or diffusion generator in actuarial work, one should answer the
following questions.
(i) State space. What exactly is generated: losses, contracts, cash-flows, mortality rates,
triangles or paths?
(ii) Units. Are the coordinates monetary, temporal, categorical, geographical, demographic or
contractual?
(iii) Constraints. Are nonnegativity, monotonicity, accounting identities, coverage limits and
legal constraints enforced?
(iv) Tail validation. Are VaR, TVaR, threshold exceedances and extreme scenarios correctly
represented?
(v) Path meaning. Does the path represent time, denoising time, calibration time, renewal
cycles, development years or an artificial interpolation parameter?
(vi) Noise meaning. Does the diffusion noise represent process risk, model uncertainty,
posterior uncertainty or merely numerical smoothing?
(vii) Audit trail. Can one explain why a generated scenario was produced?
(viii) Use case. Is the generator used for pricing, capital, stress testing, reserving, prevention,
mortality or communication? The validation standard depends on the use.
C.17 Mini-lab: deterministic quantile paths and noisy bridges
This lab compares a deterministic quantile path with a simple noisy bridge. It is not a full
diffusion model. It is a pedagogical approximation showing how adding path noise changes
intermediate distributions while keeping endpoints fixed in each paired scenario.
set.seed(123)
n <- 5000
u <- ( seq _len(n) - 0.5) / n
# Baseline and stressed severity distributions
q0 <- qlnorm(u, meanlog = 8.0, sdlog = 0.55)
q1 <- qlnorm(u, meanlog = 8.2, sdlog = 0.85)
# Deterministic displacement interpolation in quantile space
path _det <- function( t) {
(1 - t) * q0 + t * q1
Page 355/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 356 ===
C DETERMINISTIC MAPPING VERSUS DIFFUSION PROCESSES
}
# Noisy bridge around paired deterministic path.
# Noise vanishes at t=0 and t=1, so paired endpoints are preserved.
path _noisy <- function(t, eps = 0.20) {
m <- (1 - t) * q0 + t * q1
s <- sqrt(eps * t * (1 - t)) * sd(q1 - q0)
pmax(m + s * rnorm( length(m)), 0)
}
emp _ q <- function(x, alpha) {
xs <- sort(x)
xs[ pmax(1, ceiling(alpha * length(xs)))]
}
VaR <- function(x, alpha = 0.99) {
as. numeric(emp _ q(x, alpha))
}
TVaR <- function(x, alpha = 0.99) {
q <- VaR(x, alpha)
mean(x[x >= q])
}
tail _ mean _excess <- function(x, alpha = 0.99) {
q <- VaR(x, alpha)
mean(x[x >= q] - q)
}
grid <- seq(0, 1, by = 0.1)
out <- data. frame(
t = grid,
mean _det = NA _ real _, VaR _det = NA _ real _, TVaR _det = NA _ real _,
excess _det = NA _ real _,
mean _noisy = NA _ real _, VaR _noisy = NA _ real _, TVaR _noisy = NA _ real _,
excess _noisy = NA _ real _
)
for (i in seq _along( grid)) {
t <- grid[i]
xd <- path _det( t)
xn <- path _noisy(t, eps = 0.30)
out $ mean _det[i] <- mean(xd)
out $VaR _det[i] <- VaR(xd)
out $TVaR _det[i] <- TVaR(xd)
out $excess _det[i] <- tail _ mean _excess(xd)
out $ mean _noisy[i] <- mean(xn)
out $VaR _noisy[i] <- VaR(xn)
out $TVaR _noisy[i] <- TVaR(xn)
out $excess _noisy[i] <- tail _ mean _excess(xn)
}
print(out)
matplot(out $t,
out[, c("VaR _det", "VaR _noisy", "TVaR _det", "TVaR _noisy")],
type = "l", lty = 1, xlab = "t", ylab = "tail␣measure")

C.18 Mini-lab: deterministic map versus stochastic Markov kernel
legend("topleft",
legend = c("VaR␣deterministic", "VaR␣noisy",
"TVaR␣deterministic", "TVaR␣noisy"),
lty = 1, bty = "n")
The deterministic path changes tail measures smoothly because every quantile is moved along
a straight line in quantile space. The noisy bridge may increase or decrease intermediate tail
measures depending on the noise scale. This illustrates why a stochastic path is not merely a
cosmetic version of a deterministic path: it changes the interpretation of intermediate risk.
C.18 Mini-lab: deterministic map versus stochastic Markov kernel
The next toy example compares a deterministic repair map with a stochastic kernel. Both target
the same endpoint distribution, but their conditional interpretations are different.
set.seed(321)
n <- 3000
x <- rnorm(n, mean = 0, sd = 1)
# Target distribution: shifted and more dispersed
u <- rank(x, ties.method = "random") / (n + 1)
y _det <- qnorm(u, mean = 1, sd = 1.5)
# Stochastic kernel: same broad target idea, but not deterministic
conditional on x
# Here we use a noisy location-scale rule and then quantile-calibrate
the marginal.
y _raw <- 1 + 1.1 * x + 0.9 * rnorm(n)
emp _ q _vec <- function(x, u) {
xs <- sort(x)
xs[ pmax(1, ceiling(u * length(xs)))]
}
u _raw <- rank(y _raw, ties.method = "random") / n
y _sto <- as. numeric(emp _ q _vec(y _det, u _raw))
cor _det <- cor(x, y _det)
cor _sto <- cor(x, y _sto)
c(correlation _deterministic = cor _det,
correlation _stochastic = cor _sto,
mean _det = mean(y _det), mean _stochastic = mean(y _sto),
sd _det = sd(y _det), sd _stochastic = sd(y _sto))
plot(x, y _det, pch = 16, cex = 0.35,
xlab = "baseline␣x", ylab = "target␣y",
main = "Deterministic␣map")
plot(x, y _sto, pch = 16, cex = 0.35,
xlab = "baseline␣x", ylab = "target␣y",
main = "Stochastic␣kernel␣with␣same␣marginal␣target")
The deterministic map creates a functional relation between x and y. The stochastic kernel
gives the same type of target marginal but allows residual variation conditional on x. For
auditability, the deterministic map is easier; for process uncertainty, the stochastic kernel may
be more realistic.

C DETERMINISTIC MAPPING VERSUS DIFFUSION PROCESSES
C.19 Further reading and links to the notes
For deterministic maps and push-forwards, see Lectures 2 and 4. For velocity fields, action and
distributional trajectories, see Lecture 15. For stochastic counterfactuals and Schrödinger bridges,
see Lecture 14. For cost design on constrained actuarial spaces, see Appendix B. For statistical
validation, noise floors and resampling, see Lecture 7. The two mini-labs in Sections C.17
and C.18 are deliberately simple; they are meant to accompany the computational labs, where
Wasserstein uncertainty and reserve cash-flow laws are studied more directly.
C.20 Summary
Deterministic maps and diffusion processes are complementary.
• Deterministic OT maps give transparent scenario matching, rank-preserving stress and
reproducible counterfactual rules.
• Deterministic ODE flows extend maps into smooth trajectories of probability measures
and connect naturally with flow matching and continuous normalizing flows.
• Diffusion models generate through stochastic paths and are natural when intermediate
uncertainty is meaningful.
• Schrödinger bridges connect both worlds: they are entropy-regularized, stochastic analogues
of dynamic optimal transport.
• In actuarial science, the choice should be driven by the use case: audit and policy transfor-
mation often favour deterministic maps; scenario generation, runoff uncertainty, mortality
improvement, cyber contagion and climate paths often favour stochastic processes.
Actuarial takeaway
Generative AI does not remove the need for actuarial modelling. It adds new ways to
represent distributions and paths. The actuarial task remains to decide what the state
space means, what constraints must hold, what tail behaviour is acceptable, and whether
the generated object should be a deterministic transformation or a stochastic evolution.

D LAB 1: EMPIRICAL WASSERSTEIN DISTANCES UNDER SAMPLING NOISE
D Lab 1: Empirical Wasserstein Distances under Sampling Noise
Empirical Wasserstein distances are easy to compute, especially in one dimension. They are
also easy to over-interpret. If two independent samples are drawn from the same continuous
distribution, their empirical laws are almost surely different. Consequently, the empirical
Wasserstein distance between the samples is positive, even when there is no business drift, no
model change and no deterioration of the portfolio.
This lab studies that phenomenon. The aim is to distinguish a genuine distributional change
from the distance that arises because two empirical samples are finite. The actuarial question is
not only
ˆWp > 0,
but rather whether the observed distance is large relative to its sampling noise.
Actuarial takeaway
A Wasserstein monitoring report should not display only the distance between two empirical
loss distributions. It should also display a noise floor, a bootstrap uncertainty band, or a
permutation reference distribution. Otherwise, small random fluctuations may be mistaken
for portfolio drift.
D.1 Learning objectives
After completing this lab, the reader should be able to:
• compute empirical W1 and W2 distances between one-dimensional loss samples by quantile
matching;
• simulate the null distribution of an empirical Wasserstein distance under no drift;
• compare the noise floor to genuine shifts such as inflation, scale change or tail thickening;
• implement a permutation test for equality of distributions;
• implement bootstrap confidence intervals for a Wasserstein distance;
• diagnose where two loss distributions differ using quantile-difference curves.
D.2 The empirical object
Let X1,...,Xn and Y1,...,Ym be two one-dimensional samples of claim amounts, reserve
outcomes or loss ratios. Their empirical laws are
ˆµn = 1 n n∑ i=1 δXi, ˆνm = 1 m m∑ j=1 δYj.
The empirical Wasserstein distance is
Wp(ˆµn,ˆνm).
In one dimension, it is computed from empirical quantiles:
Wp p (ˆµn,ˆνm)≈ 1 K K∑ k=1 ⏐⏐⏐ˆQX(uk)− ˆQY (uk) ⏐⏐⏐ p ,
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 360/416

=== page 361 ===
D.3 The noise floor
where u1,...,uK is a fine grid in (0, 1), and ˆQX, ˆQY are empirical generalized inverse functions.
In the code below, empirical quantiles are lower generalized inverses. This is the same convention
as in the main text.
When n =m and the two empirical measures have equal weights, one can sort the samples:
Wp p (ˆµn,ˆνn) = 1 n n∑ i=1 |X(i) −Y(i)|p.
The quantile-grid version below also works when the sample sizes differ.
Computational note
In this lab, everything is one-dimensional. No linear programming solver is needed. Sorting
and empirical generalized inverses are enough.
D.3 The noise floor
Suppose first that both samples come from the same distribution:
Xi ∼µ, Yj ∼µ.
At the population level,
Wp(µ,µ) = 0.
But at the empirical level,
Wp(ˆµn,ˆνm)> 0
with high probability. This positive value is not evidence of drift. It is the sampling noise floor
of the monitoring procedure.
A practical way to estimate this noise floor is repeated random splitting:
1. pool observations from a supposedly homogeneous portfolio;
2. split them into two pseudo-portfolios of comparable size;
3. compute the Wasserstein distance between the two empirical laws;
4. repeat many times.
The resulting distribution is the reference scale for interpreting future observed distances.
Actuarial takeaway
A value such as W1 = 120 dollars is not meaningful without context. It may be large for a
portfolio of small attritional claims and negligible for a portfolio of large liability claims.
It may also be exactly what random splitting produces at the available sample size.
D.4 Three kinds of drift
This lab compares three stylized alternatives.
Inflation drift. The second sample is multiplied by 1 +r:
Y = (1 +r)X.
This shifts the severity distribution upward in a rank-preserving way.
Page 361/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 362 ===
D LAB 1: EMPIRICAL WASSERSTEIN DISTANCES UNDER SAMPLING NOISE
Scale drift. The second distribution has higher dispersion, for example a larger lognormal
standard deviation. This may leave the center of the distribution relatively stable while increasing
both lower and upper tail dispersion.
Tail drift. Only the upper tail is modified. For example, a small proportion of observations is
multiplied by a large factor. This creates a distributional change that may be modest for W1,
larger for W2, and very visible in upper quantile differences.
D.5 Permutation and bootstrap
Two simple calibration tools are useful.
Permutation test. For the null hypothesis
H0 :µ =ν,
the labels “sample X” and “sample Y” are exchangeable. A permutation test pools the data,
randomly relabels them many times, and compares the observed statistic to the relabelled
statistics.
Bootstrap uncertainty. For two independent samples, a nonparametric bootstrap resamples
each sample with replacement and recomputes the Wasserstein distance. The resulting empirical
distribution gives a standard error or percentile interval.
These two tools answer different questions. The permutation test asks whether two samples
could plausibly have come from the same distribution. The bootstrap asks how variable the
observed distance is as an estimator.
D.6 R lab
D.6.1 Basic Wasserstein function
# Lower empirical generalized inverse.
emp _ q _left <- function(x, probs) {
x <- sort(x[ is.finite(x)])
n <- length(x)
if (n == 0) stop("x␣must␣contain␣at␣least␣one␣finite␣value")
probs <- pmin( pmax(probs, 0), 1)
idx <- ceiling(n * probs)
idx[idx < 1] <- 1
idx[idx > n] <- n
x[idx]
}
# One-dimensional empirical Wasserstein distance.
# Works for unequal sample sizes by using a common quantile grid.
wass1d <- function(x, y, p = 1, grid _size = 1000, trim = 0) {
stopifnot(p >= 1)
x <- x[ is.finite(x)]
y <- y[ is.finite(y)]
if ( length(x) == 0 || length(y) == 0) {
stop("x␣and␣y␣must␣contain␣at␣least␣one␣finite␣value")
}
lo <- trim
hi <- 1 - trim

=== page 363 ===
D.6 R lab
if (lo <= 0) lo <- 1 / ( grid _size + 1)
if (hi >= 1) hi <- grid _size / ( grid _size + 1)
if (lo >= hi) stop("trim␣is␣too␣large")
u <- seq(lo, hi, length.out = grid _size)
qx <- emp _ q _left(x, u)
qy <- emp _ q _left(y, u)
mean( abs(qx - qy)^p)^(1 / p)
}
# Quick check
x <- c(1, 2, 7, 10)
y <- c(0, 4, 6, 12)
wass1d(x, y, p = 1, grid _size = 4)
wass1d(x, y, p = 2, grid _size = 4)
The argument trim can be used to ignore extreme quantile levels. This is not a replacement
for tail modelling, but it is useful for sensitivity checks.
D.6.2 Simulated severity data
set.seed(123)
# Baseline portfolio: attritional severities
n <- 1000
x <- rlnorm(n, meanlog = 8.0, sdlog = 0.75)
# Alternative 1: inflation drift
r <- 0.08
y _infl <- (1 + r) * rlnorm(n, meanlog = 8.0, sdlog = 0.75)
# Alternative 2: scale drift
y _ scale <- rlnorm(n, meanlog = 8.0, sdlog = 0.95)
# Alternative 3: tail drift
base <- rlnorm(n, meanlog = 8.0, sdlog = 0.75)
tail _flag <- runif(n) < 0.06
y _tail <- base
y _tail[tail _flag] <- 2.5 * y _tail[tail _flag]
rbind(
inflation _W1 = wass1d(x, y _infl, p = 1),
inflation _W2 = wass1d(x, y _infl, p = 2),
scale _W1 = wass1d(x, y _ scale, p = 1),
scale _W2 = wass1d(x, y _ scale, p = 2),
tail _W1 = wass1d(x, y _tail, p = 1),
tail _W2 = wass1d(x, y _tail, p = 2)
)
D.6.3 Noise floor by random splitting
noise _ floor _ split <- function(z, p = 1, B = 500, split _frac = 0.5,
grid _size = 1000, seed = NULL) {
if ( ! is. null(seed)) set.seed(seed)
z <- z[ is.finite(z)]

=== page 364 ===
D LAB 1: EMPIRICAL WASSERSTEIN DISTANCES UNDER SAMPLING NOISE
N <- length(z)
n1 <- floor( split _frac * N)
if (n1 < 1 || n1 >= N) stop("split _frac␣gives␣an␣empty␣split")
out <- numeric(B)
for (b in seq _len(B)) {
id <- sample.int(N, N, replace = FALSE)
x <- z[id[ seq _len(n1)]]
y <- z[id[(n1 + 1):N]]
out[b] <- wass1d(x, y, p = p, grid _size = grid _size)
}
out
}
# Estimate the null distribution from a homogeneous portfolio
z <- rlnorm(3000, meanlog = 8.0, sdlog = 0.75)
null _W1 <- noise _ floor _ split(z, p = 1, B = 500, seed = 1)
null _W2 <- noise _ floor _ split(z, p = 2, B = 500, seed = 1)
quantile( null _W1, c(0.50, 0.90, 0.95, 0.99))
quantile( null _W2, c(0.50, 0.90, 0.95, 0.99))
The 95% or 99% quantile of the null distribution can be used as a monitoring threshold. If
an observed distance is below that threshold, it may be sampling variation. If it is far above the
threshold, the distributional change is more credible.
D.6.4 Permutation test
perm _test _wass <- function(x, y, p = 1, B = 999, grid _size = 1000,
seed = NULL) {
if ( ! is. null(seed)) set.seed(seed)
x <- x[ is.finite(x)]
y <- y[ is.finite(y)]
n <- length(x)
m <- length(y)
if (n == 0 || m == 0) stop("x␣and␣y␣must␣be␣non-empty")
z <- c(x, y)
N <- n + m
t _obs <- wass1d(x, y, p = p, grid _size = grid _size)
t _perm <- numeric(B)
for (b in seq _len(B)) {
id <- sample.int(N, N, replace = FALSE)
xb <- z[id[ seq _len(n)]]
yb <- z[id[(n + 1):N]]
t _perm[b] <- wass1d(xb, yb, p = p, grid _size = grid _size)
}
p _value <- (1 + sum( t _perm >= t _obs)) / (B + 1)
list(
statistic = t _obs,
p _value = p _value,
permuted = t _perm
)
}

D.6.5 Bootstrap confidence interval
boot _wass _independent <- function(x, y, p = 1, B = 999, grid _size =
1000,
seed = NULL) {
if ( ! is. null(seed)) set.seed(seed)
x <- x[ is.finite(x)]
y <- y[ is.finite(y)]
n <- length(x)
m <- length(y)
if (n == 0 || m == 0) stop("x␣and␣y␣must␣be␣non-empty")
stat <- numeric(B)
for (b in seq _len(B)) {
xb <- sample(x, size = n, replace = TRUE)
yb <- sample(y, size = m, replace = TRUE)
stat[b] <- wass1d(xb, yb, p = p, grid _size = grid _size)
}
c(
observed = wass1d(x, y, p = p, grid _size = grid _size),
se = sd( stat),
q025 = as. numeric( quantile( stat, 0.025)),
q500 = as. numeric( quantile( stat, 0.500)),
q975 = as. numeric( quantile( stat, 0.975))
)
}
boot _wass _independent(x, y _infl, p = 1, B = 499, seed = 10)
boot _wass _independent(x, y _infl, p = 2, B = 499, seed = 10)
D.6.6 Quantile-difference plots
A single Wasserstein number does not tell us where the difference lies. The following function
plots
u↦−→ ˆQY (u)− ˆQX(u).
qdiff _ curve <- function(x, y, grid _size = 200, trim = 0.005) {
u <- seq(trim, 1 - trim, length.out = grid _size)
qx <- emp _ q _left(x, u)
qy <- emp _ q _left(y, u)
data. frame(u = u, qx = qx, qy = qy, diff = qy - qx)
}
qd _infl <- qdiff _ curve(x, y _infl)
qd _ scale <- qdiff _ curve(x, y _ scale)
qd _tail <- qdiff _ curve(x, y _tail)
Page 365/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 366 ===
D LAB 1: EMPIRICAL WASSERSTEIN DISTANCES UNDER SAMPLING NOISE
plot(qd _infl $u, qd _infl $ diff, type = "l", lwd = 2,
xlab = "quantile␣level␣u", ylab = "Q _Y(u)␣-␣Q _X(u)",
main = "Quantile␣difference␣curves")
lines(qd _ scale $u, qd _ scale $ diff, lwd = 2, lty = 2)
lines(qd _tail $u, qd _tail $ diff, lwd = 2, lty = 3)
abline(h = 0, lty = 3)
legend("topleft", lwd = 2, lty = 1:3,
legend = c("inflation", "scale", "tail"), bty = "n")
The curves should have different shapes. Inflation tends to move the full curve upward. Scale
drift often creates small changes near the center and larger changes away from the center. Tail
drift appears mostly near high quantile levels.
D.7 Suggested report template
A short actuarial report based on this lab could contain the following table.
Quantity Interpretation
W1 Average monetary displacement between empirical quantile curves
W2 Tail-sensitive squared displacement summary
Noise-floor median Typical distance produced by random splitting
Noise-floor 95% quantile Monitoring threshold under no drift
Permutation p-value Evidence against equality of the two distributions
Bootstrap interval Sampling uncertainty around the observed distance
Quantile-difference curve Location of the distributional change
Computational note
For a production monitoring report, record the sample sizes, the scale of the variable, the
chosen value of p, the trimming convention, the observed distance, a null threshold, and a
visual quantile-difference plot. The Wasserstein distance should be a diagnostic, not an
isolated number.

0.005 and trim = 0.025. Which conclusions are stable? Which conclusions depend on the most
extreme quantiles?
Exercise D.11 (Choice of scale). Compute Wasserstein distances on the original loss scale and
on the log-loss scale. Explain how the interpretation changes. Which scale would you use for a
portfolio with extremely heavy-tailed claims?

E LAB 2: SLICED AND PROJECTED WASSERSTEIN DISTANCES
E Lab 2: Sliced and Projected Wasserstein Distances
Exact optimal transport is geometrically meaningful, but it can be expensive in moderate or
high dimension. If the state variable is a vector of losses by line, a vector of policyholder
characteristics, a cash-flow trajectory, or a vector of model outputs, solving the full multivariate
transport problem may be numerically heavy and statistically unstable. Sliced and projected
Wasserstein distances provide a useful alternative: they reduce a multivariate comparison to
many one-dimensional comparisons.
The central idea is actuarially natural. Instead of comparing two multivariate portfolios only
through the full cloud of points, we compare them through one-dimensional views: total loss,
regional loss, first principal component, duration-weighted cash-flow, capital-weighted loss, or a
random linear combination of features. Each projected comparison is one-dimensional, hence it
can be computed by sorting and comparing empirical quantiles.
Actuarial takeaway
Sliced Wasserstein distances are useful when the actuarial object is multivariate but
one-dimensional quantile comparisons remain desirable. They are fast, interpretable, and
provide directional diagnostics: which projection of the portfolio, output vector or cash-flow
vector has moved the most?
Actuarial takeaway
In this lab, empirical one-dimensional Wasserstein distances are computed with the lower
generalized inverse convention. In R, the code below uses an explicit left-continuous
empirical quantile function rather than the default interpolation rules of quantile. This
makes the link with the convention used in the lectures transparent, especially when ties
or atoms are present.
E.1 One-dimensional projections
Let µ,ν∈Pp(Rd), with p≥ 1. For a unit vectorθ∈ Sd−1, define the linear projection
Pθ(x) =θ⊤x, forx∈ Rd.
The projected laws are
µθ = (Pθ)#µ, and νθ = (Pθ)#ν.
They are probability measures on R. Hence their Wasserstein distance can be computed using
the quantile formula
Wp p (µθ,νθ) =
∫ 1
0
⏐⏐⏐F−1 µθ (u)−F−1 νθ (u) ⏐⏐⏐ p du.
Definition E.1 (Projected Wasserstein distance in one direction). For θ∈ Sd−1, the projected
p-Wasserstein distance in directionθ is
Wp,θ(µ,ν) =Wp
(
(Pθ)#µ, (Pθ)#ν
)
.
This directional distance answers a precise question: how far are the two laws when viewed
through the scalar actuarial indexx↦→θ⊤x?
Example E.1 (Line-of-business losses). Let X = (X1,...,Xd) be a vector of losses by line
of business. If θ = (1,..., 1)/
√
d, then θ⊤X is proportional to total loss. If θ = ek, then
θ⊤X = Xk is the loss in line k. If θ is a capital-allocation vector, then θ⊤X is a capital-weighted
portfolio loss. Projected Wasserstein distances compare two multivariate models through these
actuarially meaningful views.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 368/416

=== page 369 ===
E.2 Sliced Wasserstein distance
Example E.2 (Cash-flow vectors). Let X = (X1,...,XT ) be a future cash-flow vector. A
projection θ⊤X may represent discounted present value, total nominal reserve, late-development
exposure, or a contrast between short-tail and long-tail payments. A projected Wasserstein
distance compares reserving distributions through the chosen cash-flow summary.
E.2 Sliced Wasserstein distance
Instead of selecting one direction, we may average over directions. Let σ denote the uniform
probability measure on the unit sphere Sd−1.
Definition E.2 (Sliced Wasserstein distance). For µ,ν∈Pp(Rd), the sliced Wasserstein distance
is
SWp(µ,ν) =
(∫
Sd−1
Wp p ( (Pθ)#µ, (Pθ)#ν ) dσ(θ) )1/p .
Thus SWp p is the average one-dimensional Wasserstein cost over all linear views of the
distribution. In practice, the integral is approximated by random projections. If θ1,...,θM are
independent uniform directions on the sphere, define
ˆSW p p,M(µ,ν) = 1 M M∑ m=1 Wp p ( (Pθm)#µ, (Pθm)#ν ) .
For empirical distributions with equal sample size n, each term is computed by sorting the
projected samples:
Wp p (ˆµθ,ˆνθ) = 1 n n∑ i=1 ⏐⏐⏐X(i),θ −Y(i),θ ⏐⏐⏐ p ,
where X(i),θ and Y(i),θ are the sorted projected values.
Proposition E.1 (Sliced Wasserstein is dominated by Wasserstein). For everyθ∈ Sd−1,
Wp
(
(Pθ)#µ, (Pθ)#ν
)
≤Wp(µ,ν).
Consequently,
SWp(µ,ν)≤Wp(µ,ν).
Proof. The projection Pθ is 1-Lipschitz because |θ⊤x−θ⊤y|≤∥x−y∥. Let γ∈ Π(µ,ν). Then
(Pθ,Pθ)#γ is a coupling of (Pθ)#µ and (Pθ)#ν. Hence
Wp p ( (Pθ)#µ, (Pθ)#ν ) ≤ ∫ |θ⊤x−θ⊤y|p dγ(x,y)≤ ∫ ∥x−y∥p dγ(x,y).
Taking the infimum over γ∈ Π(µ,ν) gives the first inequality. Integrating over θ gives the
second.
Proposition E.2 (Separation of distributions). If SWp(µ,ν) = 0, then µ =ν.
Proof. If SWp(µ,ν) = 0, then Wp((Pθ)#µ, (Pθ)#ν) = 0 for σ-almost every θ. Hence the
one-dimensional projected laws coincide for almost every θ. By continuity in θ, and by the
Cramér–Wold device, equality of all one-dimensional linear projections implies µ =ν.
Page 369/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 370 ===
E LAB 2: SLICED AND PROJECTED WASSERSTEIN DISTANCES
E.3 Max-sliced and selected-direction distances
Averaging over directions is not always the actuarial goal. Sometimes one wants the most adverse
direction, or a small set of interpretable directions.
Definition E.3 (Max-sliced Wasserstein distance). The max-sliced Wasserstein distance is
MSWp(µ,ν) = sup
θ∈Sd−1
Wp
(
(Pθ)#µ, (Pθ)#ν
)
.
The sliced distance is an average discrepancy; the max-sliced distance is a worst-direction
discrepancy. Both satisfy
SWp(µ,ν)≤MSWp(µ,ν)≤Wp(µ,ν).
In actuarial applications, it is often useful to report three quantities:
{Wp,ek(µ,ν) :k = 1,...,d}, SWp(µ,ν), MSWp(µ,ν).
The coordinate projections identify marginal shifts, the sliced distance gives an average multi-
variate discrepancy, and the max-sliced distance reveals the largest directional change.
Actuarial takeaway
Sliced Wasserstein is a global average over views. Max-sliced Wasserstein is a stress
diagnostic: it searches for the direction in which two distributions are most different. In
portfolio monitoring, this direction may be more informative than the average discrepancy.
E.4 Choosing projections
Random projections are convenient, but they are not the only choice. In actuarial problems,
projections should be chosen with the application in mind.
Coordinate projections. The directions e1,...,ed compare marginal distributions of each
coordinate. They are easy to interpret, but they miss dependence changes.
Aggregate directions. The vector (1,..., 1)/
√
d compares total loss or total exposure. More
generally, a vector of present-value weights compares discounted cash-flows.
Capital directions. A vector θ may represent allocation weights, stress weights or a linearized
capital sensitivity. Thenθ⊤X is a capital-relevant projection.
Data-adaptive directions. One may use principal components, supervised directions, gradi-
ents of a prediction model, or directions found by maximizing projected Wasserstein distance.
These are often informative, but they require governance: the projection is no longer purely
neutral.
Random directions. Random directions provide a cheap approximation to the full spherical
average. They are useful when one wants a stable summary rather than a named diagnostic.
The number of projections M controls the Monte Carlo error.
Computational note
A practical report may include coordinate projected distances, an aggregate-loss projection,
a present-value cash-flow projection, a random-projection sliced Wasserstein estimate, and
the largest projected distance among a large random set of directions. This provides both
interpretability and scalability.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 370/416

E.5 Mini-lab: sliced Wasserstein in R
E.5 Mini-lab: sliced Wasserstein in R
The following code computes a Monte Carlo approximation of SWp for two clouds of vectors. It
uses only random projections and empirical lower quantiles.
# Empirical lower generalized inverse on a grid u in (0, 1)
emp _ q _left <- function(x, u) {
if ( length(x) == 0L) stop("x␣must␣be␣non-empty")
if ( any( ! is.finite(x))) stop("x␣must␣contain␣only␣finite␣values")
if ( any(u <= 0 | u >= 1)) stop("u␣must␣lie␣in␣(0,␣1)")
xs <- sort( as. numeric(x))
n <- length(xs)
xs[ pmax(1L, ceiling(u * n))]
}
# Sliced Wasserstein distance between two point clouds
sliced _wasserstein <- function(X, Y, p = 2, M = 200, seed = NULL) {
if ( ! is. null(seed)) set.seed(seed)
X <- as. matrix(X)
Y <- as. matrix(Y)
if ( ncol(X) ! = ncol(Y)) stop("X␣and␣Y␣must␣have␣the␣same␣number␣of␣
columns")
if (p < 1) stop("p␣must␣be␣at␣least␣1")
if (M < 1) stop("M␣must␣be␣positive")
n <- nrow(X)
m <- nrow(Y)
d <- ncol(X)
grid _size <- max(n, m)
u <- seq(1 / grid _size, 1, length.out = grid _size)
u <- pmin(u, 1 - . Machine $ double.eps)
vals <- numeric(M)
for (k in seq _len(M)) {
theta <- rnorm(d)
theta <- theta / sqrt( sum(theta^2))
xproj <- as. numeric(X % * % theta)
yproj <- as. numeric(Y % * % theta)
qx <- emp _ q _left(xproj, u)
qy <- emp _ q _left(yproj, u)
vals[k] <- mean( abs(qx - qy)^p)
}
mean(vals)^(1 / p)
}
set.seed(123)
n <- 1000
# Two simulated bivariate loss portfolios
Sigma1 <- matrix( c(1.0, 0.3, 0.3, 1.0), 2, 2)
Sigma2 <- matrix( c(1.2, 0.7, 0.7, 1.5), 2, 2)
X <- matrix( rnorm(2 * n), n, 2) % * % chol(Sigma1)
Y <- matrix( rnorm(2 * n), n, 2) % * % chol(Sigma2) + c(0.3, 0.1)
sliced _wasserstein(X, Y, p = 2, M = 500, seed = 1)
The same function can be used for cash-flow vectors, model-output vectors, simulated capital

scenarios or policyholder profiles, after suitable scaling of the coordinates.
E.6 Mini-lab: directional diagnostics
The next code compares named directions and random directions.
projected _wasserstein <- function(X, Y, theta, p = 2, grid _size = 1000)
{
X <- as. matrix(X)
Y <- as. matrix(Y)
if ( ncol(X) ! = ncol(Y)) stop("X␣and␣Y␣must␣have␣the␣same␣number␣of␣
columns")
if ( length(theta) ! = ncol(X)) stop("theta␣must␣have␣one␣entry␣per␣
column")
if ( sum(theta^2) == 0) stop("theta␣must␣be␣non-zero")
if (p < 1) stop("p␣must␣be␣at␣least␣1")
theta <- theta / sqrt( sum(theta^2))
xproj <- as. numeric(X % * % theta)
yproj <- as. numeric(Y % * % theta)
u <- seq(1 / grid _size, 1, length.out = grid _size)
u <- pmin(u, 1 - . Machine $ double.eps)
qx <- emp _ q _left(xproj, u)
qy <- emp _ q _left(yproj, u)
mean( abs(qx - qy)^p)^(1 / p)
}
# Coordinate directions
projected _wasserstein(X, Y, c(1, 0), p = 2)
projected _wasserstein(X, Y, c(0, 1), p = 2)
# Aggregate-loss direction
projected _wasserstein(X, Y, c(1, 1), p = 2)
# Approximate max-sliced direction by random search
random _projected <- function(X, Y, p = 2, M = 500, seed = 123) {
set.seed(seed)
d <- ncol(X)
out <- data. frame(value = numeric(M), theta1 = numeric(M), theta2 =
numeric(M))
for (k in seq _len(M)) {
theta <- rnorm(d)
theta <- theta / sqrt( sum(theta^2))
out $value[k] <- projected _wasserstein(X, Y, theta, p = p)
out $theta1[k] <- theta[1]
out $theta2[k] <- theta[2]
}
out[ order(-out $value), ][1:10, ]
}
random _projected(X, Y, p = 2, M = 1000)
E.7 Limitations
Sliced and projected distances are computationally attractive, but they should not be over-
interpreted.

E.8 Exercises
• A small number of projections may miss a localized multivariate change.
• Coordinate projections can miss dependence shifts.
• Random projections are less interpretable than actuarially named projections.
• Max-sliced distances may be sensitive to sample noise if the maximizing direction is
estimated from the same data.
• Scaling of coordinates matters. Before projecting mixed actuarial features, one must
engineer the ground geometry carefully.
The best practice is therefore not to replace full OT by sliced OT blindly, but to use sliced
and projected distances as scalable diagnostics. They are especially valuable in exploratory
analysis, model monitoring and high-dimensional scenario comparison.

=== page 375 ===
F Lab 3: Wasserstein Fairness Repair
This lab studies one-dimensional Wasserstein post-processing for distributional fairness. The
setting is deliberately simple: a real-valued actuarial score, predicted loss, technical premium or
risk indicator is observed together with a group variable. The aim is to transform group-specific
score distributions toward a common target distribution, while monitoring the actuarial cost of
the transformation.
The lab illustrates three points. First, a fairness repair is a distributional operation, not a
mean correction. Second, in one dimension Wasserstein barycenters provide an explicit common
target through quantile averaging. Third, full distributional repair can conflict with prediction,
calibration, profitability or transition constraints, so partial repair and diagnostic reporting are
necessary.
Actuarial takeaway
The maps used in this lab align empirical score distributions. They do not identify the
causal effect of group membership on an individual outcome, and they do not decide which
variables are legally or ethically admissible. Such questions require additional assumptions
and institutional judgement.
Actuarial takeaway
Throughout this lab, empirical quantiles are computed using the lower generalized inverse
convention. With atoms or ties, deterministic rank averaging is used in the code for
reproducibility. Randomized ranks would be closer to the population quantile-coupling
construction when exact treatment of atoms matters.
F.1 Learning objectives
After completing this lab, the reader should be able to:
• compute one-dimensional Wasserstein disparities between group-specific score distributions;
• construct a Wasserstein barycenter target by averaging quantile functions;
• implement groupwise quantile maps sending each group distribution to the barycenter;
• implement partial Wasserstein repair and trace a fairness and accuracy frontier;
• distinguish distributional parity from calibration and predictive accuracy;
• produce an actuarial report including disparity, movement, RMSE, loss-ratio and transition
metrics.
F.2 The statistical object
Let S∈S denote a group variable with P(S = s) > 0 for every group considered, and let Z∈ R
be a score, predicted loss, technical premium or risk indicator. For each group s, define
µs = Law(Z|S =s).
Distributional demographic parity would require
µs =µs′ for all s,s′∈S.
Page 375/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 376 ===
F LAB 3: WASSERSTEIN FAIRNESS REPAIR
In practice, the groupwise laws are usually different. A Wasserstein disparity index can be based
on pairwise distances
Wp(µs,µs′),
or on distances to a common target distribution ¯µ:
∑
s∈S
αsWp p (µs, ¯µ), αs = P(S =s).
A natural Wasserstein target is the barycenter
¯µ∈ arg min
µ
∑
s∈S
αsW 2 2 (µs,µ).
In one dimension, assuming finite second moments, its quantile function has the simple form
Q¯µ(u) =
∑
s∈S
αsQs(u), u∈ (0, 1),
where Qs is the lower quantile function of µs.
Computational note
In one dimension, Wasserstein barycenters and repair maps can be computed by sorting
and interpolating empirical quantiles. No multivariate OT solver is needed.
F.3 Full and partial repair
For each group s, the monotone map from µs to ¯µ is
Ts(z) =Q¯µ
(
Fs(z)
)
,
where Fs is the distribution function of Z| S = s. With atoms, this formula should be read
with the generalized-inverse convention and, if needed, randomized ranks inside atoms. The fully
repaired score is
Zrep =TS(Z).
At the population level, under the usual one-dimensional quantile-coupling assumptions,
Law(Zrep |S =s) = ¯µ
for every group s. Thus the group-specific repaired score distributions are equal.
A partial repair interpolates between the original score and the fully repaired score:
Zλ = (1−λ)Z +λZrep, λ∈ [0, 1].
The parameter λ controls the strength of the repair. At λ = 0, nothing is changed. At λ = 1, the
groupwise score laws are aligned with the barycenter. The quadratic decrease of the Wasserstein
disparity, when used, is a property of this one-dimensional quantile interpolation, not of arbitrary
post-processing rules.
Remark F.1 (What is preserved?). The monotone repair preserves ranks within each group. If
two individuals belong to the same group and one has a larger original score than the other, the
same ordering holds after full repair. Ranks across groups may change, and the repaired score
may no longer be calibrated for the outcome of interest.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 376/416

=== page 377 ===
F.4 Fairness is not calibration
F.4 Fairness is not calibration
Let Y be the observed loss, claim indicator or response variable. If Z is a calibrated predicted
loss, one may have approximately
E[Y |Z =z]≈z.
After a groupwise transformation, calibration can fail:
E[Y |Zrep =z]̸≈z.
This is not a numerical bug. It reflects a structural tension between making the score distribution
independent of S and preserving the predictive meaning of the score. Therefore, a fairness
repair should be evaluated not only through groupwise Wasserstein distances, but also through
predictive and actuarial metrics.
For a technical premium P, useful quantities include
mean premium by group, loss ratio by group, E[|Pλ −P0|], P(|Pλ −P0|>τ),
and, when an outcome Y is available,
RMSE(Pλ,Y ), MAE(Pλ,Y ), E[Y−Pλ |S =s].
Actuarial takeaway
A distributional fairness repair may reduce group disparities while increasing mispricing,
disrupting technical premiums or changing loss ratios. The relevant object is therefore a
frontier, not a single corrected score.

# Lower empirical quantile function.
emp _ q _left <- function(x, probs) {
x <- sort(x[ is.finite(x)])
if ( length(x) == 0L) stop("x␣must␣contain␣at␣least␣one␣finite␣value")
probs <- pmin( pmax(probs, 0), 1)
idx <- ceiling(probs * length(x))
idx <- pmax(1L, pmin( length(x), idx))
x[idx]
}

# Empirical ranks in (0,1). Average ranks give a deterministic treatment
of ties.
rank01 <- function(x) {
if ( length(x) == 0L) numeric(0)
else ( rank(x, ties.method = "average") - 0.5) / length(x)

# Weighted quantile barycenter on a grid.
barycenter _ quantile <- function(z, group, weights = NULL, grid _size =
1000) {
z <- as. numeric(z)
group <- as. factor(group)
lev <- levels(group)
if ( length(z) ! = length(group)) stop("z␣and␣group␣must␣have␣the␣same␣
length")

if ( grid _size < 2L) stop("grid _size␣must␣be␣at␣least␣2")
u <- seq(1 / ( grid _size + 1), grid _size / ( grid _size + 1), length.out
= grid _size)
if ( is. null( weights)) {
weights <- as. numeric( prop. table( table(group)))
names( weights) <- lev
} else {
weights <- as. numeric( weights)
if ( length( weights) ! = length(lev)) stop("weights␣must␣match␣the␣
number␣of␣groups")
names( weights) <- lev
weights <- weights / sum( weights)
}
qmat <- sapply(lev, function(g) emp _ q _left(z[group == g], u))
qbar <- as. vector(qmat % * % weights[lev])
list(u = u, qbar = qbar, qmat = qmat, weights = weights[lev])
}

length")
z _ rep <- numeric( length(z))
for (g in levels(group)) {
idx <- which(group == g)
u <- rank01(z[idx])
z _ rep[idx] <- qbar _ eval(u, bary)
}
z _ rep
}
# Partial repair.
partial _repair <- function(z, z _ rep, lambda) {
if (lambda < 0 || lambda > 1) stop("lambda␣must␣belong␣to␣[0,1]")
(1 - lambda) * z + lambda * z _ rep
}
F.5.2 Simulating an actuarial score
The following simulation creates two groups. The true loss depends on a risk covariate X, a
group effect and noise. The score Z is a noisy predicted loss. The example is a sandbox for
distributional post-processing, not a causal or legal model.
set.seed(2026)
n <- 4000
S <- rbinom(n, size = 1, prob = 0.45)
S _fac <- factor(S, labels = c("A", "B"))
# Risk covariate with a different distribution by group.
X <- rnorm(n, mean = 0.4 * S, sd = 1)
# True expected loss and observed loss.
eta <- 7.7 + 0.55 * X + 0.25 * S
mu _true <- exp(eta)
Y <- rgamma(n, shape = 2.5, scale = mu _true / 2.5)
# A predicted premium or score, with model noise.
Z <- exp(7.7 + 0.55 * X + 0.25 * S + rnorm(n, sd = 0.12))
summary _ by _group <- function(v, group) {
tapply(v, group, function(x) {
qs <- emp _ q _left(x, c(.50, .90, .99))
c( mean = mean(x), sd = sd(x), q50 = qs[1], q90 = qs[2], q99 = qs[3])
})
}
summary _ by _group(Z, S _fac)
wass1d(Z[S _fac == "A"], Z[S _fac == "B"], p = 1)
wass1d(Z[S _fac == "A"], Z[S _fac == "B"], p = 2)
F.5.3 Computing the barycenter and full repair
bary <- barycenter _ quantile(Z, S _fac, grid _size = 1000)
Z _ rep <- repair _to _barycenter(Z, S _fac, bary)

In finite samples, the after-repair distances are not exactly zero because of unequal group
sizes, interpolation and empirical quantile approximations. They should nevertheless be much
smaller than the before-repair distances.

F.5.5 The fairness and accuracy frontier
We now vary λ from 0 to 1 and compute a small set of diagnostics.
metrics _two _groups <- function(pred, original, y, group) {
group <- as. factor(group)
lev <- levels(group)
if ( length(lev) ! = 2L) stop("this␣helper␣expects␣exactly␣two␣groups")
W1 <- wass1d(pred[group == lev[1]], pred[group == lev[2]], p = 1)
W2 <- wass1d(pred[group == lev[1]], pred[group == lev[2]], p = 2)
movement _ abs <- mean( abs(pred - original))
movement _rel <- mean( abs(pred - original) / pmax(original, 1))
rmse <- sqrt( mean((y - pred)^2))
mae <- mean( abs(y - pred))
lr <- tapply(y, group, sum) / tapply(pred, group, sum)
mean _pred <- tapply(pred, group, mean)
mean _y <- tapply(y, group, mean)

lambda _ grid <- seq(0, 1, by = 0.05)

The frontier shows how disparity decreases as the repair becomes stronger, while score
movement and predictive error may increase. In practice, the choice of λ is a governance decision,
not a numerical optimum alone.
F.5.6 Transition constraints
A repair may be unacceptable if too many policyholders experience a large movement in the
score or technical premium. The following code computes simple transition metrics.
disruption <- function(pred, original, thresholds = c(.05, .10, .25)) {
rel <- abs(pred - original) / pmax(original, 1)
out <- sapply(thresholds, function(tau) mean(rel > tau))
names(out) <- paste0("Pr _move _gt _", thresholds)
c( mean _rel _move = mean(rel), out)
}
disruption _ table <- do. call( rbind, lapply(lambda _ grid, function(lambda)
{
Z _lam <- partial _repair(Z, Z _ rep, lambda)
c(lambda = lambda, disruption(Z _lam, Z))
}))
disruption _ table
Actuarial takeaway
For insurance pricing, a fairness repair should be constrained by transition rules. A
mathematically clean distributional repair may be operationally or legally unacceptable if
it creates excessive individual movements.

F.7 Extensions
F.7 Extensions
More than two groups. The code above can be extended to any finite number of groups.
The barycenter quantile is the weighted average of all group quantile functions. For many small
groups, empirical quantiles become noisy.
Protected attribute versus risk attribute. The lab treats S as a group used for auditing
or post-processing. Whether it can be used, ignored, proxied or adjusted for depends on the legal
and business context. The mathematical repair does not decide that question.
Outcome-aware repair. The barycenter repair aligns score distributions. It does not use the
outcome Y except for evaluation. More advanced repairs may combine distributional fairness
with calibration constraints or outcome loss functions.
Multivariate outputs. For multivariate outputs, coordinatewise repair may not align joint
laws. One can use projected diagnostics or sliced Wasserstein distances; see Section E.
F.8 What to remember
Wasserstein fairness repair is a transparent way to align groupwise score distributions. In
one dimension, it is computationally simple and has an explicit rank-preserving interpretation.
Distributional parity is only one desideratum. An actuarial audit must also report calibration,
predictive performance, loss ratios, individual movements and sensitivity to the repair strength.

G Lab 5: Reserving Cash-Flow Distributions
This lab studies reserving models as generators of predictive cash-flow distributions. A stochastic
reserving method does not only produce a best estimate reserve. It produces a law on future
payment vectors. Optimal transport then provides tools for comparing predictive laws, detecting
timing changes, constructing barycentric model blends, and building distributional reserving
reports.
The lab focuses on simulated cash-flow scenarios
C = (C1,...,CT )∈ RT +,
where Ct is the incremental payment at future development year t. The same ideas apply to
calendar-year cash flows, accident-year specific cash flows, claim-count/severity decompositions,
or net cash flows including recoveries and expenses.
Actuarial takeaway
A reserve is often reported as one number. A stochastic reserving model gives a whole
distribution of future cash-flow vectors. Comparing reserving models therefore means
comparing laws on RT +, not only comparing best estimates.
G.1 Learning objectives
After completing this lab, the reader should be able to:
• represent reserving outputs as empirical laws of cash-flow vectors;
• compare present-value reserve distributions with one-dimensional Wasserstein distances;
• compare full cash-flow distributions with selected projections and sliced Wasserstein diag-
nostics;
• distinguish level changes, timing changes and tail changes;
• use cumulative cash-flow transformations to detect payment acceleration or delay;
• construct simple Wasserstein barycenters of present-value reserve distributions;
• build a practical reserving report with best estimate, tail risk, timing and distributional
distance metrics.
G.2 The actuarial object
Let
C = (C1,...,CT )
be the vector of future incremental payments generated by a stochastic reserving model. Its law
is denoted
µ = Law(C)∈P(RT +).
A scalar reserve is usually obtained by applying a present-value map
g(C) =
T∑
t=1
vtCt,
where vt are discount factors. The present-value reserve distribution is the push-forward
Law(g(C)) =g#µ.
Page 385/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 386 ===
G LAB 5: RESERVING CASH-FLOW DISTRIBUTIONS
Thus two reserving models may be compared in at least two ways:
Wp
(
g#µA,g#µB
)
,
which compares scalar present-value reserves, and
Wp(µA,µB),
which compares the full laws of cash-flow vectors.
Remark G.1 (Scalar reserves lose timing information). Two cash-flow vectors can have the same
present value but very different timing. For example, an early payment vector and a late payment
vector may have similar best estimates under low discounting, while producing very different
liquidity, claims management and reporting consequences. A vector comparison is therefore more
informative than a scalar comparison when timing matters.
G.3 Cash-flow costs
The simplest cost between two cash-flow vectorsc,c′∈ RT + is a weighted ℓp cost:
dw(c,c′)p =
T∑
t=1
wt|ct −c′ t|p, wt > 0.
The weights may be chosen as discount weights, reporting-period weights, or business-priority
weights. For example, setting wt = vt emphasizes present value; setting larger weights for small t
emphasizes near-term liquidity.
A timing-aware alternative compares cumulative cash flows. Define
At(c) =
t∑
s=1
cs, t = 1,...,T.
Then
dcum(c,c′)p =
T∑
t=1
wt|At(c)−At(c′)|p
penalizes differences in payment emergence. This cost is especially useful when one model pays
claims earlier or later than another model.
Computational note
For reserving, a cost between cash-flow vectors should be chosen according to the diagnostic
question:
• present-value cost for economic reserve comparisons;
• near-term weighted cost for liquidity monitoring;
• cumulative cost for payment-timing differences;
• tail-projection costs for capital and risk adjustment diagnostics.
G.4 Projection diagnostics
Exact multivariate Wasserstein distances in RT may be expensive when the number of scenarios is
large. A practical alternative is to compare projections. Important reserving projections include:
θ⊤ PVC =
T∑
t=1
vtCt,

G.5 A stability inequality for present values
θ⊤ totC =
T∑
t=1
Ct,
θ⊤ earlyC =
t0∑
t=1
Ct, θ⊤ lateC =
T∑
t=t0+1
Ct.
Each projection gives a one-dimensional distribution that can be compared by quantile formulas.
A sliced Wasserstein diagnostic averages over many random projections:
SWp p (µA,µB) =
∫
ST−1
Wp p ( (θ⊤)#µA, (θ⊤)#µB ) dθ.
In practice the integral is approximated by Monte Carlo directions.
Actuarial takeaway
Projected diagnostics are often easier to explain than a single high-dimensional number.
For reserves, useful projections are present value, undiscounted total, early payments, late
payments, calendar-year totals and cumulative paid-to-date profiles.
G.5 A stability inequality for present values
Let g(c) = v⊤c be the present-value map. If the cash-flow space is equipped with the Euclidean
norm, then
|g(c)−g(c′)|≤∥v∥2 ∥c−c′∥2.
Therefore present-value Wasserstein distances are controlled by vector Wasserstein distances.
PropositionG.1 (Push-forwardstabilityforreserves). Let µA,µB ∈Pp(RT ), and let g(c) = v⊤c.
Then
Wp
(
g#µA,g#µB
)
≤∥v∥qWp(µA,µB),
where q is the conjugate exponent of p, with the usual convention q = ∞ for p = 1. Here the
vector-valued Wasserstein distance is computed with the ℓp ground norm.
Proof. For any coupling γ∈ Π(µA,µB), the pair (g(C),g(C′)) is a coupling of g#µA and g#µB.
By Hölder’s inequality,
|g(C)−g(C′)|p ≤∥v∥p q∥C−C′∥p p.
Taking expectations under γ, minimizing over γ, and taking the p-th root gives the result.
Actuarial takeaway
All one-dimensional Wasserstein computations in this lab use the lower empirical quantile
function. This matches the generalized inverse convention used throughout the notes. In
empirical reserving samples with ties or atoms, this convention should be stated explicitly
rather than hidden in the software default.

emp _ q _left <- function(x, probs) {
x <- sort(x[ is.finite(x)])
if ( length(x) == 0) stop("x␣has␣no␣finite␣observations")
probs <- pmin( pmax(probs, 0), 1)
idx <- ceiling(probs * length(x))

G LAB 5: RESERVING CASH-FLOW DISTRIBUTIONS
idx[idx < 1] <- 1
x[idx]
}
# One-dimensional empirical Wasserstein distance on a quantile grid.
wass1d <- function(x, y, p = 1, grid _size = 1000, trim = 0) {
if (p < 1) stop("p␣must␣be␣at␣least␣1")
if ( grid _size < 2) stop("grid _size␣must␣be␣at␣least␣2")
x <- x[ is.finite(x)]
y <- y[ is.finite(y)]
if ( length(x) == 0 || length(y) == 0) stop("x␣and␣y␣must␣contain␣
finite␣observations")
lo <- trim
hi <- 1 - trim
if (lo <= 0) lo <- 1 / ( grid _size + 1)
if (hi >= 1) hi <- grid _size / ( grid _size + 1)
if (lo >= hi) stop("trim␣leaves␣an␣empty␣quantile␣range")
u <- seq(lo, hi, length.out = grid _size)
qx <- emp _ q _left(x, u)
qy <- emp _ q _left(y, u)
mean( abs(qx - qy)^p)^(1 / p)
}
wass1d _tail <- function(x, y, p = 1, alpha = 0.95, grid _size = 500) {
if (alpha <= 0 || alpha >= 1) stop("alpha␣must␣be␣in␣(0,␣1)")
if ( grid _size < 2) stop("grid _size␣must␣be␣at␣least␣2")
x <- x[ is.finite(x)]
y <- y[ is.finite(y)]
if ( length(x) == 0 || length(y) == 0) stop("x␣and␣y␣must␣contain␣
finite␣observations")
upper <- grid _size / ( grid _size + 1)
if (alpha >= upper) stop("alpha␣is␣too␣high␣for␣this␣grid _size")
u <- seq(alpha, upper, length.out = grid _size)
qx <- emp _ q _left(x, u)
qy <- emp _ q _left(y, u)
mean( abs(qx - qy)^p)^(1 / p)
}
TVaR _emp <- function(x, alpha = 0.99) {
if (alpha <= 0 || alpha >= 1) stop("alpha␣must␣be␣in␣(0,␣1)")
x <- x[ is.finite(x)]
if ( length(x) == 0) stop("x␣has␣no␣finite␣observations")
q <- emp _ q _left(x, alpha)
mean(x[x >= q])
}
pv _cashflows <- function(C, rate = 0.03) {
if ( ! is. matrix( C)) stop("C␣must␣be␣a␣matrix")
T <- ncol( C)
v <- 1 / (1 + rate)^( seq _len(T))
as. vector( C % * % v)
}

t( apply(C, 1, cumsum))

projection _wass <- function(X, Y, theta, p = 1, grid _size = 1000) {
if ( ! is. matrix(X) || ! is. matrix(Y)) stop("X␣and␣Y␣must␣be␣matrices")
if ( ncol(X) ! = ncol(Y)) stop("X␣and␣Y␣must␣have␣the␣same␣number␣of␣
columns")
if ( length(theta) ! = ncol(X)) stop("theta␣has␣incompatible␣dimension")
norm _theta <- sqrt( sum(theta^2))
if ( ! is.finite(norm _theta) || norm _theta == 0) stop("theta␣must␣be␣non
-zero")
theta <- theta / norm _theta
x <- as. vector(X % * % theta)
y <- as. vector(Y % * % theta)
wass1d(x, y, p = p, grid _size = grid _size)
}
sliced _wass <- function(X, Y, L = 200, p = 2, grid _size = 500) {
if ( ! is. matrix(X) || ! is. matrix(Y)) stop("X␣and␣Y␣must␣be␣matrices")
if ( ncol(X) ! = ncol(Y)) stop("X␣and␣Y␣must␣have␣the␣same␣number␣of␣
columns")
if (L < 1) stop("L␣must␣be␣positive")
d <- ncol(X)
vals <- numeric(L)
for (ell in seq _len(L)) {
theta <- rnorm(d)
theta <- theta / sqrt( sum(theta^2))
vals[ell] <- projection _wass(X, Y, theta, p = p, grid _size = grid _
size)^p
}
mean(vals)^(1 / p)
}
quantile _ diff <- function(x, y, probs = seq(.01, .99, by = .01)) {
qx <- emp _ q _left(x, probs)
qy <- emp _ q _left(y, probs)
data. frame(u = probs, q _A = qx, q _B = qy, diff = qy - qx)
}

G.6.2 Simulating reserving cash-flow scenarios
The following function simulates future incremental payment scenarios. The baseline model
has a moderate common shock. The late-payment model shifts mass towards later years. The
heavy-tail model keeps a similar mean pattern but adds a stronger scenario-level shock.
set.seed(2026)
make _pattern <- function(T, type = c("baseline", "late", "early")) {
type <- match.arg(type)
x <- seq _len(T)
if (type == "baseline") w <- dgamma(x, shape = 2.0, scale = 1.6)
if (type == "late") w <- dgamma(x, shape = 3.2, scale = 1.4)
if (type == "early") w <- dgamma(x, shape = 1.5, scale = 1.4)
w / sum(w)
}
simulate _cashflows <- function(n = 5000, T = 8,
ultimate _ mean = 1e6,
ultimate _cv = 0.35,
pattern _type = "baseline",
inflation = 0,
tail = c("moderate", "heavy")) {
tail <- match.arg(tail)
pat <- make _pattern(T, pattern _type)
trend <- (1 + inflation)^( seq _len(T) - 1)
pat <- pat * trend
pat <- pat / sum(pat)
# Scenario-level ultimate variability.
sigma _u <- sqrt( log(1 + ultimate _cv^2))
meanlog _u <- log(ultimate _ mean) - 0.5 * sigma _u^2
ultimate <- rlnorm(n, meanlog = meanlog _u, sdlog = sigma _u)
# Common shock controls dependence between future development years.
if (tail == "moderate") {
shock <- rgamma(n, shape = 8, rate = 8)
} else {
shock <- rlnorm(n, meanlog = -0.5 * 0.55^2, sdlog = 0.55)

}

different cumulative emergence path.
G.6.5 Selected projections
T <- ncol( C _A)
v <- 1 / (1 + rate)^( seq _len(T))

theta _tot <- rep(1, T)
theta _early <- c( rep(1, floor(T / 2)), rep(0, T - floor(T / 2)))

theta _slope <- seq(-1, 1, length.out = T)
projection _ table <- function(X, Y) {
c(PV = projection _wass(X, Y, theta _pv, p = 1),
Total = projection _wass(X, Y, theta _tot, p = 1),
Early = projection _wass(X, Y, theta _early, p = 1),
Late = projection _wass(X, Y, theta _late, p = 1),
Slope = projection _wass(X, Y, theta _slope, p = 1))
}

A _vs _ C = projection _ table( C _A, C _ C),
B _vs _ C = projection _ table( C _B, C _ C))
The selected projections should be interpreted. A large late projection indicates differences in
tail development years. A large slope projection indicates a timing change, because the direction
contrasts early and late payments.
