G.7 Suggested report template
A short reserving distributional report should contain at least the following items.
Diagnostic Example quantity Interpretation
Best estimate E[v⊤C] Mean present-value reserve
Tail reserve VaR.99(v⊤C), TVaR.99(v⊤C) Capital / risk adjustment view
PV distributional distance W1(g#µA,g#µB) Scalar reserve movement
Timing distance W1(Law(DA), Law(DB)) Payment acceleration or delay
Selected projections PV, total, early, late, slope Interpretable vector diagnostics
Sliced distance SW2(µA,µB) Multivariate cash-flow comparison
Cumulative distance SW2(A#µA,A#µB) Emergence-pattern comparison
Actuarial takeaway
A reserving model comparison should say whether differences reflect level, timing, dispersion
or tail dependence. Wasserstein distances are useful precisely because they can be computed
after applying the relevant actuarial projection.
G.8 Extensions
The lab can be extended in several directions.
Accident-year specific laws. Instead of one aggregate vector C, let C(a) be the future
cash-flow vector for accident year a. One can compute Wasserstein distances by accident year,
then aggregate them with exposure weights.
Calendar-year liquidity. If the rows of a reserving scenario matrix correspond to accident
years and the columns to future calendar years, the projection onto calendar-year totals is a
liquidity diagnostic.
Net cash flows. Recoveries, reinsurance and expenses can be included by defining
Cnet t =Cgross t −Rrecovery t +Eexpense t .
The same transport diagnostics apply to the law of net cash-flow vectors.
IFRS 17 movement analysis. Distributional distances can be used as explanatory diagnostics
when assumptions change from one reporting date to the next. The Wasserstein distance is not
an accounting measurement rule; it is a tool for explaining how projected cash-flow laws moved.

G.9 Exercises
Exercise G.1 (Present value as push-forward). Let C∈ RT + have law µ, and let g(c) = ∑T t=1 vtct.
Show that the present-value reserve distribution is g#µ. Explain what information is lost when
replacing µ by g#µ.
Exercise G.2 (Same total, different timing). Consider c = (100, 0, 0) and c′ = (0, 0, 100).
Compute their undiscounted totals and their present values for discount rate i > 0. Then
compute the cumulative profiles At(c) and At(c′). Explain why a cumulative cost detects timing
risk.
Exercise G.3 (Choice of weights). For the cost dw(c,c′)2 = ∑ twt(ct −c′ t)2, compare the
choices wt = 1, wt = vt, and wt = 1{t≤3}. Give one reserving question for which each choice is
appropriate.
Exercise G.4 (PV stability). Prove the push-forward stability inequality for p = 2:
W2(g#µA,g#µB)≤∥v∥2W2(µA,µB).

total, early, late and slope projections. Which projection best detects the timing shift? Which
projection best detects inflation?

the tail-restricted distance better reveal the change?

models A and B for L = 20, 50, 100, 500 random projections. How stable is the estimate? Repeat
for models A and C.

may rank models differently.

diagnostic for liquidity stress.

metric and one graphical diagnostic.

H LAB 6: LONGEVITY TRANSPORT
H Lab 6: Longevity Transport
This lab studies mortality and longevity risk through probability laws on remaining lifetimes,
ages at death and present values of life-contingent cash-flows. A mortality table, a mortality
scenario or a longevity stress induces a distribution. Optimal transport then provides a way to
compare mortality assumptions, construct longevity stresses, blend scenarios and quantify the
financial effect of mortality improvement.
The lab is deliberately simple. It uses synthetic mortality tables generated from Gompertz–
Makeham-type forces of mortality. The same workflow applies to population life tables, insured
lives, annuitant tables, projected tables and stochastic mortality scenarios.
Actuarial takeaway
Life insurance and annuity models often start with a table of probabilities qx. Optimal
transport asks a distributional question: how far does the law of the remaining lifetime
move when mortality assumptions change? The same change can then be pushed forward
through annuity, death-benefit or pension cash-flow maps.
Actuarial takeaway
Quantile convention. Throughout this lab, quantiles are generalized lower inverses,
Q(u) = inf{t : F (t) ≥u}. This matters because curtate lifetime distributions are discrete.
In the R code, empirical quantiles are therefore computed with an explicit lower-inverse
function rather than with an interpolating quantile convention.
Actuarial takeaway
Distributional, not causal. A transport map between two lifetime laws is a distributional
alignment. It is useful for stress testing and liability sensitivity, but it does not identify
what a given individual lifetime would have been under another mortality table.
H.1 Learning objectives
After completing this lab, the reader should be able to:
• convert a mortality table into a probability law of the curtate future lifetime;
• compute one-dimensional Wasserstein distances between lifetime distributions;
• build a rank-preserving longevity stress by quantile mapping;
• compare mortality scenarios through their effect on life annuity and death-benefit present
values;
• distinguish a shift in lifetime distribution from a shift in financial liability distribution;
• construct one-dimensional Wasserstein barycenters of longevity scenarios;
• build a simple longevity report with life expectancy, high lifetime quantiles, annuity values,
death-benefit values and Wasserstein diagnostics.
H.2 The actuarial object
Let (x) be a life aged x. Write
Kx ∈{0, 1, 2,...}
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 396/416

H.3 Rank-preserving longevity stress
for the curtate future lifetime, so that Kx = k means that the life survives k complete years and
dies during the following policy year. If the mortality table provides one-year death probabilities
qx,qx+1,qx+2,...,
then
P(Kx =k) = kpxqx+k, kpx =
k−1∏
j=0
(1−qx+j),
with the convention 0px = 1. The law of Kx is a probability measure on N:
µx =
∑
k≥0
kpxqx+kδk.
The corresponding age-at-death law is the push-forward of µx by k↦→ x + k + 1 if death is
recorded at the end of the year of death.
Remark H.1 (Lifetime law versus mortality table). The table (qx+k)k≥0 is a sequence of conditional
one-year death probabilities. The measure µx is the unconditional distribution of the future
lifetime. Transport distances in this lab compare the latter object.
A change in mortality assumptions transforms µ0 x into another law µ1 x. For example, µ0 x may
be a baseline table and µ1 x a longevity-improved table. In one dimension, for p≥ 1 and finite
p-th moments, the Wasserstein distance has the quantile representation
Wp p (µ0 x,µ1 x) =
∫ 1
0
|Q0(u)−Q1(u)|p du,
where Q0 and Q1 are the lower quantile functions of remaining lifetime.
Actuarial takeaway
For lifetime distributions, W1 is measured in years. It can be read as an average absolute
displacement in remaining lifetime. W2 penalizes large movements more strongly and is
more sensitive to changes in the old-age tail.
H.3 Rank-preserving longevity stress
Let F0 and F1 be the distribution functions of two lifetime laws µ0 x and µ1 x. If the source law is
continuous, the monotone transport map can be written as
T (k) =Q1(F0(k)).
For discrete lifetime laws, this pointwise formula must be read with care because F0(Kx) is not
uniform. The clean implementation uses a common probability rank:
K0 =Q0(U), K1 =Q1(U), U∼ Unif(0, 1).
This construction preserves rank in distribution. A life at the 90th percentile of the baseline
lifetime law is matched with the 90th percentile of the stressed lifetime law.
For longevity risk, the map often moves upper quantiles more than lower quantiles. This
is important for annuity portfolios, pension liabilities and long-term care benefits, where late
payments matter.
Page 397/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 398 ===
H LAB 6: LONGEVITY TRANSPORT
H.4 Life-contingent present values as push-forwards
Let v = (1 + i)−1 be a discount factor. A whole-life annuity-due paying one unit per year while
the life is alive has present value, as a function of the curtate future lifetime K,
a(K) =
K∑
j=0
vj = 1−vK+1
1−v ,
with the limiting convention a(K) = K + 1 when i = 0. The distribution of the annuity present
value is
Law(a(Kx)) =a#µx.
A death benefit of one unit paid at the end of the year of death has present value
A(K) =vK+1,
and distribution
Law(A(Kx)) =A#µx.
Thus a mortality stress induces three related but different distributional changes:
µ0 x −→µ1 x,
for lifetimes,
a#µ0 x −→a#µ1 x,
for annuity present values, and
A#µ0 x −→A#µ1 x,
for death-benefit present values.
Actuarial takeaway
Longevity improvement increases annuity values but may decrease death-benefit present
values, because death payments are delayed. The same transport on lifetimes can therefore
have opposite financial effects on different products.
H.5 Barycenters of mortality scenarios
Suppose several mortality scenarios are available:
µ(1) x ,...,µ(M) x .
They may correspond to expert views, stochastic mortality projections, population versus
insured tables, or different longevity-improvement assumptions. A one-dimensional Wasserstein
barycenter with weights λm ≥ 0, ∑ mλm = 1, has quantile function
Qbar(u) =
M∑
m=1
λmQm(u).
This gives a distributional blend of mortality scenarios. It is not a mixture. A mixture first
selects a scenario and then samples a lifetime. A Wasserstein barycenter averages the quantiles
and therefore gives a central lifetime law in the transport geometry.
Remark H.2 (Barycenter versus mixture). A mixture of longevity scenarios may be more dispersed
because it keeps scenario uncertainty as a mixture component. A Wasserstein barycenter gives a
consensus scenario. Both are useful, but they answer different actuarial questions.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 398/416

H.6 R lab
H.6 R lab
The following R code generates simple mortality tables, converts them into lifetime distributions,
and compares baseline and longevity-improved scenarios. No external package is required.
# Helper functions
make _qx <- function(age0 = 65, omega = 120,
A = 0.0005, B = 0.00002, c = 1.105,
improvement = 0) {
if (omega <= age0) stop("omega␣must␣be␣greater␣than␣age0")
ages <- age0:omega
# Gompertz-Makeham force. The improvement parameter reduces
# mortality more strongly at future attained ages.
force <- A + B * c^ages
imp <- exp(-improvement * pmax(ages - age0, 0))
qx <- 1 - exp(-force * imp)
# Close the table at omega.
qx[ length(qx)] <- 1
data. frame(age = ages, qx = pmin( pmax(qx, 0), 1))
}
curtate _pmf <- function(qx) {
if ( any( ! is.finite(qx)) || any(qx < 0) || any(qx > 1)) {
stop("qx␣must␣contain␣probabilities␣in␣[0,␣1]")
}
n <- length(qx)
prob <- numeric(n)
surv <- 1
for (k in seq _len(n)) {
prob[k] <- surv * qx[k]
surv <- surv * (1 - qx[k])
}
prob / sum(prob)
}
q _pmf <- function(prob, u) {
if ( any( ! is.finite(prob)) || any(prob < 0) || sum(prob) <= 0) {
stop("prob␣must␣be␣a␣nonnegative␣vector␣with␣positive␣mass")
}
prob <- prob / sum(prob)
u <- pmin( pmax(u, 0), 1)
cdf <- cumsum(prob)
k <- seq _along(prob) - 1
idx <- vapply(u, function(uu) which(cdf >= uu)[1], integer(1))
k[idx]
}
emp _ q _left <- function(x, u) {
if ( length(x) == 0 || any( ! is.finite(x))) stop("x␣must␣be␣finite␣and␣
non-empty")
if ( any(u < 0 | u > 1)) stop("u␣must␣lie␣in␣[0,␣1]")
xs <- sort(x)
n <- length(xs)
xs[ pmax(1, ceiling(u * n))]
}

H LAB 6: LONGEVITY TRANSPORT
wass _pmf <- function(prob1, prob2, p = 1, grid _size = 5000) {
if (p < 1) stop("p␣must␣be␣at␣least␣1")
u <- ( seq _len( grid _size) - 0.5) / grid _size
q1 <- q _pmf(prob1, u)
q2 <- q _pmf(prob2, u)
mean( abs(q1 - q2)^p)^(1 /p)
}
annuity _pv <- function(k, i = 0.02) {
if ( abs(i) < 1e-12) return(k + 1)
v <- 1 / (1 + i)
(1 - v^(k + 1)) / (1 - v)
}
death _pv <- function(k, i = 0.02) {
v <- 1 / (1 + i)
v^(k + 1)
}
sample _pmf <- function(prob, n) {
if ( any( ! is.finite(prob)) || any(prob < 0) || sum(prob) <= 0) {
stop("prob␣must␣be␣a␣nonnegative␣vector␣with␣positive␣mass")
}
sample( seq _along(prob) - 1, size = n, replace = TRUE, prob = prob)
}
TVaR _emp <- function(x, alpha = 0.99) {
q <- emp _ q _left(x, alpha)
mean(x[x >= q])
}
summ _lifetime <- function(prob) {
k <- seq _along(prob) - 1
c(
mean = sum(k * prob),
q50 = q _pmf(prob, 0.50),
q90 = q _pmf(prob, 0.90),
q95 = q _pmf(prob, 0.95),
q99 = q _pmf(prob, 0.99)
)
}
# Mortality scenarios
age0 <- 65
omega <- 120
base _ table <- make _qx(age0 = age0, omega = omega, improvement = 0.000)
imp _ table <- make _qx(age0 = age0, omega = omega, improvement = 0.012)
sev _ table <- make _qx(age0 = age0, omega = omega, improvement = 0.020)
p _base <- curtate _pmf(base _ table $qx)
p _imp <- curtate _pmf(imp _ table $qx)
p _sev <- curtate _pmf(sev _ table $qx)
rbind(
baseline = summ _lifetime(p _base),
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 400/416

)

W1 _base _severe = wass _pmf(p _base, p _sev, p = 1),

W1 _ sample <- function(x, y) {
if ( length(x) ! = length(y)) stop("x␣and␣y␣must␣have␣the␣same␣length")
mean( abs( sort(x) - sort(y)))
}
W2 _ sample <- function(x, y) {
if ( length(x) ! = length(y)) stop("x␣and␣y␣must␣have␣the␣same␣length")
sqrt( mean(( sort(x) - sort(y))^2))
}
rbind(
annuity _baseline = c( mean = mean(ann _base), VaR99 = emp _ q _left(ann _
base, .99),
TVaR99 = TVaR _emp(ann _base, .99)),
annuity _improved = c( mean = mean(ann _imp), VaR99 = emp _ q _left(ann _imp,

H.7 Diagnostics to report
A practical longevity transport report should not contain only a best-estimate annuity value. It
should report lifetime and financial distributional effects. A minimal table is:
Scenario E[Kx] Q.95(Kx) W1(K) E[a(K)] Q.99(a(K)) E[A(K)]
Baseline 0
Improved mortality
Severe longevity
Barycenter
The table separates three layers:
• lifetime quantities, such as E[Kx], Q.95(Kx), and W1(K);
• annuity liability quantities, such as E[a(K)] and Q.99(a(K));
• death-benefit quantities, such as E[A(K)].
Computational note
In a longevity report, always state whether a Wasserstein distance is computed on remaining
lifetimes, ages at death, annuity present values or death-benefit present values. These
are different push-forwards of the mortality assumptions and they have different financial
interpretations.
H.8 Extensions
The lab can be extended in several directions.
Different entry ages. Repeat the analysis for x = 55, 65, 75. The same mortality improvement
may have different financial effects depending on age.
Interest-rate sensitivity. Repeat the annuity and death-benefit push-forwards for i =
0%, 2%, 5%. Lower interest rates increase the value of late annuity payments and therefore
amplify longevity risk.
Tail-aware longevity transport. Focus on the upper tail of the lifetime distribution:
W tail 1,α (µ0 x,µ1 x) = 1 1−α ∫ 1 α |Q0(u)−Q1(u)| du.
This is useful for annuity portfolios and pension plans that are sensitive to very old ages.
Page 403/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 404 ===
H LAB 6: LONGEVITY TRANSPORT
Multi-population mortality. Compare male and female tables, insured and population tables,
or two countries. The same transport workflow applies, but interpretation must account for
selection, underwriting and population differences.
Health-state and long-term-care benefits. Replace the lifetime law by a path law on health
states. Then the state space is no longer one-dimensional, and dynamic or adapted transport
becomes more relevant.
H.9 Exercises
Exercise H.1 (From qx to a lifetime law). Let (q65,...,q120) be a mortality table with q120 = 1.
Derive the probabilities P(K65 =k) for k = 0,..., 55. Check that they sum to one.
Exercise H.2 (Life expectancy). Using the probabilities from Exercise H.1, compute E[K65].
Interpret the difference between E[K65] and complete life expectancy.
Exercise H.3 (Wasserstein in years). Let µ0 and µ1 be two curtate lifetime laws. Explain why
W1(µ0,µ1) is measured in years. Compute it using a quantile grid.
Exercise H.4 (Rank-preserving stress). Construct the quantile map from a baseline lifetime
law to an improved mortality law. Plot Q1(u) against Q0(u). What does it mean if the curve
lies above the diagonal?
ExerciseH.5 (Annuitypush-forward). Let a(K) = (1−vK+1)/(1−v). Showthatthedistribution
of the annuity present value is a#µx. Compute its mean and compare it under baseline and
improved mortality.
Exercise H.6 (Death-benefit push-forward). Let A(K) = vK+1. Explain why longevity im-
provement may decrease E[A(K)] while increasing E[a(K)].
Exercise H.7 (Interest-rate sensitivity). Repeat the annuity comparison for interest rates 0%,
2% and 5%. How does the Wasserstein distance between annuity present-value distributions
change?
Exercise H.8 (Tail lifetime distance). Define and compute
W tail 1,.90(µ0,µ1).
Compare it with global W1(µ0,µ1). Which is more relevant for an annuity portfolio?
Exercise H.9 (Barycenter of scenarios). Given three lifetime laws with quantile functions
Q1,Q2,Q3, construct the Wasserstein barycenter with weights (.5,.3,.2). Compare the barycenter
with a mixture of the three scenarios.
Exercise H.10 (Selection effect). Suppose annuitants have lower mortality than the gen-
eral population. Describe how this should appear in the lifetime quantile functions and in
W1(µpopulation,µannuitant).
Exercise H.11 (Product-specific interpretation). Compute the transport distance between
baseline and improved mortality on lifetimes, annuity present values and death-benefit present
values. Explain why the three distances do not answer the same actuarial question.
Exercise H.12 (Governance report). Design a one-page longevity transport report for an annuity
portfolio. Include at least two lifetime metrics, two financial metrics, one Wasserstein diagnostic
and one sensitivity analysis.

=== page 405 ===
I Hints and selected proof strategies
This appendix gives hints rather than full solutions. The goal is to indicate the main identity,
standard theorem, or modelling argument that unlocks each exercise. A useful convention is
to label exercises in the main text by chapter and number, for instance \label{ex:4:7} for
Exercise 4.7. The hints below use that convention.
Further reading
Most proof-based exercises rely on a small number of recurring mechanisms: push-forward
identities, construction of couplings, finite-dimensional linear-programming duality, the
uncrossing argument on the real line, Kantorovich duality, Brenier’s theorem, quantile
representations, Jensen/Hölder/Minkowski inequalities, and basic convexity of risk mea-
sures. For the mathematical background, see Villani 2003; Villani 2008; Figalli and Glaudo
2021; Ambrosio et al. 2021. For computational OT and Sinkhorn algorithms, see Peyré
and Cuturi 2019; Solomon 2018; Chewi et al. 2025. For actuarial dependence and risk
measures, see Denuit et al. 2005a; Charpentier 2024.
General proof patterns
Push-forward arguments. To prove that a map transports one law into another, show
that for every measurable set B, P(T (X) ∈ B) = ν(B). Equivalently, test against bounded
measurable functions: ∫
φ(y) d(T#µ)(y) =
∫
φ(T (x)) dµ(x).
This is often the shortest path for deductible maps, tariff maps, treaty maps and scenario
transformations.
Coupling arguments. To construct a coupling, build a joint random vector with the desired
marginals. In finite spaces, write a matrix and check row and column sums. In one dimension,
take a common U∼ Unif(0, 1) and set X =F−1 µ (U), Y =F−1 ν (U).
Duality arguments. For discrete OT, weak duality is usually one line:
∑
i
aiui +
∑
j
bjvj =
∑
i,j
γij(ui +vj)≤
∑
i,j
γijCij.
For continuous OT, replace sums by integrals and (ui,vj,Cij) by (φ(x),ψ(y),c(x,y)).
One-dimensional transport. When the cost is convex in x−y, look for an uncrossing
argument. If x1 <x2 and y1 <y2, the uncrossed pairing is cheaper than the crossed pairing. In
the limit, this yields quantile transport.
Wasserstein inequalities. Most stability bounds use either the primal coupling inequality
|Ef(X)− Ef(Y )|≤ Lip(f)Ed(X,Y ),
or the quantile formula in dimension one, followed by Hölder’s inequality.
Computational exercises. When an exercise asks for a plot or a simulation, the important
part is not the numerical value but the diagnostic curve: how does the Wasserstein distance,
robust capital, residual diagnostic, tail index or transport cost change when a modelling parameter
changes?
Page 405/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

I HINTS AND SELECTED PROOF STRATEGIES
Lecture 2: Couplings, push-forwards and insurance dependence
Hint for Exercise 2.1 (Push-forward of a deductible). Compute P ((L−d)+ ≤ y)
separately for y < 0, y = 0, and y > 0. The atom at zero is P (L≤ d) = F (d), up to the
convention at the deductible.
Hint for Exercise 2.2 (Graph couplings). For the graph part, test the event {(x,y) :
y = T (x)} under Law(X,T (X)). For a non-graph coupling, take µ = δ0 and a non-Dirac ν: no
deterministic map can split the single atom.
Hint for Exercise 2.3 (Two-point Fréchet class). Use the parameter a = P (X = 1,Y = 1).
Then the four probabilities are a, p−a, q−a, and 1 −p−q + a. Non-negativity gives the
admissible interval. The law of X +Y follows by collecting masses at 0, 1, 2.
Hint for Exercise 2.4 (Aggregation under three couplings). Use inverse-transform
simulation. The same marginal quantile functions should be used in all three cases; only the
dependence changes. Compare high empirical quantiles of X +Y, not the marginal quantiles.
Hint for Exercise 2.5 (Convexity of couplings). Interpret (1 −θ)γ0 + θγ1 as: first flip
a Bernoulli variable with probability θ, then sample from γ1 or γ0. It is a coupling because
marginals are linear. It corresponds to a copula if the marginals are continuous, but not usually
to a deterministic map.
Hint for Exercise 2.6 (Worst-case expectation). With Bernoulli marginals, E[XY ] =
P (X = 1,Y = 1) = a. Maximize and minimize a over the Fréchet interval. The endpoints are
comonotonic and countermonotonic.
Lecture 3: Discrete optimal transport
Hint for Exercise 3.1 (The 2 × 2 polytope). Use the upper-left entry t. The remaining
entries are forced by row and column sums. The objective is affine in t, so the optimum is one
endpoint unless the coefficient of t is zero.
Hint for Exercise 3.2 (Duality by hand). The primal optimum is the diagonal plan. For
dual potentials, try u = (0, 0), v = (0, 0), or shifted variants, and then check the inequalities
ui +vj ≤Cij. Complementary slackness only needs equality on positive-mass entries.
Hint for Exercise 3.3 (Independence is usually not optimal). Take x = y = (0, 1) and
uniform weights. Independence spreads mass over all four entries, while the diagonal plan has
zero quadratic cost.
Hint for Exercise 3.4 (No-crossing argument). For p = 1, draw the four points on the
line and use the triangle inequality. For p > 1, use that |x−y|p has the Monge property on
ordered points, or differentiate the function obtained by sliding the two intervals.
Hint for Exercise 3.5 (Worst-case stop-loss). Again parameterize the 2 × 2 coupling by
a = P (X = 10,Y = 10). Then compute S = X + Y and (S− 12)+. The worst case puts as much
mass as possible on the pair (10, 10).
Hint for Exercise 3.6 (Empirical computation). For one-dimensional empirical laws with
equal sample sizes, sort both samples and compute n−1 ∑ i|x(i) −y(i)|p. Multiplying one sample
by 1 +r should produce an increasing curve in|r|, with stronger tail effects for W2 than W1.
Hint for Exercise 3.7 (Dual potentials as sensitivity). The dual potentials are subgra-
dients of the optimal value with respect to marginal weights. Because total mass must remain
one, moving a small mass from source cell k to source cell i changes the value approximately by
ui −uk.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 406/416

=== page 407 ===
Lecture 4: Continuous optimal transport and Kantorovich duality
Hint for Exercise 4.1 (Monge plans are Kantorovich plans). Use the definition
of (Id,T )#µ. The first marginal is automatic; the second marginal is T#µ. Then apply the
change-of-variables formula with h(x,y) =c(x,y).
Hint for Exercise 4.2 (No deterministic splitting). A deterministic image of δ0 is always
a Dirac mass. For the Kantorovich coupling, take pδ(0,0) + (1−p)δ(0,1).
Hint for Exercise 4.3 (Weak duality). Integrate φ(x) + ψ(y) ≤c(x,y) with respect to γ.
Use the marginal constraints to replace the two integrals by
∫
φ dµ and
∫
ψ dν. For maximization
of a payoff h, apply the same argument to−h or reverse the bound accordingly.
Hint for Exercise 4.4 (The c-transform). The inequality φ(x) + φc(y) ≤ c(x,y) is
immediate from the definition of the infimum. If (φ,ψ) is feasible, then ψ(y) ≤c(x,y) −φ(x) for
every x, hence ψ(y)≤ infx{c(x,y)−φ(x)}.
Hint for Exercise 4.5 (Quantile transport). Show P (T (X) ≤y) = P (Fµ(X) ≤Fν(y)).
For exponentials, Fλ(x) = 1−e−λx, so the map is T (x) =λx/θ.
Hint for Exercise 4.6 (One-dimensional W1). Use the quantile formula with p = 1. For
uniform laws, write the quantile functions as affine functions and integrate the absolute value of
an affine function on [0, 1]. If the intervals have the same length, this collapses to the distance
between left endpoints.
Hint for Exercise 4.7 (Gaussian transport). Write Qµ(u) = m0 + σ0Φ−1(u) and
Qν(u) = m1 + σ1Φ−1(u). Therefore T (x) = m1 + (σ1/σ0)(x−m0). For W 2 2 , integrate the square
of (m0 −m1) + (σ0 −σ1)Φ−1(u); the cross term vanishes.
Hint for Exercise 4.8 (Actuarial severity stress). The rank is Fµ(x). The map preserves
this rank by construction. The advantage is interpretability and monotone tail reshaping; the
limitation is that it assumes rank preservation, which is not a causal fact.
Lecture 5: Wasserstein distances, quantiles and barycenters
Hint for Exercise 5.1 (Dirac and two-point laws). The only mass that must move is the
mass 1−p from 0 to a. For Wp p, the cost is (1−p)ap.
Hint for Exercise 5.2 (Uniform laws). Use Qµ(u) = u and Qν(u) = au. Then Wp p =∫ 1
0 |1−a|pup du =|1−a|p/(p + 1).
Hint for Exercise 5.3 (TVaR stability). Write TVaR as an average of quantiles on [α, 1].
Apply Hölder’s inequality to Qµ −Qν on that interval, then bound by the full Lp(0, 1) norm.
Hint for Exercise 5.4 (Gaussian laws). Use the same calculation as Exercise 4.7. The
one-dimensional Gaussian formula is a useful check on the multivariate trace formula.
Hint for Exercise 5.5 (Barycenter of two distributions). In quantile coordinates, the
problem is pointwise least squares: minq(1 −λ)|q−Q0(u)|2 + λ|q−Q1(u)|2. The minimizer is
the weighted average of quantiles.
Hint for Exercise 5.6 (Mixture versus barycenter). The mixture is 1 2 δ0 + 1 2 δ2. The
barycenter has quantile 1 2 Q1(u) + 1 2 Q2(u) = 1, hence is δ1. This illustrates heterogeneity versus
centrality.
Hint for Exercise 5.7 (Empirical implementation). Use a common probability grid uk.
Compute empirical quantiles for each sample, then take means of |Qx(uk) −Qy(uk)|p. Check
sensitivity to the grid and to tail trimming.
Page 407/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 408 ===
I HINTS AND SELECTED PROOF STRATEGIES
Hint for Exercise 5.8 (Vector-rank stress). Use the definition of push-forward twice. If
(Rµ)#µ = µ0 and (Qν)#µ0 = ν, then (Qν ◦Rµ)#µ = ν. The reference law µ0 fixes what rank
means in dimension d> 1, so it is a modelling choice rather than a canonical object.
Lecture 6: Entropic regularization and Sinkhorn algorithms
Hint for Exercise 6.1 (Scaling form). Write first-order conditions for the Lagrangian
with row and column multipliers. The derivative of γ logγ−γ is logγ. Exponentiating gives
γij =uiKijvj, with Kij =e−Cij/ε.
Hint for Exercise 6.2 (Two-by-two Sinkhorn). Do the row scaling and column scaling
explicitly. This exercise is mainly about seeing that Sinkhorn alternates between satisfying one
marginal and disturbing the other, with convergence through repeated rescaling.
Hint for Exercise 6.3 (Independent coupling limit). As ε→∞, Kij = e−Cij/ε → 1.
Scaling a matrix of ones to marginals (a,b) yieldsab⊤, the independent coupling.
Hint for Exercise 6.4 (Bias and self-cost). Compute entropic OT between a measure
and itself. Unless ε is very small, the regularized plan is diffuse and not concentrated on the
diagonal, so the regularized self-cost is positive. The Sinkhorn divergence subtracts this bias.
Hint for Exercise 6.5 (Implementation). Start with the transparent update u = a/(Kv),
v = b/(K⊤u), understood componentwise. Then compare row and column residuals. Use
log-domain updates if costs are large or ε is small.
Hint for Exercise 6.6 (Unbalanced prevention). Let the prevention program reduce
total expected loss or exposure. Balanced OT forces all mass to match; unbalanced OT permits
part of the mass to disappear. Interpret disappearance as prevented exposure or avoided loss
mass.
Lecture 7: Statistical optimal transport
Hint for Exercise 7.1 (Empirical consistency). Use the triangle inequality: Wp(µn,νn) ≤
Wp(µn,µ) + Wp(µ,ν) + Wp(ν,νn), and similarly in the other direction. Conclude by convergence
of the two empirical laws.
Hint for Exercise 7.2 (One-dimensional computation). Sort the samples or use empirical
quantiles on a common grid. The key is that in dimension one OT does not require solving a
linear program.
Hint for Exercise 7.3 (Noise floor under the null). Repeated random splits estimate
the sampling distribution of W1 when the two samples come from the same population. This is
the empirical noise floor: observed distances below this level are not strong evidence of drift.
Hint for Exercise 7.4 (Sliced Wasserstein for reserve cash flows). Project each ten-year
vector onto random directions θ, compute one-dimensional Wasserstein distances, and average
over directions. Increasing the number of projections reduces Monte Carlo variability.
Hint for Exercise 7.5 (Entropic smoothing). Compare exact empirical OT with entropic
OT for several ε. Small ε has low bias but high numerical instability; large ε is smoother but
more biased.
Lecture 8: Risk measures, quantiles and Wasserstein robustness
Hint for Exercise 8.1 (Robust mean under W1). Use the Kantorovich–Rubinstein dual
or simply note that h(x) =x is 1-Lipschitz on R. The upper bound is mean plus radius, and it
is approached by shifting mass upward.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 408/416

=== page 409 ===
Hint for Exercise 8.2 (Sensitivity of robust TVaR). Apply the stated Lipschitz inequality
and take the supremum over W1(ν,µ) ≤r. The factor (1 −α)−1 explains the explosion as α↑ 1.
Hint for Exercise 8.3 (Radius by bootstrap). Resample the losses with replacement,
compute W1(ˆµ∗ n,ˆµn), and take a high quantile. Repeat for several n to see the radius decrease
with sample size.
Hint for Exercise 8.4 (Pricing versus capital ambiguity). The pricing ambiguity set
usually lives on covariates X; the capital ambiguity set lives on losses L or aggregate losses. Give
an example where Law(X) changes but the conditional model Law(L|X) is unchanged.
Hint for Exercise 8.5 (Ground cost). Write two costs, e.g. c((A,V ), (A′,V′)) = λA|A−
A′| + λV| logV − logV′|. Changing the weights changes which perturbations are considered
cheap.
Hint for Exercise 8.6 (Expectation and W1). For any coupling (X,Y ), use |EX− EY|≤
E|X−Y|. Then minimize over couplings. This is the primal proof of the 1-Lipschitz stability of
the mean.
Hint for Exercise 8.7 (TVaR Lipschitz constant). Use the quantile representation of
TVaRα, then Hölder on [α, 1]. The constant is (1−α)−1/p for Wp.
Hint for Exercise 8.8 (Robust TVaR). Write the adversarial quantile perturbation as
a nonnegative function on [α, 1] subject to an Lp budget. Hölder identifies the worst direction:
concentrate according to the dual norm.
Hint for Exercise 8.9 (VaR sensitivity). Choose ε just above or below 1 −α. Then a tiny
amount of mass at a very large loss can leave weak convergence almost unchanged while making
VaRα jump.
Hint for Exercise 8.10 (Stop-loss stability). The map x↦→ (x−r)+ is 1-Lipschitz. Apply
the primal coupling inequality or the Kantorovich–Rubinstein dual.
Hint for Exercise 8.11 (Dependence-robust stop-loss). Represent the expectation as an
integral over a coupling of the marginal quantiles. The objective is linear in the coupling, so the
problem is a transport problem with reward (Q1(u) +Q2(v)−d)+.
Hint for Exercise 8.12 (Computational exercise). Use empirical quantiles for VaR and
tail averages for TVaR. For robustness, plot the bounds as functions of the radius rather than
reporting only one number.
Lecture 9: Pricing, segmentation and mutualization
Hint for Exercise 9.1 (Variance and Wasserstein distance). The optimal coupling
between Law(Z) and δ¯z is forced: pair Z with the constant ¯z. Therefore W 2 2 (Law(Z),δ¯z) =
E[(Z− ¯z)2].
Hint for Exercise 9.2 (Nested rating information). Use the Hilbert-space projection
property of conditional expectation. Since G1 ⊆G2, the L2 projection on the larger space has
smaller residual variance.
Hint for Exercise 9.3 (Shrinkage and distance to pooling). The random variable
Πλ − ¯π = λ(Π − ¯π). Use homogeneity of Wp under scaling around the same center, or compute
the quantile functions directly.
Hint for Exercise 9.4 (Policy-level versus distributional movement). The paired
coupling (Π0, Π1) is feasible for the two premium laws. Therefore the Wasserstein distance is
bounded above by the paired Lp displacement. Equality need not hold.
Page 409/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 410 ===
I HINTS AND SELECTED PROOF STRATEGIES
Hint for Exercise 9.5 (Two-class tariff). Write the tariff distribution as a two-point law.
The distance to the pooled premium is forced by coupling each class premium to the common
mean.
Hint for Exercise 9.6 (Affordability distribution). Work with A = Π/Y. A tariff change
induces a push-forward of the joint law of (Π,Y ). Compare affordability distributions, not only
premium distributions.
Hint for Exercise 9.7 (Premium caps). The map x↦→ min(x,M) is 1-Lipschitz and equals
the identity below M. Use the coupling (Π, ΠM) to bound the distance by E[(Π−M)p +]1/p.
Lecture 10: Portfolio drift, calibration and domain adaptation
Hint for Exercise 10.1 (Covariate shift and expected loss). Use the law of total
expectation. Under covariate shift, m(x) = E[L|X = x] is unchanged, so only the marginal law
of X changes.
Hint for Exercise 10.2 (Push-forward drift). If f is K-Lipschitz, then |f(x) −f(y)|≤
Kd(x,y). Apply this inequality inside any coupling and minimize over couplings.
Hint for Exercise 10.3 (Transport-transfer predictor). Use barycentric projection: for
each target point, average the source responses according to the conditional transport weights.
Check whether the plan is source-to-target or target-to-source before writing the weights.
Hint for Exercise 10.4 (Residual drift). Compute residual distributions by time period.
Drift in fitted means and drift in residuals answer different questions: the first concerns portfolio
mix, the second concerns conditional model validity.
Hint for Exercise 10.5 (Choice of metric). Standardize continuous variables, use a
substitution cost for categorical variables, and choose weights according to actuarial materiality.
Explain how geography or vehicle value can dominate the metric if left unscaled.
Hint for Exercise 10.6 (Scenario transport). Since L = exp(H), the map from hazard
to loss is not globally Lipschitz. Work either on a bounded hazard range or use local Lipschitz
constants; then compare hazard-space and loss-space displacements.
Lecture 11: Reserving, cash-flow distributions and climate prevention
Hint for Exercise 11.1 (Best estimate versus predictive law). Construct two distribu-
tions with the same mean but different variance or tail, e.g. a Dirac mass at the mean and a
two-point distribution around it. The best estimate is the same; capital is not.
Hint for Exercise 11.2 (Aggregation contraction). For p = 2, use that the sum map
a(r) = ∑ trt is Lipschitz with constant √ T under the Euclidean norm. Then apply push-forward
contraction of Wasserstein distances under Lipschitz maps.
Hint for Exercise 11.3 (Reserve barycenter). In dimension one, average quantile functions.
For three reserve laws, the barycenter quantile is ∑ kλkQk(u).
Hint for Exercise 11.4 (Inflation stress). The map is diagonal: Ta(r) = (a1r1,a2r2,a3r3).
Its Lipschitz constant is maxtat for the Euclidean norm. Use this to bound the Wasserstein
movement of cash-flow vectors.
Hint for Exercise 11.5 (Prevention profile). Compute the transformed tail by splitting
x≤M and x>M. The map T caps or reduces large losses, so compare its effect on the mean,
VaR and TVaR.
Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0 Page 410/416

Hint for Exercise 11.6 (Balanced versus unbalanced comparison). Use an example
where the normalized cash-flow shape is identical but total payment volume changes. Balanced
OT sees no shape change; unbalanced OT sees volume change.
Hint for Exercise 11.7 (Mini-project). Keep the project simple: simulate two sets of
future cash-flow vectors, compute distances for total reserves and for full vectors, then compare
what is lost by aggregation.
Lecture 12: Fairness, discrimination and Wasserstein projections
Hint for Exercise 12.1 (Two-group barycenter). Use the one-dimensional barycenter
formula: ¯Q = (1−p)Q0 +pQ1. The group repair maps are Ts = ¯Q◦Fs.
Hint for Exercise 12.2 (Gaussian score repair). For Gaussian groups, the one-dimensional
OT map is affine. Send each group mean and standard deviation to the barycenter mean and
standard deviation.
Hint for Exercise 12.3 (Calibration tension). Use the fact that distributional parity of
scores and group calibration impose restrictions on the joint law of (Y,Z,S). Unless base rates
coincide or the score is uninformative/perfect, both conditions cannot generally hold.
Hint for Exercise 12.4 (Partial repair path). The map Z(λ) = (1 −λ)Z + λTS(Z) is a
displacement interpolation in one dimension when TS is monotone. Distances to the target scale
by (1−λ).
Hint for Exercise 12.5 (Empirical implementation). Sort scores within each group,
compute a barycenter quantile grid, then map each observation through its empirical rank. Check
ties carefully.
Hint for Exercise 12.6 (Residual audit). A score repair changes Z, hence changes residuals
Y−Z. Fair score distributions do not imply fair residual or loss-ratio distributions. Compare
both before and after repair.
Hint for Exercise 12.7 (Conditional disparity). Condition on each admissible risk class
A = a. Compute Wasserstein disparities within cells, then aggregate with cell weights. This
separates distributional disparity from legitimate risk-class mix differences.
Lecture 13: Reinsurance, capital allocation and multi-marginal transport
Hint for Exercise 13.1 (Push-forward of a quota-share treaty). A quota-share treaty
is the map Tq(L) = qL or (1 −q)L, depending on whether you track ceded or retained loss. The
law is the push-forward by a scaling map; moments scale accordingly.
Hint for Exercise 13.2 (Layer distribution). For C = min{(L−a)+,ℓ}, identify three
regions: L≤a, a<L<a + ℓ, and L≥a + ℓ. Atoms occur at 0 and ℓ. Use the stop-loss identity
to compute the mean.
Hint for Exercise 13.3 (Worst-case expected layer loss). For discrete marginals, write a
transportation matrix and maximize a linear objective with reward min{(xi + yj −a)+,ℓ}. This
is a discrete OT problem with negative cost.
Hint for Exercise 13.4 (Comonotonic aggregation). With Xk = Qk(U), the aggregate
quantile is the sum of marginal quantiles when the quantile functions are continuous at the level
considered. This is the defining rank-aligned dependence.
Hint for Exercise 13.5 (Expected shortfall and multi-marginal transport). For
fixed t, the objective E[(S−t)+] is an expectation over a multi-marginal coupling. Solve or

approximate that problem for several t, then minimize over t. Tail numerical difficulty comes
from rare but high-impact configurations.
Hint for Exercise 13.6 (Euler allocation under two dependence models). Estimate
conditional expectations given tail events. Under comonotonicity, large lines are large together,
so allocations tend to reflect aligned marginal tail heaviness.
Hint for Exercise 13.7 (Treaty sensitivity). The map L↦→ min(L,a) changes the whole
distribution smoothly in a, whereas VaRα reacts to whether the cap crosses the relevant quantile.
Thus the two curves need not have the same shape.
Hint for Exercise 13.8 (Scenario reweighting). Let wi ≥ 0, ∑ iwi = 1. Maximize∑
iwiℓ(x(i)) subject to moment constraints. Add −η∑ iwi logwi to penalize concentration, or
equivalently constrain KL divergence from uniform weights.
Lecture 14: Counterfactual worlds and transport
Hint for Exercise 14.1 (Two-point counterfactual worlds). Use the Bernoulli Fréchet
interval again. The counterfactual coupling is determined by a = P (X = 1,Y = 1); endpoints
give maximal persistence or maximal switching.
Hint for Exercise 14.2 (Non-identification). Take the identity coupling Y = X and the
flip coupling Y = 1 −X when both marginals are Bernoulli(1/2). They have the same marginals
but opposite individual counterfactual stories.
Hint for Exercise 14.3 (Premium recourse under a linear tariff). Project the point
x onto the half-space {z : m(z) ≤p⋆}. The Euclidean projection formula is along the normal
vectorβ.
Hint for Exercise 14.4 (Quantile transport between normal laws). Use the same
affine map as in Exercise 4.7. Interpret it as rank-preserving: standardized normal scores are
kept fixed.
Hint for Exercise 14.5 (Counterfactual catastrophe catalogue). Compute expected
counterfactual loss for each event and for the catalogue. For tail quantities, combine all event-loss
atoms with their probabilities and sort them.
Hint for Exercise 14.6 (Prevention and coupling). The marginals alone do not identify
which original 100-loss states become 80-loss states. Construct at least two couplings with the
same before/after laws and compare their individual stories.
Hint for Exercise 14.7 (Hard actionability constraints). Only optimize over xA; keep
xN fixed. In a linear model, this is the projection of the actionable subvector onto a half-space
whose normal is the actionable coefficient vector.
Hint for Exercise 14.8 (Sequential counterfactuals). Apply the first transport map to
X1, then update X2 conditionally or through the graph mechanism. The main point is that
changing parent variables and then children differs from transporting all marginals independently.
Lecture 15: Dynamic optimal transport and actuarial trajectories
Hint for Exercise 15.1 (Inflation velocity). Differentiate Lt = atL0. The velocity in state
space is ˙at/at times the current position. The action involves E[L2 t ].
Hint for Exercise 15.2 (Quantile geodesic). In one dimension, set Qt = (1 −t)Q0 + tQ1.
Then∥Qt −Qs∥L2 =|t−s|∥Q1 −Q0∥L2, which is the constant-speed geodesic property.
Hint for Exercise 15.3 (Uniform-to-uniform trajectory). The optimal map from [0, 1]
to [0, 2] is T (x) = 2x. Hence Tt(x) = (1 +t)x, so µt =U[0, 1 +t].

Hint for Exercise 15.4 (Dirac-to-two-point target). No deterministic map can split δ0.
The Kantorovich plan can split mass from 0 to −1 and 1. Dynamic paths may require stochastic
branching.
Hint for Exercise 15.5 (TVaR along a geodesic). TVaR is linear in the quantile function.
Substitute Qt = (1−t)Q0 +tQ1 into the integral over [α, 1].
Hint for Exercise 15.6 (Gaussian covariance path). In one dimension, Gaussian quantiles
are σiΦ−1(u). The geodesic quantile is ((1 −t)σ0 +tσ1)Φ−1(u), so standard deviations interpolate
linearly, not variances.
Hint for Exercise 15.7 (Path length and churn). Take µ0 = µ2 = δ0 and µ1 = δ1.
Endpoint distance is zero, but the path length through µ1 is positive. Interpret as movement out
and back.
HintforExercise15.8(Discretedynamicaction). Usethetriangleinequality W2(µ0,µT ) ≤∑
tW2(µt,µt+1), then Cauchy–Schwarz: (∑ tat)2 ≤T∑ ta2 t.
Hint for Exercise 15.9 (Exposure growth). Balanced OT between normalized laws
reports no change if composition is unchanged. It loses total exposure. Use finite measures
αt =Etµt and compare with unbalanced OT or report exposure-scaled mass separately.
Hint for Exercise 15.10 (Mini-lab extension). The Pareto target should mostly affect
high quantiles and TVaR. Plot the curves over t; the mean may move moderately while VaR0.995
and TVaR0.99 move sharply.
Lecture 16: Unbalanced transport, exposure and portfolio growth
Hint for Exercise 16.1 (Finite-measure KL). Use the finite-measure entropy formula
KL(ρ|α) =q log(q/m)−q +m, with the usual conventions at q = 0.
Hint for Exercise 16.2 (One-point unbalanced OT). The only transport variable is
the transported mass γ≥ 0. Write the objective as c0γ + τ1KL(γ| m) + τ2KL(γ| n), then
differentiate in γ.
Hint for Exercise 16.3 (Balanced limit). As the marginal penalties go to infinity, deviations
of the transported marginals from α and β become infinitely expensive. Any finite-limit minimizer
must satisfy the balanced marginal constraints.
Hint for Exercise 16.4 (Normalization can hide growth). The normalized laws are both
δ0, so balanced OT reports zero. The finite measures have different total masses, so unbalanced
OT or a mass-difference diagnostic detects growth.
Hint for Exercise 16.5 (Two-cell exposure). Write the transport matrix and compare
row/column sums to the finite masses. Report which cell is transported, which source mass is
unused, and which target mass is newly created.
Hint for Exercise 16.6 (Generalized Sinkhorn). Start from the scaling form Γ =
diag(u)K diag(v). The KL marginal penalties yield damped updates with exponents θk =
τk/(τk +ε).
Hint for Exercise 16.7 (Penalty sensitivity). Plot transported mass, source usage and
target explanation. As τ increases, the solution should behave more like balanced transport; as
τ decreases, creation/destruction becomes cheaper.
Hint for Exercise 16.8 (Asymmetric penalties). If τ1 is large and τ2 small, losing source
mass is expensive but creating target mass is cheap. Reverse the interpretation when τ1 is small
and τ2 large.

Hint for Exercise 16.9 (Claim-intensity measures). The normalized severities are
identical, but the finite intensity measures differ by total mass. Balanced OT sees zero severity-
shape change; unbalanced comparison sees frequency growth.
Hint for Exercise 16.10 (Climate exposure). Use three zones with both spatial movement
and total insured-value growth. Let one coastal zone receive new target mass; unbalanced OT
should interpret part of it as created exposure rather than migrated old exposure.
Hint for Exercise 16.11 (Dynamic source term). Integrate the equation over space. The
divergence term vanishes under no-flux or decay conditions, leaving d dt|ρt| = ∫ ζt dρt. Interpret
this as growth or lapse of total exposure.
Lecture 17: Life, mortality and longevity transport
Hint for Exercise 17.1 (Life table as a measure). Use survival to the beginning of each
curtate year. The mass at k is pxpx+1 ···px+k−1qx+k, with the empty product equal to one. If
the table is closed at ω, add the remaining survivor mass to the final age.
Hint for Exercise 17.2 (Residual lifetime Wasserstein distance). Use the lower
generalized inverse of the two discrete distributions. On (0, 1), identify the intervals on which
each quantile is 0, 1, or 2, then integrate the absolute difference of the two step functions.
Hint for Exercise 17.3 (Quantile longevity stress). For an exponential law, Fλ(t) =
1 −e−λt and Qλ(u) = − log(1 −u)/λ. Hence T (t) = Qλ1(Fλ0(t)) = (λ0/λ1)t. If λ1 < λ0, the
map stretches lifetimes.
Hint for Exercise 17.4 (Annuity push-forward). If pk = ¶(Kx = k), then the law of
g(Kx) is ∑ kpkδg(k). If several k’s give the same present value, collect the corresponding masses.
This is exactly the definition of a push-forward by g.
Hint for Exercise 17.5 (Opposite effects). The annuity present value g(k) = ∑k j=0 vj is
increasing in k. A death benefit paid at the end of the year of death has present value vk+1,
which is decreasing in k when 0 <v < 1. Apply first-order stochastic dominance to increasing
and decreasing functions.
Hint for Exercise 17.6 (Wasserstein barycenter). In dimension one, solve the barycenter
problem pointwise in quantile space. The barycenter quantile is Qλ = (1 −λ)Q0 + λQ1. Interpret
this as a mortality scenario whose lifetime percentiles are blended rather than mixed at the
model level.
Hint for Exercise 17.7 (Liability push-forward). For lifetime vector K = (K1,...,Kn),
write the portfolio present value as G(K) = ∑ ibi ∑Ki j=0 vj, or the appropriate contract-specific
version. If µK is the joint law of lifetimes, the liability law is G#µK.
Hint for Exercise 17.8 (Joint-life dependence). The function max(Tx,Ty) depends on
the joint law, not only the two marginals. Under comonotonicity, high lifetime ranks occur
together; under independence, one life can be long while the other is not. Last-survivor annuities
are sensitive to this dependence.
Hint for Exercise 17.9 (Tail-aware longevity). Define the tail distance by integrating
quantile differences over [0.95, 1], with the normalizing factor used in the lecture. This focuses
on the longest-lived percentiles, which drive old-age annuity risk more directly than a global
average distance.
Hint for Exercise 17.10 (Multi-state transport). Build a cost matrix with zero diagonal
and larger costs for clinically or contractually more distant moves. For example, healthy to
disabled may be cheaper than healthy to long-term care, while moves involving death may be
absorbing or very costly depending on the modelling question.

Hint for Exercise 17.12 (Federated quantile sketches). Approximate W1 by a Riemann
sum of absolute differences between reported quantiles on the common grid. The sketch preserves
marginal lifetime information at reported probabilities, but loses dependence between lives,
health-state paths and within-bin tail shape.
Appendix B: Cost engineering for actuarial OT
Hint for Exercise B.1 (Loss scale). For ν = Law((1 + i)X), use the monotone coupling
(X, (1 + i)X). The p-cost is ipE[Xp] for the raw loss metric. Compare this with a log-scale cost,
where multiplicative changes become additive.
Hint for Exercise B.2 (Categorical substitution matrix). Construct a symmetric matrix
with zero diagonal. The triangle inequality is optional unless you need a true metric. Interpret
low sedan–SUV cost and higher sedan–truck cost, for instance.
Hint for Exercise B.3 (Simplex cost). Work inside the simplex. For probability vectors,
compare Euclidean, Hellinger, log-ratio or Fisher-Rao type costs. State which parts of the
simplex are allowed: log-ratio costs require positive components, while Euclidean costs can handle
boundary points more directly.
Hint for Exercise B.4 (Geographical cost). Use shortest-path distance on the territory
graph. This captures adjacency and travel across territories better than arbitrary numeric
territory codes.
Hint for Exercise B.5 (Contract distance). Decompose contract terms into deductible,
limit, coinsurance and endorsements. Use scaled absolute differences for numerical terms and
substitution costs for categorical endorsements.
Hint for Exercise B.6 (Cash-flow shape). Take two vectors with the same sum but
different timing, e.g. (100, 0, 0) and (0, 0, 100). A total-reserve metric sees no difference; a
discounted or time-weighted vector metric does.
Hint for Exercise B.7 (Weight sensitivity). Plot the transport value and plan diagnostics
as the regional weight changes. Large changes in matched pairs indicate that conclusions are
cost-engineering sensitive.
Hint for Exercise B.8 (Gromov–Wasserstein cost engineering). Do not try to define
a direct cost between points in two unrelated spaces. Define within-space dissimilarities first,
for example distances between classes inside each portfolio, then compare how well a coupling
preserves these internal distances. The conclusion depends on those internal dissimilarities, so
they must be documented.
Hint for Exercise B.9 (Cost report). A good report states the state space, units, scaling,
categorical substitution choices, fixed or non-actionable variables, sensitivity analyses, and what
the cost is not allowed to mean. Include at least one alternative cost specification.
Appendix C: Deterministic mapping versus diffusion processes
Hint for Exercise C.1 (Map versus kernel). For the map case, use the graph coupling
(Id,T )#µ0. For a non-map kernel, take a source atom and split it between two target states, or
take any kernel K(x, dy) that is not almost surely a Dirac mass.
Page 415/416 Arthur Charpentier, 2026, /creative-commons BY-NC 4.0

=== page 416 ===
I HINTS AND SELECTED PROOF STRATEGIES
Hint for Exercise C.2 (Continuity equation). Test the law µt against a smooth compactly
supported function φ. Differentiate E[φ(Xt)], use ˙Xt = vt(Xt), then integrate by parts to
identify ∂tµt +∇· (µtvt) = 0.
Hint for Exercise C.3 (Fokker–Planck equation). Apply Itô’s formula to a smooth test
function. For constant σ, the weak form gives ∂tρt = −∇· (bρt) + (σ2/2)∆ρt. The deterministic
continuity equation is recovered when σ = 0.
Hint for Exercise C.4 (One-dimensional stress). Use the probability integral transform:
F0(X) is the rank of the baseline loss, and F−1 1 (F0(X)) has target law. Rank preservation is
useful for ordered stress tests, but it rules out random branching and should not be read as a
causal mechanism.
Hint for Exercise C.5 (Same marginals, different paths). Construct one process by
linear interpolation with small noise and another by moving out and back while keeping the same
endpoints. Path-dependent quantities such as running maxima, hitting probabilities, cumulative
losses or discounted cash-flow timing can distinguish them.
Hint for Exercise C.6 (Scenario validation). Check at least marginal fit, tail fit,
dependence, temporal dynamics, calibration to historical or expert constraints, stability under
resampling and sensitivity to conditioning variables. For capital use, tail diagnostics matter more
than average visual fit.
Hint for Exercise C.7 (Tail-aware generation). A small average Wasserstein distance
can coexist with poor extreme-tail behaviour. Compare empirical and generated VaR, TVaR,
exceedance probabilities, tail quantile slopes or distances restricted to high quantile levels.
Hint for Exercise C.8 (Schrödinger bridge interpretation). The bridge minimizes
relative entropy from a reference path law subject to endpoint constraints. It changes the baseline
generator only as much as needed, in KL terms, to match the prescribed initial and terminal
laws.
Hint for Exercise C.9 (Governance choice). Choose the modelling object first. De-
terministic maps suit auditable stress transformations; ODE flows suit smooth deterministic
evolution; diffusions suit random temporal mechanisms; Schrödinger bridges suit stochastic paths
constrained by endpoint laws and a chosen reference dynamics.
Hint for Exercise C.10 (Path time versus calendar time). Denoising time is an
algorithmic time index. It need not correspond to accident year, development year or calendar
year unless the model is trained and constrained that way. Confusing the two can create artificial
claims-development or reserve-runoff interpretations.
Hint for Exercise C.11 (Reference dynamics in a bridge). The endpoint constraints
fix only the marginals at times 0 and 1. The reference law controls what intermediate paths are
considered natural. Changing it can alter volatility, persistence, jumps or dependence even when
endpoints are unchanged.
Hint for Exercise C.12 (Mini-lab extension). A Pareto target changes the upper quantiles
much more than the centre of the distribution. Compare deterministic and noisy paths using tail
curves, VaR99%, TVaR99% and the stability of simulated extremes across seeds.
